* Plaid Cymru’s 2026 manifesto and constitutional objectives.

The last group was discussed in our earlier examination; its individual claims should be checked against the original documents if we later quote or rely on them in detail.

Overall synthesis

The conversation has moved from the immediate political disagreement over four proposed transfers of power to a wider constitutional question.

Wales has meaningful devolved institutions and substantial policy responsibilities. But its authority is constrained by reserved powers, dependence on UK funding, the continuing sovereignty of Westminster and intergovernmental arrangements that rely heavily on political cooperation.

The recurring issue is not simply whether Wales should receive more powers. It is whether responsibility, authority, resources and democratic accountability are properly aligned.

And the question we have not yet fully resolved is perhaps the most important one:

What constitutional safeguards should exist to ensure that devolution remains meaningful when the UK Government and Welsh Government fundamentally disagree about the future of Wales?”  

“I’ll check the summary against primary legislation and official Welsh and UK Government material, then separate verified facts, claims that need qualification, and points that were too confidently stated.

One important limitation in the previous summary: it listed several source references without giving their titles, publication details or direct links. I’ll make the source trail explicit here rather than treating those earlier citations as sufficient proof.

Fact-check: overall result

Largely accurate

The previous summary is broadly reliable on the constitutional framework, fiscal figures, election result and intergovernmental arrangements. However, I found several points that need correction or more precise wording.

The most important is the description of Andy Burnham’s letter: it was more nuanced than a simple rejection of all further Welsh involvement. It rejected or declined particular transfers of authority while proposing enhanced Welsh participation in some existing UK-controlled arrangements.

1. The correspondence: verified, but the summary flattened important distinctions

Verified

* The First Minister wrote to the Prime Minister on 1 October 2026.
* Andy Burnham replied on 20 November 2026.
* The Welsh Government published both letters on 23 November 2026.
* The Prime Minister’s reply is indeed five pages long. GOV.WALES +2

However, the previous summary described the letter as four broadly equivalent refusals. That misses important distinctions.

Subject	What the letter actually says	More precise description
Policing	The UK Government is not persuaded legislative devolution is necessary. It offers to strengthen Welsh involvement in policing governance within the reserved model.	Rejects legislative devolution for now; offers enhanced Welsh governance and influence.
Justice	It defends the shared England and Wales system, but discusses expanding Welsh involvement in youth justice and probation funding and oversight.	Rejects the case for a wholesale change in the settlement, while leaving some practical transfers and expanded roles under discussion.

There are also important elements missing from the previous summary:

* He says governance of Welsh rail enhancements will transfer to the Wales Rail Board.

* He supports further discussion of fiscal flexibility.
* He proposes exploring further employment-support devolution and regional empowerment.
* He refers to the Welsh Government’s proposed child payment pilot.

These are relevant because the disagreement is not simply between a Welsh Government demanding powers and a UK Government refusing everything. It concerns which kinds of authority the UK Government is prepared to transfer, and which it wants to retain. GOV.WALES

2. Plaid Cymru and independence

Verified, with a qualification

The previous summary correctly distinguished Plaid Cymru’s long-term objective from the Welsh Government’s immediate programme.

* Plaid Cymru’s constitution identifies securing independence for Wales in Europe as a party objective. ymuno.plaid.cymru
* Its 2026 programme for government commits to formally requesting the devolution of the right to decide the timetable, question and process for an independence referendum. GOV.WALES +1
* The Welsh Government has explicitly stated that there will be no independence referendum during this Senedd term. That was reaffirmed in the Senedd on 10 November 2026. Welsh Parliament +1
* The National Commission for Wales is intended to support a national constitutional conversation, develop evidence and advise on devolution priorities and wider constitutional reform. GOV.WALES +1

Qualification: The National Commission should not be described as an independence commission. Its stated remit is broader. It is concerned with Wales’s constitutional future, including further devolution and regional and local decision-making.

The previous summary was also right not to interpret the election as an independence referendum.

Official results show:

* Plaid Cymru: 43 seats and 35.4% of valid votes.
* Reform UK Wales: 34 seats.
* Plaid became the largest party but did not win a majority of the 96 seats. Vote.Wales +2

3. Constitutional law

Verified

The main legal points in the summary are correct.

* The Government of Wales Act 2006 is the central legislative framework.
* The Wales Act 2017 introduced the reserved-powers model and the permanence provision.
* Section A1 says the Senedd and Welsh Government are permanent parts of the UK’s constitutional arrangements and should not be abolished without a decision of the Welsh people in a referendum.
* Section A2 recognises that the law applying in Wales includes a body of Welsh law made by the Senedd and Welsh Ministers.
* Section 107(5) preserves Westminster’s power to legislate for Wales.
* Section 107(6) recognises that Westminster will not normally legislate on devolved matters without Senedd consent. Legislation.gov.uk +2

The previous summary’s qualification about parliamentary sovereignty is also important.

Section A1 is a statutory commitment, but it does not entrench itself against amendment or repeal. Under the orthodox doctrine of parliamentary sovereignty, a future Parliament could expressly amend or repeal it.

One point worth making more clearly: the Senedd’s permanence and the extent of its powers are separate questions. Statutory recognition of the institution does not guarantee that its existing powers, funding or responsibilities will remain unchanged.

4. Wales’s legal jurisdiction

Verified

The summary correctly distinguished:

* A distinct body of Welsh law.
* Formal recognition of a distinct Welsh legal jurisdiction.
* A fully separate Welsh court and justice system.

The first already exists. The second and third are different constitutional developments.

The legislation explicitly recognises Welsh law while retaining the wider England and Wales legal jurisdiction. Legislation.gov.uk +1

The Independent Commission on the Constitutional Future of Wales also recommended a staged approach to justice devolution, identifying policing, probation and youth justice as potential starting points. It estimated that full transfer would be a substantial undertaking requiring approximately ten years. GOV.WALES

Qualification: The earlier summary sometimes grouped prosecution, policing, probation, courts and prisons together as though each were subject to precisely the same legal reservation. They are all part of the wider justice settlement, but the statutory boundaries and exceptions differ. In particular, the Senedd can legislate about responsibility for prosecution of devolved offences in certain circumstances. Legislation.gov.uk

5. Fiscal autonomy

Figures verified

The figures in the previous summary match the Welsh Government’s Draft Budget 2027–28.

Funding source	Amount	Share
UK Government settlement	£22.3bn	78.8%
Welsh Rates of Income Tax	£4.2bn	14.9%
Non-Domestic Rates	£1.2bn	4.2%
Fully devolved taxes	£432m	2.1%

The budget’s total fiscal resource and capital figure is £28.242bn. The table uses rounded amounts and percentages. GOV.WALES +1

Other claims also check out:

* The Barnett formula calculates annual changes in the block grant, not the total size of the grant. House of Commons Library
* The Welsh Government can vary its income-tax rates, but does not control the entire UK income-tax structure.
* The six Welsh rates, including property income rates, are proposed at 10p for 2027–28, subject to Senedd approval. GOV.WALES +1
* Capital borrowing of £168m is planned for 2027–28. GOV.WALES

One useful refinement: the £432m category is specifically the two fully devolved taxes, Land Transaction Tax and Landfill Disposals Tax. Welsh Rates of Income Tax are partially devolved, not fully devolved.

The overall argument—that Wales has substantial spending-allocation discretion but limited control over its overall revenue and borrowing—is a fair interpretation of these figures.

6. Reserved powers and responsibility gaps

Broadly verified

The examples were substantially accurate, but some boundaries need to be stated carefully.

Justice and policing

* Policing and much of the criminal justice system remain reserved.
* Health, housing, education and many social services are devolved.
* This creates real interdependencies between Welsh services and UK-controlled justice institutions.
* The Independent Commission specifically identified these issues in its justice work. GOV.WALES +1

Rail

* Most of the railway system in Wales remains reserved.
* Welsh Ministers have responsibility for Wales and Borders passenger services through Transport for Wales.
* The Core Valley Lines transferred to Welsh Government ownership in 2020.
* Network Rail retains responsibility for most of the wider infrastructure. research.senedd.wales +2

The previous summary was right to mention the Core Valley Lines as an exception. It would be misleading to suggest that Wales has no rail infrastructure authority whatsoever.

Energy and the Crown Estate

* The Crown Estate’s management is reserved.
* Energy and electricity are subject to significant UK-level reservations.
* Welsh institutions nevertheless have relevant responsibilities in planning, environmental policy and some renewable-energy consents. Legislation.gov.uk +2

The conclusion that these arrangements can create responsibility-authority gaps is an analytical assessment, rather than a direct statement of law. That distinction should remain explicit.

7. Intergovernmental relations

One important correction

The description of the machinery was mostly right.

The 2022 Review established:

* Portfolio-level Interministerial Groups.
* The Interministerial Standing Committee.
* The Finance Interministerial Standing Committee.
* The Prime Minister and Heads of Devolved Governments Council.
* A formal dispute-avoidance and resolution process. GOV.UK +1

The previous summary also correctly stated that the arrangements are political commitments, not legally enforceable obligations. The Review expressly says they are not intended to create new legal obligations or be justiciable. GOV.UK

However, there is a nuance about the Secretariat.

The Welsh Government’s February 2026 Cabinet Handbook calls it an independent IGR Secretariat. The 2022 Review explains that it is staffed by officials from all governments, hosted and funded by the Cabinet Office, and accountable to the Council. Its impartiality is intended to come from serving all administrations equally. GOV.WALES +1

So it is more accurate to call it a joint, impartial intergovernmental secretariat, rather than an independent external adjudicator.

The previous summary’s other important points are verified:

* A government can refer a disagreement as a potential formal dispute.
* The Secretariat assesses whether the agreed criteria have been met.
* Third-party advice and mediation can be used, but the appointment and scope require agreement between the governments.
* The Council is the final escalation stage.
* The process does not give the Council or mediator a binding adjudicative power.
* The Welsh Government’s Cabinet Handbook says no dispute has yet completed the entire process. GOV.UK +1

The conclusion that this machinery has not yet been fully tested by a completed formal dispute is therefore accurate.

8. The 30 October meeting

The official record confirms that:

* Andy Burnham and Rhun ap Iorwerth met in Glasgow on 30 October.
* The meeting followed the National Economic Council.
* The Prime Minister and Heads of Devolved Governments Council also met that day.
* The UK Government’s public account emphasised regional empowerment, reindustrialisation and growth. GOV.UK +1

Burnham’s 20 November letter describes their 30 October discussions as productive and refers to an agreement to develop policy sprints.

Correction: The previous summary called the meeting a separate bilateral without establishing from the public records reviewed whether it was formally constituted as a distinct bilateral meeting, separate from the Council and NEC discussions. The safer wording is that they met following the NEC, with a Council meeting also taking place that day.

The precise sequence, attendees and minutes remain worth checking.

“The Participation–Authority Substitution

A constitutional distinction emerging from the Burnham–ap Iorwerth correspondence

The correction I would name is the Participation–Authority Substitution.

It describes a situation in which a government responds to a request for the transfer of constitutional powers by offering the other government greater involvement in the institutions and processes that continue to exercise those powers.

In plain terms:

You are being offered a stronger voice in decisions, rather than the authority to make those decisions yourself.

That is the distinction we need to preserve when examining the November correspondence.

1. The two different propositions

Consider two possible arrangements.

Participation

Welsh influence within UK-controlled institutions

* Welsh representation on governing boards.
* Consultation before decisions are made.
* Greater involvement in setting priorities.
* Shared oversight and reporting.
* Negotiated influence over investment or delivery.

Authority

Welsh democratic control over devolved functions

* Welsh Ministers hold the relevant executive powers.
* The Senedd can legislate within the transferred competence.
* Welsh institutions determine policy priorities.
* Welsh Ministers answer to the Senedd for decisions.
* Welsh voters can hold the responsible government accountable.

These arrangements can coexist, but they are not interchangeable.

A seat on a board does not necessarily confer control over that board. A consultation right does not necessarily confer a veto. An agreement to cooperate does not necessarily transfer legal responsibility.

The precise rights matter.

2. How it appears in the four areas

The correspondence provides four different examples of the distinction.

Policy area	UK Government’s approach	Constitutional question
Policing	Greater Welsh involvement in governance, while retaining the reserved settlement	Does representation give Wales control over policing priorities and accountability?
Justice	Expanded involvement in selected areas, including youth justice and probation discussions	Does participation address the division between Welsh social policy and UK justice institutions?
Crown Estate	Retain UK management	Can Welsh interests influence decisions without controlling the institution or its assets?
Rail	Retain the wider infrastructure settlement, while transferring some enhancement governance	Does governance of particular investments amount to control over the railway system?

GOV.WALES +1

The important point is that the UK Government is not proposing precisely the same alternative in every case.

For example, transferring governance of Welsh rail enhancements is a more substantive change than simply inviting Welsh Ministers to attend a meeting. But it is not equivalent to transferring the whole infrastructure system.

We should therefore examine each offer on its own terms rather than treating all additional participation as cosmetic.

3. Why the substitution matters constitutionally

There are four consequences.

A. Accountability can remain divided

Suppose Welsh Ministers are expected to improve a public service, but a UK-controlled institution retains the power to make a crucial decision affecting it.

Welsh Ministers may be questioned by the Senedd about the outcome, but they cannot necessarily direct the institution responsible.

The public can then encounter an accountability gap:

* The Welsh Government says it does not control the relevant decision.
* The UK Government says it has consulted Wales or provided Welsh representation.
* Neither arrangement necessarily gives Welsh voters a clear way to change the decision through Welsh elections.

This is particularly relevant to policing, justice and rail.

B. Influence can be discretionary

Participation arrangements may depend on:

* Ministerial agreement.
* Board membership.
* Terms of reference.
* Consultation protocols.
* Political relationships.
* The willingness of the UK Government to accept Welsh representations.

Unless those arrangements confer enforceable rights, they may be altered without the same constitutional process required to amend statutory devolution.

That makes participation potentially less secure than transferred authority.

C. The UK Government retains the final decision

The crucial question is not whether Wales has a voice. It is what happens when the two governments disagree.

Can the Welsh Government:

* Require a decision to be reconsidered?
* Prevent a particular decision?
* Set binding Welsh policy?
* Control the relevant budget?
* Legislate to change the arrangement?

If the answer to all of these is no, participation may improve influence without changing the underlying distribution of power.

D. It changes the nature of the negotiation

A request for devolution is a request to change the constitutional allocation of responsibility.

An offer of greater participation is a proposal to improve how the existing allocation operates.

Those are different negotiations.

The first asks: Who should hold this power?

The second asks: How can Wales have more influence while the UK retains it?

Both can be legitimate policy options. But one should not be presented as though it automatically satisfies the other.

4. When is participation a meaningful alternative?

It would be unfair to assume that every shared arrangement is inadequate.

There are circumstances where participation may be preferable or more practical:

* Cross-border services need common standards and coordination.
* Specialist institutions benefit from economies of scale.
* A transfer could create disproportionate administrative costs.
* The Welsh Government may prefer shared delivery to building a separate institution.
* A joint board could genuinely give Wales binding decision-making rights.

But we need to test the arrangements rather than judge them by their titles.

I would use six questions:

Test	What we need to establish
Representation	Does Wales have a seat and a meaningful voice?
Decision rights	Can Welsh representatives influence or determine outcomes?
Veto or consent	Can decisions affecting Wales proceed against Welsh objections?
Resources	Does Wales control the funding associated with its responsibilities?
Accountability	Who answers to the Senedd and who answers to Westminster?
Security	Can the UK Government change the arrangement unilaterally?

A joint institution with binding Welsh decision rights might be a meaningful form of shared authority. A consultative seat without decision rights is a much narrower offer.

5. The deeper issue: participation can conceal constitutional asymmetry

This is where the correction connects to our earlier discussion about intergovernmental relations.

The UK Government can argue that Wales is being treated as a partner because Welsh Ministers are consulted, represented and involved in governance.

But partnership in procedure does not necessarily mean partnership in constitutional authority.

There are two different meanings of partnership:

* Administrative partnership: governments cooperate to deliver services while one retains ultimate authority.
* Constitutional partnership: governments have protected areas of authority and agreed procedures for resolving disagreements.

The existing UK settlement relies heavily on the first, while the Welsh Government is seeking a stronger role for the second.

This is not an argument that every policy must be devolved. It is an argument that the distinction between influence and authority must be made explicit.

6. A useful addition: the participation ladder

We could use a simple ladder to classify what the UK Government is actually offering.

From consultation to constitutional control

* Information
    Wales is told what has been decided.
* Consultation
    Wales can express a view before a decision.
* Representation
    Welsh representatives participate in a governing forum.
* Influence
    Welsh representatives can shape priorities and proposals.
* Shared decision-making
    Welsh agreement is required for specified decisions.
* Devolved authority
    Welsh institutions exercise their own statutory powers and answer democratically for them.

This is an analytical framework, not a formal legal classification. Actual arrangements may combine levels or provide different rights for different decisions.

The ladder helps prevent a common mistake: describing a move from consultation to representation as though it were equivalent to a transfer of power.

7. What should we ask of Burnham and ap Iorwerth?

The question should be put to both governments.

To the UK Government:

If enhanced Welsh participation is considered sufficient, what specific decision-making rights, budgetary powers, consent requirements and protections will accompany it?

To the Welsh Government:

Which functions genuinely require full devolution, which could be exercised through binding shared governance, and which benefits of UK-wide institutions would Wales want to preserve?

That prevents the debate from becoming a simplistic choice between total centralisation and complete institutional separation.

8. The Polaris concept

I would preserve this as a distinct analytical concept:

⚖️ The Participation–Authority Substitution

When increased representation, consultation or involvement is offered as an answer to a demand for transferred constitutional authority.

Core proposition

Participation can increase influence without transferring control. The two should never be treated as equivalent without examining the underlying decision rights.

Diagnostic question

When a government offers another government a stronger voice, what power does the receiving government actually gain?

Related concepts

* Democratic accountability
* Reserved powers
* Delegation versus devolution
* Intergovernmental relations
* Shared governance
* Constitutional asymmetry
* Institutional capture and unilateral discretion

I would keep this concept analytically neutral. Participation is not inherently inadequate, and devolution is not automatically the right answer in every case. The issue is whether the proposed arrangement matches the responsibility, accountability and authority being claimed.

The sentence I would retain is:

A stronger voice in the exercise of another government’s powers is not the same thing as acquiring the authority to exercise those powers yourself.

That is the correction to the earlier summary, and it gives us a useful tool for examining not only the Burnham correspondence but the wider architecture of Welsh devolution.”.  

“The primary legislation governing the allocation of powers to Wales

The starting point is the Government of Wales Act 2006, as amended, but it is not the whole settlement. The allocation of powers has been constructed through successive Acts of Parliament, especially the Wales Acts of 2014 and 2017.

I’ll look at this as a constitutional architecture rather than just a list of devolved and reserved subjects. The important questions are: who can legislate, who can exercise executive functions, which matters are reserved, and what happens when the UK and Welsh institutions disagree.

The review reveals something important for our previous discussion: the Welsh settlement is not simply a list of subjects that belong to Cardiff and subjects that belong to Westminster. It is a layered legal arrangement governing legislative competence, executive functions, institutional control and financial powers.

That matters because the different layers do not always line up.

1. The legislative history: how the present settlement was constructed

There are four particularly important Acts.

* Government of Wales Act 1998
    The original devolution settlement
    Established the National Assembly for Wales and transferred a range of existing administrative responsibilities. The original Assembly combined legislative and executive functions in one institution. Legislation.gov.uk
* Government of Wales Act 2006
    Institutional separation and stronger lawmaking powers
    Separated the legislature from the Welsh Government, established the modern institutional structure and provided the route towards primary legislative powers. Legislation.gov.uk +1
* Wales Act 2014
    Fiscal devolution
    Established important powers over taxation and borrowing, including the framework for Welsh income-tax rates and devolved taxes. Legislation.gov.uk +1
* Wales Act 2017
    The present reserved-powers model
    Replaced the old conferred-powers model with a reserved-powers model, introduced the current Schedules 7A and 7B, and inserted statutory recognition of the permanence of the Senedd and Welsh Government. Legislation.gov.uk +2

The Senedd and Elections (Wales) Act 2020 subsequently changed the institution’s name and made important changes to its electoral arrangements, including extending the franchise to 16- and 17-year-olds. It did not fundamentally redesign the division of legislative competence. Legislation.gov.uk

The key point: the Government of Wales Act 2006, as amended, is now the central constitutional statute. The 2014 and 2017 Acts substantially changed the powers arranged by it.

2. The four legal layers of the settlement

I think this is the most useful way to understand the legislation.

Layer 1 — Legislative competence

Who has the legal authority to make laws?

Governed principally by section 108A and Schedules 7A and 7B of the 2006 Act.

Layer 2 — Executive functions

Who has the authority to administer, regulate, direct and implement policy?

Governed by Part 2 of the 2006 Act, including sections 58 and 58A, alongside other statutory provisions and transfer instruments.

Layer 3 — Institutional authority

Who controls the public bodies, agencies, tribunals and institutions carrying out those functions?

Governed by the relevant subject-specific legislation, the reserved-authority rules and other statutory arrangements.

Layer 4 — Financial authority

Who raises the money, controls the budget and can borrow?

Governed by the fiscal provisions of the 2006 Act as amended, the Wales Acts and the fiscal framework.

These are distinct legal questions. A transfer at one layer does not automatically transfer the others.

That is a crucial qualification to our earlier discussion of the Participation–Authority Substitution.

3. Layer 1: legislative competence — section 108A and Schedules 7A and 7B

The current model is known as the reserved-powers model.

Section 108A provides that an Act of the Senedd is outside its legislative competence if, among other things, it:

* Extends beyond the permitted territorial limits.
* Applies outside Wales, subject to certain exceptions.
* Relates to a reserved matter in Schedule 7A.
* Breaches restrictions in Schedule 7B.
* Is incompatible with Convention rights. Legislation.gov.uk

This is important because it means the Senedd does not need Westminster to give it permission to legislate separately on every devolved subject.

The starting presumption is that it can legislate unless a legal restriction applies.

Schedule 7A: what is reserved?

Schedule 7A contains the reserved matters.

Some major categories include:

Category	Examples
Constitution	The Crown, the Union of Wales and England, the UK Parliament
National security	Defence, intelligence, national security
International affairs	Foreign relations and international trade
Justice	Courts, judges, criminal and civil proceedings
Crime and policing	Crime prevention, public order, policing and police and crime commissioners
Crown Estate	Management of the Crown Estate
Other UK-wide functions	Various transport, energy, economic and regulatory matters

Legislation.gov.uk +2

Two details are especially relevant to our examination.

First, policing is expressly reserved.

Schedule 7A, Part 2, Section B5, paragraphs 39–42 reserves crime prevention and investigation, public order, policing, and police and crime commissioners, subject to a specific exception concerning certain powers of entry, search and seizure relating to devolved offences. Legislation.gov.uk

Second, management of the Crown Estate is expressly reserved.

That is not merely a consequence of the general reservation of constitutional matters. The legislation specifically preserves the reservation of Crown Estate management. Legislation.gov.uk

Schedule 7B: additional restrictions

This schedule is particularly important and deserves more attention than it received in our earlier conversation.

It places restrictions on what the Senedd can do even when legislation concerns a devolved subject.

For example:

* It generally prevents the Senedd from modifying the law on reserved matters.
* It restricts changes to private law and criminal law.
* It restricts changes to certain protected legislation.
* It imposes consent requirements when Senedd legislation would confer, modify or remove certain functions of reserved authorities.

Legislation.gov.uk +1

The last point is significant.

A subject may be devolved, but the public body responsible for carrying out part of it may be a reserved authority. The Senedd can therefore encounter restrictions when trying to make that body participate in a Welsh policy arrangement.

This is a statutory source of the responsibility-authority problems we identified earlier.

The Independent Commission on the Constitutional Future of Wales criticised these rules as complicated and difficult to operate, particularly where Welsh legislation needs to involve UK-controlled public bodies in devolved policy. GOV.WALES

4. Layer 2: executive functions — sections 58 and 58A

This is where the legal arrangement becomes more interesting.

Legislative competence and executive authority are not the same thing.

Section 58 provides a mechanism for transferring ministerial functions from UK Ministers to Welsh Ministers.

It can provide for functions to be:

* Transferred to Welsh Ministers.
* Exercised concurrently or jointly with UK Ministers.
* Exercised by UK Ministers only with Welsh ministerial agreement.
* Exercised by UK Ministers only after consultation with Welsh Ministers.

Legislation.gov.uk

This is directly relevant to our Participation–Authority Substitution concept.

The Act itself provides a legal mechanism for different degrees of Welsh involvement without necessarily transferring the whole policy area.

But those arrangements differ in legal strength.

For example:

Arrangement	Welsh position
Consultation	Welsh Ministers must be consulted where the instrument requires it
Agreement	UK Ministers cannot exercise the specified function without Welsh agreement
Concurrent exercise	Both governments can exercise the specified function
Transfer	The function is transferred to Welsh Ministers

These are not equivalent arrangements.

Section 58A also provides that executive ministerial functions within devolved competence are exercisable by Welsh Ministers, subject to its provisions and exceptions. Legislation.gov.uk

An important implication: an area can be legally reserved in some respects while Welsh Ministers nevertheless have specific executive responsibilities or participation rights under legislation or transfer arrangements.

This is one reason why simply labelling a subject devolved or reserved can be misleading.

5. Layer 3: institutional authority

This is the part of the settlement I think we should examine more closely in the next stage.

The legislation distinguishes between:

* Devolved Welsh authorities.
* Reserved authorities.

A devolved Welsh authority is broadly a public body whose functions and institutional accountability meet the statutory tests for that status.

A reserved authority includes UK Ministers, government departments and other public authorities that do not qualify as devolved Welsh authorities.

The distinction matters because Schedule 7B restricts the Senedd’s ability to change the functions, constitution or funding arrangements of reserved authorities without the appropriate UK ministerial consent. Legislation.gov.uk +1

This creates an important legal distinction:

Having legislative competence over a policy subject does not necessarily mean having authority over every public institution involved in delivering that policy.

For example, Welsh legislation concerning public health may need cooperation from a UK-controlled body whose powers are not under Welsh ministerial control.

That is not automatically a constitutional defect. There may be legitimate reasons for maintaining UK-wide institutions.

But it does mean the actual distribution of institutional authority must be examined separately from the distribution of policy responsibilities.

6. Layer 4: financial authority

The Wales Act 2014 and the subsequent amendments to the 2006 Act created a framework for Welsh taxation and borrowing.

The important distinction is between:

* Taxation powers.
* Budget allocation powers.
* Borrowing powers.
* Control over the block grant.

Wales has devolved taxes and income-tax rate-setting powers, but it does not control the whole tax system or the overall UK fiscal settlement.

Section 121 of the Government of Wales Act 2006 provides the statutory basis for Welsh Ministers’ borrowing, including limited resource borrowing and borrowing for capital expenditure subject to Treasury approval. Legislation.gov.uk +1

This reinforces our earlier finding: financial authority is a separate dimension of devolution. Transferring a policy responsibility without the necessary financial powers can leave the receiving government dependent on another government’s budget decisions.

7. The constitutional safeguards

There are three particularly important provisions.

Section A1 — Permanence

The Senedd and Welsh Government are recognised as permanent parts of the UK’s constitutional arrangements.

Section A2 — Recognition of Welsh law

The legislation recognises that the law applying in Wales includes a body of Welsh law made by the Senedd and Welsh Ministers.

Both provisions were introduced through the Wales Act 2017. Legislation.gov.uk +1

Section 107(5) and (6) — Westminster’s legislative authority and the Sewel Convention

Section 107(5) preserves the UK Parliament’s power to legislate for Wales.

Section 107(6) recognises that Westminster will not normally legislate on devolved matters without Senedd consent. Legislation.gov.uk

The combination is important:

* Wales has its own legislature and legal system of devolved law.
* Westminster retains ultimate legislative sovereignty.
* The statutory recognition of permanence does not entrench the Senedd against repeal.
* The Sewel Convention does not create an enforceable legal veto.

There is therefore a distinction between the constitutional recognition of Welsh institutions and the legal entrenchment of their authority.

8. One further mechanism: section 109

This deserves specific attention because it is a mechanism for changing the boundary itself.

Section 109 allows Schedules 7A and 7B to be amended by Order in Council.

A draft order must be approved by both Houses of the UK Parliament and the Senedd. Legislation.gov.uk

This means the reserved-powers settlement is not entirely fixed in the primary legislation.

It can be adjusted through a statutory mechanism involving both legislatures.

That is significant for the current debate: not every change to the allocation of legislative competence requires a completely new Wales Act.

However, section 109 does not mean that all executive functions, institutional arrangements or financial powers can be transferred through that mechanism. The relevant statutory route depends on what exactly is being changed.

9. What this tells us about the Burnham correspondence

We can now make a more precise legal assessment of the four areas.

Area	Relevant statutory question
Policing	What changes to Schedule 7A and related legislation would be required to transfer legislative competence and executive responsibility?
Justice	Which individual functions are reserved, which are already devolved, and which can be transferred or shared under existing statutory powers?
Crown Estate	What legislative changes would be needed to remove the express reservation of its management, and what would happen to ownership, revenue and institutional responsibilities?
Rail	Which powers concern infrastructure ownership, investment, regulation, passenger services and enhancement governance, and which are already exercisable by Welsh institutions?

This is where I would be particularly careful with the language of the letters.

A commitment to a new board, a transfer of a specific executive function, a change to legislative competence and a transfer of institutional ownership are four different legal outcomes.

They should not be described interchangeably as devolution.

10. My revised constitutional reading

The primary legislation reveals three structural features that we should carry into the next stage of our examination.

First: Wales has a real legislative settlement.

The Senedd is not simply an administrative body. It has primary legislative powers within its competence, and its Acts have legal force.

Second: the settlement contains multiple layers of control.

Legislative competence, executive functions, public-body authority and financial powers do not necessarily sit with the same government.

Third: Westminster retains a constitutional advantage.

It retains legal sovereignty, controls the reserved matters and has substantial influence over the financial and institutional framework. Welsh institutions have meaningful powers, but those powers are not constitutionally entrenched to the same degree as the sovereignty of the UK Parliament.

My main correction to our earlier analysis is this:

The Welsh devolution settlement is not merely a division between devolved and reserved subjects. It is a division of legislative, executive, institutional and financial authority, with different legal rules governing each layer.

That is a more accurate foundation for examining whether the current arrangement is coherent.

Primary legislation: the core reading list

These are the documents I would treat as the legal spine of the project.

* Government of Wales Act 2006 — current revised text⁠￼.
* Government of Wales Act 1998 — original devolution legislation⁠￼.
* Wales Act 2014 — taxation and borrowing⁠￼.
* Wales Act 2017 — explanatory notes and constitutional changes⁠￼.
* Wales Act 2017, Schedule 1 — reserved matters⁠￼.
* Wales Act 2017, Schedule 2 — restrictions on Senedd legislation⁠￼.
* Senedd and Elections (Wales) Act 2020⁠￼.

The next particularly valuable exercise would be a provision-by-provision examination of Schedule 7A and Schedule 7B, identifying which reservations are genuinely UK-wide, which create dependencies on UK-controlled institutions, and which restrict the Senedd’s ability to make its existing devolved responsibilities work effectively.

That would move us from a general debate about the merits of devolution to a precise map of where Welsh authority begins, where it stops, and why.”  

“Before 1998, Wales had administrative devolution, but no elected national legislature or devolved Welsh Government.

The distinction is fundamental. Wales had a recognisable system of Welsh administration, Welsh-specific public institutions and a Secretary of State representing Welsh interests in the UK Cabinet. But ultimate legislative authority remained at Westminster.

In other words, Wales was governed through a combination of UK Government departments, the Welsh Office, local authorities and appointed public bodies—not through a democratically elected Welsh Parliament.

1. The arrangement before 1964: Westminster and administrative decentralisation

Following the Laws in Wales Acts of the sixteenth century, Wales was incorporated into the English legal and administrative system.

Important historical developments included:

* The Acts of Union of 1536 and 1543 integrated Wales into the English legal and governmental structure.
* The Court of Great Sessions, which had provided a distinctive Welsh judicial structure, was abolished in 1830.
* Wales and England consequently developed as a single legal jurisdiction.
* Welsh-specific legislation was still possible, but was generally enacted by the UK Parliament.

There were some Welsh administrative institutions before 1964:

* A Welsh Department within the Board of Education was established in 1907.
* A Welsh Board of Health followed in 1919.
* The Council for Wales and Monmouthshire was established in 1948 as an unelected advisory body.

The Council could advise and draw attention to Welsh needs, but it was not a Welsh government. senedd.wales +2

2. 1964–1997: the Welsh Office model

In 1964, the Labour Government created the post of Secretary of State for Wales.

The Welsh Office was established in 1965, with offices in Cardiff and Whitehall.

This was a significant development because it created a dedicated central government department responsible for administering an expanding range of public functions in Wales.

The Secretary of State for Wales was:

* A UK Government minister.
* A member of the UK Cabinet.
* Accountable to the UK Parliament.
* Responsible for administering functions transferred to the Welsh Office.
* Able to represent Welsh administrative and policy interests within the UK Government.

The Welsh Office gradually acquired responsibilities including:

* Housing.
* Local government.
* Planning.
* Roads and highways.
* Education.
* Health.
* Agriculture.
* Economic development.
* Welsh language matters.
* Environmental and countryside functions.

By 1997, it employed more than 2,500 civil servants. Research Briefings +2

What did this mean in practice?

The UK Parliament passed primary legislation.

The Secretary of State and Welsh Office administered policy and exercised executive powers within the authority given to them by Parliament.

Welsh local authorities and public bodies delivered many services.

The Secretary of State could make decisions specifically affecting Wales, but was accountable to Westminster rather than to a Welsh legislature.

That is what we mean by administrative devolution: government functions are administered separately for Wales, without transferring ultimate legislative authority to a Welsh democratic institution.

3. The quango system: an important part of the picture

There was another layer that deserves attention.

Many public functions were delivered through appointed bodies rather than directly by the Welsh Office.

These were commonly called quangos: quasi-autonomous non-governmental organisations.

Examples included bodies involved in:

* Economic development.
* Housing.
* Arts and culture.
* Education and training.
* Environmental management.
* Regional development.

The House of Commons Welsh Affairs Committee reported that the number of quangos in Wales doubled from 40 to 80 between 1979 and 1994. By 1995, approximately 1,400 people had been appointed to them. First Report +1

This matters to our wider examination because it shows that the absence of a Welsh legislature did not mean the absence of Welsh-specific administration.

Rather, Wales had a substantial administrative infrastructure, but many of its governing arrangements were either centrally controlled or populated through appointments.

The democratic accountability question was therefore already present before devolution.

4. The 1979 referendum: an earlier attempt at devolution

The 1970s saw an attempt to establish an elected Welsh Assembly.

The Labour Government’s proposals were developed following the Kilbrandon Commission on the Constitution.

The proposed Assembly would have had executive responsibilities but would not have possessed the kind of primary legislative authority that the Senedd has today.

The Wales Act 1978 provided the legislative framework, subject to approval in a referendum.

Rejected

On 1 March 1979, Welsh voters rejected the proposals:

Vote	Share
No	79.7%
Yes	20.3%

The legislation was subsequently repealed. The proposed Assembly was never established. UK Parliament +2

This is important: the 1998 settlement was not the first attempt at Welsh devolution. It followed an earlier rejection and a substantial period in which administrative devolution continued without an elected national legislature.

5. 1997–1999: the transition to democratic devolution

The 1997 Labour Government proposed a new settlement.

On 18 September 1997, Wales voted narrowly in favour of establishing a National Assembly:

Vote	Share
Yes	50.3%
No	49.7%

The majority was just 6,721 votes. House of Commons Library +1

The Government of Wales Act 1998 received Royal Assent on 31 July 1998.

The first Assembly election took place on 6 May 1999, and the Assembly began operating in July 1999.

The initial settlement was relatively limited.

The Assembly:

* Could exercise executive functions transferred from the Secretary of State.
* Could make secondary legislation where authorised by Acts of Parliament.
* Could scrutinise and approve Welsh policy and spending.
* Could not pass its own primary legislation.
* Could not independently raise taxes.

The Assembly initially operated as a single corporate body combining executive and scrutiny functions. First Report +2

This was a significant constitutional change, but it was not yet the modern Senedd settlement.

6. Comparing the arrangements

Before and after devolution

Before 1998

Administrative devolution

* Secretary of State for Wales.
* Welsh Office.
* UK Parliament makes primary legislation.
* Welsh-specific administration.
* Local authorities and appointed public bodies deliver services.
* No elected Welsh national legislature.

From 1999

Executive devolution

* Elected National Assembly.
* Welsh executive functions transferred.
* Secondary legislative powers.
* Democratic scrutiny within Wales.
* Westminster retains primary legislative authority.

From 2007 onwards

Legislative devolution

* Separate Welsh legislature and executive.
* Primary legislative powers developed in stages.
* The 2011 referendum approved broader lawmaking powers.
* The 2017 Wales Act introduced the reserved-powers model.
* The modern Senedd can legislate within its statutory competence.

7. The deeper constitutional significance

I think there are three historical points particularly relevant to our current examination.

First: Wales already had administrative differentiation before democratic devolution.

The Welsh Office was not simply a London department with a Welsh name. It administered substantial functions specifically for Wales, and its responsibilities grew considerably over time.

Second: administrative devolution did not provide equivalent democratic accountability.

The Secretary of State was accountable to Westminster. Welsh people could vote in UK general elections, but they could not elect a Welsh legislature to scrutinise the Welsh administration independently.

Third: the modern settlement evolved from an administrative structure rather than replacing it wholesale.

The 1998 Act transferred many existing functions into a new democratic structure. Later legislation progressively separated institutions, expanded lawmaking powers and changed the rules governing which matters could be devolved.

That historical inheritance helps explain why some institutional arrangements remain complicated today.

8. A particularly important question for our wider investigation

There is a constitutional question here that I would not want us to overlook:

Was the modern Welsh settlement designed to give Wales democratic control over an existing Welsh administrative apparatus, or to establish a genuinely new distribution of sovereignty and governmental authority?

Historically, it did much more of the former at first, and progressively moved towards the latter.

That helps explain the successive stages:

* 1964–65: administrative devolution through the Welsh Office.
* 1979: proposed executive devolution rejected.
* 1997–99: elected executive devolution established.
* 2007: separation of legislature and executive, with enhanced legislative arrangements.
* 2011–17: broader lawmaking powers and the reserved-powers model.

My suggestion for the next historical question is the transition from the 1998 Act to the 2006 Act. That is where Wales moved from an Assembly that largely administered powers granted by Westminster towards a legislature with its own primary lawmaking competence. It is also where the present distinction between administrative participation and constitutional authority becomes much easier to understand.

Core historical sources

* Senedd — History of Devolution⁠￼.
* House of Commons Library — Twenty Years of Devolution in Wales, 1998–2018⁠￼.
* House of Commons Library — Devolution in Wales: A Process, Not an Event⁠￼.
* Commission on Devolution in Wales — Empowerment and Responsibility, 2014⁠￼.
* House of Commons Welsh Affairs Committee — Historical background to the 2006 settlement⁠￼.”  

“A history of modern Welsh nationalism

Modern Welsh nationalism is best understood as a movement that has changed its emphasis several times: from cultural survival, to home rule, to political self-government, and then to a broader debate about national sovereignty and the future of the Union.

It has never been a single, uniform movement. Welsh nationalism has included language campaigners, cultural traditionalists, socialists, liberals, constitutional reformers and advocates of full independence. Some have wanted an independent Wales; others have wanted Wales to govern more of its own affairs while remaining within the United Kingdom.

One important distinction throughout this history is that Welsh national identity is much broader than support for Plaid Cymru or Welsh independence.

1. The nineteenth-century foundations: culture becomes political

The modern movement developed during the nineteenth century, against a background of industrialisation, religious dissent, the growth of Welsh cultural institutions and concern about the decline of the Welsh language.

Several developments mattered:

* Nonconformity: Wales developed a strong tradition of independent Protestant chapels, often in opposition to the established Church of England. This helped create a distinctive Welsh public culture.
* The language: Welsh remained central to national identity, but industrialisation, migration and the growing dominance of English placed it under pressure.
* National institutions: the University of Wales was established in 1893; the National Library and National Museum followed in the early twentieth century.
* Political home rule: campaigners increasingly argued that Wales should have institutions capable of making decisions about Welsh affairs.

One influential figure was Michael D. Jones, a Congregationalist minister who advocated Welsh cultural and political autonomy. He is sometimes described as a father of modern Welsh nationalism.

In 1886, Cymru Fydd (Young Wales) emerged, advocating Welsh home rule. It was associated with prominent Liberal figures, including T. E. Ellis and David Lloyd George.

It is important not to project today’s independence debate backwards: much of this early home-rule movement sought self-government within a wider British political framework, rather than a separate Welsh state. senedd.wales +2

2. 1925: Plaid Cymru and political nationalism

Plaid Genedlaethol Cymru, later known as Plaid Cymru, was founded in 1925 at Pwllheli.

Its establishment marked a shift: Welsh nationalism now had a dedicated political party whose central purpose was to advance Welsh national interests and self-government.

One of its leading founders was Saunders Lewis.

The early party was relatively small and culturally distinctive. It placed considerable emphasis on:

* Protecting and restoring the Welsh language.
* Preserving Welsh culture and traditions.
* Establishing Welsh political self-government.
* Challenging the assumption that Wales should simply be administered as an extension of England.

Early Plaid was not identical to the party of today. Saunders Lewis’s nationalism was culturally conservative, Catholic and strongly language-centred. Later generations of Plaid politicians would develop quite different political and economic approaches.

The party gradually moved towards conventional electoral politics, particularly under Gwynfor Evans. National Library of Wales +2

3. The 1960s and 1970s: language activism, protest and a new political confidence

This was a formative period. Welsh nationalism became more visible, more activist and more diverse.

Three developments were especially important.

The flooding of Tryweryn (1965). Liverpool Corporation’s reservoir project submerged Capel Celyn, a Welsh-speaking community in the Tryweryn valley. The scheme was authorised by an Act of Parliament despite opposition from Welsh local authorities and the community. It became a lasting symbol of the feeling that decisions affecting Wales could be made elsewhere, with Welsh objections overridden.

The language movement. In 1962, Saunders Lewis delivered his radio address Tynged yr Iaith — The Fate of the Language. He warned that Welsh risked disappearing as a community language and called for direct action. This helped inspire the formation of Cymdeithas yr Iaith Gymraeg (the Welsh Language Society), which used civil disobedience and public protest to demand recognition for Welsh.

Its campaigns included bilingual road signs, Welsh-language public services and eventually a Welsh-language television channel. The Welsh Language Act 1967 and Welsh Language Act 1993 were important legislative milestones; S4C began broadcasting in 1982. OpenLearn - Open University +2

Plaid’s electoral breakthrough. Gwynfor Evans won the Carmarthen by-election in 1966, becoming Plaid Cymru’s first MP. The party subsequently won three Welsh seats in the February and October 1974 general elections.

The movement was no longer confined to cultural campaigning or a small circle of intellectuals. It had an electoral presence, a language campaign and a wider argument about who should make decisions for Wales. National Library of Wales +1

The 1979 referendum: a major setback

The proposed Welsh Assembly was rejected in the 1979 referendum by approximately 79.7% to 20.3%.

This was a defeat for constitutional devolution, but it did not end Welsh nationalism. The language movement continued, and political nationalism gradually recovered during the later 1980s.

The distinction matters: opposition to that particular Assembly proposal did not necessarily mean opposition to Welsh identity, language protection or every form of self-government. senedd.wales +1

4. The 1980s and 1990s: language rights and the return of devolution

During the 1980s, Welsh nationalism developed along several connected but distinct paths.

* Language campaigners pursued official recognition, education, broadcasting and protection for Welsh-speaking communities.
* Plaid Cymru continued to contest elections and broadened its appeal beyond Welsh speakers.
* Opposition to the Conservative governments of the period contributed to renewed interest in Welsh political representation.
* The campaign for a Welsh-language television service helped secure S4C in 1982.
* The Welsh Language Act 1993 strengthened the position of Welsh in public administration and the legal system.

Plaid also moved towards a more explicitly civic conception of Welsh nationalism: Welsh nationhood was not to be defined exclusively by ancestry, birthplace or fluency in Welsh. People who made Wales their home could belong to the political nation.

This was an important change. It made it easier to build alliances with people who supported Welsh self-government but did not speak Welsh, and with people whose primary concerns were economic, social or democratic rather than linguistic. OpenLearn - Open University +2

1997: the constitutional breakthrough

The referendum on establishing a Welsh Assembly passed by a very narrow margin: 50.3% voted Yes and 49.7% No.

The National Assembly for Wales began operating in 1999.

This did not establish independence. It established democratic devolution: a Welsh institution with executive and, initially, limited secondary legislative powers.

But it changed the political landscape. Welsh self-government was no longer simply a demand made by nationalists. It had become an established part of the UK’s constitutional arrangements. senedd.wales +1

5. 1999–2011: nationalism becomes part of governing Wales

The establishment of the Assembly changed Plaid Cymru’s position.

In the first Assembly election in 1999, Plaid became the second-largest party. It was now operating inside the institution that Welsh nationalists had campaigned to establish.

The party increasingly had to address the practical questions of governing:

* Health and social care.
* Education.
* Economic development.
* Transport.
* Rural communities.
* Public finance.
* The distribution of powers between Cardiff and Westminster.

In 2007, Plaid entered a coalition government with Welsh Labour under the One Wales agreement. Plaid’s Ieuan Wyn Jones became Deputy First Minister.

This was a significant development: a party committed to Welsh independence was now participating in the administration of a devolved government within the United Kingdom.

In 2011, a referendum approved the transfer of full primary lawmaking powers to the Assembly in its devolved areas. This was another major step in the development of Welsh political autonomy. OpenLearn - Open University +2

6. 2011–2020: from devolution to the independence debate

During this period, the argument increasingly shifted from whether Wales should have devolution to what devolution should mean.

The Scottish independence referendum in 2014 was particularly influential. It prompted renewed discussion in Wales about the future of the Union and whether Wales should have the same constitutional choices as Scotland.

YesCymru emerged from this environment. It began organising in 2014 and formally launched as a membership organisation in 2016. Unlike Plaid Cymru, it was designed as a non-party campaigning movement specifically for Welsh independence.

That distinction is important:

* Plaid Cymru is a political party with a wider programme of policies and a long-standing constitutional commitment to independence.
* YesCymru is a campaign organisation whose central objective is independence, and which seeks support across party lines.

The two movements overlap, but they are not interchangeable. Nation.Cymru +1

The 2016 Brexit referendum also affected the constitutional debate. It raised questions about Wales’s relationship with the UK, the European Union and the distribution of power between the different nations of the UK.

The Senedd was renamed in 2020, formally recognising its status as the Welsh Parliament. The institution had developed considerably from the limited Assembly established in 1999. senedd.wales +1

7. The 2020s: a broader and more varied independence conversation

In recent years, Welsh independence has become a more visible subject of public debate.

The arguments advanced by supporters include:

* Democratic self-determination.
* Greater control over taxation and public spending.
* The ability to develop economic policy around Welsh circumstances.
* Control over natural resources and energy.
* A different relationship with the European Union.
* Greater accountability for decisions made in Wales.

Opponents and sceptics raise questions about:

* The fiscal and economic viability of independence.
* The distribution of public spending and taxation.
* The costs of establishing separate state institutions.
* Defence, currency, borders and international relations.
* The practical relationship between an independent Wales and the rest of the UK.

These are questions about the proposed constitutional and economic settlement, not simply questions of national identity.

It is also important not to equate support for more devolution with support for independence. Someone can feel strongly Welsh, want more powers for the Senedd, and still prefer Wales to remain within the UK.

Likewise, someone can support independence without speaking Welsh or having a family history rooted in Wales.

The modern movement contains all these positions.

8. The main transformations

The history can be condensed into this timeline:

Period	Main development	Central concern
1880s–1900s	Cymru Fydd and home rule	Welsh political recognition
1925–1950s	Plaid Cymru established	National self-government and cultural survival
1960s–70s	Language activism and electoral growth	Language rights, democratic voice and control over Welsh affairs
1979	Devolution referendum rejected	Constitutional setback
1980s–90s	Language rights and renewed home-rule campaign	Recognition and accountability
1997–99	Assembly established	Democratic devolution
2007–11	Plaid enters government; legislative powers expand	Governing and strengthening autonomy
2014 onwards	YesCymru and renewed independence debate	National sovereignty and the future of the Union
2020s	Independence becomes a more prominent constitutional question	What powers should Wales ultimately possess?

9. Five things worth keeping distinct

I think these distinctions are particularly important for understanding the correspondence between Burnham and ap Iorwerth.

1. Welsh national identity: a sense of belonging to a Welsh nation. It does not automatically imply a particular constitutional preference.

2. Welsh cultural nationalism: the protection and development of Welsh language, culture, history and institutions.

3. Welsh political nationalism: the belief that Wales should have its own political institutions and greater control over its affairs.

4. Welsh constitutional nationalism: the argument that the present devolution settlement should be substantially strengthened, potentially towards federalism or a much more autonomous Wales.

5. Welsh independence: the proposition that Wales should become a sovereign state.

These strands overlap, but they are not identical.

10. The underlying historical pattern

One recurring theme runs through the whole story: the question of whether Wales is merely a distinctive part of the British state to be administered appropriately, or a nation entitled to exercise political authority in its own right.

That question has appeared in different forms:

* In the nineteenth century, through demands for home rule.
* In the 1960s, through the reaction to Tryweryn and campaigns for language recognition.
* In the 1970s, through the first devolution referendum.
* In the 1990s, through the campaign for an elected Assembly.
* In the 2010s and 2020s, through debates about fiscal powers, constitutional autonomy and independence.

There is an important connection to our earlier discussion of the Participation–Authority Substitution.

Historically, Welsh nationalists have repeatedly sought more than recognition, consultation or representation. They have sought the capacity to make decisions and to be democratically accountable for them.

That does not mean every Welsh voter wants independence, or that every reserved power should necessarily be transferred. It does mean that a political settlement which offers Wales a stronger voice without addressing where ultimate decision-making authority lies may leave the underlying constitutional question unresolved.

My central observation: modern Welsh nationalism has not simply been a continuous campaign for independence. It has been a changing movement for national recognition, cultural security and democratic authority. Independence is one possible destination within that history, but it was not the original or exclusive objective of everyone involved.

For further reading, the most useful starting points are the National Library of Wales’s Plaid Cymru centenary history⁠￼, the Open University’s history of Welsh nationalism and language activism⁠￼, and the Senedd’s history of devolution⁠￼.”.  

“MOD control of military bases in Wales under further constitutional separation

The starting point is that the Ministry of Defence currently controls its military estate in Wales as part of the United Kingdom’s defence system. The Welsh Government does not have a general power to direct, close, approve or veto military operations from those bases.

If the Union became more federal, or Wales acquired substantially greater autonomy, that position would not necessarily change. If Wales became independent, however, the question would become much more substantial: would the bases become Welsh assets, remain British military installations under agreement, or be transferred or closed?

Those are three different constitutional scenarios, and they need separating.

1. The current position: defence is reserved to Westminster

The Government of Wales Act 2006, Schedule 7A, paragraph 11, reserves:

* Defence of the realm.
* The naval, military and air forces of the Crown.
* Visiting forces.
* International headquarters and defence organisations.

So defence is not simply an area in which the UK Government happens to retain practical control. It is expressly outside the Senedd’s legislative competence. Legislation.gov.uk +1

There is also a practical agreement between the Welsh Government and MOD: the 2010 Defence Concordat.

It says that:

* Defence of the UK remains the direct responsibility of the UK Government.
* MOD should consider Welsh Government responsibilities when developing defence policy affecting Wales.
* Welsh Ministers should consider defence implications when exercising devolved functions.
* Both sides should consult one another where their responsibilities overlap.

But the Concordat is non-statutory and not legally binding. It establishes cooperation, not Welsh control over defence. GOV.WALES

That is a good example of the distinction we have been examining: consultation and coordination do not amount to decision-making authority.

2. What military infrastructure does the UK currently have in Wales?

Official MOD Defence Footprint 2026; selected sites, not an exhaustive list.

That creates an important practical issue.

An independent Wales might want stronger environmental safeguards, restoration obligations or public-access arrangements. But if the UK retained a military installation under a treaty, the two states would have to agree how those obligations operated alongside military requirements.

That is not merely theoretical. The existing defence relationship already involves negotiations between devolved environmental responsibilities and reserved defence functions.

6. The NATO question

This deserves particular attention.

An independent Wales would not automatically inherit the UK’s NATO membership. Article 10 of the North Atlantic Treaty provides for the invitation of additional European states by unanimous agreement among existing members. NATO Official text +1

There are several possible arrangements:

* Wales joins NATO in its own right.
* Wales establishes a bilateral defence agreement with the UK.
* Wales remains outside NATO but develops other security partnerships.
* Wales negotiates a combination of these arrangements.

If Wales joined NATO, it would have its own obligations as a member state. It would not simply be a region whose security policy was determined in London.

There would also be a question about whether any UK military facilities retained in Wales served NATO purposes, exclusively UK purposes, or both.

Those distinctions would need to be written into agreements.

7. My assessment: the bases are a constitutional question, not just a defence question

I would put this into three separate categories:

Issue	Current position	Under independence
Command of armed forces	UK Government	Welsh Government would determine its own defence arrangements
MOD land and installations	UK-controlled estate	Ownership and use negotiated
Military operations	UK authority	Welsh sovereignty, subject to any agreed foreign military presence
Environmental regulation	Welsh responsibilities interact with MOD exemptions and arrangements	Welsh law, with any treaty-based exceptions negotiated
Defence industry	Integrated into UK procurement and supply chains	Commercial and defence relationships negotiated
NATO	UK membership	Welsh accession would require a separate process

There is also a distinction between the right to own or control a military base and the right to host another country’s armed forces. An independent Wales could retain a UK facility by agreement without surrendering sovereignty over the surrounding territory. Equally, it could decide that a particular installation should close or be transferred.

I would not assume that independence necessarily means the removal of every British military presence. Nor would I assume that Wales would automatically inherit every military asset located within its borders.

8. A point worth investigating further: the defence estate as a negotiating asset

The MOD’s footprint gives Wales a tangible interest in the negotiations that goes beyond abstract constitutional questions.

Consider RAF Valley, Sennybridge, the Welsh training ranges, MOD Sealand and the defence-industrial facilities.

These are not interchangeable assets. Some are military installations, some are training infrastructure, some are testing facilities and some are industrial capabilities.

A future settlement could therefore produce a mixed arrangement:

* Some facilities transferred to Wales.
* Some closed or repurposed.
* Some retained by the UK under lease or treaty.
* Some operated jointly.
* Some defence-industrial facilities continuing as commercial enterprises, irrespective of the constitutional outcome.

The UK Defence Footprint 2026 also identifies a £50 million Welsh Defence Growth Deal, including support for access to MOD test ranges and defence manufacturing. That demonstrates that the existing relationship has an economic-development dimension as well as a military one. GOV.UK Assets

The central constitutional point is this: if Wales became independent, the UK could no longer rely on its present domestic constitutional authority to operate military bases there. It would need Welsh agreement or some other recognised international legal basis.

That is a substantial change in the relationship between the two governments.

For our wider investigation, I would examine RAF Valley and the Welsh training estate separately. They raise different strategic, territorial, economic and sovereignty questions.”.   

“Yes. We have accumulated quite a substantial set of questions, and I think it would be useful to bring them together into a single research agenda rather than keep opening new branches.

The central thread running through the whole conversation is:

How does Wales exercise meaningful democratic authority within the United Kingdom, what limits that authority in practice, and what would have to change if the constitutional relationship developed further?

I would divide the outstanding work into eight connected investigations. I’ve separated questions we have identified but not yet answered from conclusions we have already established.

Welsh constitutional investigation: outstanding questions

1. The legal architecture of devolution

We have reviewed the history of the Government of Wales Acts, but not yet completed the provision-by-provision examination.

Outstanding questions:

* What exactly is reserved to Westminster under Schedule 7A of the Government of Wales Act 2006?
* Which reservations are genuinely UK-wide matters, and which preserve particular institutional arrangements?
* How does Schedule 7B restrict the Senedd’s ability to legislate, even in areas which are ostensibly devolved?
* How often do reserved-authority consent requirements prevent or complicate Welsh legislation?
* Which executive functions can be transferred under sections 58 and 58A without transferring legislative competence?
* What can be changed through section 109 Orders in Council, and what requires primary legislation?
* How much of the current settlement is protected by statute, and how much depends on political convention?

The important distinction: legislative competence, executive power, institutional control and financial authority are four separate things. We need to understand how they interact.

Our next substantive exercise should be a structured examination of Schedules 7A and 7B.

2. The Burnham–ap Iorwerth correspondence

We have established that the letter of 20 November 2026 does not simply reject every Welsh request. It offers different forms of involvement and selected governance changes, while retaining UK control over important decisions.

There are still significant questions.

About the correspondence itself:

* What precisely did Rhun ap Iorwerth request in his 1 October letter?
* Which requests were accepted, partially accepted, rejected or deferred?
* What evidence and reasoning did the UK Government give for each response?
* Were alternative arrangements genuinely negotiated, or were they primarily devised by the UK Government?
* What commitments have actually been made, and which are expressions of future intent?

About the process:

* What was discussed at the Glasgow meeting on 30 October?
* Was there a separate bilateral discussion, and what records exist?
* Were the relevant Welsh Ministers and UK departmental ministers consulted?
* Were any objections or alternative proposals recorded?
* Was the correspondence part of the formal intergovernmental relations machinery, or an informal political exchange?
* Was any formal dispute procedure initiated or considered?

The meeting, the correspondence and the public announcement need to be treated as separate events until the records establish their relationship.

3. Participation–Authority Substitution

Analytical model to develop

This is the concept we identified when we noticed that greater Welsh involvement in UK-controlled institutions was sometimes being offered instead of the transfer of powers.

The central question is:

When does participation become a meaningful form of shared government, and when does it merely give Wales a voice without giving it authority?

We should apply six tests to each policy area:

Test	Question
Representation	Does Wales have a seat or voice?
Decision rights	Can Welsh representatives determine outcomes?
Consent	Can a decision proceed against Welsh objections?
Resources	Who controls the money?
Accountability	Who answers to the Senedd?
Security	Can Westminster change the arrangement unilaterally?

Then apply them individually to:

* Policing.
* Justice and probation.
* Crown Estate.
* Rail infrastructure.

The key is not to assume that shared governance is necessarily inadequate. It may be a perfectly legitimate arrangement. But we need to establish what rights Wales actually acquires.

4. The responsibility–authority gap

Needs systematic examination

We have identified a recurring problem: the Welsh Government can be responsible for policies whose outcomes depend on institutions it does not control.

We need to investigate this more thoroughly.

Questions:

* Where does Wales have responsibility for outcomes but insufficient authority over the institutions that influence them?
* Which of these gaps are deliberate constitutional reservations?
* Which are historical accidents or consequences of institutional complexity?
* Which could be addressed through better intergovernmental cooperation?
* Which genuinely require legislative or executive devolution?
* Does Wales have sufficient funding and fiscal flexibility to discharge its responsibilities?

We should examine five areas:

* Justice and public safety.
* Rail and transport.
* Energy and natural resources.
* Environmental protection.
* Economic development and industrial policy.

For each, we should map:

The five-part responsibility test

* Responsibility: Who is expected to deliver the outcome?
* Authority: Who has the legal power to make the decision?
* Resources: Who controls the money and assets?
* Coordination: Who must cooperate with whom?
* Accountability: Who can voters hold responsible?

This could become one of the most useful analytical frameworks in the whole investigation.

5. Fiscal autonomy and the economic foundations of self-government

We have examined the Welsh budget and the distinction between block-grant funding, devolved taxation and borrowing.

But several questions remain.

* How does the Barnett formula compare with a needs-based funding model?
* How much additional fiscal discretion could Wales exercise without becoming independent?
* Which taxes could realistically be devolved further?
* What would greater borrowing powers mean in practice?
* Could Wales exercise substantially greater fiscal autonomy while retaining shared UK-wide redistribution?
* What is the difference between having a fair funding settlement and having control over the resources needed to govern?
* How would changes to Crown Estate revenue, energy income and natural-resource management affect the Welsh fiscal position?

An important distinction here is that fiscal autonomy does not have to mean fiscal separation.

We should examine possible arrangements that allow Wales more control over taxation and spending while retaining shared UK-wide economic institutions.

6. Welsh nationalism and the constitutional mandate

We have traced modern Welsh nationalism from nineteenth-century home-rule movements through Plaid Cymru, language activism, devolution and the modern independence debate.

The next questions are less about chronology and more about political meaning.

* How much Welsh national identity is cultural, linguistic, civic or political?
* How many people want more devolution without wanting independence?
* How many support independence as a principle, and how many support it as a practical proposition?
* What is the relationship between Plaid Cymru’s long-term independence objective and its immediate programme for further devolution?
* Does the 2026 election provide a mandate for particular constitutional changes, and if so, which ones?
