* How should we distinguish the electoral mandate for a government from a popular mandate for constitutional transformation?
* Could resistance to particular devolution requests strengthen Welsh national consciousness among people who were previously indifferent to constitutional questions?

That last question connects directly to your earlier observation about political optics.

We should be careful not to treat a Plaid victory as a referendum on independence. The result establishes electoral support for the party’s programme, but not a single shared motivation among its voters.

7. Intergovernmental relations and constitutional safeguards

We have established that the 2022 intergovernmental relations arrangements provide structures for consultation, escalation and dispute resolution.

But they do not create a binding adjudication system or an enforceable Welsh veto.

The outstanding questions are:

* What happens when the UK Government simply declines to agree with the Welsh Government?
* How effectively can Wales compel consultation or obtain a reasoned response?
* Can the Prime Minister and Heads of Devolved Governments Council resolve disputes when the UK Government is itself a party?
* What is the practical independence of the IGR Secretariat?
* Why has no dispute completed the full formal procedure, as reported in the Welsh Government’s February 2026 Cabinet Handbook?
* Are disagreements being resolved informally, avoided, or left unresolved?
* What constitutional safeguards would make the settlement more reliable?

Possible reforms to examine include:

* A statutory duty to cooperate.
* Mandatory consultation periods.
* Published reasons for rejecting devolved-government proposals.
* Independent mediation.
* More clearly defined dispute escalation.
* Stronger legislative consent protections.

We should assess these individually rather than assume every disagreement requires binding arbitration.

8. Defence, MOD bases and the territorial implications of independence

Initial overview only

This is the newest branch, and we have only begun to explore it.

The initial question was what happens to MOD control of military bases in Wales if the Union undergoes further separation.

We should now distinguish three scenarios:

* Greater Welsh autonomy while remaining in the UK.
* A more federal United Kingdom.
* Welsh independence.

The outstanding questions:

* What is the complete MOD estate in Wales, including military installations, training areas, testing sites and industrial facilities?
* Which sites are owned by the Crown or MOD, which are leased, and which operate under other arrangements?
* What military functions does each site perform?
* Which sites have strategic importance to the UK beyond Wales?
* What would happen to RAF Valley, Sennybridge, MOD Sealand and the Welsh training estate under each constitutional scenario?
* Could the UK retain bases in an independent Wales under leases or defence treaties?
* What would Welsh sovereignty mean for access, policing, environmental regulation and jurisdiction within those installations?
* How would NATO membership or other defence arrangements be negotiated?
* What would happen to defence manufacturing, procurement, testing and employment?
* Could Wales develop its own defence capability while maintaining a close security relationship with the UK?

We should also distinguish the ownership of a site, the command of forces operating there, and the right of another state to maintain a military presence. Those are separate legal questions.

The questions that connect everything

There are four overarching questions which I think are more important than any individual policy dispute.

A. What does self-government actually mean?

Is it sufficient for Wales to administer services, or must it control the laws, institutions, resources and decisions that determine their outcomes?

B. What should remain shared?

Which matters genuinely benefit from UK-wide institutions, common standards and pooled resources, and which require Welsh democratic control?

C. What protects the settlement?

Can Wales rely on political conventions and intergovernmental goodwill, or does it need enforceable constitutional protections?

D. What is the actual constitutional destination?

Is the objective improved devolution, federalism, a confederal arrangement, independence, or a settlement that combines shared and separate institutions?

Suggested order of investigation

I would keep the work sequential. Some questions depend on answers to earlier ones.

Order	Investigation	Why it comes here
1	Schedules 7A and 7B	Establish the actual legal boundaries
2	Executive functions and institutional control	Understand what can change without full legislative devolution
3	Four issues in the Burnham correspondence	Apply the legal framework to real disputes
4	Participation–Authority Substitution	Test whether proposed arrangements transfer meaningful power
5	Responsibility–authority gaps	Identify where the settlement creates practical problems
6	Fiscal autonomy	Establish the resources available to exercise authority
7	Intergovernmental safeguards	Examine whether the UK settlement protects Welsh interests
8	Nationalism and democratic mandate	Understand the political context and constitutional choices
9	MOD estate and defence arrangements	Explore the implications of different constitutional futures

The historical development of nationalism and the history of devolution provide context throughout, rather than needing to be repeated as separate research exercises.

My proposed working principle

I would keep four columns in our research notes for every disputed issue:

Category	What we are trying to establish
Law	What is legally permitted, required or prohibited?
Politics	What does each government or party actually want?
Practicality	What institutional, financial or operational difficulties exist?
Unresolved	What evidence, negotiation or constitutional decision is still missing?

That should prevent us from confusing a legal restriction with a political preference, or a practical difficulty with proof that a power cannot be devolved.

I suggest we resume at investigation 1: Schedule 7A and Schedule 7B, examining the actual reservations and restrictions one by one. That gives us the legal foundation against which everything else—including the Burnham letter, Welsh nationalism and the future of the MOD estate—can be assessed.

The aim is not to assume that every power should move to Cardiff, or that every UK-wide institution should disappear. It is to establish, carefully and transparently, where authority lies, why it lies there, and what choices Wales actually has.”  

“Schedule 7A: what is reserved to Westminster?

Schedule 7A of the Government of Wales Act 2006 sets out the matters over which the Senedd does not generally have legislative competence. It was inserted by the Wales Act 2017, which changed Wales from the earlier conferred-powers model to a reserved-powers model.

The basic rule is important:

* The Senedd can legislate on matters that are not reserved, provided it satisfies the other legal limits on its competence.
* It cannot generally legislate in relation to a matter reserved in Schedule 7A.
* Schedule 7B adds further restrictions, including restrictions on modifying the functions of certain UK public authorities.

So Schedule 7A is not simply a list of subjects that Westminster administers. It is a list of matters that remain constitutionally protected from ordinary Senedd legislation.

I’ll set out the actual structure and the significant reservations, rather than just give a few examples. There are detailed exceptions within some entries, so the wording of the legislation matters.

1. How the Schedule is organised

Schedule 7A has two main parts:

* Part 1 — General reservations: broad constitutional and governmental matters.
* Part 2 — Specific reservations: detailed subject-by-subject provisions, arranged under subject headings.

The official revised text is current to 22 November 2026. The Wales Act 2017 brought the new system into force on 1 April 2018. Legislation.gov.uk +1

Part 1: General reservations

These are the seven general reservations.

Paragraph	Reserved subject	What it covers
1–4	Constitution	The Crown, succession, the Union of Wales and England, Westminster Parliament, and specified Crown matters
5	Civil Service	The UK Civil Service, including its management, recruitment, discipline and employment conditions
6–7	Political parties	Registration, funding and accounting of political parties, subject to an exception for support for Senedd members’ duties
8	Single legal jurisdiction of England and Wales	Courts, judges, civil and criminal proceedings, sentencing, prosecutors, pardons, private international law and judicial review
9	Tribunals	Tribunal membership, appointments, functions, procedures and appeals, subject to exceptions for qualifying devolved Welsh tribunals
10	Foreign affairs	International relations, relations with foreign states and international organisations, international trade regulation and international development cooperation
11	Defence	Defence of the realm, armed forces, visiting forces, international defence organisations and trading with the enemy

There are significant exceptions. For example, the Senedd can legislate about responsibility for prosecuting offences it has competence to create, and can establish and regulate qualifying devolved tribunals. Legislation.gov.uk +1

2. Part 2: Specific reservations

This is the much larger part. It contains the detailed reservations, including the exceptions and interpretations which determine their actual boundaries.

The following is the complete list of subject headings, with the principal matters reserved under each.

The paragraph numbers below refer to the current Schedule 7A. The descriptions summarise the statutory entries rather than reproduce every individual provision verbatim.

Head A — Financial and Economic Matters

Fiscal, economic and monetary policy, including:

* UK taxation and excise duties.
* Government borrowing and lending.
* Control of UK public expenditure.
* The Bank of England.
* Currency, coinage and legal tender.
* Financial services and financial regulation.

Important exceptions: devolved taxes, including their collection and management, and local taxes such as council tax and non-domestic rates.

This is why Wales can vary its devolved income-tax rates and operate Land Transaction Tax, but cannot establish its own currency or independently control the UK monetary system. 58612_Ch4-EN v0.2.docx +1

Head B — Home Affairs

This is a particularly important reservation for our investigation.

It includes:

* Elections: Westminster and European elections, and specified aspects of Senedd and Welsh local elections.
* Nationality and immigration: citizenship, immigration, asylum and travel documents.
* National security: security policy, terrorism and official secrets.
* Surveillance: interception of communications, communications data, covert surveillance and surveillance systems.
* Crime, public order and policing: crime prevention, detection and investigation, public order, policing and police and crime commissioners.
* Anti-social behaviour.
* Modern slavery.
* Prostitution.
* Emergency powers.
* Extradition.
* Rehabilitation of offenders.
* Criminal records and disclosure and barring.
* Dangerous items, including firearms, poisons and knives.
* Misuse of and dealing in drugs and psychoactive substances.

There are exceptions. For example, the Senedd can legislate on certain entry, search and seizure powers relating to offences which it has competence to create.

The policing reservation is particularly direct: paragraphs 39–42 expressly reserve crime prevention and investigation, public order, policing and police and crime commissioners. Legislation.gov.uk +1

Head C — Trade and Industry

This covers a broad range of economic regulation:

* Business associations and business names.
* Insolvency.
* Competition and restrictive trade practices.
* Intellectual property.
* Import and export control.
* Sea fishing outside the relevant devolved arrangements.
* Consumer protection.
* Product standards, safety and liability.
* Weights and measures.
* Telecommunications and wireless telegraphy.
* Internet services and electronic encryption.
* Postal services and post offices.
* UK Research and Innovation and Research Councils.
* Certain industrial development functions.
* Protection of trading and economic interests.
* Regulation of distortive or harmful subsidies.

This is not a general reservation of all economic development. Welsh Government retains substantial powers over economic development, but important parts of the regulatory framework for businesses and markets remain reserved. Legislation.gov.uk +2

Head D — Energy

The reserved areas include:

* Electricity generation, transmission, distribution and supply.
* Oil and gas, including offshore installations and pipelines.
* Coal and coal mining.
* Nuclear energy, nuclear installations and nuclear safety.
* The Office for Nuclear Regulation.
* Production, distribution and supply of heat and cooling.
* Energy conservation where implemented through prohibition or regulation.

There are important exceptions.

For example:

* Welsh Ministers have powers over licensing onshore petroleum extraction within the Welsh onshore area.
* Land restoration following coal mining is excepted.
* Certain heat networks and non-regulatory energy-efficiency encouragement are devolved.

This is why Welsh Government can have significant planning, environmental and renewable-energy responsibilities without controlling the electricity system as a whole. Legislation.gov.uk +1

Head E — Transport

The reserved transport matters include:

Roads and vehicles

* Road freight services and goods vehicle operator licensing.
* Vehicle and trailer construction and equipment standards.
* Road traffic offences.
* Driver licensing and driving instruction.
* Drivers’ hours.
* Certain traffic regulations and speed-limit exemptions.
* International road transport.
* Public service vehicle operator licensing.
* Vehicle insurance and registration.

Rail

* Railway services.
* The Channel Tunnel Act 1987.

Other transport

* Marine and air transport matters.
* Technical specifications for fuels and energy sources used in transport.
* Carriage of dangerous goods.

There are substantial exceptions, including devolved aspects of traffic management, speed limits, traffic signs, pedestrian crossings, taxi licensing and certain financial assistance for railway services.

The distinction we previously identified is important: railway services are reserved, but this does not mean every aspect of rail investment, infrastructure, local transport planning or public funding is reserved. Legislation.gov.uk +1

Head F — Social Security, Child Support, Pensions and Compensation

This includes:

* Social security schemes supported by public funds.
* State pensions and benefits.
* Child support maintenance.
* Collection and enforcement of specified maintenance payments.
* Occupational and personal pensions.
* Civil Service pensions.
* Compensation schemes for public sector workers.
* Armed forces compensation and related schemes.

There are specific exceptions for certain Welsh public officeholders and local authority members’ pension arrangements.

This reservation is important because Wales has extensive devolved responsibilities for poverty reduction, education, housing and social services, but does not control the main social security system. 58612_Ch4-EN v0.2.docx +1

Head G — Professions

The regulation of specified professions is reserved:

* Architects.
* Auditors.
* Health professionals.
* Veterinary surgeons.

There are exceptions for the regulation of social work and social care professions.

The distinction is between regulating a profession and delivering a devolved service. For instance, Welsh Government controls much of health-service organisation, but the regulation of doctors, nurses and many other health professionals is reserved. 58612_Ch4-EN v0.2.docx +1

Head H — Employment

This covers:

* Employment rights and duties.
* Industrial relations.
* Trade unions and industrial action.
* Minimum wage.
* Working time and paid leave.
* Employment agencies.
* Employment tribunals.
* Transfer of undertakings.
* Industrial training boards.
* Job-search and employment-support arrangements.

There are exceptions, notably the subject matter of the Agricultural Sector (Wales) Act 2014.

This is a significant reservation because employment is a major part of economic and social policy, but Wales has only limited legislative control over the underlying employment-law framework. Legislation.gov.uk +1

Head J — Health, Safety and Medicines

The reserved matters include:

* Abortion.
* Xenotransplantation.
* Embryology, surrogacy and genetics.
* Medicines and medical products.
* Medical supplies and poisons.
* Certain health and safety matters.
* Certain public-health and radiation-protection matters.
* Welfare foods.

The details and exceptions matter here. It would be wrong to conclude that health is generally reserved: the organisation and delivery of health services are largely devolved, while particular regulatory and ethically sensitive matters remain reserved. 58612_Ch4-EN v0.2.docx +1

Head K — Media, Culture and Sport

This includes:

* Broadcasting and media regulation.
* The BBC and Ofcom.
* Public lending rights.
* The Government Indemnity Scheme.
* Property accepted in satisfaction of tax.
* Specified sporting regulation and safety arrangements.

Broadcasting is particularly significant. Welsh Government can support Welsh-language culture and cultural institutions, but it does not control the overall broadcasting regulatory framework. 58612_Ch4-EN v0.2.docx +1

Head L — Justice

This is separate from the general reservation of the single legal jurisdiction.

It includes:

* Legal professions and legal services.
* Legal aid.
* Judicial remuneration.
* Courts administration and related matters.
* Prisons and detention institutions.
* Management of offenders.
* Probation.
* Prisoner escort and transfer functions.
* Certain youth justice functions.
* Criminal injuries compensation.
* Miscarriages of justice compensation.
* Public records and public-sector information.

The reservation of prisons and offender management expressly includes probation and specified youth justice matters.

This is central to our earlier discussion: Welsh Government controls many of the social services and preventative policies that affect offending and rehabilitation, while major parts of the justice system remain reserved. Legislation.gov.uk +1

Head M — Land and Agricultural Assets

This covers:

* Land registration and registration of interests in land.
* Land Registry functions.
* Certain land charges and registration systems.
* Specified Crown and agricultural assets.

This is not a reservation of land-use planning generally. Planning is largely devolved. It concerns particular registration systems and assets, with the detailed statutory boundaries determining what is retained at UK level. 58612_Ch4-EN v0.2.docx

Head N — Miscellaneous

The remaining specific reservations include:

* Equal opportunities, subject to significant Welsh exceptions.
* Control of weapons of mass destruction and specified weapons matters.
* Ordnance Survey.
* Time, time zones and the calendar.
* Certain registration and civil-status matters.
* Specified building standards for Crown and other particular buildings.

The equality reservation is not a blanket prohibition on Welsh equality policy. The Senedd can encourage equal opportunities and impose certain duties on devolved Welsh authorities, but cannot generally legislate to replace the UK-wide equality-law framework. Legislation.gov.uk +1

There is no Head I. The Schedule proceeds from H to J. The statutory headings are not a continuous alphabet.

3. What the full list tells us

The list is extensive: it is not simply a collection of traditional sovereign functions such as defence, foreign affairs and currency.

It also reserves numerous matters that affect everyday domestic government.

I would group the reservations into four broad constitutional types:

1. Core state sovereignty

Crown, Union, Westminster Parliament, defence, foreign affairs, nationality, immigration, national security and currency.

2. UK-wide institutional systems

Courts, legal jurisdiction, tribunals, Civil Service, social security, pensions, broadcasting, policing and major regulators.

3. Economic and regulatory frameworks

Employment law, financial services, competition, insolvency, telecommunications, electricity, rail services, medicines and professional regulation.

4. Particular assets, functions and administrative systems

Crown Estate management, Ordnance Survey, land registration, specified research bodies, public records and particular compensation schemes.

This classification is my analytical grouping, not a classification used in the Act.

4. Three corrections to common descriptions of the settlement

There are three points I particularly want to emphasise.

First: a reserved subject is not necessarily a subject with no Welsh involvement.

Welsh Ministers may have executive functions, consultation rights, administrative responsibilities or shared functions in an area where the Senedd does not have general legislative competence.

For example, Wales has some executive and administrative responsibilities relating to justice, despite justice being substantially reserved.

Second: an exception is legally significant.

The Schedule frequently reserves a broad subject and then gives Wales particular powers within it. The exceptions are not informal concessions; they are part of the statutory boundary.

The Wales Act 2017 explanatory notes explain that the total reservation is determined by reading the reservation together with its exceptions and any carve-outs. 58612_Ch4-EN v0.2.docx

Third: Schedule 7A is not the whole story.

Even if a Senedd Bill does not relate to a reserved matter, it can still be outside competence because of Schedule 7B, territorial restrictions or human-rights compatibility requirements.

Schedule 7B also limits the Senedd’s ability to alter the functions of certain reserved authorities. 58612_Ch4-EN v0.2.docx +1

5. What I think we should examine next

There is a particularly revealing distinction in this Schedule.

Some reservations are about matters that are conventionally handled at national level: defence, foreign affairs, currency and immigration.

Others preserve the structure of particular UK-wide institutions: policing, prisons, employment regulation, rail services, professional regulation and broadcasting.

And some reserve individual functions or bodies rather than entire policy areas.

Those are three different constitutional rationales, and they should not be treated as if they were interchangeable.

For our next step, I suggest we examine Head B — Home Affairs and Head L — Justice in detail, paragraph by paragraph. Together they will show exactly how policing, criminal law, courts, prisons, probation and youth justice are divided between Cardiff and Westminster. That will give us a concrete example of the responsibility–authority gap we have been trying to understand.

Primary legal sources

* Government of Wales Act 2006 — Schedule 7A, current revised text⁠￼.
* Wales Act 2017 — Explanatory Notes, including the explanation of reservations and exceptions⁠￼.
* Senedd — Powers and the reserved-powers model⁠￼.”.  

“The Senedd can represent Welsh democratic will on matters reserved by Schedule 7A, but it cannot generally turn that democratic mandate into Welsh law or direct the UK institutions responsible for those matters.

That is a central tension in the devolution settlement.

The answer is not that the Senedd has no role. It has several ways to express, scrutinise and advocate Welsh preferences. But those mechanisms vary considerably in strength, and most do not give it final decision-making authority.

The distinction I would make is between:

* Representation — expressing what Welsh voters and their elected representatives want.
* Influence — attempting to change UK Government decisions.
* Consent — having a formal right to agree or refuse.
* Authority — possessing the legal power to make the decision itself.

Schedule 7A limits the fourth, but it does not eliminate the first three.

1. The mechanisms available to the Senedd

There are six principal routes.

Mechanism	What the Senedd can do	Its limitation
Plenary debates and motions	Formally express the elected legislature’s position	Usually does not bind Westminster
Committee inquiries	Investigate reserved matters affecting Wales and publish findings	Cannot take over the UK department’s responsibilities
Questions and scrutiny	Question Welsh Ministers about their dealings with UK Government	Cannot exercise direct ministerial control over UK Ministers
Intergovernmental relations	Press for negotiations, consultation and agreement	Most arrangements rely on cooperation
Political advocacy	Make representations to UK Ministers and Parliament	UK Government can disagree
Constitutional reform proposals	Request transfers of powers or changes to Schedule 7A	The UK Parliament must agree to the necessary legislation

The Senedd’s own explanation confirms that its committees can investigate issues affecting Wales, while its plenary proceedings allow Members to debate and vote on them. senedd.wales +1

This means the Senedd can legitimately debate defence, immigration, policing, broadcasting or social security, even though it cannot generally legislate on those subjects.

It can say, for example:

* Welsh voters want a different approach to policing.
* Wales needs a different relationship with the Crown Estate.
* The UK Government’s immigration policy has particular consequences for Welsh communities.
* Defence installations should be subject to stronger consultation with Welsh institutions.

But expressing that position does not itself change the law or compel the UK Government to act.

2. The important distinction: who represents Wales at Westminster?

There are actually two democratic representation systems operating simultaneously.

Welsh democratic representation

Senedd Members (MSs)

Elected by people in Wales to represent them in the Welsh legislature.

They scrutinise Welsh Government, legislate within devolved competence and represent Welsh interests in constitutional discussions.

UK democratic representation

Members of Parliament (MPs)

Elected by constituencies in Wales to represent their constituents in the UK Parliament.

They participate in legislation and scrutiny on reserved matters, including defence, immigration, policing and social security.

The important point is that Welsh MPs do not collectively constitute a Welsh national delegation with a formal obligation to follow the Senedd’s position.

They are individual UK parliamentarians, subject to their parliamentary responsibilities, party affiliations and constituency mandates.

Consequently, the Senedd might pass a motion expressing a particular Welsh position on policing, while Welsh MPs at Westminster might divide over the issue.

There is no constitutional rule requiring Westminster to treat a Senedd resolution as the authoritative expression of Welsh democratic consent.

The two systems provide representation, but they do not have equal authority over reserved matters.

3. Does the Senedd have any formal consent rights?

This is where we need to be particularly precise.

The Sewel Convention provides that Westminster will not normally legislate on devolved matters without the consent of the relevant devolved legislature.

But it does not give the Senedd a general consent right over legislation concerning reserved matters.

There is an important qualification: a UK Bill dealing principally with a reserved subject may also contain provisions affecting devolved responsibilities or the Senedd’s competence. Those provisions may engage the legislative consent process.

For example, a UK policing Bill might contain provisions concerning devolved Welsh services or alter the functions of Welsh Ministers. The fact that the Bill concerns policing does not automatically mean every provision is outside the consent process.

However, even where legislative consent is required, refusal does not legally prevent Westminster from passing the legislation. The UK Parliament retains legal sovereignty. House of Commons Library +1

So we should distinguish:

* Reserved subject: no general Senedd legislative authority.
* Devolved subject: Senedd has legislative authority.
* Legislative consent: a conventionally recognised opportunity to express consent where UK legislation affects devolved matters.
* Political consultation: an opportunity to influence decisions without necessarily possessing a formal veto.

4. Applying this to the matters we have been discussing

Here are some concrete examples.

Reserved matter	What the Senedd can represent	What it cannot generally do
Policing	Welsh priorities for public safety, policing outcomes and community confidence	Direct police forces or legislate for policing structures
Justice	Welsh concerns about courts, prisons, probation and rehabilitation	Control the reserved courts and prison system
Crown Estate	Welsh views on ownership, management, revenue and natural resources	Direct Crown Estate management
Defence	Welsh concerns about military installations, employment and environmental effects	Direct MOD operations or determine UK defence policy
Rail services	Welsh transport priorities and the effects of reserved railway arrangements	Take over reserved railway functions simply by passing a Senedd Act
Immigration	Represent the interests of migrants and Welsh communities affected by immigration policy	Set immigration rules or determine visa policy
Social security	Express Welsh priorities concerning poverty and benefit adequacy	Redesign the main UK social security system

The Senedd can also legislate on adjacent devolved matters where doing so does not cross the reserved boundary. For instance, it can make laws about devolved housing and social services even where those services interact with reserved immigration or justice functions. senedd.wales +1

5. The deeper democratic problem

I think this reveals something important about the constitutional arrangement.

Imagine a Senedd election in which Welsh voters give a party a clear mandate to pursue a particular change to policing.

The elected Welsh Government could:

* Publish its proposals.
* Introduce a motion in the Senedd.
* Obtain a vote supporting the proposed change.
* Commission research and committee scrutiny.
* Formally request negotiations with the UK Government.
* Ask Welsh MPs to support the proposal at Westminster.

But the UK Government could still refuse.

That refusal would not necessarily be unlawful. The UK Government would be exercising authority within the constitutional settlement.

The Welsh Government could then argue that the refusal disregarded a Welsh democratic mandate. The UK Government could respond that the matter is reserved and that it has its own UK-wide democratic mandate.

Both governments would be invoking democratic legitimacy, but only one would possess the relevant legal authority.

The democratic accountability gap

Welsh voters

Elect a Welsh legislature with a mandate to represent their interests.

Senedd expresses Welsh democratic preference

UK Government retains the relevant reserved power

Decision may be made without Welsh institutional consent

The Senedd can challenge, criticise and seek to change the decision, but cannot generally compel a different outcome.

That is a structural feature of the current settlement, rather than necessarily a failure by any particular government.

6. What could be changed without making Wales independent?

There are several possible constitutional reforms.

Reform	Potential effect
Stronger statutory consultation	UK Ministers would have to consult the Senedd before specified reserved decisions affecting Wales
Mandatory reasoned responses	UK Government would have to explain formally why it rejected a Senedd position
Welsh consent requirements for specified decisions	Certain UK decisions affecting Wales could require Welsh agreement
Shared decision-making institutions	Welsh and UK representatives could exercise specified functions jointly
Transfer of executive functions	Welsh Ministers could acquire authority to administer particular reserved functions
Transfer of legislative competence	The Senedd could acquire the power to legislate in specified areas

These are not equivalent reforms. Consultation gives a voice; consent provides a stronger procedural protection; shared decision-making creates joint authority; transferring competence gives Wales its own legislative power.

Some could be achieved through changes to executive arrangements or intergovernmental agreements. Others would require changes to primary legislation and the devolution settlement.

7. The question I think we have now uncovered

There is a distinction between two propositions:

Proposition A: Wales has democratic representation in relation to reserved matters because Welsh voters elect MPs to Westminster, and those MPs participate in decisions on those matters.

Proposition B: Wales has a distinct national democratic mandate, expressed through the Senedd, which ought to carry formal weight when Westminster makes decisions specifically affecting Wales.

The first is already part of the constitutional arrangement.

The second is a much larger constitutional proposition, and it is not fully recognised in the present settlement.

This is where our earlier Participation–Authority Substitution concept becomes particularly relevant. The Senedd can be consulted, represented on committees, invited into discussions and given opportunities to influence policy. But unless it has defined decision rights, those arrangements do not necessarily give it constitutional authority.

My suggested next question is: should the Senedd have a formal Welsh consent mechanism for reserved decisions that have a substantial and specific impact on Wales, even if the underlying subject remains reserved to Westminster?

That would let us explore a middle ground between the present arrangements and full devolution of every affected policy area.”.  

“The question exposes a possible gap in the Welsh settlement: Westminster can retain responsibility for a policy while making decisions that have particularly significant consequences for Wales, without the Senedd having a formal right to agree or refuse those decisions.

There are arguments for changing that, and important questions about how far such a change should go. I would separate the principle, the scope and the legal mechanism.

1. The case for a Welsh consent mechanism

There are four main arguments.

A. Democratic representation

Welsh voters elect the Senedd to represent Welsh interests. Where a decision has a substantial and specific impact on Wales, there is a case for requiring the Welsh legislature to have a formal role before it is made.

Otherwise, the Senedd can be democratically accountable for the consequences of a decision without having a corresponding right to influence or approve it.

B. Territorial accountability

A UK-wide policy may have very different effects in different parts of the country.

For example:

* A decision about a military installation may have concentrated consequences for a Welsh community.
* A change to policing arrangements may affect Welsh public safety and devolved services.
* A decision about Crown Estate assets may have substantial consequences for Welsh coastal communities and public revenue.
* A decision about rail infrastructure may affect Welsh economic development and connectivity.

The fact that a subject is reserved does not mean its effects are evenly distributed across the UK.

C. Better scrutiny

A formal consent procedure would require the UK Government to explain its proposals and the Senedd to examine their consequences.

It could bring local evidence, Welsh policy priorities and cross-government implications into the decision before it becomes difficult to change.

D. More reliable intergovernmental relations

At present, much depends on consultation, negotiation and political goodwill. A formal mechanism could establish minimum procedural rights which do not depend entirely on the relationship between the governments of the day.

These arguments are consistent with concerns raised by the Senedd’s Legislation, Justice and Constitution Committee about the need for clearer engagement and a more robust constitutional process. Select Committee on the Constitution +1

2. The case against an unrestricted veto

There are also substantial objections.

A. Reserved powers would become conditional powers

If Westminster retained responsibility for defence but needed Senedd permission for every significant defence decision affecting Wales, it would no longer have exclusive decision-making authority in the ordinary sense.

It would be a form of shared or concurrent authority.

That may be a legitimate constitutional arrangement, but it would need to be acknowledged as such.

B. UK-wide decisions can have unavoidable territorial effects

Defence, immigration, national security, macroeconomic policy and international obligations can all have substantial consequences in Wales.

A Welsh veto could potentially prevent a decision which the UK Government considers necessary for the security or operation of the UK as a whole.

C. Defining the threshold could become contentious

What counts as a substantial and specific impact?

A UK-wide immigration policy affects Wales, but so does an individual immigration decision affecting a Welsh employer or family. Those should not necessarily trigger the same constitutional procedure.

D. There could be conflicting democratic mandates

The Senedd represents Welsh voters. Westminster represents the UK electorate, including voters in Wales.

A disagreement between the two institutions would raise a difficult question: when should the territorial democratic mandate prevail, and when should the UK-wide mandate prevail?

The House of Lords Constitution Committee explored these tensions in its recent examination of legislative consent. Witnesses differed over whether stronger legal consent rules would protect devolution or substantially restrict Westminster’s ability to legislate for the whole UK. Select Committee on the Constitution

3. The crucial distinction: not every consent mechanism needs to be a veto

I would distinguish four possible models.

Model 1

Mandatory consultation

Westminster must consult the Senedd before making a qualifying decision.

The UK Government retains the final decision, but must demonstrate that Welsh views were considered.

Model 2

Formal Senedd approval or objection

The Senedd votes on the proposal. An objection triggers a requirement for the UK Government to reconsider and publish a formal response.

The UK Government can ultimately proceed, but must account publicly for doing so.

Model 3

Conditional consent

The decision cannot normally be implemented in Wales without Senedd consent.

Westminster retains responsibility for the reserved subject, but Welsh agreement is required for specified territorial decisions.

Model 4

Joint decision-making

Certain decisions require agreement between UK and Welsh Ministers, or a joint institution.

Neither government has unilateral authority over the specified decision.

These models represent increasingly substantial changes to the constitutional settlement. The fourth would amount to a genuine redistribution of decision-making authority.

4. What would qualify as a substantial and specific Welsh impact?

This is probably the most important design question.

I would propose that a decision qualify when it meets one or more of the following tests:

Test	Meaning
Territorial concentration	Its consequences fall disproportionately on Wales or a Welsh region
Institutional impact	It materially affects the functions of Welsh public institutions
Financial impact	It imposes substantial costs or removes significant revenue from Welsh public services
Land and resources	It affects significant Welsh land, natural resources or public assets
Long-term consequences	It creates a lasting commitment or materially changes future Welsh options
Public-service interaction	It significantly affects the delivery of devolved services

There should also be a materiality threshold. Routine administrative decisions should not require a constitutional consent procedure.

I would distinguish three levels:

* Ordinary impact: information and consultation.
* Substantial Welsh impact: formal Senedd scrutiny and a reasoned UK Government response.
* Exceptional or irreversible Welsh impact: a presumption that Welsh consent is required, subject to narrowly defined exceptions.

This would be more proportionate than giving the Senedd a general veto over every reserved decision.

5. Applying the idea to our four main case studies

This is where the proposal becomes concrete.

Area	Possible Welsh consent trigger	What remains UK-wide
Policing	Major structural changes to policing in Wales or the territorial allocation of policing resources	National security, UK-wide criminal law and cross-border coordination
Justice	Major changes to Welsh prison provision, court infrastructure or arrangements with direct effects on devolved services	The overall legal jurisdiction and UK justice framework
Crown Estate	Major decisions about Welsh Crown Estate assets, disposal, management or long-term exploitation	Wider UK Crown Estate governance and UK constitutional arrangements
Rail	Major Welsh infrastructure closures, strategic investment decisions or changes to Welsh network provision	UK-wide railway standards, cross-border network coordination and reserved rail regulation
Defence	Establishment, closure or major change of military facilities with substantial Welsh territorial consequences	Operational command, national defence strategy and military deployment

The MOD example illustrates the distinction particularly well.

A decision to deploy British forces abroad is a UK defence decision. It would be difficult to justify a Welsh veto.

But a decision to establish a major permanent military installation in Wales, with substantial land, environmental and community consequences, raises a different question.

A consent mechanism could be designed to distinguish between those two things without transferring general defence authority to Cardiff.

6. Is there already a precedent for strengthening consent?

Yes, although the existing arrangements do not go as far as the proposal we are discussing.

The Sewel Convention, recognised in section 107(6) of the Government of Wales Act 2006, provides that Westminster will not normally legislate on devolved matters without Senedd consent.

But it does not generally cover legislation on reserved matters simply because they have substantial effects in Wales. Nor is it legally enforceable. House of Commons Library +1

There is also a Welsh Statutory Instrument Consent Mechanism. This gives the Senedd an opportunity to consider certain UK secondary legislation which amends primary legislation within its competence. However, the House of Lords Constitution Committee recorded that this mechanism has no legal effect on the UK Government. Select Committee on the Constitution

The House of Lords’ 2025–26 Constitution Committee inquiry into the governance of the Union is especially relevant. It considered stronger legislative consent procedures, early consultation, formal explanations when the Senedd’s wishes are not followed, and the difficulties of making consent legally binding while Westminster remains sovereign. Select Committee on the Constitution

That provides a substantial existing body of constitutional evidence to work from.

7. A possible Welsh territorial consent framework

One way to structure this would be a new statutory framework, perhaps called a Welsh Territorial Impact and Consent Procedure.

It could work as follows:

Proposed decision process

* UK Government identifies a qualifying decision
    It publishes an assessment of the likely effects on Wales.
* Senedd receives formal notification
    The relevant committee examines the proposal and may take evidence from affected communities and institutions.
* Senedd votes
    It can approve, reject or approve subject to conditions.
* Governments negotiate
    If the Senedd objects, a defined period of ministerial negotiation and, where appropriate, mediation follows.
* Final decision and accountability
    The UK Government must either secure agreement or publish a reasoned statement explaining why it is proceeding without it.

For a limited category of particularly significant decisions, the framework could require consent rather than merely a reasoned response.

There would need to be carefully defined exceptions for emergencies, national security and urgent international obligations, with retrospective reporting and scrutiny.

There would also need to be a clear mechanism for resolving disagreements about whether the threshold has been met. Otherwise, the UK Government could simply classify a decision as outside the process.

8. The constitutional issue underneath all this

There are two different ways of understanding the UK.

The first is a parliamentary-sovereignty model: Westminster is the ultimate legislature, and the devolved institutions exercise powers that Parliament has granted.

The second is a shared-sovereignty or quasi-federal model: the nations have their own democratic authority, and certain decisions affecting them require agreement between governments or legislatures.

The present Welsh settlement is much closer to the first model, although it contains some features of shared governance.

A territorial consent mechanism would move it towards the second model without necessarily making Wales independent or transferring the underlying reserved policy area.

It would also need to address whether the same principle should apply to Scotland and Northern Ireland, rather than create an entirely separate Welsh constitutional arrangement.

9. The questions we should settle before designing it

I would not collapse these into one yes-or-no question. There are four distinct decisions:

1. Trigger: Should consent apply to all reserved decisions with a substantial Welsh impact, or only decisions with direct, disproportionate and lasting effects?

2. Strength: Should Senedd objection trigger reconsideration and a reasoned response, or should certain decisions actually require Senedd approval?

3. Exceptions: Which decisions should remain exclusively within UK authority, particularly defence operations, national security and international obligations?

4. Enforceability: Should the procedure be a political commitment, a statutory duty, or a legally enforceable requirement?

These choices determine whether the mechanism is principally about better democratic representation, stronger consultation, or an actual redistribution of constitutional authority.

The most revealing distinction for our investigation is this: the Senedd could acquire a right to be heard, a right to receive reasons, a right to negotiate, or a right to withhold consent. Those are four different constitutional powers. Calling all of them a Welsh consent mechanism would obscure the very issue we are trying to resolve.”.  

“The important distinction is between a matter that needs to be handled at UK-wide level and a matter reserved because the law preserves a particular UK or England-and-Wales institution, system or administrative arrangement.

Those are not the same thing. A function can be reserved because coordination across the UK is genuinely necessary; it can also be reserved because Westminster has chosen to retain a common institutional structure. Sometimes both explanations apply.

I would use three categories rather than a simple UK-wide/not-UK-wide split:

* Inherently or strongly UK-wide: the subject has a compelling cross-border, sovereign or common-market rationale.
* Institutionally reserved: the reservation protects an existing legal jurisdiction, regulator, public body or administrative system. That arrangement may be defensible, but it is not necessarily the only workable one.
* Mixed: there is a legitimate UK-wide interest, but the current reservation also preserves a particular allocation of power or institutional design.

1. The strongest cases for genuinely UK-wide reservation

These are the areas where there is a persuasive reason for a common UK-level authority, even if that does not mean every operational function must be centralised.

Reservation	Why a UK-wide approach has a strong rationale
Defence of the realm	Command of the armed forces, collective defence, military alliances and national defence planning require a coherent sovereign authority.
Foreign affairs and international relations	The UK needs to speak and act as one state in diplomatic relations and treaty-making. Devolved governments can still have legitimate roles in areas affecting their responsibilities.
Currency and monetary policy	A common currency, central bank, exchange-rate policy and monetary framework are inherently connected across the UK.
Macroeconomic and fiscal framework	UK-wide borrowing, debt management, redistribution and control of overall public expenditure have strong common-system arguments. This does not settle how much tax autonomy Wales should have within that framework.
Immigration and nationality	A common citizenship and immigration system is closely connected to the UK’s international borders and legal status. There can still be a case for devolving integration and some related services.
National security and intelligence	Intelligence coordination, counter-terrorism and protection against threats to the state have a strong UK-wide rationale.

These are the clearest examples of reservations where the underlying function is genuinely difficult to divide into four independent national systems.

But even here, UK-wide responsibility does not automatically mean UK-only decision-making. Wales can have a voice in how defence investment, immigration policy or economic policy affects Welsh communities without becoming responsible for the sovereign function itself.

The Independent Commission on the Constitutional Future of Wales identifies economic and fiscal policy, foreign affairs, nuclear policy and national security as matters reserved across all three devolved settlements. GOV.WALES +1

2. Reservations that preserve particular institutional arrangements

These are more interesting constitutionally. The question is not simply whether there is a UK-wide interest, but whether the present institutional structure is necessary.

Reservation	What the reservation preserves	Why it is open to examination
Single legal jurisdiction of England and Wales	A shared court system, judiciary, procedural framework and legal jurisdiction	Scotland and Northern Ireland have their own legal jurisdictions. A distinct Welsh jurisdiction could be recognised without necessarily creating wholly separate courts or legal professions.
Justice and policing	UK-level control of policing, courts, prisons, probation and much of criminal justice in Wales	Justice and policing are devolved in Scotland and Northern Ireland. The current Welsh arrangement is therefore not an unavoidable UK-wide model.
Rail infrastructure	A largely UK-controlled railway infrastructure and investment structure	Wales has distinct transport needs and already has devolved transport responsibilities. Cross-border railway coordination does not necessarily require Westminster to retain ultimate control of all Welsh infrastructure.
Crown Estate management	Centralised management of Crown Estate assets and revenues in Wales	Scotland has a different arrangement, with management devolved. That makes the Welsh reservation look like a constitutional choice rather than an unavoidable UK-wide requirement.
Broadcasting and media regulation	A UK-wide broadcasting regulatory and institutional framework	Common technical standards and a shared media market may be sensible, but they do not rule out Welsh representation, distinct funding arrangements or some devolved functions.
Employment law	A common UK-wide framework of employment rights and protections	A common minimum standard may be valuable, but it does not necessarily rule out Welsh powers over enforcement, workplace policy or additional protections.

The first four are particularly revealing. The Independent Commission found that the Welsh settlement differs from Scotland and Northern Ireland on legal jurisdiction, justice and policing, and rail infrastructure. It also observed that the reservation of justice and policing has consequences for areas such as antisocial behaviour and alcohol licensing, which are much more local in character. GOV.WALES +1

That is a significant point: a reservation can reach beyond the subject that originally justified it. It can pull adjacent matters into the reserved sphere because they are legally or administratively attached to a particular institution.

3. The mixed category: legitimate common interests, but not necessarily exclusive UK control

Some reservations have a strong UK-wide rationale, but their current form goes further than that rationale necessarily requires.

* Social security: UK-wide pooling and redistribution protect solidarity and spread financial risk. But Scotland has substantial devolved social security powers, and Northern Ireland has its own arrangements. A common UK safety net does not logically require every benefit, delivery mechanism or supplementary payment to be controlled centrally.
* Energy: National grid coordination, electricity markets, nuclear policy and strategic energy security have strong UK-wide dimensions. But local generation, energy efficiency, planning, community energy and the management of land and assets have distinctly territorial dimensions.
* Financial regulation: Common financial markets and prudential regulation have a strong case for UK-wide rules. That does not mean every economic-development or investment decision must be centralised.
* Employment rights: Common minimum rights can prevent a race to the bottom. But a common floor is different from requiring every aspect of employment policy to be identical.

The Commission’s analysis is useful here: it recognises the case for common employment protections and social-security risk sharing, while also identifying the costs of weak Welsh influence over decisions that affect devolved policy. On energy, it explicitly questioned whether some reservations remained strategically justified. GOV.WALES

4. A further category: reservations that protect the machinery of the state

Schedule 7A also reserves matters such as:

* The Civil Service of the State.
* Political-party registration and funding.
* The constitution of specified reserved public authorities, including their establishment, dissolution, functions and funding.

These are not all inherently UK-wide policy questions. They protect the organisation and operation of particular institutions.

There are reasonable arguments for common standards and national coordination. But it is worth asking whether the whole institutional arrangement needs to be reserved, or only particular functions.

For example, reserving the registration of political parties is not the same kind of constitutional necessity as reserving defence or currency. It is a decision about where a particular regulatory function sits.

The distinction is visible in the statute itself: Schedule 7A reserves certain subjects, while paragraph 197 separately protects the constitution and functions of specified authorities. Legislation.gov.uk +1

5. What this tells us about the Welsh settlement

There is a historical dimension here. The Independent Commission found that the powers initially transferred to Wales in 1999 largely reflected the executive responsibilities previously exercised by the Secretary of State for Wales. There was no comprehensive review of what powers a democratically elected Welsh legislature ought to have.

The settlement was subsequently deepened, but not comprehensively redesigned. GOV.WALES

That means some of today’s boundaries may reflect the history of administrative devolution more than a considered constitutional judgement about which level of government is best placed to exercise each function.

I would therefore apply four tests to each reservation:

* Scale: Does the issue genuinely require a UK-wide decision?
* Interdependence: Would separate Welsh authority create serious practical problems, or could those be managed through agreements and shared institutions?
* Institutional necessity: Is the existing UK-wide institution essential, or simply the arrangement that has historically been chosen?
* Accountability: Does retaining the power at UK level produce better outcomes and clearer accountability for people in Wales?

My provisional classification would be:

Strongest case for UK-wide reservation

Defence, foreign affairs, currency, monetary policy, national security, immigration and the overarching fiscal framework.

Strongest case for examining institutional preservation

Legal jurisdiction, justice and policing, rail infrastructure, Crown Estate management and the structure of certain public authorities.

Best candidates for shared or differentiated arrangements

Social security, employment protections, energy regulation, broadcasting and financial or economic development functions.

One qualification matters: a matter being institutionally reserved does not establish that it should be devolved. It establishes that the case for retaining it needs to be made on institutional effectiveness, cost, coordination and democratic accountability — rather than simply asserting that it is a UK-wide matter.

My central observation: the Welsh settlement contains some reservations that protect the integrity of the UK as a state, and others that protect the existing way the UK state has chosen to organise itself. Those are different constitutional arguments. Treating them as interchangeable makes it much harder to have a serious discussion about which powers Wales should hold.

The next useful distinction would be between a common UK-wide standard and a common UK-wide institution. The former often has a strong case even where the latter does not.”.  

“Schedule 7B is important because it creates a second layer of limits beyond the list of reserved subjects in Schedule 7A.

Schedule 7A asks: “Is this subject reserved?” Schedule 7B also asks: “Even if this subject is devolved, is the Senedd trying to change a legal rule or public authority that it is not permitted to change?”

That is how a bill can concern an ostensibly devolved policy area — such as health, education, housing or local government — yet still encounter a competence barrier because of the way it affects a reserved authority, reserved law or protected enactment.

The distinction is between having legislative competence over a policy field and having unrestricted power over every legal and institutional component involved in delivering it.

1. The restrictions, in practical terms

Schedule 7B contains several different kinds of restriction. They should not be treated as one general limitation.

Provision	What it restricts	Practical consequence
Paragraphs 1–2	Modification of the existing law on reserved matters	A Senedd Act cannot alter reserved law, except for limited changes ancillary to a genuinely devolved provision and no broader than necessary.
Paragraph 3	Modification of private law	Contract, property, tort, trusts and related law are protected in principle, but can be modified where the modification serves a purpose that is not itself reserved.
Paragraph 4	Modification of certain criminal law	The Senedd cannot alter specified serious offences or the basic framework of criminal responsibility, mental elements and sentencing.
Paragraphs 5–7	Protected enactments and specified constitutional or institutional provisions	Certain statutes, provisions of the Government of Wales Act and functions of the Comptroller and Auditor General are protected from Senedd amendment.
Paragraphs 8–11	Functions and constitution of reserved authorities	Certain changes to public bodies require the consent of a UK Minister, even when the policy purpose is devolved.

These are statutory restrictions, not merely conventions or political understandings. Section 108A(2)(d) of the Government of Wales Act 2006 makes breach of Schedule 7B a ground on which a provision is outside the Senedd’s legislative competence. Legislation.gov.uk +1

2. The reserved-authority problem: the most significant practical constraint

This is the part most directly relevant to your question.

Paragraphs 8–11 distinguish between:

* Devolved Welsh authorities — broadly, public authorities that meet the statutory definition or are listed as such.
* Reserved authorities — Ministers of the Crown, UK government departments and other public authorities that are not devolved Welsh authorities.

The distinction is about the legal status of the institution, not simply whether it is carrying out a devolved policy function.

Under paragraph 8, the Senedd generally needs the appropriate UK Minister’s consent to legislate to:

* Give a reserved authority a new function or impose a new duty on it.
* Change its constitution, including relevant provisions concerning assets, liabilities, funding and receipts.
* Create, change or remove functions specifically exercisable in relation to that authority.

Paragraph 10 also generally requires consent to remove or modify functions of public authorities other than devolved Welsh authorities, subject to exceptions. Paragraph 11 deals separately with certain functions of Ministers of the Crown. The exceptions in paragraph 9 and elsewhere matter, so this is not an absolute requirement for every provision involving a public body. Legislation.gov.uk +2

The important consequence: a Welsh law can be about a devolved subject and still require permission from Westminster because it places a legal obligation on an institution that Westminster classifies as reserved.

Example: safeguarding

The Independent Commission’s written evidence describes the earlier dispute over requiring police participation in Safeguarding Children Boards and Safeguarding Adult Boards.

The policy objective — protecting vulnerable people through multi-agency cooperation — sits naturally within devolved social services and safeguarding responsibilities.

But imposing a statutory duty on the police engages the legal functions of a reserved authority. That can bring the ministerial-consent rules into play.

The Commission highlighted the constitutional asymmetry: the Senedd faced restrictions on placing duties on reserved authorities, whereas Westminster did not face an equivalent statutory restriction when legislating in ways that affected Welsh Government functions. GOV.WALES +1

This is a concrete example of the difference between responsibility for a policy outcome and authority to legislate for every institution needed to achieve it.

3. Private law: devolved policy can require changing general legal rules

Paragraph 3 protects areas of private law, including:

* Contract.
* Agency and bailment.
* Tort.
* Unjust enrichment and restitution.
* Property.
* Trusts and succession.

But there is an important exception: the restriction does not apply where the modification has a purpose, other than modifying private law, that does not relate to a reserved matter.

That allows the Senedd to change aspects of private law when doing so is genuinely necessary to implement devolved policy.

For example, legislation on housing, renting, social care or environmental protection may need to alter contractual or property rights. The Senedd is not automatically prevented from doing so merely because the legislation changes private law.

The difficulty is that the boundary must be legally justified. A provision cannot simply be presented as housing legislation if its real purpose is to alter a reserved subject.

4. Criminal law: a common legal framework is protected

Paragraph 4 protects specified serious offences, including:

* Treason.
* Homicide offences.
* Certain serious offences against the person.
* Sexual offences.
* Offences associated with perjury.

It also protects the general framework governing criminal responsibility and capacity, mental elements such as intention and recklessness, secondary liability, and sentencing and other criminal disposals.

This does not mean the Senedd cannot create criminal offences. It can create offences to enforce legislation within devolved competence, subject to the restrictions.

The distinction is between creating offences to enforce devolved policy and changing the underlying criminal-law framework shared across England and Wales. Legislation.gov.uk +1

5. Protected enactments: a different kind of constitutional lock

Paragraph 5 protects specified enactments from modification by Senedd legislation. The Human Rights Act 1998 and Civil Contingencies Act 2004 are examples.

This is different from the ordinary reservation of a subject. The restriction protects the specified statutory provisions themselves, rather than simply preventing the Senedd from legislating about a reserved policy area.

The Wales Act 2017 explanatory notes make that distinction explicit: protected enactments are protected in their precise content, whereas the ordinary reserved-matters rules operate by reference to the subject matter of legislation and its purpose. 58612_Ch4-EN v0.2.docx +1

There are also restrictions protecting specified provisions of the Government of Wales Act itself and functions of the Comptroller and Auditor General. The exact list has been amended over time, so it is important not to rely on an old reproduction of the original Schedule 7B.

6. Why this can become a hidden constraint

The Independent Commission on the Constitutional Future of Wales examined these restrictions in detail.

It identified two particular problems:

First: institutional classification. To know whether consent is needed, Welsh legislators must establish whether the relevant body is a devolved Welsh authority or a reserved authority. They must also examine which particular function the body is exercising.

Second: functional complexity. A body can exercise both devolved and reserved functions. A provision may therefore be unobjectionable in relation to one function but require consent in relation to another.

The Commission described the paragraphs 8–11 rules as complex, unclear and difficult to operate. It argued that they could act as a hidden impediment to legislation in policy areas generally understood to be devolved. GOV.WALES

This is a significant constitutional observation, although it is the Commission’s assessment rather than a judicial finding that every such restriction is unreasonable.

7. How the restrictions operate in practice

The process is not simply that Westminster can declare a Welsh Bill invalid whenever it dislikes the policy.

* Welsh Government develops a Bill and obtains legal advice on competence.
* If a provision requires ministerial consent under Schedule 7B, that consent normally needs to be secured before the Bill is introduced.
* If the Bill is passed and a competence question remains, the Counsel General or Attorney General can refer it to the Supreme Court during the four-week period after passage.
* The Supreme Court determines the legal competence question. It does not decide whether the policy is desirable.

The Welsh Government’s own legislation handbook says that the UK Government has committed to responding to consent requests within a maximum of 60 working days. GOV.WALES +1

8. The constitutional asymmetry

There is a broader issue here.

Senedd	UK Parliament
Must stay within section 108A and Schedule 7B.	Can legislate for Wales, including in devolved areas.
Can encounter ministerial-consent requirements when changing functions of reserved authorities.	Does not face an equivalent general Schedule 7B restriction when changing functions of devolved Welsh authorities.
Its Acts can be challenged for lack of competence.	Its Acts are not subject to the same devolved-competence test.

The Sewel convention says Westminster will not normally legislate on devolved matters without Senedd consent, but that is not an equivalent legal constraint. The Welsh Government’s Cabinet Handbook explicitly recognises the difference. GOV.WALES +1

9. What is the underlying constitutional issue?

I would distinguish three questions:

* Policy competence: Is the subject devolved?
* Institutional competence: Can the Senedd legislate for the particular public bodies involved?
* Legal-system competence: Can the Senedd change the general legal rules necessary to implement its policy?

Schedule 7A principally governs the first question. Schedule 7B adds restrictions on the second and third.

That creates a potentially important gap: Wales may be democratically responsible for a policy area but lack unrestricted authority over the institutions and legal mechanisms needed to deliver it.

It is not necessarily a flaw in every instance. There are legitimate reasons to protect common criminal-law principles, human-rights legislation and the integrity of national institutions. But the restrictions also preserve a particular allocation of institutional authority between Cardiff and Westminster.

The question I would carry forward is this: should the Senedd need Westminster’s permission to place duties on a UK-controlled institution when those duties are necessary to deliver a policy for which Wales is already democratically and financially responsible?

That is a more precise constitutional question than simply asking whether policing, justice or another subject should be devolved.”.  

“The honest answer is: they are a recurring feature of Welsh legislative preparation, but there is no reliable published figure showing how many times they have actually delayed, altered or prevented a Senedd Bill.

There is evidence that the problem is more than theoretical. But we need to distinguish three things:

* A Bill being identified as potentially requiring consent.
* Consent being requested and granted, possibly after negotiation or changes.
* Consent being refused, delayed or withheld in a way that prevents the proposed legislation.

Those are different measures, and the available public evidence does not give us a complete count of all three.

1. What the evidence establishes

The Independent Commission on the Constitutional Future of Wales examined the issue in its written evidence.

Its account was particularly striking. It described the Schedule 7B paragraphs 8–11 regime as:

* Complex and difficult to navigate.
* Difficult to operate in practice.
* A hidden impediment to legislation in areas generally understood to be devolved.
* A source of uncertainty even among experienced government lawyers.

It also described the restrictions as a more frequent and frustrating obstacle than some of the other Schedule 7B restrictions. That is the Commission’s assessment, based on its experience of the Welsh settlement; it is not a statistical measurement of the number of affected Bills. GOV.WALES +1

2. Examples of where the problem has arisen

A. Safeguarding and police participation

The clearest historical example is the Social Services and Well-being (Wales) Act 2014.

The Welsh legislation sought to require police participation in Safeguarding Children Boards and Safeguarding Adult Boards.

The policy was about safeguarding and social services — devolved responsibilities. But imposing duties on the police raised the question of whether the Senedd could change the functions of a reserved authority without UK ministerial consent.

The Commission identifies this as one of the reasons the restrictions were introduced. GOV.WALES +1

B. Concurrent functions created by UK legislation

A second, more structural problem arises when Westminster legislation gives Welsh Ministers a function alongside UK Ministers.

The Welsh Government’s evidence to the Commission says it developed a practice of seeking an exemption from paragraph 11 of Schedule 7B when agreeing to certain UK Bills that created concurrent functions.

Why?

Because otherwise the new function could become difficult for the Senedd to modify or remove later without UK ministerial consent.

The Commission records that a growing list of such exemptions has accumulated in paragraph 11(6). GOV.WALES

This is important because the restriction can affect the future shape of devolved government, not just the initial passage of a Bill.

C. Even seemingly minor administrative provisions

The Welsh Government’s evidence also identifies paragraph 8(1)(c), which concerns functions specifically exercisable in relation to reserved authorities.

Its example is a function as apparently modest as allowing Welsh Ministers to send a report to a reserved authority.

Even that can require UK ministerial consent, depending on the precise statutory arrangement. GOV.WALES

That demonstrates why the problem cannot be measured simply by counting major policy disagreements.

3. Why we don’t see a simple count of blocked legislation

The Welsh Government’s legislation handbook requires Bill teams to examine these issues during policy development.

Where consent is necessary, it should normally be secured before the Bill is introduced. The UK Government has committed to responding to requests within a maximum of 60 working days. GOV.WALES +1

That means the problem may be resolved before the public ever sees a Bill.

For example, officials might:

* Identify that a proposed duty would affect a reserved authority.
* Seek ministerial consent.
* Negotiate an exception or alternative wording.
* Remove or narrow the provision.
* Introduce the Bill once the competence position is settled.

Only some of those outcomes would appear as a visible constitutional dispute.

So a low number of publicly reported refusals would not establish that the restriction rarely matters.

4. An important distinction from legislative consent motions

There is another statistic that is easy to confuse with this issue.

Senedd Research reported that during the Sixth Senedd (2021–26):

* 76 UK Parliament Bills had legislative consent memoranda laid.
* Those memoranda covered 1,718 clauses.
* 202 memoranda were laid in total, including supplementary memoranda.

Those figures concern the Senedd being asked to consent to Westminster legislating in devolved areas. They do not measure UK ministerial consent requests under Schedule 7B for Senedd legislation. research.senedd.wales

The distinction matters because the two mechanisms operate in opposite directions.

Mechanism	Who is asking whom?
Schedule 7B ministerial consent	Senedd legislation needs permission from a UK Minister for specified provisions.
Legislative consent motion	Westminster seeks the Senedd’s consent to legislate in devolved areas.

5. What would give us a proper answer?

I would want a published annual record containing:

Measure	What it would reveal
Number of Schedule 7B consent requests	Frequency of the formal process
Number granted	How often agreement is reached
Number refused	How often UK Ministers block a provision
Average response time	Whether the process causes delay
Number of Bills amended or narrowed	The extent of indirect interference
Number of provisions abandoned before introduction	The hidden effect on legislative ambition
Number of paragraph 11(6) exemptions negotiated	How often the Welsh Government has to secure future legislative freedom

I have not found a comprehensive public dataset reporting those figures.

The conclusion I would draw is carefully limited: Schedule 7B consent requirements are an established and recurring constraint in the legislative process, with documented examples and a recorded history of negotiated workarounds. The evidence supports the claim that they complicate Welsh lawmaking. It does not yet support a numerical claim about how many Bills they have stopped.

And there is a constitutional point worth retaining: a system can constrain legislative autonomy without frequently producing an outright refusal. If the Senedd must routinely negotiate with Westminster over the institutions needed to implement devolved policy, that dependence is itself part of the settlement.”.   

“The short answer is: a substantial range of administrative, regulatory, funding, approval and implementation functions can be placed in Welsh Ministers’ hands without changing the subjects on which the Senedd can legislate. Sections 58 and 58A provide two different legal routes to do this.

The distinction is particularly useful when considering whether Wales can acquire more control over a policy without first negotiating a full transfer of legislative competence.

1. Section 58: transfer specific ministerial functions

Section 58 of the Government of Wales Act 2006 allows functions exercised by UK Ministers in relation to Wales to be transferred to:

* Welsh Ministers collectively;
* The First Minister; or
* The Counsel General.

It is deliberately flexible. An Order in Council can provide for several different arrangements.

Arrangement	What it means
Full transfer	Welsh Ministers exercise the function instead of the UK Minister.
Concurrent exercise	Welsh and UK Ministers can both exercise the function.
Joint exercise	The function is exercised jointly under the terms of the arrangement.
Welsh Minister subject to UK agreement	Welsh Ministers can exercise the function only with the relevant UK Minister’s agreement.
Welsh Minister subject to consultation	Welsh Ministers must consult the UK Minister before exercising it.
UK Minister subject to Welsh agreement	The UK Minister cannot exercise the function in relation to Wales without Welsh agreement.
UK Minister subject to Welsh consultation	The UK Minister must consult Welsh Ministers before exercising it.

The Order in Council requires approval by both Houses of Parliament and the Welsh Ministers. It can also make consequential amendments to legislation and other instruments. Legislation.gov.uk +1

What kinds of functions?

Section 58 is broad enough to cover statutory functions already held by UK Ministers, including functions relating to:

* Grant administration and financial assistance.
* Licensing and regulatory approvals.
* Inspection and enforcement.
* Environmental and agricultural administration.
* Transport administration.
* Decisions about the exercise of particular statutory powers.
* The making of subordinate legislation, where the relevant ministerial power is transferred.

That last point needs care. A power to make regulations under a UK Act can be transferred as a ministerial function. This does not give the Senedd additional legislative competence: it gives Welsh Ministers the particular delegated lawmaking power specified in the transfer.

Schedule 3 also allows transfers to cover certain cross-border bodies and, in limited circumstances, English border areas. Legislation.gov.uk +1

2. Section 58A: executive powers that accompany devolved responsibilities
