Section 58A was inserted by the Wales Act 2017. It works differently.

It provides that certain executive ministerial functions are exercisable by Welsh Ministers automatically, rather than requiring an individual Order in Council for each function.

The statutory definition is important. These are functions of the Crown of a kind exercisable on its behalf by a Minister of the Crown, including functions involving expenditure or other financial matters, but excluding functions conferred or imposed by legislation or the prerogative.

The legislation describes this as providing Welsh Ministers with common-law-type executive powers. Legislation.gov.uk +1

There are two main routes:

A. Functions within devolved competence

Welsh Ministers can exercise executive functions to the extent that their exercise is within devolved competence.

For example, this supports the ordinary executive activity involved in administering devolved services and pursuing devolved policies.

B. Functions ancillary to a Welsh function outside devolved competence

Welsh Ministers can also exercise executive functions that facilitate or support a function they exercise outside devolved competence.

This is significant because it recognises that Welsh Government responsibilities do not always fit neatly inside the legislative competence boundary.

However, section 58A does not transfer every UK ministerial power. Its definition excludes functions conferred or imposed by legislation or the prerogative. Those statutory functions may instead require a section 58 transfer or another legislative arrangement.

There are also exceptions under which Welsh Ministers exercise certain functions concurrently with UK Ministers, rather than exclusively. Legislation.gov.uk +1

3. What section 58A cannot do

Section 58A contains its own limits on devolved competence.

Under subsections (7) and (8):

* Welsh Ministers cannot use these executive powers to make subordinate legislation containing provisions that the Senedd itself would be outside competence to enact.
* They cannot exercise a non-legislative function in a way that would require an Act of the Senedd conferring that function to be outside competence.

So section 58A cannot be used as a back door to expand the Senedd’s legislative jurisdiction.

This is an important distinction: executive authority can sometimes extend beyond the boundaries of the Senedd’s legislative competence, but only within the particular limits Parliament has set. Legislation.gov.uk +1

4. What this could mean in particular policy areas

Here are illustrative possibilities, not claims that each function has already been transferred.

Policy area	Possible executive transfer or arrangement	What would remain reserved
Policing	Welsh approval or oversight functions, funding administration, specified governance functions	General police law, policing powers and the reserved institutional framework
Justice	Administration of particular youth justice or probation programmes; funding and commissioning functions	Courts, criminal law, prisons and other retained justice powers
Rail	Specific investment approvals, funding decisions or infrastructure-management functions	Wider reserved railway framework and UK-wide network functions
Crown Estate	Defined management or administrative functions concerning Welsh assets	Ownership, the wider Crown Estate framework and any functions not transferred
Energy	Specific consent, administrative or funding functions where legally transferable	Reserved electricity-market, nuclear and strategic energy functions

There is an important distinction between transferring a ministerial function and transferring a public authority’s function. Section 58 is a mechanism for transferring functions of Ministers of the Crown. Where a function belongs to a separate reserved public authority, a different statutory mechanism or legislation may be required. Schedule 7B can also become relevant.

5. The constitutional significance: authority without a new devolution settlement

This gives us three distinct levels of change:

Level 1 — Executive transfer

Welsh Ministers administer, approve, fund, regulate or implement a function previously exercised by UK Ministers.

Sections 58 / 58A

Level 2 — Shared executive authority

Welsh and UK Ministers exercise functions jointly or concurrently, or one government’s exercise is conditional on the other’s agreement or consultation.

Section 58 arrangements

Level 3 — Legislative devolution

The Senedd acquires competence to make primary legislation in a subject or area previously reserved.

Requires a change to legislative competence

The first two levels can be achieved without the third.

That means a government could agree to transfer meaningful responsibility for particular aspects of policing, rail, justice administration or Crown Estate management without first changing the Schedule 7A reservations.

But there is a trade-off. An executive transfer does not necessarily give Wales the ability to redesign the underlying statutory framework. Nor does it necessarily protect the transferred function from later alteration or revocation through the relevant legal process.

6. The question this raises for the Welsh negotiations

There is a useful distinction between three requests that can sound similar in political correspondence:

* Give Wales a voice — consultation, representation or participation in a UK institution.
* Give Wales executive authority — Welsh Ministers acquire the ability to make specified decisions.
* Give Wales legislative authority — the Senedd can determine the law governing the subject.

Sections 58 and 58A offer routes to the second, not a substitute for the third.

This is particularly relevant to the policing, justice, rail and Crown Estate discussions. A refusal to transfer legislative competence does not, by itself, establish that executive functions cannot be transferred.

The next question worth examining is whether the UK Government’s current offers in those four areas amount to meaningful executive authority, or mainly enhanced participation in UK-controlled institutions. That would let us apply the distinction directly to Burnham’s letter.”.  

“Yes. The key is to test each offer against a simple question:

Does Wales acquire a decision-making power of its own, or does it acquire a stronger position inside a system whose decisions remain legally controlled elsewhere?

I would assess the four areas separately. A seat on a board, a right to be consulted and a power to approve a decision are constitutionally different things. So is administering a budget from deciding how that budget is raised and allocated.

The documents point to a mixed answer. Justice contains a real, though narrow, transfer of executive responsibility. Rail offers some meaningful shared governance, but not Welsh control of infrastructure. Policing offers the prospect of greater influence, without yet specifying powers. The Crown Estate offer is principally consultation and representation.

Here is the breakdown, using the Prime Minister’s letter of 20 November and the associated agreements available by 24 November 2026.

1. Policing — enhanced participation, not yet transferred authority

No defined transfer of powers

Burnham offers:

* A “clear role” for Welsh Government in policing governance.
* Further engagement with the Home Office and Wales Office.
* An opportunity to shape future policing governance arrangements.
* Stronger coordination between policing and devolved services.
* A Wales-wide approach to local policing governance.

He also describes a future policing model built around local government structures, with powers and levers for those responsible for governing local policing.

But there is a significant omission: the letter does not specify which statutory decisions Welsh Ministers would acquire the power to make.

It does not identify transferred powers over police budgets, appointments, strategic priorities, inspection, performance intervention or operational accountability.

The existing Policing Partnership Board for Wales and the pan-Wales Assistant Chief Constable role are cited as examples of existing cooperation, not new transfers of authority. GOV.WALES +1

Assessment: primarily enhanced participation, with a possible future governance role. Until the legal powers and decision rights are specified, it cannot be treated as a transfer of executive authority to Welsh Ministers.

2. Justice — some genuine executive transfer, but limited in scope

Specific funding transfer agreed

This is the most substantial example of actual executive devolution among the four areas.

The March 2026 agreement provides for:

* Transfer to Welsh Government of funding to reduce the use of remand for children in Wales.
* Transfer of remaining early-intervention funding, including the Turnaround programme.
* Both changes taking effect from April 2027.
* Welsh Government directing this funding in accordance with Welsh priorities.
* Further discussions about an expanded role in the Youth Justice Core Grant.
* Development of a clearer Welsh Government role in youth justice oversight and strategy.

The probation offer is less developed. The two governments and Welsh police and crime commissioners committed to produce an MoU by the end of 2026, focusing on cooperation between probation and devolved services. A wider review of probation governance is also promised. GOV.WALES +2

The distinction is important:

Function	Position
Certain youth justice funding	Transfer agreed, effective April 2027
Welsh allocation of that funding	Welsh Government gains meaningful discretion
Youth justice oversight	Expanded role being developed
Probation partnership	Cooperation and flexibility being negotiated
Probation management and statutory authority	Not transferred
Courts, prisons and wider criminal justice	Remain reserved

Assessment: a genuine but restricted transfer of executive responsibility in youth justice. Much of the wider justice offer remains partnership working rather than devolution.

It is worth noting that transferring a funding stream is not the same as transferring responsibility for the institution or service that the money supports.

3. Crown Estate — representation without management authority

No transfer of management

Burnham explicitly rejects:

* Devolving management of the Crown Estate in Wales.
* Preparing now for a future transfer.

Instead, he points to:

* A Commissioner with special responsibility for Wales.
* Wales-specific reporting.
* A Wales Forum.
* Greater consideration of Welsh priorities.
* Continued cooperation on ports, offshore wind supply chains and economic development.

The letter expressly envisages retaining the Crown Estate’s operational independence. GOV.WALES

What is missing?

* Welsh control over management decisions.
* Welsh authority over the investment strategy for Welsh assets.
* Control over the relevant revenue.
* Welsh authority to determine how those assets are managed or developed.

A Wales-specific reporting arrangement may improve transparency. A Wales Forum may improve access and influence. Neither is equivalent to Welsh Ministers acquiring the legal authority to direct the management of the estate.

Assessment: participation, consultation and improved visibility. No meaningful transfer of executive management authority is offered.

4. Rail infrastructure — meaningful joint governance, but ultimate control remains elsewhere

Shared governance and delegated delivery

This one requires more care because there is more substance than simply giving Wales a seat at a meeting.

The Prime Minister identifies several changes:

* Governance of Welsh rail enhancements is to sit with the Wales Rail Board.
* Great British Railways (GBR) is to work with Transport for Wales.
* A GBR Wales and Borders structure is intended to have local operational and financial decision-making powers.
* Welsh Ministers are to be involved in shared objectives and investment planning.
* GBR will have a statutory obligation to consider Welsh needs.
* A formal partnership between GBR and Transport for Wales is being developed.

The underlying March 2026 rail MoU gives us a much clearer picture of what these arrangements mean.

What Wales actually gains

The Wales Rail Board is formally recognised as a strategic body. It can:

* Review funding allocations, business plans and performance.
* Oversee strategic delivery of enhancement programmes.
* Manage a pipeline of future priorities.
* Hold delivery partners to account for agreed outcomes.
* Develop governance arrangements for joint decision-making and changes to projects.

The proposed GBR Wales and Borders structure is also intended to have its own budget allocation and decision-making authority within GBR. GOV.WALES +1

Those are meaningful governance and delivery arrangements.

What Wales does not gain

The same MoU makes several important qualifications:

* The MoU does not alter the statutory functions of either government.
* It is not legally binding.
* The Wales Rail Board’s work remains subject to the governance and assurance requirements attached to the relevant funding sources.
* Shared objectives do not themselves determine funding allocations or statutory functions.
* GBR retains responsibility for its reserved infrastructure functions.
* The UK Secretary of State retains the national funding framework and strategic responsibilities.

The Board can therefore influence, scrutinise and oversee the enhancement programme without acquiring independent authority over the whole Welsh rail infrastructure budget or network. GOV.WALES

Assessment: a meaningful increase in shared governance and delegated operational authority within the GBR structure, but not a transfer of rail infrastructure responsibility to Welsh Ministers or the Senedd.

5. Comparison: what kind of power is actually on offer?

Area	What Wales receives	What remains elsewhere	Character of offer
Policing	Greater governance role to be developed	Statutory policing authority and reserved framework	Participation, prospective governance
Justice	Specific youth justice funding and discretion over its use	Wider justice institutions and probation authority	Limited executive transfer plus partnership
Crown Estate	Commissioner, reporting and Wales Forum	Management, investment and operational control	Representation and consultation
Rail	Wales Rail Board, shared objectives, local GBR decision-making	National infrastructure funding and reserved statutory functions	Shared governance and delegated delivery

6. Apply the six tests of actual authority

This is where the distinction becomes particularly clear.

Test	Policing	Justice	Crown Estate	Rail
Welsh representation	Yes	Yes	Yes	Yes
Welsh decision-making power	Not yet specified	Yes, over specified youth funding	No management power	Yes, within defined governance arrangements
Control of relevant resources	Not specified	Specific funding streams	No	Limited; subject to funding arrangements
Ability to direct delivery	Not specified	Within transferred funding programmes	No	Some influence and oversight, not overall control
Accountability to Senedd	Not established for policing	Welsh Government accountable for its funding decisions	No transfer of management accountability	Limited to Welsh functions and arrangements
Security of authority	No transfer identified	Specific agreement and implementation required	No transfer	MoU is non-binding; statutory functions unchanged

The table needs one qualification: a function can be genuinely delegated within a UK institution without becoming a Welsh Government function. GBR’s internal decision-making authority is an example.

That is still a form of decentralisation, but it is not the same as constitutional devolution.

7. The wider constitutional pattern

There is a common approach running through the four offers:

The UK Government’s preferred model

* Retain the overarching reserved legal framework.
* Preserve UK-level institutional control.
* Give Welsh institutions greater involvement in governance.
* Transfer selected funding or operational responsibilities.
* Improve coordination between reserved and devolved services.
* Use boards, forums, partnerships and agreements to manage the interfaces.

That is a recognisable model of administrative and collaborative decentralisation. It is not simply an empty offer: the youth justice funding changes and rail governance arrangements have practical substance.

But it also leaves a major constitutional question unresolved: who has the final authority when the governments disagree?

For example:

* Can Welsh Ministers require a change to policing priorities?
* Can they direct Crown Estate investment in Wales?
* Can they determine which rail infrastructure projects receive funding?
* Can they change the statutory framework governing probation?

The offers do not give Wales general authority to do those things.

There is also a legal durability question. The rail MoU expressly says it does not alter statutory functions and is not legally binding. The youth justice funding agreement is more concrete, but its implementation and scope still matter. The policing and Crown Estate offers do not establish new transferred statutory powers.

8. The distinction I would preserve

I would describe the four offers as follows:

* Policing: enhanced Welsh involvement in reserved governance.
* Justice: limited executive devolution in youth justice, with wider partnership arrangements.
* Crown Estate: improved Welsh representation and transparency, without management devolution.
* Rail: shared governance and delegated operational authority, without full infrastructure devolution.

This is more precise than saying that Burnham simply offered no devolution at all. Equally, it would be inaccurate to present the four offers as equivalent to transferring powers to Wales.

The First Minister’s response makes the underlying disagreement explicit: he argues that cross-border cooperation should be managed through intergovernmental arrangements rather than used as a reason to retain powers in Whitehall. 23 November 2026

The next constitutional question is whether these arrangements give Wales a protected right to participate in decisions, or merely an opportunity to participate at the discretion of UK institutions. That is the difference between institutional partnership and a durable sharing of power.”.  

“The dividing line is that section 109 can change the boundaries of the Senedd’s legislative competence; it is not a general power to redesign the Welsh constitution or transfer every government function.

It is, however, a more substantial mechanism than a simple administrative adjustment. It can change what the Senedd is legally permitted to legislate about.

1. What section 109 can change

Section 109(1) of the Government of Wales Act 2006 expressly authorises an Order in Council to amend Schedule 7A or Schedule 7B.

That permits changes to two important parts of the settlement.

What can be changed	Example
Reserved subjects in Schedule 7A	Remove or narrow a reservation, or transfer a defined subject into devolved competence.
Exceptions to reservations	Define particular Welsh powers within a subject that otherwise remains reserved.
Schedule 7B restrictions	Remove or narrow restrictions on the Senedd modifying private law, criminal law or functions of reserved authorities, within the statutory structure.
Protected enactment provisions	Amend the relevant competence restrictions, subject to the scope of the Order and its consequential provisions.
Consequential statutory provisions	Amend other legislation where appropriate in connection with the changes to Schedule 7A or 7B.

The final point is important. Section 109(2) expressly permits modifications to other enactments, prerogative instruments and documents where these are appropriate in connection with the Order. Legislation.gov.uk +1

Example: policing

An Order could amend Schedule 7A to remove or narrow specified policing reservations.

That would expand the Senedd’s legislative competence in the relevant area.

It could also make appropriate consequential amendments to other legislation.

It would not automatically transfer every policing function, police asset, funding stream or operational responsibility to Welsh Ministers. Those matters would need to be addressed separately.

2. What requires a different legal mechanism?

The distinction becomes clearer when we look at the kind of change being proposed.

Proposed change	Legal route
Remove a defined reservation	Section 109 Order in Council
Create a Welsh exception to a reservation	Section 109 Order in Council
Relax a Schedule 7B restriction	Section 109 Order in Council
Transfer a UK Minister’s existing statutory function to Welsh Ministers	Section 58 Order in Council
Make a UK Minister’s exercise of a function subject to Welsh agreement	Section 58 Order in Council
Transfer functions of an independent UK public authority	Usually requires legislation or another specific statutory mechanism
Create a new statutory Welsh institution with legally defined powers	Usually primary legislation
Establish new statutory duties, powers or regulatory structures not achievable through existing mechanisms	Primary legislation
Change the basic constitutional structure of the Senedd or Welsh Government	Primary legislation
Create a new taxation or borrowing framework outside existing statutory powers	Primary legislation

The word usually matters. Section 109(2) can make consequential amendments to other legislation, so not every amendment to an existing Act necessarily requires a separate Westminster Bill.

But it cannot be treated as an unlimited power to amend unrelated legislation or establish an entirely new constitutional settlement.

3. The approval process for a section 109 Order

There is a significant safeguard built into the procedure.

* Draft Order prepared
    The proposed changes to Schedule 7A or 7B are drafted.
* Senedd approval
    The draft must be approved by resolution of the Senedd.
* House of Commons approval
    The draft must also be approved by resolution of the Commons.
* House of Lords approval
    The draft must also be approved by resolution of the Lords.
* Order made in Council
    Following those approvals, the Order can be made.

This is an affirmative approval procedure. Neither the UK Government acting alone nor the Senedd acting alone can make the change.

Section 109(5) also protects the validity and continuing operation of existing Senedd Acts when the competence schedules are subsequently altered, unless another enactment provides otherwise. Legislation.gov.uk

4. The important distinction between sections 58 and 109

These provisions can be used to achieve different kinds of constitutional change.

Section 109

Change what Wales can legislate about.

The Senedd gains or loses legislative competence over a subject or a particular aspect of it.

Section 58

Change who exercises an existing ministerial function.

Welsh Ministers acquire specified executive powers, potentially concurrently or jointly with UK Ministers.

Primary legislation

Create or redesign the statutory machinery.

New institutions, legal duties, powers, regulatory systems or wider constitutional arrangements can be established or altered where the existing statutory mechanisms are insufficient.

5. Applying this to the four areas we’ve been examining

Area	Possible section 109 change	What might still need other legislation or arrangements
Policing	Remove or narrow reservations relating to policing	Transfer police governance functions, establish Welsh institutional structures, allocate assets and funding
Justice	Devolve specified elements of youth justice, probation or other justice matters	Transfer statutory functions, reorganise institutions, establish funding and accountability arrangements
Crown Estate	Change the relevant reservations affecting the Senedd’s legislative competence	Transfer management, ownership or statutory functions and deal with revenue arrangements
Rail	Change reservations affecting Welsh legislative authority over specified rail matters	Transfer infrastructure functions, assets, funding and operational responsibilities

This is where the distinction becomes useful in evaluating the UK Government’s position.

A refusal to transfer legislative competence does not necessarily mean that the UK Government cannot transfer executive functions under section 58.

Likewise, an agreement to transfer executive functions does not necessarily require a change to Schedule 7A.

6. A further constitutional point: section 109 is not entrenchment

Although section 109 requires the approval of both the Senedd and both Houses of Parliament, it does not make the resulting settlement permanently protected.

Westminster retains its general legislative power under section 107(5) to legislate for Wales.

Primary legislation could amend the Government of Wales Act itself, including the section 109 mechanism, subject to the ordinary constitutional and political constraints.

So section 109 is a negotiated statutory route for changing the competence settlement, not a guarantee that Westminster can never change it unilaterally.

7. What this means for the constitutional debate

There is a useful distinction between three levels of ambition:

* Adjust the boundaries: use section 109 to amend the reserved-matters list and Schedule 7B restrictions.
* Transfer the machinery: use section 58 and other available mechanisms to put specific executive functions in Welsh hands.
* Redesign the settlement: use primary legislation to establish new institutions, statutory powers, funding arrangements and constitutional safeguards.

These are not necessarily competing approaches. They could be combined.

For example, a negotiated policing settlement could use section 109 to devolve specified legislative competence, section 58 to transfer existing ministerial functions, and primary legislation to establish any new Welsh policing governance structure.

The point I would carry forward is that the current settlement already contains a statutory route for changing the boundary between reserved and devolved subjects without passing an entirely new Wales Act. The fact that this route exists makes it important to distinguish a refusal to use the available mechanism from a claim that a change would require wholesale constitutional legislation.

Primary sources: Government of Wales Act 2006, section 109⁠￼ · Section 58, transfer of ministerial functions⁠￼ · Section 108A, legislative competence⁠￼.”.  

“The Welsh devolution settlement is substantially established in statute, but much less securely protected against unilateral change by Westminster.

That distinction is crucial. The law establishes the Senedd, defines its powers, allocates executive responsibilities and provides mechanisms for changing the settlement. But many of the conventions governing how the UK and Welsh Governments should respect one another are political commitments rather than enforceable legal restrictions.

I would separate the settlement into three layers:

* Statutory architecture: what institutions exist, what powers they have and how those powers can be changed.
* Constitutional conventions: how Westminster and the devolved governments are expected to behave towards one another.
* Political safeguards: agreements, intergovernmental machinery and expectations that depend on governments choosing to cooperate.

1. What is protected by statute?

The central statute is the Government of Wales Act 2006, substantially amended by the Wales Acts of 2014 and 2017 and the Senedd and Elections (Wales) Act 2020.

Element of the settlement	Statutory basis	What the law provides
Existence of the Senedd	Government of Wales Act 2006, Part 1	Establishes the legislature and its composition and procedures.
Welsh Government	Part 2	Establishes the executive and defines its institutional structure.
Permanence	Section A1, inserted by Wales Act 2017	Declares both institutions permanent and states that they are not to be abolished except on the basis of a decision by the people of Wales in a referendum.
Recognition of Welsh law	Section A2	Recognises that Wales has a body of law made by the Senedd and Welsh Ministers.
Legislative competence	Sections 107–108A and Schedules 7A and 7B	Defines what the Senedd can legislate about and the restrictions it must observe.
Executive functions	Sections 58 and 58A, among others	Provides mechanisms for transferring and exercising executive powers.
Taxation and borrowing	Government of Wales Act as amended by Wales Act 2014	Establishes devolved taxation and borrowing powers.
Changing competence	Section 109	Provides a statutory route for changing the reserved-matters and restrictions schedules.

These are legal provisions, not merely political promises. The Senedd’s legislative competence is a legal question which courts can determine. A provision outside competence is not law to the extent of that incompatibility. Legislation.gov.uk +2

The important qualification: permanence is not entrenchment

Section A1 is constitutionally significant, but it does not make the Senedd legally indestructible.

The UK Parliament retains legislative sovereignty. It could expressly amend or repeal section A1 through subsequent primary legislation.

So there are two different propositions:

* The UK Parliament has enacted a statutory commitment that the Senedd and Welsh Government are permanent and should not be abolished without a Welsh referendum.
* The UK Parliament has not legally surrendered its ability to amend that commitment.

The first is a real statutory protection. The second limits how secure that protection is.

2. What depends on political convention?

This is where the difference becomes much more pronounced.

A. The Sewel Convention

Section 107(6) of the Government of Wales Act recognises that Westminster will not normally legislate on devolved matters without Senedd consent.

But the convention itself is not legally enforceable.

The Supreme Court confirmed in Miller (2017) that statutory recognition did not turn Sewel into a legal rule which courts could enforce. Westminster can legislate without consent, although doing so may carry significant political consequences. House of Commons Library +2

This means the Senedd has:

* A recognised constitutional expectation of consent.
* A formal legislative consent process.
* An opportunity to express disagreement.

It does not have a legally enforceable veto over Westminster legislation in devolved areas.

B. Respect for devolved responsibilities

There is a wider convention that the UK Government and Parliament should respect the responsibilities of devolved institutions.

This underpins the practical operation of devolution, but it does not prevent Westminster from legislating in devolved areas.

The Government of Wales Act expressly preserves Westminster’s power to legislate for Wales in section 107(5). Legislation.gov.uk +1

C. Intergovernmental cooperation

The 2022 Intergovernmental Relations Review established:

* The Prime Minister and Heads of Devolved Governments Council.
* The Interministerial Standing Committee.
* Portfolio-level Interministerial Groups.
* The Finance Interministerial Standing Committee.
* A joint Secretariat.
* A dispute avoidance and resolution procedure.

These arrangements are important, but the governments explicitly described them as a statement of political intent. They do not create new legal obligations, override existing legal relationships or establish justiciable rights. GOV.UK +1

In practical terms, the dispute process gives governments a way to escalate disagreements. It does not give Wales a binding adjudicator capable of ordering Westminster to change its policy.

3. What about funding? A particularly important vulnerability

The fiscal settlement combines statutory powers with intergovernmental agreements.

Element	Legal or political foundation
Welsh tax-raising powers	Statute
Welsh borrowing powers	Statute
Ability to allocate devolved budgets	Statutory responsibilities and budgetary procedures
Barnett formula arrangements	Administrative and intergovernmental funding arrangements
Needs-based funding factor	Fiscal framework agreement
Block grant adjustment methodology	Fiscal framework agreement
Disputes about fiscal calculations	Agreed dispute-resolution arrangements

The Welsh fiscal framework agreement sets out the needs-based funding factor, tax adjustments, borrowing arrangements and dispute procedures. It implements statutory powers, but the agreement itself is not equivalent to constitutional entrenchment of those funding arrangements. GOV.WALES +2

That distinction matters enormously.

Wales can have statutory responsibility for delivering a service without having an equally entrenched statutory guarantee of the funding methodology that supports it.

4. Putting the layers together

The Welsh settlement: three levels of protection

Statutory protection

Institutions, powers and legal boundaries

The Senedd and Welsh Government exist in statute. Their responsibilities and legislative competence are defined by law. Courts can enforce those boundaries.

Statutory recognition, political enforcement

The principle of consent

Sewel is recognised in the Government of Wales Act, but the convention is not legally enforceable. Westminster retains the legal power to legislate.

Political agreement

Cooperation, funding and dispute resolution

Intergovernmental arrangements and much of the fiscal framework depend on agreed procedures and governments honouring their commitments.

5. The asymmetry at the heart of the settlement

This is perhaps the most important point in the whole discussion.

The Senedd is legally constrained by the devolution settlement. It cannot simply legislate on reserved matters, modify protected law or disregard the Schedule 7B restrictions.

Westminster, meanwhile, retains the legal power to legislate for Wales, including in devolved areas.

The Senedd’s protection against that power is principally political: the Sewel Convention, the expectation of consent and the political consequences of disregarding Welsh democratic opposition.

The result is an asymmetrical arrangement:

Question	Senedd	Westminster
Can it legislate on devolved matters?	Yes, within statutory competence	Yes, under parliamentary sovereignty
Can it legislate on reserved matters?	Generally no	Yes
Can it change the statutory competence settlement alone?	No	Yes, through primary legislation
Is there a convention governing legislation in the other’s sphere?	Welsh institutions observe the constitutional settlement	Sewel says Westminster will not normally legislate in devolved areas without consent
Can a court enforce Sewel?	No	No

The House of Lords Constitution Committee has also examined this imbalance, arguing for more reliable consultation, clearer procedures and better explanations when Westminster proceeds without devolved consent. Select Committee on the Constitution +1

6. What would stronger protection actually require?

There are several possible reforms, each addressing a different weakness.

Reform	What it would protect
Statutory duty to consult the Senedd	A guaranteed opportunity to participate before UK decisions affecting devolved responsibilities
Statutory duty to seek consent	A stronger procedural expectation, although not necessarily an absolute veto
Legally protected consent for defined categories	Welsh authority over specified decisions
Statutory intergovernmental dispute resolution	A more formal process and potentially enforceable procedural obligations
Statutory fiscal framework	Greater security for funding calculations and agreed financial arrangements
Stronger protection of devolved competence	Greater difficulty in changing the boundaries without Welsh agreement

There is an important distinction between making a procedure legally enforceable and making its outcome binding.

For example, legislation could require Westminster to:

* Notify the Senedd.
* Publish an impact assessment.
* Seek consent.
* Allow a defined period for consideration.
* Publish reasons if it proceeds without consent.

A court might be able to enforce those procedural requirements without having the power to decide the underlying political disagreement.

That would be a different model from giving the Senedd an absolute veto.

7. The central conclusion

The Welsh settlement is not merely a collection of informal arrangements. Its institutional foundations and legislative powers are firmly established in statute.

But the mutual respect, consent and cooperation needed to make an asymmetric devolution settlement work are much less securely protected.

The distinction is:

* Wales has statutory authority over specified responsibilities.
* Westminster has retained ultimate legal sovereignty.
* The convention that Westminster should normally respect devolved democratic authority is politically important but not judicially enforceable.
* The machinery for resolving disagreements is agreed, but not binding.

That leaves a fundamental constitutional question: is Wales a government with legally protected responsibilities but politically negotiated protection against interference, or should it have legally protected authority over at least some of the decisions that currently remain at Westminster’s discretion?

That is the point at which discussion moves from improving the operation of devolution to considering a different constitutional settlement.”.  

“Now that the Welsh Government has published the actual five-page letter, we can be more precise. It is dated 1 October 2026, addressed to Prime Minister Andy Burnham, and follows their July discussion in Glasgow and a letter Burnham sent on 21 September. It is not simply a request for four powers: it sets out a broader constitutional and fiscal programme. GOV.WALES

The letter has six substantive elements.

1. The overarching request: parity with Scotland

Ap Iorwerth’s starting proposition is that Wales should have:

“the same constitutional and fiscal tools already available in Scotland.”

He argues that the May Senedd election gave his government a mandate to secure the powers, funding and financial flexibility needed to improve public services and grow the economy.

He wants a practical programme of constitutional reform, not powers for their own sake. The stated purposes are better integration of public services, improved outcomes and economic growth. GOV.WALES

2. Policing and justice: devolution within a single Welsh framework

This is presented as a particularly urgent request.

He asks for:

* Devolution of policing to Wales.
* Devolution of justice, bringing policing and justice within a single Welsh devolved framework.
* A clear pathway towards policing devolution, using the forthcoming Police Reform Bill as an opportunity.
* Early devolution of youth justice as a practical first step.
* Welsh influence over the UK Government’s reforms of youth justice across England and Wales.

His argument is that Wales controls many of the services affecting offending, rehabilitation and community safety — health, education, housing and local government — but does not control the corresponding reserved justice system.

He also identifies a financial problem: UK justice decisions can create additional demand and costs for devolved Welsh services without Welsh Government having a decision-making role over the underlying policy or funding.

He specifically points out that more than half of police funding in Wales comes from devolved sources. GOV.WALES

The actual request is therefore broader than improved consultation: he wants responsibility for policing and justice transferred, with youth justice an early step towards that settlement.

3. Rail: full devolution of infrastructure

This is also explicit.

He requests full devolution of rail infrastructure, arguing that Wales needs control over investment decisions and the ability to integrate infrastructure and passenger services.

He uses the Core Valley Lines as evidence that Transport for Wales can own, manage and deliver major rail infrastructure.

He also makes three connected funding requests:

* A fair funding settlement accompanying infrastructure devolution.
* Redesignation of HS2 as an England-only project, so Wales receives its fair funding consequentials.
* Wales receiving its fair share of UK transport investment.

He welcomes the UK Government’s commitment to a £14 billion Welsh rail investment programme over 15 years, but treats that as distinct from the constitutional question of who controls the infrastructure. GOV.WALES

That distinction matters: he is not presenting a £14bn investment commitment as a substitute for rail devolution.

4. Crown Estate: transfer of control and economic benefit

He requests devolution of the Crown Estate in Wales.

The stated reasons are:

* Greater democratic accountability for Welsh assets.
* Welsh priorities determining decisions about Welsh natural resources.
* Maximising the economic benefits generated by those assets.
* Supporting investment and communities throughout Wales.

He points to Scotland’s Crown Estate arrangements as evidence that devolution is practicable.

He also acknowledges that the practical implications need work. He identifies the Group on the Future of the Crown Estate in Wales, established to examine the legal, financial, energy, local-government and economic-development dimensions.

He asks the UK Government to engage with that group and agree a path towards devolution as its work progresses. GOV.WALES

5. Funding flexibility and financial fairness

This section contains two immediate requests, as well as a wider fiscal objective.

Request	What he wants
Wales Reserve	Permanently remove all restrictions on access, bringing Wales into line with Scotland.
Vacant Land Tax	Progress the transfer of Senedd competence to introduce the tax, beginning with an urgently issued joint consultation with HM Treasury.
Wider fiscal settlement	Fairer, needs-based funding reflecting Welsh demographic and economic circumstances.
Borrowing and fiscal powers	Greater and more flexible fiscal and borrowing powers to improve resilience and long-term investment.

He stresses that the Wales Reserve restrictions leave the Welsh Government able to draw down well under 1% of its budget from the reserve in the following financial year.

On the Vacant Land Tax, he says the Welsh Government has been engaging with the UK Government for eight years and wants the joint consultation issued urgently. GOV.WALES

This is important because it shows that his financial requests are not confined to increasing the block grant. He wants greater control over the timing and use of resources, as well as additional tax competence.

6. A Wales Bill: the legislative vehicle for wider reform

The letter proposes a Wales Bill as the most effective way of bringing the reforms together.

He wants early engagement between the governments on its:

* Scope.
* Timing.
* Content.

He also identifies additional areas that should be considered in discussions about the Bill:

* Broadcasting.
* Port regulation.
* Air Passenger Duty.
* Energy regulation and pricing.
* Welfare.

On welfare, he argues that separating reserved welfare policy from devolved services makes it harder to provide coherent, person-centred support.

He uses the Welsh Child Payment pilot, Cynnal, as a practical example. He asks Burnham to ensure that its payments remain exempt from tax and benefit calculations, so families receive their full value. GOV.WALES

Notice the distinction: these additional areas are framed as subjects for further discussion and possible devolution, not all as fully specified immediate transfers.

7. Intergovernmental relations and the UK Internal Market Act

The final section goes beyond the powers being transferred to Wales.

Ap Iorwerth asks for collective discussions involving Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland on:

* Global events and their consequences.
* Economic growth.
* The cost-of-living crisis.
* The UK–EU relationship.
* Resilience and community cohesion.

He also specifically calls for:

* Stronger intergovernmental relations.
* Improvements to the operation of the Sewel Convention.
* Action to address the negative impact of the UK Internal Market Act on devolved settlements. GOV.WALES

That last point is constitutionally significant. He is asking not only for more powers to be transferred, but also for changes to the rules governing how existing devolved powers operate.

8. The complete request, classified by type

This classification distinguishes the letter’s explicit requests from broader proposals and supporting arguments.

Category	Specific request
Full devolution	Policing and justice
Early or staged devolution	Youth justice
Full devolution	Rail infrastructure
Full devolution	Crown Estate
Immediate fiscal change	Remove restrictions on Wales Reserve
Immediate legislative progress	Vacant Land Tax competence and joint consultation
Fiscal reform	Fairer needs-based funding, greater fiscal and borrowing powers
Further devolution discussions	Broadcasting, port regulation, Air Passenger Duty, energy regulation and pricing, welfare
Legislative programme	Negotiate and introduce a Wales Bill
Specific welfare safeguard	Protect Cynnal payments from tax and benefit calculations
Intergovernmental reform	Strengthen Sewel and intergovernmental relations
Devolution protection	Address the UK Internal Market Act’s effects on devolved settlements

9. What is the underlying constitutional proposition?

I think it is important not to reduce this letter to a list of departmental demands.

There are three connected propositions:

* Parity: Wales should have constitutional and fiscal tools comparable to Scotland.
* Coherence: powers over interconnected public services should be held together sufficiently to allow Welsh democratic government to coordinate them.
* Fairness: Wales should have greater control over its resources, a fairer funding settlement and a meaningful role in decisions affecting its economy and communities.

The proposed Wales Bill is the mechanism intended to bring these together.

There is also a notable sentence near the end: the constitutional settlement should ultimately be decided by the people of Wales. That is a statement about the source of constitutional legitimacy, not simply a request for additional administrative powers. GOV.WALES

One correction to our earlier discussion is particularly important: when we assessed Burnham’s response against the four headline areas, we were looking at only part of the original request. The 1 October letter also contains specific demands on the Wales Reserve, Vacant Land Tax, welfare, the Cynnal payment, broadcasting, ports, Air Passenger Duty, energy, Sewel and the UK Internal Market Act.

Those should all be included when judging whether the 20 November response addressed the request as a whole.”.  

“The important distinction is between accepting a transfer of power, accepting a narrower practical change, and offering more Welsh involvement while retaining the power at UK level. Burnham’s reply does not treat all of Rhun ap Iorwerth’s requests alike.

On the correspondence as a whole, the pattern is: some practical cooperation was accepted; several requests were only partly accommodated; the major transfers of constitutional authority were rejected or left for further discussion.

1. The classification

Ap Iorwerth’s request	Burnham’s response	Classification
Full devolution of policing	Rejected the transfer. Offered the Welsh Government a clearer role in the governance of Welsh police forces within the existing England-and-Wales system.	Partially accepted
Devolution of justice	Rejected a separate devolved justice system. Offered to explore a bigger, clearer Welsh role in probation funding and oversight, and a wider role in youth justice.	Partially accepted
Full devolution of rail infrastructure	Rejected. Great British Railways would retain responsibility for infrastructure, working with Transport for Wales. The Wales Rail Board would take on governance of Welsh rail enhancements.	Partially accepted
Devolution of the Crown Estate in Wales	Rejected both transferring its management and preparing for a future transfer.	Rejected
Greater fiscal flexibility and removal of restrictions on the Wales Reserve	The Treasury discussions on looser financial rules were continuing. Ap Iorwerth described this as a step forward and asked for agreement quickly.	Partially accepted / still under negotiation
Greater fiscal and borrowing powers; fairer funding	Burnham pointed to additional funding for Wales and ongoing fiscal discussions, but the response did not accept the wider request for Scotland-level fiscal powers or a comprehensive funding settlement.	Partially accepted at most
Senedd competence for a Vacant Land Tax	No clear commitment to grant the requested competence is apparent in the reported response.	Not accepted; effectively deferred or unanswered
More devolved employment support	Burnham proposed looking at how further employment-support responsibilities could be devolved to Wales’s regions and communities.	Accepted in principle; details deferred
A bigger Welsh role in youth justice	Burnham offered a wider Welsh role in youth justice.	Partially accepted; details deferred
A bigger Welsh role in probation	Burnham said ministers were exploring a bigger and clearer Welsh role in probation funding and oversight.	Partially accepted; still under discussion
A Wales Bill to deliver wider constitutional reform	No agreement to the proposed comprehensive legislative programme is apparent.	Deferred / no commitment
Further devolution discussions on broadcasting, ports, Air Passenger Duty, energy and welfare	No clear commitment to transfer these powers or open a defined programme of negotiations is apparent in the reported reply.	Deferred / unanswered
Protect the Cynnal Welsh Child Payment from tax and benefit calculations	No clear acceptance of the requested protection is apparent in the reported response.	Unanswered
Stronger intergovernmental relations and Sewel arrangements	No substantive new safeguard or binding change is apparent.	Unanswered / deferred
Address the effects of the UK Internal Market Act on devolution	No clear commitment to the requested change is apparent.	Unanswered / deferred

The clearest reporting on the actual offers is that Burnham proposed further employment-support devolution, a larger Welsh role in probation and youth justice, and continued Treasury discussions on financial flexibility. He did not offer the central transfers of authority requested. Swansea Bay News +2

2. What was actually accepted?

There are a few meaningful areas of movement, but they are narrower than the First Minister’s proposals:

* Employment support: an offer to explore further devolution, including a role for Welsh regions and communities.
* Probation: a possible larger Welsh role in funding and oversight.
* Youth justice: a possible wider Welsh role.
* Rail governance: Welsh involvement through the Wales Rail Board in decisions about enhancements, without transferring control of the infrastructure network.
* Fiscal flexibility: continued negotiations with the Treasury about loosening the rules governing Welsh finances.
* Economic partnership: the proposed Partnership for Growth is to form part of economic discussions.

These are not all the same kind of concession. Some are offers to explore; some are governance arrangements; some are negotiations that have not yet produced an agreed settlement. Swansea Bay News +1

3. What was rejected outright?

The four major constitutional asks were all refused in substance:

* Policing: no transfer of responsibility to the Welsh Government.
* Justice: no separate Welsh justice system or transfer of the overall justice settlement.
* Rail infrastructure: no transfer of ownership or overall infrastructure responsibility to Wales.
* Crown Estate: no transfer of management, and no preparatory work towards a future transfer.

The distinctions matter. Policing, justice and rail received offers of greater Welsh participation within UK-controlled arrangements. The Crown Estate request received a more categorical refusal: Burnham rejected even preparing for a future handover. Swansea Bay News +1

4. What was deferred or left unanswered?

Several requests do not appear to have received a substantive answer or a defined route forward:

* Vacant Land Tax competence.
* The proposed comprehensive Wales Bill.
* Broadcasting, port regulation, Air Passenger Duty, energy regulation and welfare.
* The Cynnal payment safeguard.
* Strengthening the Sewel Convention and intergovernmental arrangements.
* Addressing the UK Internal Market Act’s effects on devolved powers.

I would call these unanswered or deferred, rather than definitively rejected. Silence in the published response is not quite the same as an explicit refusal.

5. The overall balance

The shape of the response

Major transfers of authority

Rejected

Policing, justice, rail infrastructure and Crown Estate management.

Practical Welsh involvement

Some movement

Employment support, probation, youth justice and rail enhancement governance.

Fiscal flexibility

Negotiating

Some willingness to loosen financial rules, but no comprehensive agreement on fiscal autonomy.

Wider constitutional programme

Largely unanswered

Wales Bill, further policy areas, Sewel, UK Internal Market Act and other safeguards.

One qualification: Burnham’s reference to an additional £450 million for the Welsh Government after the Budget is a funding offer, not acceptance of the First Minister’s request for greater control over taxation, borrowing or the Wales Reserve. Those are different forms of financial power. Swansea Bay News +1

My reading: this is not a blanket refusal to work with the Welsh Government. It is a refusal to transfer the central constitutional powers, accompanied by offers to improve Welsh participation in the systems that retain them. That is precisely the distinction we have been calling Participation–Authority Substitution: more influence within a system is not the same as acquiring authority over it.”.  

“I think the source of the confusion is that “England and Wales” is being used here as a Treasury funding classification, not a description of where HS2 actually goes. And that distinction is doing an enormous amount of work.

You’re right about the geography: HS2’s route is in England. No HS2 track is being built in Wales. The Welsh Government has explicitly challenged the classification on that basis. Welsh Government News +1

1. How the UK Government justifies it

The argument goes:

* Heavy rail infrastructure is reserved to the UK Government in Wales.
* The UK Government therefore has responsibility for heavy rail infrastructure in both England and Wales.
* It says that when it spends money on HS2 in England, it is spending within a policy area for which it is responsible across both countries.
* Consequently, it classifies HS2 as an England-and-Wales project for Barnett purposes, rather than giving Wales a population-based share of the expenditure.

That is the Government’s stated position, reiterated in a written answer on 9 November 2026. UK Parliament +1

2. Why that argument is contested

It conflates two different questions:

* Who has constitutional responsibility for a policy area?
* Where is the money actually being spent, and who benefits from that investment?

The UK Government answers the first question. The Welsh Government is challenging the consequences of that answer for the second.

Scotland and Northern Ireland receive Barnett consequentials from HS2 because heavy rail infrastructure is devolved to them. Wales does not, because it is not devolved on the same basis. UK Parliament +1

There is also a material financial consequence. The National Infrastructure Commission for Wales estimated in its 2026 infrastructure assessment that the cumulative loss to Wales associated with the HS2 classification would reach approximately £845 million by 2029–30. That is its estimate of the funding foregone, not a sum legally owed to Wales under the existing formula. The National Infrastructure Commission for Wales

3. The constitutional circularity

This is the part worth examining closely:

* Wales does not control the relevant rail infrastructure powers.
* Because it does not control those powers, the UK Government says it must spend directly on rail infrastructure in Wales.
* Because the UK Government retains those powers, it classifies HS2 expenditure as covering England and Wales.
* Because of that classification, Wales does not receive the same direct Barnett consequential as Scotland and Northern Ireland.

So the existing distribution of powers helps determine the funding classification, and the funding classification then limits Wales’s ability to invest through its own devolved budget.

That is not proof, by itself, that the classification is unlawful. But it is a legitimate constitutional and fiscal dispute. The Welsh Government’s request to designate HS2 as an England-only project is an attempt to break that circularity.

4. And there is a further problem with Burnham’s response

Ap Iorwerth specifically asked for HS2 to be redesignated as an England-only project. The UK Government’s position, reaffirmed in November 2026, remains that heavy rail expenditure is classified on an England-and-Wales basis. It has not accepted that request. UK Parliament +1

The question I would separate out next is this:

Should a UK-wide government be able to use the fact that a power is reserved to it as sufficient justification for withholding Barnett consequentials on a project that is physically located entirely in England?

That is the real issue. It is not a dispute about where the railway runs. It is a dispute about how the constitutional settlement is being used to allocate public money.”.   

“Yes. Let’s separate the reasons Burnham actually gave from whether those reasons were supported by evidence in the letter. That matters because the reply contains some concrete examples and existing arrangements, but much of its case rests on assertions about the risks of changing the settlement.

The source is Burnham’s five-page letter of 20 November 2026. I’ll take each subject in turn, including what he offered instead. GOV.WALES

1. Policing — retain the England-and-Wales system

Decision: No legislative devolution. Offer to strengthen the Welsh Government’s role in policing governance.

Reasoning given:

* The integrated England-and-Wales policing system is said to be essential for interoperability and seamless cross-border working.
* Welsh forces benefit from access to national and specialist capabilities.
* Separating policing would create new operational and constitutional boundaries within the single England-and-Wales jurisdiction.
* A strategic Wales-wide approach could improve coordination with devolved services without changing the reserved settlement.

Evidence or examples cited:

* The pan-Wales Assistant Chief Constable role.
* The Policing Partnership Board for Wales.
* Existing cooperation between the Home Office, Welsh Government and policing partners.
* The proposed police reforms, including governance arrangements based on local government structures and stronger oversight of specialist capabilities and force-wide efficiencies.

What is missing: The letter does not provide comparative evidence showing that devolving policing would actually damage interoperability, nor does it explain why formal agreements, shared services or joint operational arrangements could not preserve cross-border capabilities under a devolved model.

It identifies a genuine operational consideration, but does not demonstrate that retaining Westminster’s legislative control is necessary to protect it. GOV.WALES

2. Justice — retain the shared jurisdiction

Decision: No transfer of the overall justice system. Continue developing selected Welsh responsibilities.

Reasoning given:

* Wales benefits from the resources, expertise and resilience of a shared England-and-Wales justice system.
* Different Welsh circumstances can still be accommodated within that shared system.
* Partnership between justice institutions and devolved services is necessary to improve outcomes for victims, reduce reoffending and build safer communities.
* The existing Criminal Justice Board for Wales and Wales-specific forums provide mechanisms for coordination.

Evidence or examples cited:

* The Lord Chancellor’s meeting with the Deputy First Minister and the Minister for Government Efficiency in Cardiff.
* Existing Criminal Justice Board arrangements.
* An agreement to devolve funding for certain youth justice initiatives.
* Work exploring a larger Welsh role in probation funding and oversight.

What is missing: The letter does not engage with the central argument for devolution: that Welsh institutions control many of the services which influence offending, rehabilitation and reintegration, while lacking authority over much of the justice system itself.

It gives examples of collaboration, but does not establish that collaboration is an adequate substitute for aligning decision-making powers.

It also does not set out a comparative assessment of the costs, outcomes or institutional risks of a devolved Welsh justice system. GOV.WALES

3. Crown Estate — retain central management

Decision: Reject devolution of management, and explicitly reject even preparing for a possible future transfer.

Reasoning given:

* The Crown Estate’s existing model is said to offer the best way of maximising economic and social benefits for Wales.
* Its scale, financial capacity and specialist expertise are said to support long-term investment.
* A diversified portfolio is said to allow risks to be managed across different investments.
* Coordination with England is described as crucial to investors in floating offshore wind.

Evidence or examples cited:

* The Welsh Government expert group’s identification of Welsh ports, offshore-wind supply chains and wider economic development as key opportunities.
* UK Government investment in Welsh port infrastructure.
* The Crown Estate’s Wales-specific annual review and financial reporting.
* A Wales Commissioner and Wales Forum intended to improve the representation of Welsh priorities.
* Existing floating offshore wind investments and the connection with developments in England.

What is missing: The letter does not provide financial comparisons showing that central management produces better outcomes for Wales than devolved management would. It does not quantify the supposed advantages of portfolio diversification, demonstrate that investors require central ownership or management, or explain why a devolved Welsh institution could not cooperate with the Crown Estate and investors across borders.

It also does not really answer the democratic-accountability argument: improved reporting and a Wales Forum give Wales a voice, but do not give Welsh institutions control over the decisions.

The most striking point is that the letter rejects not only the transfer but preparation for a future transfer. That makes this a decision about the constitutional destination, not merely the practical timing. GOV.WALES

4. Rail infrastructure — retain Great British Railways control

Decision: Reject full devolution. Transfer governance of Welsh rail enhancements to the Wales Rail Board and develop partnership arrangements.

Reasoning given:

* Welsh rail has significant cross-border connections with England.
* Infrastructure management and maintenance require consistency on both sides of the border.
* The network also has connections with the rest of Great Britain, including Scotland.
* Great British Railways is said to be better placed to coordinate investment in passenger services, maintenance and enhancements.
* The UK Government argues that separating infrastructure responsibility would fragment the system and harm services.

Evidence or examples cited:

* A Welsh rail investment pipeline valued at up to £14 billion.
* The agreement to transfer governance of Welsh rail enhancements to the Wales Rail Board.
* The alliance between Transport for Wales and Network Rail.
* Partnership delivery of the Burns stations.
* The proposed Railways Bill, a new Memorandum of Understanding and a developing partnership agreement between Great British Railways and Transport for Wales.

What is missing: The letter does not demonstrate that full devolution would cause fragmentation. It does not compare alternative models, such as Welsh control of infrastructure combined with binding cross-border operating agreements, shared standards and joint investment planning.

Nor does it explain why cross-border coordination requires central ownership or decision-making, rather than cooperation between authorities.

The £14 billion pipeline is evidence of proposed investment, not evidence that Wales needs to remain outside infrastructure control. Investment commitments and constitutional authority are separate questions. GOV.WALES

5. Fiscal flexibility and economic development

Decision: Continue discussions rather than settle the wider fiscal requests in the letter.

Reasoning given:

* Burnham says he wants to support local and regional investment and economic growth.
* He refers to the Government’s commitment to its fiscal rules.
* He says a roadmap to further fiscal flexibility was discussed and Treasury discussions are ongoing.
* He points to additional funding for Wales following the Budget.

Evidence or examples cited:

* £450 million made available to the Welsh Government on top of the devolution settlement.
* £2.5 billion for nuclear power on Ynys Môn.
* £500 million for the Port Talbot electric arc furnace.
* £445 million to support Welsh rail.
* A commitment to work on a Partnership for Growth.

What is missing: These are examples of spending and investment, not a detailed answer to the requests for greater Welsh control over taxation, borrowing and the Wales Reserve. The letter does not explain which restrictions the Treasury is prepared to remove, on what timetable, or what fiscal powers it is unwilling to transfer.

The reference to fiscal rules is a constraint the UK Government says it intends to maintain, but the letter does not show why particular Welsh borrowing or reserve reforms would breach those rules. GOV.WALES

6. Employment support and regional empowerment

Decision: Offer further exploration of devolving employment support, with greater responsibility potentially reaching Welsh regions and communities.

Reasoning given:

* Economic growth should be driven more directly by local and regional needs.
* Employment, skills, health and practical support should be joined up around individuals rather than fragmented across services.
* Welsh regions should be able to use both reserved and devolved powers to support growth.

Evidence or examples cited:

* Alan Milburn’s review, which Burnham invokes in support of integrating employment, skills, health and practical support for young people.
* The existing division of responsibilities between UK and Welsh Governments.

What is missing: There is no detailed proposal for which employment-support functions would transfer, what funding would accompany them, or whether the Welsh Government would have genuine decision-making authority. The letter commits to developing the idea through policy discussions, not to a completed transfer. GOV.WALES

7. Cynnal Welsh Child Payment

Decision: Officials are working with the Welsh Government to understand the proposed pilot and what UK Government support would be needed.

Reasoning given: The letter gives no substantive policy argument for or against the requested exemption from tax and benefit calculations.

Evidence or examples cited: A meeting between UK and Welsh Government ministers in September, followed by official-level discussions.

What is missing: No decision on the requested tax and benefits treatment, no explanation of the relevant legal or administrative obstacles, and no timetable for resolving them. This is a clear case of further work rather than acceptance. GOV.WALES

8. The other requests in the 1 October letter

There is an important distinction between requests Burnham addresses and requests he largely leaves untouched.

Request	What Burnham’s letter actually says
Vacant Land Tax competence	No substantive response
Permanent removal of Wales Reserve restrictions	General reference to a fiscal-flexibility roadmap and Treasury discussions; no specific commitment
A comprehensive Wales Bill	No commitment to introduce one
Broadcasting, ports, Air Passenger Duty, energy regulation and welfare	No substantive response to the proposed further devolution of these areas
Strengthening Sewel and intergovernmental relations	No specific proposal
UK Internal Market Act’s impact	No substantive response

The letter does refer to ports and energy as areas for economic-development cooperation, but that is not the same as agreeing to devolve port regulation or energy powers. Similarly, officials working on Cynnal is not agreement to the requested welfare safeguard. GOV.WALES +1

9. What kind of evidence is this?

I think this is the most useful way to assess the quality of the response.

Type of reasoning	Where it appears	What it establishes
Operational argument	Policing, justice and rail	There are real cross-border systems and coordination requirements
Institutional argument	Crown Estate and justice	Existing institutions have expertise, resources and established arrangements
Practical examples	Policing Board, Criminal Justice Board, Wales Rail Board	Cooperation already happens and can potentially be expanded
Financial argument	Crown Estate, fiscal flexibility	The UK Government believes central scale and financial capacity offer advantages
Political and policy preference	Regional empowerment and shared governance	Burnham prefers collaboration and decentralisation within existing structures
Comparative evidence	Largely absent	The letter does not systematically compare the existing settlement with the proposed devolved alternatives

10. The central evidential gap

There is a difference between establishing that a shared system has benefits and establishing that the powers must remain reserved.

For example:

* Cross-border policing is necessary. That does not, by itself, establish that policing governance must remain under Westminster.
* A shared justice system has resources and expertise. That does not, by itself, establish that Wales cannot have its own justice institutions while sharing services or arrangements.
* Railways need coordination across borders. That does not, by itself, establish that infrastructure ownership and investment decisions must remain centralised.
* The Crown Estate benefits from financial scale. That does not, by itself, establish that Welsh management could not participate in a shared investment structure.

Those are possible arguments for retaining the current settlement, but they need to be tested against alternative institutional designs.

My assessment of the letter’s reasoning, as a matter of evidence rather than political preference: it explains why the UK Government values the existing arrangements and identifies some practical benefits of cooperation. It does not provide a systematic comparative case demonstrating that devolution would produce worse outcomes, nor does it engage in depth with the independent commissions and reports cited by the Welsh Government.

That last omission matters. Ap Iorwerth was not simply asking Burnham to consider an abstract constitutional preference. He was putting forward a case based on existing Welsh institutions, expert reviews and the practical consequences of divided responsibilities.

The next useful step would be to take the four major refusals and compare Burnham’s stated reasons against the evidence in the relevant independent reports—particularly the Commission on Justice in Wales, the Independent Commission on the Constitutional Future of Wales, and the Welsh rail and Crown Estate work. That would let us test the arguments rather than just repeat the positions.”.  

“The mechanism is not that refusing devolution automatically produces support for independence. It is that repeated inability to resolve legitimate constitutional and policy disputes can weaken the political relationships that make a union work.

A union depends on more than common institutions and a shared legal framework. It also depends on people believing that:

* their elected institutions can make a meaningful difference;
* their government can be held responsible for the decisions affecting them;
* constitutional disagreements can be resolved through negotiation;
* the centre treats the constituent nations as partners, rather than administrative regions.

The difficulty with Burnham’s response is that it offers more cooperation without resolving the underlying dispute over authority. That creates several potential routes by which the union’s legitimacy can erode.

1. It turns a policy disagreement into a constitutional disagreement

Consider the policing request.

The Welsh Government’s argument is that it controls many of the services that affect crime and prevention, but not policing itself.

The UK Government’s answer is essentially: the existing policing system works across England and Wales, so retain it, but give Wales more involvement.

That leaves the underlying problem intact:

* Welsh Ministers are expected to coordinate public services that influence policing demand.
* They cannot take full responsibility for policing priorities or the institutional framework.
* The UK Government retains the decisive authority.
* The two governments can disagree about priorities, but Wales cannot resolve the disagreement through its own democratic institutions.

The same structural problem appears in justice, rail and the Crown Estate.

The union becomes harder to operate when the division of powers repeatedly prevents governments from delivering coherent policy together.

This is not just a theoretical concern. The Independent Commission on the Constitutional Future of Wales identified the instability of the settlement and the absence of meaningful redress against unilateral UK Government decisions as problems undermining confidence and constructive relations. GOV.WALES +1

2. It creates an accountability problem for voters

This is particularly important politically.

Suppose someone in Wales is unhappy with:

* police performance;
* rehabilitation and reoffending;
* railway investment;
* economic development from Welsh natural resources.

They want to know which government to hold responsible.

But the responsibilities are divided, sometimes between institutions that have different political priorities.

The February 2026 Cardiff University/YouGov survey illustrates the difficulty. Among 1,544 Welsh adults:

* 69% either answered incorrectly or did not know which government was responsible for policing.
* 45% incorrectly attributed policing to the Welsh Government.
* Only 31% correctly identified Westminster as responsible. Cardiff University

That produces a particularly awkward situation:

The Welsh Government can be blamed for things it does not control, while the UK Government can retain authority without always being visibly accountable for the consequences.

This makes democratic accountability less effective. It also creates room for constitutional arguments to become part of ordinary complaints about public services.

3. It makes moderate demands harder to satisfy

This is perhaps the most significant political mechanism.

A voter does not need to support independence to want:

* Welsh control over policing;
* Welsh management of the Crown Estate;
* a fairer rail funding arrangement;
* greater control over Welsh finances.

Indeed, the April 2026 Ipsos survey found:

The figures show that support for more Welsh powers was substantially higher than support for independence in that survey. Ipsos

Now consider the political consequence.

If a government refuses a request for independence, it is refusing a demand for independence.

If it refuses a request for more devolved authority from people who do not necessarily want independence, it risks making the existing union appear incapable of accommodating those people.

That can change the question in voters’ minds:

* Initially: Why can’t Wales have more control over this particular service?
* Later: Why does Westminster keep refusing to let Wales govern itself in these areas?
* Eventually, for some: If the union cannot accommodate meaningful self-government, what is the point of remaining in it?

That progression is a potential mechanism, not a prediction that every voter will follow it.

4. It gives nationalists an argument that does not depend on persuading people to become nationalists

This is where political inability becomes important.

Plaid Cymru does not have to persuade every dissatisfied voter that independence is economically or constitutionally preferable.

It can point to a sequence of events:

* Welsh voters elect representatives with a mandate for greater Welsh powers.
* The Welsh Government presents specific proposals.
* The UK Government refuses the central transfers.
* It offers participation and cooperation instead.
* The Welsh Government remains unable to exercise the authority it says it needs.
* The constitutional dispute remains unresolved.

The resulting argument is about the capacity of the union to accommodate democratic self-government.

That argument can appeal to people who identify strongly as Welsh and British, people who support devolution but oppose independence, and people who simply want public services to work better.

The Independent Commission itself found that enhanced devolution was the most popular of the three broad constitutional options it considered, ahead of federalism and independence. It also warned that the current settlement was vulnerable to erosion. GOV.WALES +1

The implication is important: a government can strengthen the political case for constitutional change without its opponents having to win an argument for independence first.

5. It creates a fiscal grievance that can be attached to the constitutional grievance

HS2 is a particularly clear example.

The argument is not simply that Wales wants more money.

It is that:

* Wales has no rail infrastructure devolution equivalent to Scotland’s.
* The UK Government retains the relevant authority.
* HS2 expenditure is classified as benefiting England and Wales.
* Wales therefore does not receive the same Barnett consequentials as Scotland and Northern Ireland.
* The Welsh Government argues that this leaves Wales financially disadvantaged.

The UK Government has a constitutional explanation for its treatment of HS2, but the Welsh Government disputes the outcome.

For a Welsh voter, this can become a much more tangible grievance than an abstract debate about constitutional architecture:

Why does Wales not receive funding associated with a railway that does not run through Wales?

The dispute then reinforces the argument that the existing division of powers and funding does not work fairly for Wales.

6. It can damage the relationship between governments even when both want the union to continue

This is not merely a Plaid Cymru argument.
