The Welsh Government’s own 2023–24 intergovernmental relations report said that the previous UK Government’s approach had damaged the union, citing failures of engagement and breaches of the Sewel Convention. It also acknowledged areas of constructive cooperation. GOV.WALES

The Independent Commission identified a related institutional problem: intergovernmental cooperation depends on trust, parity of esteem and sustained political commitment. It recommended statutory duties of cooperation rather than relying entirely on political goodwill. GOV.WALES +1

This is the distinction between a union that works through consent and a union that works through the legal supremacy of its central Parliament.

The latter can continue to exist constitutionally while becoming less cooperative politically.

7. The feedback loop

Here is the process I think we should be testing against the correspondence.

Welsh voters express a demand for greater self-government

Not necessarily independence

Welsh Government proposes practical constitutional changes

UK Government retains core authority and offers participation

The original policy and accountability problems remain unresolved

Confidence in the union’s capacity to accommodate Wales may decline

Demand for stronger constitutional guarantees or a different settlement grows

A possible political feedback loop, not an established prediction of voter behaviour.

There is an important counterargument: the UK Government may believe that shared institutions, common standards and cross-border coordination are themselves union-strengthening. That is a legitimate constitutional objective. But it needs to demonstrate that the arrangements deliver effective outcomes and that Welsh participation is meaningful.

If it cannot, the argument for retaining authority becomes increasingly difficult to distinguish from an argument for retaining control.

8. The fundamental political risk

I would frame the risk this way:

The UK Government may be defending the institutional structure of the union while weakening the democratic consent on which the union depends.

Those are not the same thing.

A union can preserve its formal constitutional structure while losing public confidence in its fairness, responsiveness and capacity to accommodate different national interests.

And it does not have to happen through a dramatic constitutional confrontation. It can happen through accumulated experiences of being unable to get decisions changed, funding arrangements revised or democratic mandates translated into practical authority.

The evidence does not establish that Burnham’s letter will cause a shift towards independence. It does establish that the underlying tensions are real, that a substantial constituency wants more Welsh powers, and that independent constitutional work has identified weaknesses in the mechanisms intended to manage those tensions.

The question I would put to the UK Government is therefore not simply whether it believes the Union should remain intact. It is what practical constitutional route it is offering to people in Wales who want the Union to remain intact but want Wales to exercise more meaningful democratic authority within it.

If the answer is effectively more meetings, more consultation and more seats on UK-controlled boards, that is a political problem the UK Government needs to reckon with.”.   

there are rational reasons to have a union btw but let’s let the lads use some of the brain cells they are rumoured to possess.  

“We’re now at Question 22 of the original 26-topic agenda: the Union itself.

We’ve covered quite a lot, although we haven’t followed the original list strictly from beginning to end.

Where we are in the original agenda

No.	Topic	Progress
1	Democratic mandate	Discussed
2	Welsh identity and self-determination	Discussed
3	Adequacy of the current settlement	Discussed
4	Parity with Scotland	Discussed
5	Comparison with English regional devolution	Partly explored
6	Subsidiarity	Discussed
7–10	Policing, justice, Crown Estate and rail	Examined in detail
11–15	Viability, economies of scale, cross-border cooperation, funding and economic opportunity	Substantially explored
16	The 30 October Glasgow meeting	Discussed
17	The correspondence	Examined in detail
18	Who makes decisions, and how?	Discussed
19	Constitutional and political etiquette	Still needs attention
20	Consultation and intergovernmental relations	Discussed
21	Future negotiations	Partly explored
22	How the Union is affected	Current discussion
23	Northern Ireland implications	Not yet examined
24	English constitutional asymmetry	Not yet examined
25	Constitutional precedent	Not yet examined
26	Reversibility and security of the settlement	Not yet examined

The current sub-sequence: examining Burnham’s response

We’ve also developed a more immediate set of questions about the actual correspondence:

* What exactly did Rhun ap Iorwerth request? — answered.
* Which requests were accepted, partially accepted, rejected or deferred? — answered.
* What evidence and reasoning did Burnham give for each response? — answered.
* How could that response weaken the Union through political inability? — we’ve just examined this.

So we’re at the fourth question in that sequence, within the broader twenty-second topic.

What remains particularly important

I would preserve the next four original topics rather than skip ahead:

* 23 — Northern Ireland: what does this tell us about the UK’s ability to accommodate different constitutional arrangements?
* 24 — England: why does England have no equivalent national devolved government, and how does that affect the balance?
* 25 — Precedent: what would Welsh devolution mean for the constitutional settlement elsewhere?
* 26 — Reversibility: how secure are devolved powers if Westminster can ultimately change the settlement?

That takes us towards the end of the original agenda. We’re not starting over; we’re building on the constitutional groundwork we’ve already done.”.  


“The comparison reveals something quite important: the UK is capable of accommodating different constitutional arrangements, but it does not apply one consistent model of self-government across its constituent nations.

Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland do not have identical settlements. That is not, in itself, a constitutional defect. Asymmetry can be a way of recognising different histories, political mandates and national circumstances.

The harder question is whether the UK can accommodate those differences on a basis of democratic consent, meaningful authority and fair treatment — or whether the centre is willing to tolerate different arrangements only while it retains control over the boundaries.

I suggest we compare the three nations against the same tests: democratic authority, accountability, legal powers, financial powers, coordination and constitutional security.

1. The three settlements are materially different

Scotland — extensive national self-government

Scotland has its own Parliament, Government, legal jurisdiction, justice system and police service. It also has substantially greater income-tax and social-security powers than Wales.

Northern Ireland — extensive but distinctive power-sharing

Northern Ireland has its own Assembly, Executive, legal jurisdiction, justice system and police service. It also has devolved employment law, social security, energy and transport responsibilities. Its institutions are embedded in the Good Friday Agreement and a wider British-Irish settlement.

Wales — substantial domestic devolution, but important structural reservations

Wales has its own Parliament, Government and developing body of Welsh law. But policing, most justice functions and the single England-and-Wales legal jurisdiction remain at UK level. Its fiscal and borrowing powers are also more limited than Scotland’s.

OECD +4

This is not simply a matter of Wales having fewer powers in every category. Northern Ireland and Scotland have different powers from each other too. The important point is that the UK has already accepted that national self-government does not need to follow one uniform template.

2. The comparison by policy area

Area	Scotland	Northern Ireland	Wales
Police	Devolved	Devolved	Reserved
Justice system	Devolved	Devolved, with exceptions	Largely reserved
Legal jurisdiction	Separate	Separate	Shared with England
Employment law	Mostly reserved	Devolved	Reserved
Social security	Significant powers devolved	Devolved	Mostly reserved
Energy	Mostly reserved, with some powers	Significant powers devolved	Mixed; important powers reserved
Transport	Substantial powers	Substantial powers	Substantial but more constrained
Income tax	Rates and bands on earned income	No equivalent general income-tax powers	Welsh rates, but not bands
Crown Estate	Management devolved	Not equivalent to Scotland’s arrangement	Management remains UK-controlled

Northern Ireland Assembly +4

Two comparisons are especially relevant to the correspondence.

Justice: Scotland and Northern Ireland both have their own justice systems and police services. Their existence demonstrates that the UK can accommodate different legal and policing institutions within the same sovereign state. It does not prove that every feature of those systems could simply be copied into Wales, but it does mean that the proposition that a shared UK state necessarily requires a single England-and-Wales justice and policing framework is not a general constitutional rule.

Crown Estate: Scotland has had management of the Scottish Crown Estate devolved. That is a particularly direct example of the UK accommodating different arrangements for the management of Crown assets. The Welsh request is therefore not asking for an entirely unprecedented constitutional model. GOV.UK Assets +1

3. Northern Ireland is a particularly important comparison

Northern Ireland’s settlement is different for reasons that go well beyond administrative convenience.

The 1998 Belfast/Good Friday Agreement was negotiated between:

* the UK Government;
* the Irish Government;
* the political parties in Northern Ireland.

It was endorsed by referendums in Northern Ireland and the Republic of Ireland.

It established three interconnected strands:

* Strand One: Northern Ireland’s internal democratic institutions and power-sharing government.
* Strand Two: North-South cooperation between Northern Ireland and Ireland.
* Strand Three: East-West cooperation between the UK and Ireland, including the British-Irish Council.

It also established the principle of consent: Northern Ireland remains part of the UK unless a majority of its people vote otherwise. It protects the right to identify as British, Irish or both. 25 years on +1

There are three lessons here.

First: constitutional accommodation can involve more than transferring powers from London.

Northern Ireland’s institutions recognise that its population has different national identities and constitutional aspirations. Its settlement provides structures through which those differences can be accommodated.

Second: meaningful self-government can coexist with cross-border cooperation.

Northern Ireland has its own justice system and police service, but also participates in UK-wide and cross-border arrangements. Its separate institutions do not make cooperation impossible.

Third: constitutional arrangements can be tailored to particular national circumstances.

The UK did not insist that Northern Ireland adopt the Scottish or Welsh model. It negotiated a distinctive arrangement because the circumstances demanded one.

There is an important qualification: Northern Ireland’s power-sharing institutions have experienced prolonged periods of suspension and political deadlock. The existence of an elaborate constitutional agreement does not guarantee that political cooperation will always work. Northern Ireland Affairs Committee +1

4. Scotland demonstrates that further devolution can be negotiated incrementally

Scotland’s settlement also developed over time.

The Scotland Act 1998 established the Parliament. Subsequent legislation, particularly the Scotland Acts 2012 and 2016, expanded its powers.

The 2016 Act provided for:

* greater control over income-tax rates and bands;
* additional social-security responsibilities;
* further borrowing powers;
* additional taxation responsibilities.

The Scottish fiscal framework was negotiated alongside those changes. It is not simply a case of Westminster handing over powers without considering the financial consequences. GOV.UK Assets +1

This is relevant to Wales because it demonstrates that the UK has already negotiated substantial changes to the distribution of powers and resources within the Union.

It also raises a question about consistency: if further devolution was considered compatible with the Union in Scotland, what is the constitutional principle that makes comparable arrangements unacceptable in Wales?

There may be practical differences and reasons to negotiate different terms. But those reasons need to be explained on their merits.

5. What does the comparison tell us about the UK’s ability to accommodate difference?

I would draw five conclusions.

A. Asymmetry is already an accepted constitutional principle

The UK does not require Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland to have identical institutions or powers.

That means the argument that Wales cannot have a particular power simply because it would make the UK settlement less uniform is not convincing as a general objection.

Uniformity is not the existing constitutional model.

B. Shared systems are a choice, not always a necessity

The policing and justice comparison is particularly revealing.

The UK can accommodate:

* a Scottish justice system;
* a Northern Irish justice system;
* a combined England-and-Wales justice jurisdiction.

These arrangements coexist.

Therefore, when the UK Government says a separate Welsh justice system would create operational complications, it needs to explain why those complications cannot be managed through cooperation, rather than treating the current arrangement as constitutionally indispensable.

C. The UK can negotiate new settlements when there is political will

Scotland’s expanding powers demonstrate that the devolution settlement is capable of development.

Northern Ireland demonstrates that a settlement can be redesigned around particular constitutional and political circumstances.

Wales has its own distinct institutional history and democratic mandate. There is no inherent reason why its settlement must remain fixed at its current boundaries.

D. Different powers require different financial arrangements

Devolving a function without transferring adequate resources can create an unfunded responsibility.

Scotland’s fiscal framework demonstrates that funding arrangements can be negotiated alongside new responsibilities.

Northern Ireland’s settlement also shows that some policy areas can be devolved even when wider UK fiscal arrangements remain important.

For Wales, the question is therefore not merely whether a power can be transferred. It is whether the transfer includes the funding, borrowing capacity and institutional resources needed to exercise it effectively.

E. Constitutional security is not equally distributed

All three settlements ultimately sit within a system of parliamentary sovereignty. None is a fully federal constitution in which the constituent nations possess entrenched constitutional sovereignty.

But Northern Ireland has additional protections arising from the Belfast/Good Friday Agreement and the international treaty between the UK and Irish Governments.

Scotland has a distinct constitutional history and a substantial political debate over self-determination, but no equivalent statutory entitlement to hold an independence referendum whenever its Parliament chooses. The Supreme Court confirmed in 2022 that the Scottish Parliament could not legislate for such a referendum without Westminster’s agreement. 25 years on +2

Wales has statutory recognition and permanence provisions for its institutions, but no equivalent of the Good Friday Agreement’s consent framework for determining its constitutional future.

6. The comparison exposes a question about political consistency

I would put the three cases alongside one another:

Question	Scotland	Northern Ireland	Wales
Has the UK accommodated a distinct legal jurisdiction?	Yes	Yes	No
Has the UK accommodated devolved policing and justice?	Yes	Yes	No
Has the UK accommodated substantial fiscal differentiation?	Yes	Yes, differently	More limited
Has the UK accommodated a distinct constitutional settlement?	Yes	Yes, with treaty foundations	Yes, but with fewer protections
Has the UK changed the settlement over time?	Yes	Yes	Yes

The last two rows matter. Wales has not been entirely excluded from constitutional development. Its settlement has changed considerably since 1999. The question is whether that development is keeping pace with the demands now being made of it.

7. What this means for the Union

There are two competing interpretations.

The UK Government’s possible interpretation:

Different arrangements are acceptable where there are particular historical, geographical or operational reasons, but the UK must retain certain central institutions and powers to maintain coherence, economic stability and common standards.

The Welsh Government’s possible interpretation:

The existence of different arrangements elsewhere demonstrates that the UK can accommodate Welsh self-government too. Refusing to consider comparable powers while offering only participation in centrally controlled institutions makes Wales an unequal partner.

Neither interpretation should be assumed without examining the evidence.

But the political question is substantial:

If the UK can accommodate different constitutional arrangements in Scotland and Northern Ireland, what is the principled limit on its willingness to accommodate them in Wales?

And if the answer is that Wales’s circumstances are different, the UK Government needs to explain those differences rather than simply invoke the benefits of the existing England-and-Wales arrangements.

8. The next question I would add to our investigation

I think we should distinguish three possible explanations for the differences:

* Constitutional necessity: the power genuinely needs to remain at UK level for reasons that cannot be addressed through cooperation.
* Historical inheritance: the power remains reserved because of how the UK state developed, rather than because central control is demonstrably necessary today.
* Political preference: the UK Government prefers to retain control, even where other constitutional arrangements are demonstrably workable.

Those explanations have very different implications.

If a reservation is necessary, there should be evidence of why.

If it is historical, it can be reconsidered.

If it is political preference, then the disagreement is fundamentally about who should have the authority to decide.

That distinction will be particularly useful when we get to the English constitutional question and the security of the devolution settlement.

The central issue is not whether the UK can accommodate asymmetry. It demonstrably can. It is whether it is willing to apply that flexibility consistently when Wales asks for greater democratic authority.”.  

“Yes, Northern Ireland’s political situation is a plausible part of the UK Government’s wider constitutional caution. But I would distinguish that from saying it is the demonstrated reason for Burnham’s decisions on Wales.

There is some unusually relevant evidence in the political context surrounding this correspondence.

1. Northern Ireland is a particularly sensitive part of the Union

The UK Government has several reasons to be cautious about constitutional instability there:

* The Good Friday Agreement established a carefully negotiated settlement involving the UK and Irish Governments.
* Northern Ireland’s constitutional status is subject to a specific principle of democratic consent.
* The power-sharing institutions have previously collapsed, including from 2022 to 2024.
* Brexit and the arrangements for Northern Ireland’s relationship with the EU have generated substantial political tensions.
* The next Assembly elections are scheduled for 2027.

The Institute for Government explicitly identified potential instability following the 2027 Northern Ireland elections as one of the pressures facing Burnham’s government, alongside nationalism in Scotland and Wales. Institute for Government +2

This is not a hypothetical concern invented to explain the Welsh correspondence. It is an identified issue in contemporary constitutional analysis.

2. Burnham has already demonstrated sensitivity around the Northern Ireland constitutional question

There is another particularly interesting piece of evidence.

During his first official visit to Northern Ireland as Prime Minister in September 2026, Burnham said an Irish unity referendum was “off the table”.

This produced controversy:

* Nationalist politicians objected because the Good Friday Agreement protects the route to a border poll.
* Unionist representatives welcomed his apparent position.
* Northern Ireland Secretary Chris Bryant subsequently clarified that Burnham meant a referendum was not under consideration at that time, and reaffirmed the Government’s commitment to the Agreement.

The constitutional distinction is important: the Secretary of State must call a poll if the statutory conditions indicate that a majority would favour Irish unity. A Prime Minister cannot simply remove that possibility from the constitutional settlement. The Constitution Society

This episode suggests the Government is conscious of the political sensitivity surrounding the Union’s future, but also illustrates the risks of appearing to close down constitutional questions.

3. There is now a cross-national political dimension

This is especially relevant to our investigation.

The Institute for Government observed in July 2026 that Burnham was taking office with nationalist first ministers in all three devolved nations for the first time:

* John Swinney in Scotland;
* Rhun ap Iorwerth in Wales;
* Michelle O’Neill in Northern Ireland.

It identified this as a more complex political landscape for the UK Government to negotiate. Institute for Government

There is also a September 2026 development: the leaders of the three nationalist parties signed a memorandum calling for the UK Government to prepare for constitutional change in their respective nations.

The parties explicitly recognised that their constitutional circumstances and routes to self-determination differ. That matters: Welsh independence, Scottish independence and Irish reunification are not legally interchangeable processes. House of Commons Library +1

From Downing Street’s perspective, however, these developments may look like a broader challenge to the existing constitutional order.

That creates a plausible incentive to avoid concessions that might be interpreted as encouraging further constitutional change elsewhere.

4. But there is a danger in treating Wales through the lens of Northern Ireland

This is where I would be careful.

Welsh demands for policing, justice, rail and Crown Estate devolution are not demands for Irish reunification, nor are they necessarily demands for Welsh independence.

They are requests to change the distribution of powers within the United Kingdom.

Northern Ireland’s settlement has particular requirements because of:

* the history of conflict;
* the relationship with Ireland;
* the Good Friday Agreement;
* the principle of consent;
* power-sharing between communities.

Those circumstances cannot simply be transferred to Wales.

Indeed, Northern Ireland demonstrates that the UK can accommodate a distinctive national settlement without requiring every other nation to adopt it.

There is a constitutional irony here: if the UK Government is anxious about Northern Ireland’s stability, refusing to accommodate reasonable democratic demands elsewhere could create additional pressures on the Union rather than reduce them.

5. Three possible ways Northern Ireland anxiety could influence the Welsh response

Possible influence	How it could operate	Evidence status
Fear of constitutional contagion	Concessions to Wales might encourage demands for further change in Scotland and Northern Ireland	Plausible political inference; not demonstrated as Burnham’s stated reasoning
Desire to avoid reopening the Union question	Government prioritises public services and economic delivery over constitutional negotiation	Supported by Burnham’s public statements and the Government’s stated priorities
Concern about nationalist coordination	Three nationalist-led devolved governments could create a stronger collective negotiating position	Explicitly identified as a challenge by the Institute for Government

The Constitution Society +2

6. The paradox

There is a potentially important contradiction in the Government’s position.

It wants to:

* maintain the Union;
* preserve stability in Northern Ireland;
* maintain the Good Friday Agreement;
* avoid further destabilising constitutional disputes;
* improve relationships with devolved governments.

But if its response to constitutional demands is consistently to retain central authority and offer only limited participation, it may contribute to the dissatisfaction that makes those relationships more difficult.

The Northern Ireland experience should arguably teach the UK Government that constitutional stability depends on respecting the arrangements through which different national communities exercise democratic authority.

It cannot simply be achieved by avoiding discussion of constitutional change.

My provisional conclusion: Northern Ireland anxiety is a credible part of the wider political environment shaping the UK Government’s approach. The evidence supports the proposition that Burnham is operating in a politically sensitive Union-wide context. It does not establish that fear of Northern Ireland tensions caused him to reject Welsh devolution.

7. One question I think we should add to our investigation

There is a distinction between two approaches to maintaining the Union:

* Containment: avoid constitutional change, resist demands for additional powers and try to keep existing arrangements stable.
* Accommodation: accept that constitutional arrangements need to evolve to maintain democratic consent and political cooperation.

Northern Ireland’s experience, particularly the Good Friday Agreement, offers a significant example of accommodation.

The question for Wales is whether the UK Government is applying the lessons of that experience across the Union, or whether it is treating Northern Ireland’s constitutional sensitivity as a reason to be more cautious about change everywhere.

I would investigate that before attributing a specific motive to Burnham. It gets us closer to the institutional and political incentives behind the correspondence.”.  

“The key point is that Welsh devolution would not automatically require constitutional change elsewhere, but it could expose inconsistencies in the existing settlement and create pressure to address them.

There are five main implications.

1. Scotland: the question of parity

Scotland already has several powers Wales is requesting.

For example:

* policing and justice are devolved;
* management of the Scottish Crown Estate is devolved;
* Scotland has greater income-tax and social-security powers;
* Scotland has a separate legal jurisdiction.

If Wales acquired comparable powers, Scotland would not automatically lose anything. But it could create a new political question:

If Wales can exercise these powers, should Scotland’s settlement be adjusted too?

There are two possible directions:

* Parity upwards: Scotland asks for additional powers to maintain its relative autonomy.
* Convergence: the UK develops a more consistent set of powers across Scotland and Wales, while preserving justified differences.

This is not a new phenomenon. The House of Lords Constitution Committee has previously documented how changes to one nation’s settlement can generate demands from the others. It also warned that successive governments had developed devolution reactively, without an overarching constitutional strategy. Select Committee on the Constitution +2

2. Northern Ireland: a precedent for differentiated self-government

Northern Ireland’s position is more complicated.

Its settlement is rooted in the Good Friday Agreement, including:

* power-sharing;
* the principle of consent;
* North-South institutions;
* British-Irish intergovernmental cooperation.

Welsh devolution would not alter those arrangements or the constitutional status of Northern Ireland.

However, it could reinforce an existing constitutional principle: different nations within the UK can have different institutional structures and different distributions of authority.

There is a potentially important consequence here.

If the UK Government accepts that devolved policing, justice and other responsibilities can operate successfully in Scotland and Northern Ireland, it would need to explain why equivalent arrangements in Wales are constitutionally or operationally different.

That does not mean Wales must receive identical powers. It means the burden of explaining the difference becomes more important.

Northern Ireland’s special treaty-based settlement would remain distinct. House of Commons Library +1

3. England: the constitutional question that becomes harder to avoid

This is arguably the biggest structural implication.

England has no single devolved national government equivalent to the Scottish Government, Welsh Government or Northern Ireland Executive.

Instead, England has:

* Westminster;
* UK Government departments;
* elected mayors;
* combined authorities;
* local government.

The UK Government therefore performs two roles simultaneously:

* It governs the United Kingdom as a whole.
* It governs England in areas that have been devolved to the other nations.

This creates the familiar English question: who represents England’s distinct interests when UK-wide decisions are being made?

If Wales acquires more powers, the asymmetry becomes more pronounced unless England’s arrangements also develop.

There are several possible responses:

Model	Constitutional consequence
More English regional devolution	More decision-making authority at regional and local levels
English Parliament	A separate national legislature for England
Federal UK	Defined powers for each constituent nation, with UK-wide institutions
Continue current arrangements	Westminster continues its dual role

The House of Commons Library has documented this underlying asymmetry, including the different powers of devolved nations and English mayors. House of Commons Library +1

There is no automatic requirement to create an English Parliament because Wales receives more powers. But the question of how England is governed becomes increasingly relevant to the coherence of the overall settlement.

4. Westminster: the precedent for changing the boundaries of devolution

This is the legal and institutional dimension.

Under the current settlement, Westminster retains legislative sovereignty. The UK Parliament can legislate for Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland, including in devolved areas.

The Sewel Convention provides that Westminster will not normally legislate on devolved matters without the relevant legislature’s consent, but it does not create a legally enforceable veto. Institute for Government +1

If Wales receives further powers, the UK Government and Parliament would need to decide how to implement them.

That could involve:

* a Wales Act;
* changes to reserved matters under section 109 of the Government of Wales Act 2006;
* transfers of executive functions under section 58;
* revised fiscal arrangements;
* new intergovernmental agreements.

The precedent would be that the boundaries of devolved authority can be renegotiated again.

This matters because it challenges the idea that the existing settlement is a finished constitutional design.

It is not. It has evolved repeatedly since 1999. House of Commons Library +1

5. The UK could move towards federalism without formally becoming a federation

This is an important distinction.

There are different degrees of constitutional change:

A possible constitutional progression

* Existing asymmetric devolution
    Different powers for different nations, ultimately subject to Westminster’s legislative sovereignty.
* Enhanced devolution
    More powers for Wales, alongside further development of Scottish and Northern Irish arrangements.
* Protected devolution
    Stronger statutory guarantees, consent arrangements and formal dispute resolution.
* Federal-style arrangements
    A more clearly defined distribution of constitutional powers, with stronger protections for constituent nations.

These are possible directions, not inevitable stages or a prescribed sequence.

The Independent Commission on the Constitutional Future of Wales considered enhanced devolution, a federal UK and independence as three viable constitutional options. It specifically observed that enhanced devolution could provide greater stability with relatively little disruption to the rest of the UK, while federalism would require more fundamental UK-wide reform. GOV.WALES +1

This is important because granting Wales additional powers would not necessarily mean beginning a process towards independence or federalism.

It could instead be an attempt to make the existing Union function more effectively.

6. The potential precedent problem for the UK Government

I think there are two competing risks here.

If the UK Government grants Wales more powers:

* Scotland may seek comparable or additional powers.
* Northern Ireland may consider whether particular arrangements should be revisited.
* England’s constitutional arrangements may attract renewed scrutiny.
* Westminster may face pressure to establish a more coherent constitutional framework.

If the UK Government refuses Wales more powers:

* Wales may increasingly question whether the Union can accommodate its democratic preferences.
* Scotland may interpret the refusal as evidence of the limits of devolution.
* Northern Ireland’s parties may scrutinise the UK’s willingness to honour negotiated constitutional arrangements.
* The absence of an overarching settlement becomes more visible.

Neither route is consequence-free.

But there is a crucial distinction between constitutional precedent and constitutional contagion.

A Welsh decision to devolve policing would not legally oblige Scotland or Northern Ireland to change anything. It would, however, become a new reference point in future negotiations.

7. The question of whether the UK has a coherent constitutional principle

This is where I would bring together our earlier discussions.

The House of Lords Constitution Committee observed in its 2016 report that the Union has historically accommodated diversity through bespoke arrangements, but warned that the wider implications of asymmetry need to be considered when changing devolution settlements. Select Committee on the Constitution +1

That gives us a useful test for Burnham’s position.

For each refusal, we can ask:

* Is there a genuine UK-wide requirement?
* Is the current arrangement necessary to meet it?
* Could a different distribution of powers meet the same requirement?
* Would changing the Welsh settlement create a problem elsewhere?
* If so, could that problem be resolved through a wider constitutional agreement?

This avoids treating every demand for devolution as automatically justified, while also avoiding the assumption that existing arrangements must be retained.

8. The question I think sits underneath all of this

The UK has three devolved national institutions, an asymmetrical constitution, no single written constitutional settlement, and a Parliament that retains ultimate legal authority.

It has developed through successive negotiations rather than through a single agreed constitutional design.

That brings us to a much larger question:

Is the UK Government trying to preserve a constitutional settlement, or trying to preserve the ability to decide how that settlement develops?

Those are different things.

A government can support devolution in principle while resisting any transfer that reduces its own discretion. Conversely, it can retain UK-wide authority over genuinely common interests while agreeing that constituent nations should have protected powers of their own.

The next topic, 26 — Reversibility and security of the settlement, follows naturally from this. It asks whether the powers Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland exercise are genuinely protected constitutional arrangements, or ultimately permissions that Westminster can change.

That is where the legal architecture of the Union becomes especially important.”.  

“This is the question that gets to the heart of the constitutional settlement.

Devolution is legally established, democratically mandated and institutionally real. But it is not fully constitutionally protected against Westminster changing it.

That distinction is crucial. Wales does not simply have powers temporarily lent by a government in London. The Senedd has statutory legislative authority, Welsh Ministers have statutory executive responsibilities, and courts can enforce the boundaries of those powers.

But Westminster retains the legal authority to change the legislation that establishes those boundaries.

Let’s separate the different kinds of security.

1. What does Westminster actually have the power to change?

Under section 107(5) of the Government of Wales Act 2006:

The Act does not affect the power of the Parliament of the United Kingdom to make laws for Wales.

Section 107(6) then recognises the Sewel Convention: Westminster will not normally legislate on devolved matters without Senedd consent.

The word normally matters.

The convention is a political commitment, not a legally enforceable veto. The Supreme Court confirmed this in Miller in 2017. Legislation.gov.uk +1

This means Westminster retains the legal ability to:

* amend the Senedd’s legislative competence;
* change which subjects are reserved;
* amend the powers of Welsh Ministers;
* change the financial arrangements;
* legislate in devolved policy areas;
* potentially repeal or replace the legislation establishing devolution.

There are political and constitutional constraints on doing these things, but they are not all legally enforceable constraints.

2. But isn’t the Senedd legally permanent?

Yes. And this is an important protection.

The Wales Act 2017 inserted Part A1 into the Government of Wales Act 2006.

Section A1 says:

* The Senedd and Welsh Government are permanent parts of the UK’s constitutional arrangements.
* They are not to be abolished except on the basis of a decision by the people of Wales in a referendum.

Section A2 also formally recognises the ability of the Senedd and Welsh Ministers to make Welsh law. Legislation.gov.uk +1

However, there is a distinction between protecting the existence of the institutions and protecting the powers exercised by those institutions.

What is being protected?	Position
Existence of the Senedd	Statutory permanence commitment
Existence of Welsh Government	Statutory permanence commitment
Welsh law as a recognised body of law	Statutory recognition
Current range of devolved powers	Not entrenched against Westminster legislation
Current funding arrangements	Not constitutionally guaranteed in perpetuity
Senedd consent to Westminster legislation	Political convention, not legal veto
Right to retain a particular devolved function	Not generally protected against subsequent Westminster legislation

In other words, Westminster has legislated to recognise that the institutions are permanent, but it has not surrendered its ultimate legislative authority over the powers they exercise.

The permanence provision is a significant constitutional commitment. It is not equivalent to an entrenched constitutional provision that a future Westminster Parliament cannot alter.

3. Scotland has substantially the same problem

The Scotland Act 2016 inserted section 63A into the Scotland Act 1998.

It uses almost identical language:

* the Scottish Parliament and Government are permanent;
* they should not be abolished except on the basis of a decision by the people of Scotland in a referendum.

The Sewel Convention was also given statutory recognition in section 28(8).

But the UK Parliament retains its general legislative authority under section 28(7). Legislation.gov.uk +1

So Scotland has a stronger range of devolved powers than Wales, but not an entirely different underlying principle of parliamentary sovereignty.

This is worth remembering when comparing the two countries: more extensive devolution does not necessarily mean more secure devolution.

4. Northern Ireland has an additional constitutional protection

Northern Ireland is different.

Section 1 of the Northern Ireland Act 1998 establishes the consent principle:

* Northern Ireland remains part of the United Kingdom unless a majority votes otherwise in a border poll.
* If a majority votes for a united Ireland, the UK Government must bring forward proposals to give effect to that outcome, as agreed with the Irish Government.

This reflects the Belfast/Good Friday Agreement, which is also an international agreement between the UK and Irish Governments. GOV.UK +2

That creates a different kind of constitutional security.

It does not mean every devolved power in Northern Ireland is immune from Westminster legislation. The Northern Ireland Assembly’s legislative powers are also established by statute, and Westminster retains legislative authority.

But the fundamental question of Northern Ireland’s constitutional status is subject to a specific consent mechanism with international treaty foundations.

Wales has no equivalent statutory mechanism requiring a referendum before its constitutional status or relationship with the UK can be changed.

That is a significant difference.

5. How secure are the powers in practice?

I would distinguish four levels of protection.

Level 1

Legal authority in ordinary operation

Welsh institutions have statutory powers. Westminster departments cannot simply disregard the devolution settlement because they disagree with a Welsh policy. Courts can determine whether Welsh legislation is within competence.

Level 2

Statutory constitutional recognition

The Senedd and Welsh Government are recognised as permanent institutions. This establishes a formal commitment by Westminster.

Level 3

Political protection

Sewel, intergovernmental cooperation, political commitments and the expectation of respecting Welsh democratic mandates all constrain Westminster politically.

Level 4

Entrenched constitutional protection

Wales does not have a constitutional settlement that legally prevents a future Westminster Parliament from legislating to reduce its powers.

The important qualification is that Level 4 is not entirely absent from every aspect of the UK’s constitution: Northern Ireland has specific treaty-based protections concerning its constitutional status. But there is no equivalent general entrenchment of Welsh devolved powers.

6. What happens if Westminster changes the settlement without Welsh consent?

There are several possible scenarios.

Scenario	Legal position	Constitutional consequence
Westminster legislates in a devolved policy area	Legally possible	Breach of Sewel convention may cause political conflict
Westminster removes a devolved power	Possible through primary legislation	Significant constitutional confrontation
Westminster reduces Welsh funding	Generally possible through changes to funding arrangements and legislation	Potential dispute over the ability to discharge devolved responsibilities
Westminster changes Senedd competence through the statutory section 109 route	Requires the prescribed approval process, including Senedd approval	Formal negotiated constitutional change
Westminster attempts to abolish the Senedd	Conflicts with section A1’s referendum commitment	Major constitutional crisis, though the provision itself is not entrenched against repeal

One important distinction: section 109 provides a route for changing the reserved-matters schedules through an Order in Council, with approval from the Senedd and both Houses of Parliament. It is not the only possible legislative route. Westminster retains the ability to legislate through primary legislation under section 107(5). Legislation.gov.uk +1

7. Why this matters for the Burnham correspondence

Bring this back to the four major requests.

Ap Iorwerth wants additional authority over policing, justice, rail and the Crown Estate.

Suppose Westminster agreed to transfer those powers.

Would Wales then have a constitutionally protected right to retain them?

Not necessarily.

Unless the new settlement also changed the underlying constitutional protections, a future Westminster Parliament could potentially legislate to reverse those transfers.

That creates an interesting problem:

* Wales negotiates additional powers.
* The powers are transferred through legislation.
* Welsh institutions build services, expertise and long-term plans around them.
* A subsequent UK Government decides it wants to change the arrangement.
* Westminster retains the legal authority to do so.

This is the distinction between receiving powers and securing powers.

A transfer can be substantial without being constitutionally entrenched.

It also raises a practical concern: governments and public bodies need to make long-term investments. If the allocation of authority can be unilaterally changed, that can affect planning, institutional confidence and the stability of public services.

8. What would stronger constitutional protection look like?

There are several possible reforms.

Reform	What it would protect
Statutory duty to obtain Welsh consent	Makes consent a formal requirement for specified changes
Stronger Sewel legislation	Gives the convention a clearer legal framework
Protected areas of devolved competence	Limits unilateral Westminster intervention in defined policy areas
Independent constitutional dispute resolution	Provides a process for resolving disagreements
Statutory fiscal framework	Gives greater security to funding and borrowing arrangements
Referendum requirement for major reductions in devolved powers	Gives the electorate a direct role in fundamental constitutional change
A federal constitution	Establishes a more clearly defined and protected distribution of powers

There are different degrees of protection here. A statutory requirement to consult is not equivalent to a requirement to obtain consent. And even a statutory consent requirement would need to address whether a future Parliament could amend or repeal it.

A genuinely entrenched settlement would require a much more fundamental constitutional redesign.

9. The central comparison

I would summarise the three nations like this:

Question	Wales	Scotland	Northern Ireland
Permanent institutions recognised in statute?	Yes	Yes	Institutions established in statute
General protection against Westminster changing devolved powers?	No entrenched protection	No entrenched protection	No general entrenchment of devolved powers
Sewel recognised in statute?	Yes	Yes	Different consent and power-sharing arrangements
Fundamental constitutional status protected by a specific consent mechanism?	No equivalent	No equivalent referendum mechanism for independence	Yes
International treaty foundation for the settlement?	No equivalent	No equivalent	Yes, Good Friday Agreement

Legislation.gov.uk +3

10. My conclusion: devolution is established, but its security is incomplete

There is a distinction between a constitution that says:

These institutions and powers exist because Parliament has established them.

And one that says:

These institutions and powers exist because the constituent nations have an agreed constitutional entitlement to exercise them, which cannot be unilaterally withdrawn.

The UK’s devolution settlement is much closer to the first model.

That does not mean Westminster is likely to abolish the Senedd or casually reverse devolution. Political consequences, democratic mandates, conventions and constitutional expectations matter enormously.

But the legal power remains.

And this brings us back to the central weakness in the Burnham correspondence.

If the UK Government wants Wales to trust the Union, it is not enough to offer more participation in UK-controlled institutions. It needs to consider whether the authority Wales already exercises, and any additional authority it negotiates, is sufficiently protected against unilateral change.

Otherwise, Wales is being asked to trust a constitutional relationship in which one party retains the ultimate legal power to change the terms.

That is a question of constitutional confidence, not simply the number of powers devolved.

Where this leaves our original investigation

We’ve now completed the 26-topic agenda at the level of initial examination.

I would suggest one final synthesis before we close this stage: bring together the four major themes we’ve uncovered:

* Democratic authority: what Welsh voters have asked their institutions to do.
* Operational coherence: whether the division of responsibilities enables effective government.
* Constitutional equality: how Wales compares with Scotland and Northern Ireland.
* Constitutional security: whether the powers Wales exercises are adequately protected.

Those four strands give us a framework for evaluating the correspondence without assuming either that every Welsh request must be granted or that the existing settlement is necessarily the right one.”.  

“The Welsh devolution question: a four-part constitutional synthesis

The central issue emerging from our examination is not simply whether Wales should receive more powers. It is whether the current Union gives Welsh democratic institutions sufficient authority, resources and constitutional security to govern effectively — and whether the UK Government has a convincing reason for retaining the powers it refuses to transfer.

The four themes fit together. They are not four separate complaints: each exposes a different part of the same constitutional relationship.

1. Democratic authority: what have Welsh voters asked their institutions to do?

The starting point is the democratic mandate.

In the Senedd election on 7 May 2026, Plaid Cymru won 43 of 96 seats, with 35.4% of the vote. It became the largest party and formed the Welsh Government. Vote.Wales +1

There are two things we need to say accurately about that result:

* It provides a mandate for Plaid Cymru to pursue the commitments on which it campaigned, including further devolution.
* It does not establish that a majority of Welsh voters support every proposed transfer of power, or that they voted for independence.

Those are different propositions.

The important democratic question is whether the Welsh Government can meaningfully implement its programme within the existing settlement.

Ap Iorwerth’s argument is that Wales needs the powers, funding and financial flexibility to improve public services and grow its economy. His October letter translated that into specific requests:

* policing and justice;
* rail infrastructure;
* Crown Estate management;
* fiscal and borrowing flexibility;
* greater control over additional policy areas.

These were not simply symbolic requests for constitutional recognition. They were presented as instruments for delivering the government’s programme.

The UK Government’s response therefore has a democratic dimension beyond whether it agrees with each policy proposal.

If Westminster refuses a transfer, it is also limiting the range of decisions the elected Welsh Government can make on those subjects.

The democratic tension: Welsh voters elect a government to pursue a programme, but the UK Government retains authority over some of the institutions and resources that the Welsh Government says it needs to deliver it.

That does not mean every election commitment must automatically be granted. It does mean that the UK Government needs to explain how it intends to accommodate the democratic mandate within the constitutional settlement.

There is a significant development today, 24 November: ap Iorwerth has called for a new alliance to ensure Wales’s constitutional future is shaped by the people of Wales and their elected representatives. He says the correspondence has reignited grassroots discussion about empowering Wales. Welsh Government News

That is evidence of the dispute moving beyond the original ministerial exchange into a wider constitutional conversation.

2. Operational coherence: can the division of responsibilities deliver effective government?

This is where the argument becomes practical.

The central issue is the relationship between responsibility and authority.

The Welsh Government has responsibility for substantial parts of public service delivery, including:

* health;
* education;
* housing;
* local government;
* social services;
* transport policy;
* economic development within devolved competence;
* environmental and planning responsibilities.

But important powers affecting those services remain at UK level.

The resulting arrangements can create what we have been calling responsibility–authority gaps.

Consider four examples.

Area	Welsh responsibility	Important UK-level authority	Potential problem
Policing	Prevention, health, education, housing, community safety	Police governance and core policing powers	Welsh services influence demand but cannot control the entire policing response
Justice	Rehabilitation support, social services, education, housing and reintegration	Courts, criminal law, prisons and much of probation and youth justice	Policy outcomes depend on institutions controlled by different governments
Rail	Welsh transport planning, passenger services and infrastructure in some areas	Wider rail infrastructure and network decisions	Welsh priorities depend on decisions over which Wales has limited authority
Crown Estate	Planning, economic development, environmental policy and local priorities	Management of Crown Estate assets and associated revenues	Wales has responsibilities for territorial development without equivalent control over important assets

The precise boundaries vary by policy area. This is not an argument that the UK has no legitimate role in any of them.

The important question is whether the current division creates avoidable barriers to effective government.

The Independent Commission on the Constitutional Future of Wales examined these problems in detail. It recommended:

* legislative and executive devolution of justice and policing, with an agreed transition;
* full devolution of rail services and infrastructure, with shared governance for cross-border services;
* Welsh responsibility for the Crown Estate;
* stronger intergovernmental cooperation;
* greater financial flexibility.

It also specifically recommended shared governance where necessary for effective operations. GOV.WALES +1

That last point matters enormously.

The Commission was not proposing that Wales should cease cooperating with England, Scotland or the rest of the UK.

It was proposing that cooperation should not be used as a reason to prevent Welsh democratic institutions from exercising authority.

The operational test

For each reserved function, we should ask:

* Does the function genuinely require UK-wide coordination?
* Does coordination require UK-wide ownership or control?
* Could a Welsh authority exercise the function while participating in shared UK arrangements?
* Would transferring the function improve accountability and service integration?
* What funding, staff, expertise and institutional capacity would be needed?

The distinction between questions 1 and 2 is especially important.

A railway can be cross-border without every infrastructure decision being made by one government. A police service can cooperate across borders without every aspect of its governance being centrally controlled.

The operational argument for devolution is not that Wales should do everything independently. It is that Wales should have authority over the decisions it needs to coordinate its own responsibilities, while agreeing the necessary shared arrangements with other governments.

3. Constitutional equality: how does Wales compare with Scotland and Northern Ireland?

The UK already operates an asymmetrical constitution.

Scotland has a distinct legal jurisdiction, devolved policing and justice, greater income-tax powers and devolved Crown Estate management.

Northern Ireland has its own justice and policing arrangements, significant policy responsibilities, and a distinctive power-sharing settlement underpinned by the Good Friday Agreement.

Wales has a different allocation of responsibilities and a shared legal jurisdiction with England.

These differences are not inherently problematic. Different nations can reasonably have different constitutional arrangements.

But they create a question of principle.

When does constitutional difference reflect legitimate national circumstances, and when does it reflect an unequal distribution of authority that has simply been inherited?

The comparison with Scotland is particularly relevant to the October letter.

Ap Iorwerth explicitly asked for Wales to have access to the same constitutional and fiscal tools already available to Scotland.

The Welsh Government’s position is not that every Scottish institution must be replicated. It is that Scotland demonstrates that devolved policing, justice, Crown Estate management and more extensive fiscal powers can coexist with the Union.

The UK Government can argue that particular differences are necessary. But those differences should be explained and evidenced individually.

It is not sufficient to say that the existing England-and-Wales system has advantages if the question is whether an alternative Welsh arrangement could achieve those advantages too.

The Northern Ireland qualification

Northern Ireland is not a straightforward template for Wales.

Its settlement reflects conflict, national identity, cross-border relationships and a specific international agreement.

But it does demonstrate that the UK can accommodate a distinctive constitutional settlement when the political circumstances require it.

It also demonstrates that constitutional stability can depend on consent, institutional guarantees and relationships between governments, rather than simply central control.

The English dimension

There is a further asymmetry.

Westminster governs the UK while also acting as the principal national legislature for England in areas devolved elsewhere.

If Wales receives additional powers, that does not automatically require an English Parliament or federalism.

But it makes the question of England’s own constitutional arrangements increasingly difficult to avoid.

The UK needs to explain how it can sustain a Union in which its constituent nations have different powers, while England’s national interests are represented through the same institutions that govern the entire state.

The constitutional equality question is therefore not whether every nation must have identical powers. It is whether there is a fair and principled basis for the differences that exist.

4. Constitutional security: how protected is the authority Wales already has?

This is arguably the deepest structural issue.

The Government of Wales Act 2006 establishes the Senedd’s legislative powers. The Wales Act 2017 added statutory permanence and recognition of Welsh law.

But section 107(5) preserves Westminster’s power to legislate for Wales.

Section 107(6) recognises the Sewel Convention, under which Westminster will not normally legislate in devolved areas without Senedd consent. The convention is not a legally enforceable veto. Legislation.gov.uk +1

The result is an unusual arrangement:

* Wales has elected institutions with genuine statutory authority.
* Welsh legislation is subject to defined constitutional limits.
* The Senedd and Welsh Government are recognised as permanent.
* But Westminster retains ultimate legislative sovereignty.

This creates two related questions.

First: are existing Welsh powers secure?

The Independent Commission concluded that the current settlement is vulnerable to gradual erosion and recommended statutory intergovernmental cooperation and stronger protection for devolved powers. GOV.WALES +1

Second: would additional powers be secure?

Even if Westminster agreed to transfer policing, justice or rail responsibilities, those powers would not automatically become entrenched against future Westminster legislation.

This is why a discussion about further devolution should also be a discussion about constitutional safeguards.

A settlement that transfers powers without protecting the principles governing their exercise may leave the underlying problem unresolved.

What stronger security could involve

* A statutory duty of cooperation between the UK and devolved governments.
* A requirement for devolved consent before altering the distribution of devolved powers, with carefully defined exceptions.
* Independent mechanisms for resolving constitutional disputes.
* Greater statutory protection for fiscal arrangements.
* Clearer rules governing UK legislation that affects devolved responsibilities.
* A formal process for reviewing the settlement periodically.

These are not all equivalent. Consultation, consent and a legally protected veto are different things. But they all address the same underlying question: how can the constituent nations have confidence that the terms of the Union will not be changed unilaterally?

5. How the four themes connect

This is the synthesis I would retain as the central analytical model.

Four dimensions of constitutional confidence

1. Democratic authority

Can elected Welsh institutions act on their mandate?

2. Operational coherence

Do powers and responsibilities fit together sufficiently to govern effectively?

3. Constitutional equality

Are differences between the nations principled and explainable?

4. Constitutional security

Are devolved powers protected against unilateral interference?

Constitutional confidence in the Union

The combined effect of meaningful authority, effective government, fair treatment and secure arrangements

The four dimensions reinforce one another.

For example:

* Welsh voters elect a government with commitments to improve public services.
* The government finds that it lacks authority over institutions that affect those services.
* It requests further powers, pointing to arrangements already operating in Scotland.
* Westminster refuses the central transfers and offers greater participation instead.
* The Welsh Government remains unable to exercise the authority it says is necessary.
* The existing powers and any future concessions remain vulnerable to changes made at Westminster.
* Confidence in the ability of the Union to accommodate Welsh democratic government may decline.

This is the potential political feedback loop we identified earlier.

It does not mean that every Welsh voter will become an independence supporter. It means that people who want the Union to work may become increasingly dissatisfied with the constitutional mechanisms through which it operates.

The Independent Commission’s report is particularly relevant here. It warned that the current settlement was vulnerable to erosion, that intergovernmental relations had fallen short of what citizens expected, and that the Union required stronger legal and procedural foundations. GOV.WALES +1

6. Applying the four tests to Burnham’s response

We can now evaluate the correspondence without collapsing everything into a simple argument for or against devolution.

Constitutional test	What the Welsh Government sought	What the UK Government offered	Outstanding issue
Democratic authority	Transfer of specified decision-making powers	Greater Welsh participation in existing systems	Who ultimately decides?
Operational coherence	Align powers across connected public services	Shared governance and cooperation	Can coordination work without Welsh authority?
Constitutional equality	Powers comparable to Scotland in selected areas	Retention of the England-and-Wales model	What justifies the difference?
Constitutional security	More secure powers, funding and intergovernmental arrangements	Continued discussion and existing mechanisms	What prevents unilateral changes?

The response contains genuine offers of cooperation and some movement on fiscal flexibility, youth justice, probation and rail enhancement governance.

But it does not resolve the central questions in the four categories.

That is the key distinction between a practical response to individual policy requests and a response to the constitutional problem that connects them.

7. What would a constructive UK-wide response need to contain?

I would identify six components.

1. A published rationale for reservations: explain why each major function needs to remain at UK level, rather than simply describing the benefits of existing arrangements.

2. A proper comparative assessment: examine whether Welsh devolution could preserve interoperability, shared expertise and cross-border coordination.

3. A negotiated timetable: identify which functions could transfer immediately, which require staged preparation and which genuinely need to remain shared.

4. A financial settlement: agree the funding, borrowing and institutional capacity required to exercise any new powers effectively.

5. Constitutional safeguards: establish how future changes to the settlement would be negotiated and how Welsh consent would be protected.

6. A wider Union conversation: consider the implications for Scotland, Northern Ireland and England, rather than treating each constitutional dispute as an isolated negotiation.

There is already an evidence base for much of this. The Independent Commission set out recommendations covering precisely these areas, including justice, policing, rail, Crown Estate, financial management and intergovernmental relations. GOV.WALES +1

8. The overall conclusion

I would not frame this as Wales versus Westminster, or as a contest between nationalism and unionism.

The more fundamental question is whether the Union can function as a relationship between nations with meaningful democratic authority, rather than as a system in which the centre ultimately determines how much authority the others may exercise.

The four dimensions give us a way to examine that:

* Democratic authority asks whether Welsh voters can meaningfully influence the decisions made in their name.
* Operational coherence asks whether the constitutional division of powers enables governments to deliver effectively.
* Constitutional equality asks whether differences between the nations have defensible reasons.
* Constitutional security asks whether the arrangements can be trusted to endure.

A Union can survive considerable asymmetry. It can also survive disagreement between governments. What becomes more difficult is sustaining confidence when democratic mandates, operational responsibilities, constitutional treatment and legal authority repeatedly pull in different directions.

The UK Government’s case for retaining particular powers may be defensible. But it needs to demonstrate why, and it needs to offer a credible route for resolving the problems that Welsh institutions say they cannot solve under the current arrangements.

The ultimate test is not whether Wales gets every power it asks for. It is whether the Union has a credible, fair and secure process through which Wales can seek the powers it needs, challenge the powers it believes are wrongly retained, and have those questions resolved without depending entirely on the goodwill of the government in Westminster.

That, to me, is the central constitutional question emerging from this entire investigation.

9. The documentary spine we have now established

For the archive, I would retain these as the core source documents:

* The 1 October 2026 First Minister’s letter: the Welsh Government’s case and specific requests.
* The 20 November 2026 Prime Minister’s reply: the UK Government’s reasons, offers and refusals.
* The 23–24 November 2026 Senedd response: the immediate political and constitutional reaction.
* The Independent Commission’s January 2024 final report: the prior independent evidence base and proposed reforms.
* The Government of Wales Act 2006, as amended: the actual statutory framework within which the dispute takes place.

The correspondence is now part of a wider constitutional debate, rather than a self-contained exchange between two First Ministers. Ap Iorwerth’s announcement today of a proposed new alliance is a concrete indication of that development. GOV.WALES +4

One final distinction to preserve: we have established a substantial case that the current settlement has identifiable tensions and vulnerabilities. We have not established that independence is the necessary answer, that every requested transfer would improve outcomes, or that the UK Government’s stated operational concerns are all invalid. Those questions require separate evidence.

That is what makes this a useful constitutional analysis: it leaves the political destination open while making the institutional problems much harder to dismiss.”.   

“The key distinction is between Wales becoming sovereign and Wales becoming an EU member. They would be two separate constitutional processes, and EU membership would not happen automatically on independence.

A sovereign Wales could apply to join the EU. The central questions would be whether it met the accession requirements, whether the existing EU member states agreed unanimously, and how quickly the negotiations could be completed. The EU has no automatic fast-track route for a newly independent part of a former member state.

1. The legal route

Under Article 49 of the Treaty on European Union, any European state that respects the EU’s fundamental values can apply. Wales would therefore be eligible to apply as a sovereign European state; it would not need to prove that it had been an EU member before.

But the process has several gates:

* Wales submits an application.
* The European Commission assesses it and the EU considers whether to grant candidate status.
* The EU’s member states must unanimously agree to open negotiations.
* Wales negotiates the terms of membership and demonstrates that it can apply and enforce EU law.
* The European Parliament consents, the member states unanimously approve the accession treaty, and every member state ratifies it under its own constitutional rules. EUR-Lex +2

That unanimity requirement matters. Even if Wales were technically well prepared, accession would still require political agreement across the EU.

2. What might make Wales different from a typical applicant?

Wales would start from an unusual position.

Factors that could make the technical process easier:

* It has spent decades within the EU legal and regulatory system as part of the UK.
* Its public administration, courts, universities, businesses and regulators have extensive experience of EU-derived rules.
* It would not be building an entirely new state from scratch in every policy area.
* It has an established relationship with European institutions and networks.

Factors that could make it harder:

* Brexit means Wales is no longer operating as part of the EU single market and customs union.
* Some UK rules and regulatory arrangements have diverged from EU requirements.
* An independent Wales would need to establish or confirm the full range of state-level institutions and responsibilities needed for membership.
* Its financial contribution, representation in EU institutions, transitional arrangements and relationship with the UK would need to be negotiated.

The Commission’s accession process is based on the ability to adopt and enforce the EU’s body of law, not simply on whether a country used to follow it. EUR-Lex +1

3. How quickly? Historical comparisons

There is a useful range in previous accessions:

Country or group	Application to membership
Finland	About 3 years (1992–95)
Austria, Sweden and Finland	Negotiations took around 13 months once formally opened
Central and eastern European countries joining in 2004	Around 6 years of negotiations
Croatia	10 years from application to membership (2003–13)

These examples show that accession can be relatively quick for a well-prepared, economically developed European state, but there is no guaranteed timetable. House of Commons Library +2

For Wales, I would use the following as illustrative planning scenarios, not predictions:

* Around 2–4 years after independence: an unusually streamlined process, with extensive preparation beforehand, strong political support across the EU and agreement on transitional arrangements.
* Around 4–7 years: a more extended process involving institutional preparation, regulatory alignment and negotiations over the terms of membership.
* 8–10 years or longer: if political disagreements, disputes over the independence settlement or objections from individual EU member states delayed progress.

The clock could also start before formal independence if Wales and the EU agreed to extensive preparatory engagement. But that would not itself make Wales a member.

4. The biggest uncertainty: the politics of independence

There is no direct precedent for a territory leaving a former EU member state after that state has itself left the EU, then applying as a newly sovereign country. The Scottish independence debate has examined this problem, but it remains legally and politically untested. House of Commons Library +1

Three things would matter enormously:

A. Whether independence was negotiated with the UK

An agreed constitutional settlement would give Wales a much clearer route to establishing statehood, resolving assets and liabilities, and preparing an application. It would not guarantee EU accession, but it would avoid adding a major dispute over the legitimacy and circumstances of independence.

B. Whether the EU wanted to accommodate Wales

The EU’s formal rules would still apply. All member states would have to agree to the accession process and ultimately ratify the treaty. A single government’s objection could delay or prevent it. EUR-Lex +1

C. Whether Wales was prepared

Wales would need a clear programme for regulatory alignment, state institutions, public finances, border arrangements and implementation of EU law. Its previous membership experience could help, but it would not remove those requirements.

5. What is the actual political position in Wales?

There is an important distinction between Plaid Cymru’s constitutional objective and the Welsh Government’s current position.

* Plaid Cymru: its constitution identifies securing Welsh independence in Europe as a primary objective. Institute for Government
* Welsh Government: in evidence to the Senedd on 12 November 2026, the Government said its preference was for Wales and the UK as a whole to rejoin the EU. It described closer relations through a customs union and/or single market as an interim objective. Welsh Parliament

So the current Welsh Government position is not simply that an independent Wales should apply separately. Its stated preference is for the UK to return to the EU.

6. My overall assessment of the question

I would separate three questions rather than collapse them into one probability:

Question	What can be established
Could a sovereign Wales legally apply?	Yes, under Article 49.
Could Wales meet the practical requirements?	It has substantial existing institutional and regulatory experience, but would need preparation and assessment.
Would the EU admit it, and when?	Not guaranteed. It requires unanimous political agreement and ratification; the timing would depend on negotiations and political circumstances.

The point I would particularly keep in view is that EU accession could be negotiated as part of the process of becoming independent, rather than treated as a distant project to begin only afterwards. Wales could prepare institutions, map regulatory gaps and establish contacts with the Commission while negotiating its constitutional future.

That might make a relatively short accession process more achievable, although it could not guarantee one.

There is also a fascinating constitutional question underneath this: if Wales became independent but wanted to retain close economic and political integration with both the EU and the rest of Britain, what kind of relationship would it seek with each? EU membership would not require Wales to abandon close relations with England, Scotland or Northern Ireland. It would, however, mean managing a new external EU border and negotiating the practical arrangements around it.

The next question I would examine is whether Wales could negotiate an independence settlement with the UK that allowed EU accession preparations to begin before independence formally took effect. That is where the timetable could be affected most substantially.”.  

“Not easily in the short term — but the UK would have alternatives over time. The important distinction is between replacing a military training facility and replacing everything RAF Valley does.

I think there are two Welsh defence sites getting crossed together here:

* RAF Valley, on Anglesey: an RAF airfield and fast-jet pilot training establishment.
* Sennybridge Training Area, near Brecon: a major British Army training area on Mynydd Epynt, used for infantry, artillery, live firing and dry training.

The Brecon Beacons themselves are not one single military base. Sennybridge is the major training estate nearby. House of Commons Library +2

2. Could the Army replace Sennybridge elsewhere?

There are other substantial training areas in Great Britain. The MOD lists 16 major training areas across the UK, and its estate includes sites such as Salisbury Plain, Otterburn, Catterick and Dartmoor. GOV.UK +1

But Sennybridge has some particular characteristics:

* Approximately 12,000 hectares of MOD freehold land, plus around 2,500 hectares of leased forest land.
* A large upland training environment.
* Live artillery and infantry weapons training.
* Accommodation for visiting units.
* A built-up training village for urban combat exercises.

It is a substantial facility, not simply a convenient patch of countryside. Coflein +1

Not a straightforward substitution

The Army could redistribute some training to other UK facilities, but replacing Sennybridge’s combination of terrain, range capacity and infrastructure would mean additional costs, scheduling pressures and potentially investment in other sites. I haven’t found a public MOD assessment quantifying the cost or timescale of replacing it.

3. What if independent Wales refused access?

This is where the constitutional question becomes important.

At present, Sennybridge is MOD freehold land, requisitioned in 1939. But MOD ownership would not make it sovereign British territory after Welsh independence.

If Wales became a sovereign state:
