* Sennybridge would be within Welsh territorial jurisdiction.
* The UK could not simply continue conducting military exercises there by virtue of its existing ownership.
* Continued use would need to be addressed in the independence settlement, through a lease, basing agreement or defence cooperation treaty.
* Wales could negotiate conditions, restrictions, payments, environmental obligations and oversight — or decline to permit continued use.

The exact treatment of MOD land and property would itself be a matter for the independence settlement. Ownership and the right to conduct military operations are separate questions.

4. What about RAF Valley?

That is a different strategic problem.

RAF Valley is used for fast-jet pilot training and operational low-flying training. The House of Commons Library identifies it as a significant RAF facility. House of Commons Library

The British Army cannot substitute for that. The RAF would need another suitable airfield and the necessary training infrastructure, airspace arrangements and support facilities.

And Wales would have its own negotiating interests: RAF Valley, Sennybridge, Castlemartin, Manorbier and other defence sites are not interchangeable assets.

5. The strategic bargaining question

I would frame this less as could Britain do without Wales? and more as:

What would the UK be willing to negotiate to retain access to facilities that are valuable to its defence capability?

There are several possible arrangements:

Arrangement	What it would mean
Continued UK access	Long-term agreement to use specified facilities
Joint defence arrangement	Welsh and UK forces share facilities and responsibilities
Commercial-style lease	UK pays Wales for access and use
Restricted access	Specific training permitted under Welsh conditions
No continued access	UK relocates training and facilities

There is a reciprocal consideration too: an independent Wales might value defence cooperation, employment, investment and training opportunities. It would not necessarily want to exclude British forces.

My main conclusion: the UK has alternatives to Sennybridge, but not an effortless like-for-like replacement. RAF Valley presents a separate set of considerations. That gives Wales something meaningful to negotiate over, without implying that either side would necessarily want a defence rupture.

And it connects directly to our earlier discussion of EU accession: an independent Wales would be negotiating not just its relationship with the EU, but also a new defence and security relationship with its nearest neighbour.”.  

“Wales supplies a strategically significant amount of water to England, particularly to the English Midlands and North West. The UK could develop alternatives, but it could not replace those supplies instantly or without substantial infrastructure, planning and environmental consequences.

There is also a constitutional dimension: water is not just a commodity that happens to cross the border. The reservoirs, catchments and abstraction points are located in Welsh territory, so an independent Wales would have a direct interest in the terms on which that resource was used.

1. How much water crosses the border?

The most useful current overview is the National Infrastructure Commission for Wales’s 2026 assessment. It identifies two major transfer systems:

Source	Maximum stated transfer	Main English beneficiaries
Elan Valley reservoirs, through the Elan Aqueduct	More than 320 million litres/day	Birmingham and the Midlands
Lake Vyrnwy and the River Dee	Up to 205 million litres/day	Liverpool, Cheshire and Manchester
Combined stated capacity	More than 525 million litres/day	Midlands and North West

These are maximum stated capacities, not a measurement of the average amount actually delivered every day. The National Infrastructure Commission for Wales +1

525 million litres

A day — the combined stated capacity of those two major transfer systems.

For perspective, the two principal Welsh water companies supplied 927 million litres per day in Wales in 2023. So the potential export capacity is very substantial relative to Welsh public water supply. But that comparison is between capacity and actual supply, not two directly equivalent daily measurements. The National Infrastructure Commission for Wales

You may also encounter a figure of around 600 million litres a day in Senedd debates. That comes from a 2023 discussion which included wider River Dee regulation and abstraction figures. I would not treat it as a precise current measurement of water physically delivered across the border. Senedd Cymru +1

2. How important is that to England?

England’s water companies put approximately 14,110 million litres per day into their distribution networks in 2025–26. The 525 million litres of Welsh transfer capacity is equivalent to roughly 3.7% of that national volume. The National Infrastructure Commission for Wales +1

That sounds relatively small at national scale, but the geographical distribution matters enormously.

* Birmingham and parts of the Midlands have a long-established reliance on the Elan Valley system.
* Liverpool, Cheshire and Manchester benefit from the Vyrnwy and Dee system.
* Replacing those supplies means finding water in the right place, treating it and getting it through a connected network. A surplus somewhere in England is not automatically available to customers elsewhere.

So the national percentage understates the significance to particular regions.

3. What could England do instead?

There are several alternatives, but none is a magic tap.

Alternative	What it offers	Limitations
New reservoirs	Additional stored water	Expensive, slow, land-intensive and environmentally contentious
Transfers within England	Move water from wetter to drier regions	Requires major pipelines, pumping and permissions
Leakage reduction	Recover water already treated and lost	Requires sustained investment; not all leakage is economically or technically recoverable
Water recycling	Treat wastewater for further use	Requires new treatment infrastructure and public confidence
Desalination	Produce water from seawater	Energy-intensive and geographically constrained
Demand reduction	Reduce household and industrial consumption	Requires behavioural change, metering and efficiency investment

There is a particularly interesting example already under development: the proposed Severn–Thames Transfer.

Thames Water’s proposed scheme would move water from the Severn towards the Thames during drought conditions. Its proposed sources include existing United Utilities abstraction from Lake Vyrnwy, water redeployment around Shrewsbury, treated wastewater and abstraction from the Severn itself. Thames Water

Notice something important: one of the proposed alternatives still relies on Welsh water. It is not a clean substitute for Welsh cooperation.

There is also a wider capacity problem. England’s 2025–26 water report says leakage remained just under 19% of water entering distribution, while the government says new reservoirs, transfers and recycling schemes are complex and take time. The same report describes an ongoing drought and many companies falling behind their plans. GOV.UK +1

4. Could England replace Welsh supplies without Welsh consent?

We need to distinguish two constitutional situations.

Under the present Union: Welsh water resources are already subject to Welsh environmental and abstraction regulation, alongside the relevant English regulatory responsibilities. Natural Resources Wales regulates abstraction licences and works with the Environment Agency on cross-border water-resource planning. cyfoethnaturiolcymru.gov.uk +1

Under an independent Wales: the position would change substantially.

* Welsh reservoirs and water infrastructure within Welsh territory would fall within Welsh sovereignty, subject to whatever property settlement was negotiated.
* England could not simply assume that existing arrangements gave it an unrestricted right to continue taking water.
* Cross-border rivers would require cooperation and agreements over abstraction, river flows, environmental protection and drought management.
* Existing infrastructure could be retained under a negotiated supply agreement, lease or treaty.

One qualification: Wales would not have an unlimited right to disregard the environmental consequences of its decisions or any applicable international obligations. Nor would every drop of water flowing from Wales automatically become exclusively Welsh property. The legal position would depend on the source, infrastructure, abstraction point and agreements.

5. What does this mean in negotiations?

I would put water alongside the military facilities we just discussed, but with an important difference.

Asset or resource	What the UK would need to replace or negotiate
Sennybridge	Military training capacity and access
RAF Valley	Airfield and pilot-training capacity
Elan Valley	Large-scale water supply to the Midlands
Vyrnwy and Dee	Water supply to the North West
Cross-border rivers	Long-term management and environmental agreements

Water is especially interesting because it is a continuing resource, not a one-off asset. It has recurring economic value, and the value of reliable supply may increase as climate change makes water availability less predictable.

My central finding is that England could diversify away from Welsh water, but it would be a substantial infrastructure and water-security programme, not an immediate substitution. The current English plans themselves demonstrate that the UK is interested in developing more sources and transfers, while still incorporating existing Welsh supplies.

And there is a question of fairness that goes beyond independence: what does Wales receive, financially and environmentally, for supplying water to English cities?

That is a legitimate question even within the present Union. It could become a major part of the economic and constitutional negotiations of an independent Wales.”.  

“I think farming belongs in this discussion as a major element of Wales’s strategic importance to the UK, particularly when considered alongside water, land use, energy and defence.

Livestock is central, but the significance is broader than the quantity of meat Wales produces. Welsh agriculture occupies a distinctive landscape, supports rural economies and food-processing networks, and contributes to the UK’s domestic food supply. It would be a mistake to assess its importance solely by agriculture’s percentage of UK GDP.

1. Livestock: Wales has a distinctive national role

The latest Welsh agricultural survey, for June 2025, records:

* 8.8 million sheep and lambs.
* 1.08 million cattle and calves.
* More than 90% of Welsh land used for agricultural purposes, including common grazing.
* Nearly two-thirds of farm land in permanent grassland, with another 14% in rough grazing. GOV.WALES

For comparison, the UK had around 30 million sheep and lambs and 9.3 million cattle and calves in 2025. On those approximate figures, Wales holds around 29% of the UK’s sheep and lamb population and 12% of its cattle. The survey dates and methods are not perfectly identical, so treat those as indicative proportions. GOV.WALES +1

This is not just a cultural image of Wales. It reflects geography. Much of the country is hilly, with soil and climate conditions that limit arable production but favour grass-based livestock farming. Replacing that production by simply growing more cereals would not be realistic across much of the same land. GOV.WALES +1

2. The economic value is more than sheep and beef

The Welsh Government’s provisional 2025 figures put agricultural output at approximately £2.4 billion. Some particularly important components were:

Welsh agricultural output, 2025	Value
Cattle	£515m
Sheep	£344m
Milk and milk products	£929m
Poultry	£67m
Eggs	£131m
Cereals	£40m
Horticulture	£69m

Cattle and sheep together accounted for an estimated 36% of agricultural output. Dairy is another major pillar: milk and milk products were worth nearly £1 billion. GOV.WALES

That matters because the sector is not one homogeneous industry. It includes:

* Beef and lamb production.
* Dairy farming, milk collection and processing.
* Cheese and other food manufacturing.
* Poultry and egg production.
* Livestock markets, auctioneering and veterinary services.
* Abattoirs, meat processing, distribution and cold-chain logistics.
* Feed, agricultural machinery, fencing and other supporting businesses.

The wider Welsh food and drink economy is considerably larger than the farm-gate figures. The Welsh Affairs Committee recorded industry evidence putting its value at more than £9.3 billion, although that is a broader sectoral estimate, not the value of agricultural production alone. UK Parliament

3. The land itself is strategically important

This is an aspect I would not overlook.

Wales has a relatively small proportion of its agricultural land in arable production. In 2025, cereals occupied only around 3% of agricultural land. Much of the rest is grassland and rough grazing. GOV.WALES

That means Welsh farming contributes something different from the large arable-producing areas of eastern and southern England.

Its wider functions include:

* Maintaining productive grassland and grazing systems.
* Managing upland landscapes and common land.
* Maintaining rural populations and local economic activity.
* Supporting biodiversity and landscape management, although outcomes depend heavily on farming practices.
* Managing land within important water catchments.
* Contributing to soil management, carbon storage and flood-risk management.

The last three are particularly relevant to our earlier discussion of water. The way Welsh land is managed can affect water quality, river flows, sediment and downstream flood risk. The relationship between farming and water infrastructure is therefore not simply that farms occupy the land around reservoirs: land management can influence the performance and environmental condition of the catchments themselves.

4. How replaceable is Welsh agricultural production for the UK?

There are several ways the rest of the UK could compensate for losing access to Welsh agricultural output:

* Increase production elsewhere in Britain.
* Import more beef, lamb and dairy products.
* Change consumption patterns.
* Invest in different farming systems and food-processing capacity.

But there are limits to each.

The UK Food Security Report 2024 recorded domestic production-to-supply ratios of 114% for lamb, 105% for milk and 85% for beef in 2023. That means the UK was a net exporter of lamb and milk by volume, but still relied on imports for part of its beef supply. GOV.UK Assets

Wales’s agricultural contribution is therefore not simply a matter of whether the UK could feed itself without Welsh farms. It is also about:

* Losing a substantial share of domestic sheep production.
* Reducing the geographical diversity of food production.
* Increasing reliance on imports or production in other regions.
* Disrupting established processing and distribution networks.
* Potentially increasing pressure on land elsewhere.

There is a further vulnerability: livestock farming itself relies on inputs. The UK imports animal feed, particularly protein-rich feed ingredients, and grass-based systems are not entirely insulated from that dependence. So Welsh agriculture is strategically valuable, but it is not a self-contained food-security system. GOV.UK Assets +1

5. Wales’s agricultural importance is not just a UK domestic issue

The export relationship is significant.

The Welsh Affairs Committee’s 2025 report noted that Welsh farming exports rely heavily on the EU and that post-Brexit trade barriers have increased costs and reduced competitiveness. It also highlighted the potential competitive pressure from Australian and New Zealand trade agreements, particularly for beef and lamb. UK Parliament

That gives Welsh agriculture an additional strategic dimension:

* Wales has an established agricultural export economy.
* Its producers have an interest in access to European markets.
* EU sanitary and phytosanitary arrangements could materially affect trade costs.
* An independent Wales seeking EU membership would have to consider how its agricultural sector could benefit from, and adapt to, the single market and EU agricultural rules.

This is one reason I would not treat agriculture as a minor issue in the sovereignty discussion.

6. The constitutional implications

I would distinguish three forms of importance:

Type of importance	Welsh agricultural significance
Food security	Significant contribution to domestic meat and dairy production
Economic interdependence	Integrated supply chains, processing, employment and exports
Territorial and environmental	Land management, water catchments, rural communities and ecosystems

There is a qualification worth making: agriculture is not an absolute bargaining weapon. The UK could import food, develop production elsewhere and change its supply arrangements. But doing so would involve costs, time, economic disruption and greater exposure to international markets.

Nor would an independent Wales necessarily want to disrupt agricultural trade with England. English consumers and Welsh producers have mutual interests in maintaining it.

The more interesting constitutional question is whether Wales would have the ability to set agricultural policy around its own circumstances, rather than having its distinctive farming economy affected by UK-wide decisions that do not adequately account for Welsh conditions.

The Welsh Affairs Committee itself argued in 2025 that Welsh farming is distinct and that UK policies affecting it should have Wales-specific impact assessments. It also highlighted concerns about agricultural funding, trade and the long-term viability of the sector. UK Parliament

7. Putting this alongside the other strategic assets

Wales’s strategic contribution to the UK

Agriculture and food

Livestock, dairy, food processing, rural employment and land management.

Water

Major reservoir and river systems supplying English population centres.

Energy and natural resources

Renewable generation, grid connections, land and marine resources.

Defence and military training

Training estates, airfields and strategically useful geography.

My broader observation: Wales’s strategic importance is cumulative. No single resource necessarily gives it decisive leverage over the rest of the UK. But water, food production, energy, land, transport geography and defence infrastructure together create a substantial pattern of mutual dependence.

That is relevant to the independence question because it complicates the idea that Wales would simply be a small state asking the rest of the UK for concessions. It would also be a state with resources, infrastructure, productive capacity and geographic assets that its neighbours would have strong reasons to continue accessing through negotiated agreements.

I would add one more subject to our analysis: Welsh forestry and timber. It belongs in this same resource-security picture, particularly when considering construction materials, carbon sequestration, biomass and rural land use.”.  

“Forestry is a particularly interesting addition because it connects industrial capacity, construction, energy security, carbon storage, water management and rural land ownership. It is not simply another natural resource Wales happens to possess.

There is also a distinction worth making at the outset: Wales has a substantial forest resource, but it has not yet developed the full industrial capacity to capture the value of that resource. That gap is itself strategically important.

1. How much forestry does Wales have?

313,000 ha

Woodland cover

2025 estimate

15%

Land area

Covered by woodland

£718m

Forestry sector GVA

Estimated 2023

£800m+

Ecosystem services

Estimated annual value, 2023

GOV.WALES

The woodland area consists of approximately:

* 174,000 hectares of broadleaved woodland.
* 139,000 hectares of coniferous woodland.

The distinction matters. Broadleaved woodland is important for native biodiversity, landscape and hardwood products. Coniferous woodland is particularly important for commercial softwood production, construction timber, fencing, packaging and wood-based products. GOV.WALES +1

The Welsh Government Woodland Estate is especially significant. Natural Resources Wales manages approximately 123,000 hectares, around 40% of Wales’s forest resource, and aims to bring up to 835,000 cubic metres of timber to market each year. naturalresources.wales

That is a substantial publicly managed productive asset.

2. Timber: the industrial opportunity

Here is where the strategic picture becomes more interesting.

The UK remains heavily dependent on imported wood products. In 2025:

* It imported 6.2 million cubic metres of sawnwood.
* It imported 3.4 million cubic metres of wood-based panels.
* The total value of wood-product imports was approximately £9.1 billion.
* Apparent consumption of wood was 49.1 million cubic metres on a wood-raw-material-equivalent basis. Forest Research

Wales grows timber, but its processing industry does not yet capture as much value as it could.

The Welsh Timber Industrial Strategy identifies a particular weakness: Wales produces suitable timber but does not currently manufacture some of the higher-value structural products, such as:

* Cross-laminated timber (CLT).
* Glulam structural beams.
* Wood-fibre insulation.
* Other advanced engineered timber products.

These products can be used in buildings, including multi-storey construction. Instead, a substantial proportion of available Welsh timber goes into lower-value applications such as fencing, pallets and packaging. GOV.WALES +1

That is an industrial-development opportunity: Wales could move further up the value chain, retaining more manufacturing, skilled employment, investment and technical expertise within the country.

The 2025 Timber Industrial Strategy explicitly identifies increasing supply, developing processing capacity, creating confidence in demand and improving resource efficiency as priorities. GOV.WALES +1

3. Why timber matters to British strategic resilience

The UK is exposed to international timber markets. It imports much of its sawn timber, panels, pulp and paper, and wood pellets. In 2025, the value of wood-product imports was about £9.1 billion. Forest Research

That creates several vulnerabilities:

* Construction: timber is used in housing, roof structures, flooring, doors, windows and engineered building components.
* Infrastructure and maintenance: fencing, sleepers, packaging, utility poles and other products depend on reliable timber supply.
* Manufacturing: furniture, paper, packaging and panel-board industries need predictable raw materials.
* Energy: wood fuels and pellets form part of the biomass market, although their climate benefits depend on sourcing, processing, transport and what they replace.

Wales cannot replace all those imports. Its forests are not large enough to make the UK self-sufficient in wood. But Welsh production is part of the UK’s domestic supply base, and its potential to produce more processed timber matters when international supply is disrupted or prices rise.

There is a particularly important distinction between exporting raw logs and exporting finished products. If Wales sells a log for processing elsewhere, it loses much of the potential manufacturing value, employment and technical capability. If it processes the timber into structural beams, insulation or building components, more of that value stays in Wales.

4. Forestry is also water and climate infrastructure

This is where forestry connects directly to the water question we discussed earlier.

Well-managed woodland can:

* Slow the movement of rainfall into streams and rivers.
* Stabilise soil and reduce erosion.
* Filter sediment and some pollutants.
* Provide shade that helps moderate water temperatures.
* Store carbon in vegetation and soils.
* Provide connected habitats and support biodiversity.

Natural Resources Wales’s 2025 State of Natural Resources report identifies woodland as contributing to flood and drought risk reduction, water-flow regulation and soil-moisture retention. It also reports that Welsh forests sequestered approximately 1.2 million tonnes of CO₂ in 2020, with a projection of 1.6 million tonnes by 2050. cms.cyfoethnaturiol.cymru +1

But there is an important qualification: planting trees does not automatically improve every catchment.

Poorly chosen species, badly located plantations, compacted forestry roads, clear-felling practices and inadequate riparian protection can cause environmental damage. Some conifer plantations can also affect water yield and water quality. Forest design has to account for the whole catchment, not just timber output.

That makes Welsh forestry relevant to the security of water supplies for both Wales and England. It is part of the land-management system upstream of rivers and reservoirs.

5. The relationship with farming

I would not treat forestry and farming as competing land uses in every instance. There is a significant opportunity in agroforestry: integrating trees into working farms.

Examples include:

* Trees providing shelter for sheep and cattle.
* Hedgerows and shelterbelts reducing wind exposure.
* Timber and fruit production alongside livestock.
* Trees along streams protecting watercourses.
* Wooded areas providing habitat and wildlife corridors.
* Farm woodland providing supplementary income.

This could be particularly relevant to upland and marginal farms where conventional agricultural diversification can be difficult.

There is a potential income-diversification model here: livestock, agricultural production, timber, environmental payments and carefully managed recreation. It would need to be designed around each farm’s circumstances, rather than imposed as a universal prescription.

The small sawmill sector is important too. A June 2026 Welsh supply-chain review found that smaller operators help bring timber from farms, estates, small woodlands and local forestry into productive use. Their activities often overlap with farming, fencing, forestry contracting, joinery and other rural businesses. Woodknowledge Wales

That is a good example of how the economic value of forestry spreads beyond the large commercial plantations.

6. Energy: useful, but don’t confuse biomass with a free climate solution

Wood can provide:

* Wood chips for heating.
* Pellets for industrial and domestic heating.
* Residues for certain manufacturing processes.
* Potential feedstocks for bio-based products.

Forestry residues can be useful, particularly where they would otherwise be wasted. But burning whole trees for energy is not automatically carbon-neutral in the timescale relevant to climate targets. Regrowth takes time, and the carbon consequences depend on the forest, harvesting method, alternative use of the wood and energy source being displaced.

There is also a strategic distinction between using wood for energy and using wood in long-lived products. A structural timber beam can store carbon in a building for decades while replacing more carbon-intensive materials. Burning the same timber releases its stored carbon much sooner.

That is why the Welsh Timber Industrial Strategy emphasises higher-value and longer-lived uses, reuse and recycling, rather than simply increasing harvesting volumes. GOV.WALES +1

7. What would an independent Wales control?

This is an important constitutional distinction.

Forestry policy is already substantially devolved. Wales has its own forestry policy, Natural Resources Wales regulates and manages the Welsh Government Woodland Estate, and Welsh Ministers can set priorities for woodland creation, management and timber development.

Independence would not suddenly give Wales a forestry policy competence it currently lacks.

What would change is the wider sovereign framework within which that policy operated:

Area	Position and potential change under independence
Woodland policy	Already substantially devolved; Wales could continue and develop it
Welsh Government Woodland Estate	Would become an asset requiring treatment in the independence property settlement
Forestry regulation	Already substantially Welsh; would sit within a sovereign Welsh state
Timber trade	Wales could negotiate its own trade arrangements, subject to its wider treaty obligations
Industrial development	Wales could design its own industrial strategy and procurement policy
Carbon accounting	Wales would need its own national emissions and land-use accounting arrangements
EU environmental rules	Would need to be aligned with EU requirements if Wales sought membership

The public woodland estate is especially significant. Natural Resources Wales manages it on behalf of the people of Wales, and describes it as a public asset. It is not simply a collection of commercially owned plantations. Natural Resources Wales +1

8. How replaceable would Welsh forestry be for the UK?

The UK could buy timber elsewhere. Indeed, it already does so on a very large scale.

But there are three different things to replace:

* The physical timber: potentially replaceable through imports and increased production elsewhere, although at a cost and with exposure to international markets.
* The processing and industrial capacity: harder to replace quickly if Welsh sawmills, contractors and manufacturers lost access to their raw materials or relocated.
* The ecosystem services: much harder to replace. A Welsh forest that protects a Welsh catchment, stores carbon and supports biodiversity cannot simply be substituted by buying timber from Scandinavia.

The third point is often missed in economic discussions. A forest has value even when no timber is being harvested.

The replaceability distinction

Contribution	Replaceability for the UK
Raw timber	Possible, with import dependence
Processed timber products	Possible, but industrial capacity takes investment and time
Rural employment and supply chains	Not directly replaceable
Welsh catchment management	Geographically specific
Carbon storage and biodiversity	Not interchangeable with imported timber
Landscape and recreation	Place-specific

9. The sovereignty and negotiation implications

There is a further point I would add to our developing picture of Welsh strategic assets.

Wales has an opportunity to develop a domestic timber industry that serves its own housing programme, its agricultural economy and its environmental objectives. It could also supply English and European markets.

The UK would have an interest in maintaining access to that production. But an independent Wales would have an interest in retaining more of the value generated from its forests.

That creates several possible areas for negotiated cooperation:

* Long-term timber supply agreements.
* Cross-border construction and manufacturing supply chains.
* Shared forestry research and disease surveillance.
* Common standards for sustainable timber.
* Joint management of cross-border catchments.
* Carbon accounting and biodiversity cooperation.
* Research into climate-resilient tree species.

It would not need to become a zero-sum relationship.

10. The strategic picture we’re building

I would now group Wales’s assets into six overlapping categories:

Strategic category	Welsh contribution
Food security	Livestock, dairy, poultry, agricultural land and food processing
Water security	Reservoirs, rivers, catchments and water transfers
Materials security	Timber, wood fibre, forestry and potential advanced manufacturing
Energy security	Wind, hydro, marine energy and other natural resources
Defence and security	Military training land, airfields and strategic geography
Environmental security	Carbon sinks, biodiversity, soil, forests and landscape management

The categories overlap. Forestry supports water security; agriculture depends on water and land management; renewable energy uses land and infrastructure; and all of them affect rural employment and economic resilience.

One important qualification: these are not six independent bargaining chips that Wales could simply withhold. They are interconnected resources and systems, and some are much more replaceable than others.

But taken together, they show why the economic relationship between Wales and the rest of the UK would need to be negotiated as a relationship between neighbouring states with substantial mutual dependencies.

The next particularly useful area to investigate would be Welsh mineral resources and quarrying — slate, aggregates, limestone, metals and other industrial minerals. That would complete much of the picture of Wales’s land-based resource economy, alongside agriculture, water and forestry.”.  

“Yes — and this adds an important dimension to the resource picture. Wales is not only a source of renewable energy and electricity for the wider UK; it also has a significant fuel-processing and power-generation role.

But I want to distinguish three things, because they are often blurred together:

* Fuel extracted in Wales.
* Fuel imported into Wales and processed or handled there.
* Electricity generated in Wales and supplied to the wider UK grid.

Those are different forms of strategic importance, and the distinction matters when assessing how dependent the UK actually is on Welsh territory and infrastructure.

1. Milford Haven: a major UK gas gateway

This is probably the most important part of what you are remembering.

The Milford Haven Waterway hosts two major LNG import terminals:

* South Hook LNG
* Dragon LNG

Together, they have regasification capacity equivalent to approximately 20–25% of UK gas demand and typically handle around 70–80% of UK liquefied natural gas imports. The National Infrastructure Commission for Wales

The process is:

* Liquefied natural gas arrives by ship from overseas.
* It is stored and regasified at Milford Haven.
* It enters the UK gas transmission network.
* It supplies households, industry and gas-fired power stations across the UK.

This makes Milford Haven a strategically important entry point for imported energy.

One important correction: this is not Welsh extraction of natural gas. It is Welsh territory and infrastructure being used to receive and distribute internationally sourced fuel.

The distinction actually makes the strategic relationship more interesting: the UK depends on infrastructure located in Wales to access part of its international gas supply.

2. Pembroke refinery: substantial petroleum production

The Valero Pembroke Refinery is another major asset.

Its stated processing capacity is approximately 270,000 barrels of crude oil per day. It produces:

* Petrol
* Diesel
* Aviation fuel
* Heating oil
* Low-sulphur fuel oil

It has around 500 employees and deep-water shipping facilities on the Milford Haven Waterway. valero.com

This is particularly significant for aviation and transport fuel supply.

But again, the distinction matters: the refinery imports its crude feedstock. Wales provides the industrial site, infrastructure, workforce, shipping facilities and processing capacity, rather than supplying all the crude oil itself.

Welsh international trade statistics illustrate the importance of this activity. In the year ending June 2026, oil accounted for £2.8 billion of Welsh goods exports. Oil was also a substantial import category, so these figures reflect a complex processing and trading relationship rather than Welsh-origin crude production. GOV.WALES

3. Coal: actual extraction in Wales

There is still an operating coal mine at Aberpergwm, in Neath Port Talbot.

Government production returns record approximately 120,000 tonnes of output over the four quarters of 2025, based on the reported monthly figures. The mine employed around 250–265 people during those periods. GOV.UK +3

However, I would not exaggerate its national importance:

* It is a specialised source of anthracite.
* It is not a major source of electricity-generation coal.
* The UK has no coal-fired power stations operating since September 2024.
* Its importance is more specialised industrial supply than general energy security.

So Wales still has fuel extraction, but coal is no longer the major pillar of the UK energy system.

4. Electricity: Wales contributes to the national grid

The figures here are substantial.

The Welsh Government’s 2023 energy-generation report recorded:

Welsh electricity generation, 2023	Output
Natural gas	14.5 TWh
Renewable electricity	7.8 TWh
Total	23.2 TWh
Estimated Welsh consumption	14.8 TWh

That gave Wales a substantial electricity surplus in that year. GOV.WALES

The major gas-fired stations include:

* Pembroke Power Station.
* Connah’s Quay Power Station.

The National Infrastructure Commission for Wales identifies the Pembroke station as the second-largest gas-fired power station in Europe. The National Infrastructure Commission for Wales

There is an important recent development, though: Welsh generation has fallen. In 2024, it accounted for 8% of UK electricity generation, down 6.7% on the previous year. Wales was still producing a substantial quantity, but the previous surplus should not be assumed to persist at the same level. GOV.UK Assets

5. How replaceable is this for the UK?

Here is how I would rank the different functions in terms of the practical challenge of replacing them. This is a qualitative assessment, not an official MOD or energy-system ranking.

Welsh facility or output	What the UK would need to replace
Milford Haven LNG terminals	Major alternative LNG import and regasification capacity, plus pipeline connections
Pembroke refinery	Refining capacity, petroleum-product imports and distribution arrangements
Pembroke and Connah’s Quay power stations	Dispatchable electricity generation and grid-balancing capacity
Aberpergwm coal	Specialised anthracite supply
Welsh renewable generation	Replacement generation, transmission and balancing capacity

The LNG terminals are especially interesting. The UK could import more gas through other terminals or pipelines, but that would require alternative shipping capacity, terminal availability and network arrangements. Milford Haven is not simply a warehouse that can be replaced by ordering a different shipment.

And the refinery is an industrial asset, not just a hole in the ground. Replacing its capacity would require considerable investment and time.

6. The constitutional implications

This adds something important to our earlier analysis of Welsh strategic assets.

Resource or infrastructure	Welsh contribution
Gas	Major international LNG entry point
Petroleum	Large-scale refining and fuel distribution
Coal	Continuing specialised extraction
Electricity	Gas-fired generation and renewable electricity
Water	Major supply infrastructure serving English cities
Food	Substantial livestock and dairy production
Timber	Domestic materials and industrial development potential
Defence	Strategically useful military sites and training facilities

There is a recurring pattern: the UK has located nationally important infrastructure in Wales, while many of the benefits and decisions associated with that infrastructure operate at UK level.

That is not automatically unfair. National infrastructure often serves several territories. But it raises legitimate questions about:

* Who owns the assets?
* Who receives the revenue?
* Who bears environmental and infrastructure costs?
* Who decides whether facilities expand or close?
* What compensation or economic benefits flow to local communities?
* What happens to access arrangements if Wales becomes sovereign?

And it gives us a more sophisticated way of examining the independence question.

An independent Wales would not simply be negotiating over its agricultural products or selling electricity to England. It would be negotiating over the continued operation of infrastructure embedded in the UK’s energy-security architecture.

One particularly important distinction: Milford Haven’s terminals, the Pembroke refinery and the power stations are commercially operated assets, not necessarily assets owned by the Welsh or UK governments. Sovereignty over the territory would not automatically transfer ownership of those businesses to a Welsh government. But it would give Wales jurisdiction over the territory, subject to negotiated rights and existing legal obligations.

That is a much more realistic basis for analysing the economic relationship than assuming independence would mean either automatic exclusion of British interests or automatic continuation of current arrangements.”.  

“Yes, I think you’re identifying a real gap in the way the argument is often presented — but there’s an important distinction between Wales being a net fiscal recipient and Wales being a net economic contributor to the UK.

The latest figures give a fairly clear answer to the first question, but not a complete answer to the second.

* On the conventional public-finance calculation: Wales receives more public expenditure than the tax revenue and other public receipts attributed to it.
* On the wider economic calculation: Wales produces substantial goods and services, supplies infrastructure and resources used elsewhere in the UK, and provides environmental and strategic benefits. Those contributions cannot simply be read off the public-finance deficit.

Those are different accounting questions. A region can have a fiscal deficit and still make an important positive contribution to the wider economy.

1. What the Treasury-style fiscal numbers actually say

The Welsh Government’s November 2026 Economic and Fiscal Report, using the latest ONS regional public-finance figures, gives:

Wales · financial year 2024–25

£41.5bn

Public revenue attributed to Wales

£22.8bn

Net fiscal deficit

£7,149 per person

Revenue raised is lower than public expenditure attributed to Wales.

GOV.WALES +1

Wales’s public expenditure was £20,168 per person, around 8% above the UK average. Its revenue was approximately £13,019 per person. GOV.WALES +1

So, if the question is strictly:

Does the Welsh tax base currently generate enough public revenue to cover all the public expenditure allocated to Wales?

The answer is no.

But that does not establish that Wales is economically unproductive, that its resources are a net cost to the UK, or that the same fiscal relationship would continue under independence.

2. The figures that describe economic contribution

There are other numbers which tell a different story.

Measure	Welsh figure	What it tells us
GDP, 2024	£100.3bn	Annual economic output
GVA, 2024	£87.4bn	Value generated by production
Goods exports, July 2025–June 2026	£20.0bn	International trade in goods
Agriculture, forestry, energy and water	Important sectors	Productive and resource contribution

Wales generated 3.3% of UK GVA in 2023, with a higher proportion of its output coming from manufacturing and public services than the UK average. Its goods exports reached £20 billion in the year to June 2026. GOV.WALES +2

These figures are not small. But GDP and exports measure economic activity, not the net benefit accruing to the UK Exchequer. For example, the value of oil exports from a refinery includes imported crude, so the export value is not the same as Welsh domestic value added.

3. You’re right about natural capital — it is being valued

This is the particularly important part of your observation.

The Treasury’s 2026 Green Book explicitly requires government appraisals to consider natural capital where proposals affect it. That includes:

* Land and landscapes.
* Forests and timber.
* Rivers and water.
* Minerals and raw materials.
* Biodiversity.
* Carbon storage and other regulating services.

It also directs analysts towards the government’s ENCA natural-capital guidance. UK government guidance on appraisal +1

So these resources are not invisible to government economists.

The Welsh Government’s Sustainable Farming Scheme business case provides a useful illustration. Its natural-capital assessment estimated that Welsh natural assets provided approximately £2.5 billion in annual ecosystem-service value in 2022, with an estimated asset value of £95.7 billion.

Some of the annual estimates included:

Welsh natural-capital service	Estimated annual value
Agricultural biomass	£522m
Water provisioning	£272m
Greenhouse-gas regulation	£239m
Timber provisioning	£52m
Renewable electricity provisioning	£40m
Wood fuel provisioning	£12.5m

These are estimated ecosystem-service values, not amounts of cash received by the Welsh Government. They also do not measure the full strategic value of the resources to the rest of the UK. GOV.WALES

The ONS makes the same distinction in its UK natural-capital accounts: it estimates both annual service flows and the longer-term asset value of natural resources. It explicitly warns that these are partial valuations, and that some benefits, including flood protection from natural resources, are not yet included. Office for National Statistics

4. But there’s a crucial gap between Treasury appraisal and regional fiscal accounting

This is where I would refine your argument.

There are three different accounting systems:

Accounting system	What it measures	Does it capture Wales’s wider strategic value?
Public finances	Tax receipts and public expenditure	Only indirectly
National economic accounts	Production, income, investment and trade	Captures much of the market activity
Natural-capital and economic appraisal	Environmental services, resource stocks and effects of decisions	Captures some otherwise unpriced benefits

The ONS is unusually explicit about the first distinction. Its country and regional public-finance statistics are not used to manage or monitor fiscal policy. They are estimates produced to show how UK revenue and expenditure can be allocated geographically. Office for National Statistics

The methodology also has a limitation relevant to our discussion: it allocates expenditure according to who benefits, rather than simply where money is spent. Some national expenditure, such as defence and debt interest, is apportioned across the population.

That means the £22.8 billion deficit is not a calculation of the cost to the UK of retaining Wales, nor a calculation of Wales’s total economic contribution.

And the Treasury’s Green Book does not automatically add the value of Welsh water, forestry, catchments and strategic infrastructure to Wales’s tax receipts. Those benefits are assessed in particular policy and investment appraisals, rather than consolidated into one regional balance.

5. What I think is missing from the public debate

I would distinguish five categories of Welsh contribution that need to be considered together.

A broader Welsh contribution account

1. Direct economic production

Manufacturing, agriculture, food processing, refining, energy generation, timber, tourism and exports.

2. National infrastructure services

LNG terminals, refinery capacity, electricity generation, reservoirs, transport corridors and defence facilities.

3. Natural capital

Water catchments, carbon storage, timber, agricultural land, biodiversity, soil and flood management.

4. Strategic resilience

Domestic food and fuel capacity, alternative supply routes, energy security, military training capacity and resource diversity.

5. Interregional economic relationships

Supply chains, employment, cross-border trade, investment, water agreements and access to infrastructure.

The last two are particularly difficult to value. What is the economic value of having a reliable alternative LNG entry point? What is the value of a water supply that reduces the risk of restrictions in Birmingham? What is the value of domestic food production when international supply chains are disrupted?

Those questions are capable of economic analysis, but the answer depends on the scenario being assessed. It would be misleading to assign arbitrary figures to them.

6. So, does Wales contribute a net positive?

I would give you three separate answers:

On the current public-finance measure: no. Wales has a substantial allocated fiscal deficit.

On economic production and trade: Wales makes a substantial positive contribution. It produces approximately £100 billion of annual GDP and exports significant quantities of goods.

On the full strategic and natural-capital relationship: there is no sufficiently comprehensive, consolidated calculation to establish a single net figure. Existing accounting recognises many of the benefits, but does not combine them into a balance sheet that can be directly compared with the regional fiscal deficit.

I would therefore avoid claiming that the resources we’ve identified cancel out the £22.8 billion deficit. We don’t have evidence to support that calculation.

But I would equally avoid allowing the fiscal deficit to stand in for an assessment of Wales’s total value to the UK.

7. The exercise I think would genuinely answer your question

We could construct a Welsh Strategic and Economic Contribution Account, using five columns:

Column	What we would quantify
A. Fiscal	Tax revenue, public expenditure, subsidies and transfers
B. Productive	GVA, exports, employment, profits and supply-chain value
C. Infrastructure	Water, energy, fuel processing, defence and transport services
D. Natural capital	Water provisioning, timber, agricultural biomass, carbon, soil and ecosystems
E. Strategic resilience	Import substitution, supply security, replacement costs and critical infrastructure dependencies

For each item, we would record:

* Its annual monetary value, where measurable.
* Who owns it.
* Who receives the economic benefit.
* Who bears the environmental and social costs.
* Whether it is replaceable elsewhere in the UK.
* The cost and timescale of replacement.
* Whether the value is already captured in another column, to avoid double-counting.

The final result should have two totals: a conventional economic and fiscal account, and a wider strategic and natural-capital account. We should not add asset values to annual flows or count the same benefit twice.

My view is that this is the right analytical exercise. Not because it would necessarily prove Wales is a net fiscal contributor, but because it would test whether the familiar fiscal-deficit figure is being used to answer a much bigger question than it was designed to answer.

And that is particularly relevant to the independence debate: the financial relationship between Wales and the UK is not just a question of how much public money is spent in Wales. It is also a question of what the UK economy receives from Welsh territory, infrastructure, productive capacity and natural resources.”.  

“Yes. I think I understand the distinction you’re making now, and it is an important one. You’re talking about the economic value of having access to something, rather than simply the money that changes hands when it is used.

Those are not the same thing.

A resource can be economically valuable precisely because it is available, reliable, geographically well-positioned and underpinned by infrastructure — even when it isn’t being used to its maximum capacity.

I would bring three concepts into this.

1. The value of availability

Take Welsh water.

The conventional calculation might look at:

* How many litres are transferred.
* What the water costs to supply.
* What revenue is generated.
* What infrastructure and maintenance cost.

But that misses some of the value.

There is also:

* The cost of constructing an alternative supply.
* The cost of operating that alternative.
* The value of having a reliable supply during drought.
* The economic disruption avoided by preventing shortages.
* The value of having a geographically independent source of supply.

Economists sometimes describe this through option value and insurance value.

You are not just paying for water consumed today. You are benefiting from having a dependable supply available when other sources are constrained.

2. The value of strategic redundancy

The same principle applies to fuel infrastructure and defence.

Consider Milford Haven.

Its strategic value is not simply the annual volume of LNG passing through the terminals. It is also the fact that the UK has:

* A major additional point of entry for gas.
* Deep-water port infrastructure.
* Storage and regasification capacity.
* Connections to the national transmission network.
* An alternative route for international energy supplies.

That has a resilience value which becomes particularly apparent during disruption.

Likewise, military training land in Wales is not valuable only according to how many soldiers train there in a particular year. It also provides geographical and operational capacity that would be expensive and time-consuming to reproduce.

A facility can have considerable strategic value even when its utilisation rate is relatively low.

This is the distinction between productive value and insurance value.

3. The value of having a strategic reserve

I think this is the concept you’re getting closest to.

A strategic reserve is valuable because it gives a country options.

Consider:

Welsh resource	Immediate economic value	Additional reserve or insurance value
Water	Supply to English cities	Drought resilience and alternative supply
LNG infrastructure	Gas imports and distribution	Energy security and supply-route diversification
Oil refinery	Petroleum products	Domestic refining capacity during international disruption
Military training areas	Training activity	Operational capacity and geographical flexibility
Agricultural land	Food production	Domestic food resilience and productive capacity
Forestry	Timber and wood products	Carbon storage, future timber supply and catchment protection
Renewable energy	Electricity generation	Reduced exposure to imported fuels

The right-hand column is not necessarily visible in ordinary regional GDP or fiscal accounts.

And importantly, these benefits can be non-linear. A water supply might be worth a relatively modest amount in an ordinary year, but extraordinarily valuable during a severe drought.

The same applies to energy infrastructure during an international supply crisis.

4. There is an established economic framework for this

We don’t have to invent an entirely new accounting discipline.

Several existing approaches are relevant:

* Natural capital accounting: values the stock of natural assets and the services they provide.
* Real options analysis: values the ability to retain choices and respond to future circumstances.
* Resilience economics: examines the economic costs avoided through preparedness and redundancy.
* Contingent valuation: estimates willingness to pay for benefits that do not have ordinary market prices.
* Replacement-cost analysis: estimates what it would cost to reproduce a resource or service elsewhere.

The Treasury Green Book and its supplementary guidance already recognise many of these approaches. But they are normally applied to individual investment or policy decisions, not assembled into a comprehensive account of Wales’s strategic contribution to the UK.

There is an important methodological warning: replacement cost is not automatically the same as economic value. We would need to establish that the alternative actually provides an equivalent service, and account for the probability and consequences of disruption.

5. Cultural stock is an interesting parallel

Your comparison with cultural value is useful.

A language, cultural tradition, historic landscape or body of local knowledge has value that cannot be adequately represented by the annual income it generates.

Yet it can support:

* Social cohesion.
* Institutional continuity.
* Tourism and creative industries.
* Intergenerational knowledge.
* A sense of belonging and identity.
* The ability of communities to sustain themselves.

Some of those benefits can be measured. Others resist meaningful monetisation.

The same is true of strategic resources. Their value is partly in the economic activity they generate, and partly in the options they preserve for future generations.

I would not force every value into pounds per head. Sometimes the honest result is a quantified economic value alongside a clearly documented strategic value.

6. I would revise our proposed Welsh account

Rather than simply adding more economic sectors to a spreadsheet, I would give the analysis two dimensions.

Welsh Strategic Contribution Account

Dimension A

Economic flows

* Tax and public revenue.
* Production and GVA.
* Exports and supply chains.
* Employment and investment.
* Direct infrastructure revenue.

Dimension B

Strategic stocks and capabilities

* Natural resources and land.
* Critical infrastructure.
* Reserve and surge capacity.
* Supply-chain resilience.
* Geographic and military significance.
* Environmental and cultural assets.
* Future development options.

Then assess the relationship between them:

* What would the UK lose if access were interrupted?
* What would it cost to replace the capability?
* How long would replacement take?
* What alternative would be available during the transition?
* What economic damage could disruption cause?
* What value does continued access provide even without disruption?

I would also add a third consideration: who bears the cost of maintaining the reserve?

For example, Welsh communities may experience the environmental, land-use and infrastructure consequences of hosting assets whose strategic benefits accrue across the UK.

That is a question of distribution, not just total economic value.

7. The bigger constitutional implication

I think this changes the question we should be asking about Wales’s fiscal relationship with the UK.

Instead of:

Does Wales generate enough tax revenue to pay for the public expenditure allocated to it?

We should also ask:

What is the total economic, strategic and resilience value that the UK derives from having Wales within its economic and territorial system, and what would it cost to reproduce those benefits under a different arrangement?

That second question is much harder to answer, but it is not an unreasonable question. It is particularly relevant when assessing the economic consequences of constitutional separation.

One final distinction: the value of a resource to the UK is not automatically the same as the financial return received by Wales or its population. A resource can be highly valuable to the Union while the region hosting it receives a relatively small proportion of its wider benefits.

That is arguably one of the most important questions in the entire analysis: not just what Wales contributes, but how the value of that contribution is distributed.

And I think we should preserve your strategic-reserve point as a central analytical principle rather than burying it inside a miscellaneous category of economic benefits.”.  

“Absolutely. I think the best way to do this is to create a comprehensive discussion inventory, rather than jump straight into a polished argument.

I’ll preserve the progression of our conversation, the questions you raised, the distinctions we developed and the claims that still need checking. I’ll also distinguish between things we established as facts, analytical propositions, and hypotheses that need further evidence.

This is deliberately a working document, not yet a verified account. Some of the figures and claims we discussed may need correction, qualification or replacement during the fact-checking stage.

Wales: Constitutional Sovereignty, Strategic Resources and Economic Interdependence

Comprehensive discussion inventory — 24 November 2026

1. Welsh sovereignty and potential EU membership

1.1 The possibility of an independent Wales joining the EU

* An independent Wales would be eligible to apply for EU membership as a sovereign European state.
* The relevant legal route is Article 49 of the Treaty on European Union.
* Independence and EU membership would be two separate constitutional processes.
* Welsh independence would not automatically restore EU membership.
* Wales would need to apply and satisfy the accession requirements.
* The application would require:
    * Assessment by the European Commission.
    * Agreement among EU member states to proceed.
    * Candidate status and accession negotiations.
    * Adoption and implementation of the EU acquis (the body of EU law).
    * European Parliament consent.
    * Unanimous approval by member states.
    * Ratification of an accession treaty by all relevant parties.

1.2 Potential advantages for Wales

* Wales previously participated in the EU as part of the United Kingdom.
* Welsh institutions, businesses, universities and regulators have considerable experience of EU-derived law and regulation.
* Wales would not be starting entirely from scratch in terms of regulatory familiarity.
* Existing administrative and economic relationships with European institutions could provide useful foundations.
* Some institutional and regulatory preparation could potentially happen before independence.

1.3 Potential complications

* Brexit means Wales is no longer part of the EU single market or customs union.
* Some UK regulatory arrangements have diverged from EU requirements.
* An independent Wales would need to establish or confirm sovereign-state institutions.
* It would need to negotiate:
    * Financial contributions.
    * Representation in EU institutions.
    * Transitional arrangements.
    * Border arrangements.
    * Regulatory alignment.
    * Application and enforcement of EU law.

1.4 Accession timetable

We discussed illustrative scenarios, rather than established predictions:

* Around 2–4 years after independence under unusually favourable circumstances.
* Around 4–7 years if negotiations and institutional preparations took longer.
* Eight to ten years or longer if political disagreements or accession obstacles intervened.

Important qualifications:

* There is no automatic timetable.
* There is no direct precedent for a territory becoming independent from a former EU member state after that member state has left the EU, then applying separately.
* Preparatory discussions before independence might reduce delays.
* Preparatory discussions would not constitute EU membership or guarantee admission.

1.5 Welsh political positions

* Plaid Cymru’s constitutional objective includes Welsh independence in Europe.
* The Welsh Government’s stated preference, as discussed, was for Wales and the UK as a whole to rejoin the EU.
* The Welsh Government also supported closer European economic relations as an interim objective.
* These are different political routes:
    * An independent Wales joining the EU.
    * Wales remaining within the UK while the UK seeks to rejoin.
* They should not be conflated.

2. Wales’s strategic military importance

2.1 RAF Valley

* RAF Valley is situated on Anglesey.
* It is an important RAF training establishment.
* It supports fast-jet pilot training and associated aviation activities.
* It is not interchangeable with an Army training area.
* If Wales became independent, RAF Valley would be within Welsh sovereign territory.
* Continued UK use would require an agreed legal arrangement.
* Possible arrangements include:
    * Long-term lease.
    * Defence cooperation treaty.
    * Joint operation.
    * Restricted training access.
    * Relocation of UK operations.

2.2 Sennybridge and the Brecon area

* Sennybridge Training Area is a major military training estate near Brecon.
* It provides:
    * Upland training terrain.
    * Live-fire facilities.
    * Artillery and infantry training opportunities.
    * Built-up training environments.
    * Accommodation and supporting infrastructure.
* It is not simply a convenient piece of open countryside.
* The Army has other training areas elsewhere in Great Britain, including Salisbury Plain and Otterburn.
* Some training could potentially be redistributed.
* However, replacing Sennybridge’s combination of terrain, infrastructure and range capacity would require:
    * Additional investment.
    * Scheduling changes.
    * Alternative land.
    * Environmental and planning permissions.
    * Potentially greater transport and operating costs.

2.3 Sovereignty and defence access

* Existing UK Ministry of Defence ownership would not automatically make Welsh territory sovereign British territory after independence.
* Ownership of land and the right to conduct military operations are separate questions.
* The independence settlement would need to address:
    * Military property.
    * Existing leases.
    * Access rights.
    * Training arrangements.
    * Environmental liabilities.
    * Employment.
    * Defence cooperation.

2.4 Strategic bargaining implications

* Wales could have meaningful negotiating interests because the UK benefits from facilities situated in Welsh territory.
* The UK would have an interest in preserving access where facilities are operationally valuable.
* Wales might also benefit from continued UK military activity through:
    * Employment.
    * Investment.
    * Local economic activity.
    * Training cooperation.
    * Defence relationships.
* Independence would not necessarily mean excluding British forces.
* Continued access would be a matter for negotiation rather than automatic entitlement.

3. Wales’s water resources and their importance to England

3.1 Major water-transfer systems

We identified two major systems:

- Elan Valley reservoirs and the Elan Aqueduct.

* Supply Birmingham and parts of the Midlands.
* Stated transfer capacity of more than 320 million litres per day.

- Lake Vyrnwy and the River Dee system.

* Supply Liverpool, Cheshire and parts of Greater Manchester.
* Stated transfer capacity of up to 205 million litres per day.

- Combined stated capacity:

* More than 525 million litres per day.

Important distinction:

* This is the stated capacity of the systems, not necessarily their average daily delivery.
* We must not treat maximum transfer capacity and actual consumption as interchangeable figures.

3.2 Scale relative to Wales

* We compared the potential transfer capacity with Welsh public water supply.
* The combined transfer capacity is substantial relative to Welsh domestic water consumption.
* However, the comparison needs careful qualification because it compares infrastructure capacity with actual supply.

3.3 Importance to England

* Welsh water is particularly important to certain English population centres.
* Birmingham and parts of the Midlands have a long-established relationship with Elan Valley water.
* Liverpool and surrounding areas benefit from the Vyrnwy and Dee systems.
* National percentages can obscure local dependence.
* Water cannot be treated as a completely interchangeable commodity because:
    * Location matters.
    * Infrastructure matters.
    * Water treatment matters.
    * Pipeline capacity matters.
    * Environmental conditions matter.

3.4 Alternatives available to England

Potential alternatives include:

* New reservoirs.
* Water transfers within England.
* Leakage reduction.
* Water recycling.
* Desalination.
* Demand reduction.
* New treatment and distribution infrastructure.

However:

* These alternatives require investment.
* Some require long planning and construction periods.
* Environmental constraints may apply.
* Water availability is geographically uneven.
* A surplus in one region does not automatically resolve shortages elsewhere.

3.5 The Severn–Thames Transfer

* We discussed the proposed Severn–Thames Transfer.
* It illustrates England’s interest in moving water from the Severn towards the Thames.
* Proposed arrangements include a mixture of existing water abstraction, water redeployment, treated wastewater and new infrastructure.
* One proposed element continues to involve Lake Vyrnwy-related water arrangements.
* This complicates the assumption that English alternatives would necessarily eliminate dependence on Welsh water.

3.6 Independence and water sovereignty

* Under independence, reservoirs, rivers and infrastructure within Wales would be subject to Welsh sovereignty and jurisdiction.
* Existing ownership and contractual arrangements would still need to be considered.
* England would not automatically retain an unrestricted right to extract water.
* Cross-border water management would require agreements.
* Agreements could address:
    * Abstraction.
    * River flows.
    * Drought conditions.
    * Environmental standards.
    * Infrastructure access.
    * Payment.
    * Maintenance.
    * Dispute resolution.

3.7 Water as a strategic reserve

We subsequently developed a more important distinction:

* Water’s value is not limited to the amount consumed.
* Its availability during drought has additional value.
* Reliable supply reduces economic disruption.
* Alternative supply routes provide resilience.
* Catchment management protects long-term water quality and availability.
* The value of a dependable water source may increase substantially during periods of scarcity.

4. Welsh agriculture and food security

4.1 Livestock farming

We identified livestock as a particularly important part of Welsh economic and strategic capacity.

The figures discussed from the 2025 Welsh agricultural survey included:

* Approximately 8.8 million sheep and lambs.
* Approximately 1.08 million cattle and calves.
* A substantial proportion of the UK’s sheep population.
* A significant contribution to the UK’s cattle population.

The approximate comparative proportions discussed were:

* Around 29% of the UK’s sheep and lamb population.
* Around 12% of the UK’s cattle and calf population.

These comparisons need checking against consistent survey dates and methodologies.

4.2 Agricultural geography

* More than 90% of Welsh land was described as being used for agricultural purposes, including common grazing.
* Much of Welsh agricultural land is permanent grassland or rough grazing.
* A relatively small proportion is used for cereals and conventional arable production.
* Much of Wales’s geography favours grass-based livestock production.
* Replacing livestock production with arable production would not be straightforward across much of the same land.

4.3 Agricultural output

The provisional 2025 figures discussed included:

Category	Estimated output value
Cattle	£515m
Sheep	£344m
Milk and milk products	£929m
Poultry	£67m
Eggs	£131m
Cereals	£40m
Horticulture	£69m

* Agricultural output was estimated at approximately £2.4 billion.
* Cattle and sheep together accounted for approximately 36% of agricultural output.
* Dairy was another particularly important component.

4.4 Wider food economy

Agriculture supports a wider network:

* Livestock markets.
* Abattoirs.
* Meat processing.
* Dairy processing.
* Cheese production.
* Cold-chain distribution.
* Veterinary services.
* Feed production and supply.
* Agricultural machinery.
* Fencing.
* Transport and logistics.
* Rural employment.

The wider Welsh food and drink economy was discussed as being worth more than £9.3 billion, using a broader industry estimate.

4.5 Strategic food-security value

* Wales contributes to domestic meat and dairy production.
* The UK has significant livestock and dairy production dependencies on Welsh farms.
* Replacing Welsh output could involve:
    * More production elsewhere in Britain.
    * Increased imports.
    * Changes in consumption.
    * Investment in alternative farming systems.
* These alternatives have costs and implications for:
    * Food security.
    * Import dependence.
    * Rural employment.
    * Land use.
    * Processing capacity.
    * Supply-chain resilience.

4.6 Agricultural trade and EU relationships

* Welsh agriculture has an established export relationship with European markets.
* Beef, lamb and dairy are particularly important.
* Brexit-related sanitary and phytosanitary requirements affect trade costs.
* EU membership could change market access and regulatory conditions.
* An independent Wales would need to negotiate agricultural trade arrangements and align with EU agricultural requirements if seeking membership.

4.7 Agriculture and land stewardship

Agriculture also contributes to:

* Landscape management.
* Rural communities.
* Common land management.
* Biodiversity.
* Soil management.
* Water catchment conditions.
* Flood-risk management.
* Carbon storage.

These contributions are dependent on farming practices and cannot automatically be treated as positive in every case.

5. Welsh forestry and timber

5.1 Woodland resources

The figures discussed included:

* Approximately 313,000 hectares of woodland.
* Approximately 15% of Wales’s land area.
* Around 174,000 hectares of broadleaved woodland.
* Around 139,000 hectares of coniferous woodland.

5.2 Public woodland estate

* Natural Resources Wales manages approximately 123,000 hectares.
* This represents around 40% of Wales’s woodland resource.
* The public estate is a substantial managed asset.
* It provides both commercial timber and wider environmental services.
* It would need to be considered in any independence property settlement.

5.3 Timber production and industrial development

* Wales has a significant commercial forestry resource.
* Coniferous forestry provides much of the commercially useful softwood.
* Broadleaved woodland supports biodiversity, landscape and hardwood products.
* Welsh timber can be used for:
    * Construction.
    * Fencing.
    * Packaging.
    * Pallets.
    * Furniture.
    * Engineered timber.
    * Wood-based panels.
    * Insulation.

5.4 Import dependence

The UK imports substantial quantities of wood products.

The 2025 figures discussed included:

* 6.2 million cubic metres of sawnwood imports.
* 3.4 million cubic metres of wood-based panel imports.
* Approximately £9.1 billion in wood-product imports.

5.5 Welsh industrial opportunity

* Wales has potential to capture more value from its own timber.
* Some higher-value engineered timber products are not yet manufactured at sufficient scale in Wales.
* Examples include:
    * Cross-laminated timber.
    * Glulam structural beams.
    * Wood-fibre insulation.
    * Advanced engineered timber components.

- The Welsh Timber Industrial Strategy identifies the need to:

* Increase sustainable supply.
* Develop processing capacity.
* Improve demand certainty.
* Increase resource efficiency.
* Develop higher-value manufacturing.

5.6 Forestry and environmental security

Forests contribute to:

* Carbon storage.
* Soil stabilisation.
* Water-flow regulation.
* Sediment reduction.
* Water quality.
* Biodiversity.
* Flood-risk management.
* Landscape and recreation.

Important qualification:

* Poor forestry management can damage water quality and biodiversity.
* Species choice, road construction, harvesting and replanting matter.
* Forestry needs to be assessed at catchment level.

5.7 Forestry and agriculture

We discussed agroforestry as an opportunity to integrate trees with farming.

Potential applications:

* Shelter for livestock.
* Hedgerows and shelterbelts.
* Farm timber production.
* Riparian planting.
* Fruit and other supplementary products.
* Habitat corridors.
* Additional farm income.

5.8 Forestry and energy

* Timber residues can contribute to energy production.
* Wood chips and pellets have heating applications.
* Biomass is not automatically carbon-neutral.
* Long-lived timber products may offer greater carbon-storage benefits than burning the same material.
* Sustainable timber policy should distinguish between:
    * Material use.
    * Energy use.
    * Reuse and recycling.
    * Carbon-storage benefits.

5.9 Strategic significance

* Timber is a domestic materials resource.
* Welsh production can reduce some UK import exposure.
* Welsh processing capacity could support construction and manufacturing.
* Forests provide environmental services that cannot be reproduced by importing timber.
* Forestry is therefore both an industrial asset and a natural-capital asset.

6. Welsh fuel, energy and petroleum infrastructure

This was a significant addition to the resource discussion.

6.1 Milford Haven LNG terminals

We identified:

* South Hook LNG.
* Dragon LNG.

Their combined regasification capacity was discussed as equivalent to approximately 20–25% of UK gas demand, with a large proportion of UK LNG imports passing through Milford Haven.

The operational process:

* LNG arrives by ship.
* It is stored.
* It is regasified.
* It enters the UK gas transmission network.
* It supplies consumers, industry and gas-fired power stations.

Important distinction:

* This is imported gas handled in Wales, not gas extracted in Wales.
* The strategic value comes from infrastructure, location, storage, shipping and network connections.

6.2 Pembroke refinery

* The Valero Pembroke Refinery has a stated processing capacity of approximately 270,000 barrels of crude oil per day.
* It produces:
    * Petrol.
    * Diesel.
    * Aviation fuel.
    * Heating oil.
    * Fuel oil.
* It is a significant industrial facility.
* It depends on imported crude feedstock.
* Its contribution is refining, processing, distribution and associated industrial capacity, not necessarily Welsh-origin oil.

6.3 Welsh oil exports

* Welsh oil exports were discussed as approximately £2.8 billion in the year ending June 2026.
* Oil imports are also substantial.
* Export values must not be mistaken for Welsh domestic value added.
* The distinction between imported feedstock and finished-product exports is important.

6.4 Coal extraction

* Aberpergwm coal mine in Neath Port Talbot continues to operate.
* Its output was discussed as approximately 120,000 tonnes over four quarters of 2025.
* It produces specialised anthracite.
* It is not a major source of electricity-generation coal.
* Its significance is more specialised industrial supply than general UK electricity security.

6.5 Electricity generation

Welsh electricity generation figures discussed for 2023:

Generation	Output
Natural gas	14.5 TWh
Renewables	7.8 TWh
Total	23.2 TWh
Estimated Welsh consumption	14.8 TWh

* Wales produced more electricity than its estimated consumption in 2023.
* Gas-fired generation was particularly important.
* Pembroke and Connah’s Quay are significant gas-fired power stations.
* Wales contributed approximately 8% of UK electricity generation in 2024, according to the figure discussed.
* Generation has fluctuated, so the 2023 surplus should not be treated as a permanent annual position.

6.6 Strategic significance

Welsh energy infrastructure provides:

* International LNG entry capacity.
* Petroleum refining.
* Fuel distribution.
* Electricity generation.
* Renewable generation.
* Industrial employment.
* Supply-chain resilience.

6.7 Sovereignty and ownership

* An independent Wales would have territorial jurisdiction over these facilities.
* Commercial ownership would not automatically transfer to the Welsh Government.
* Existing property, company and contractual rights would need to be respected or negotiated.
* The UK would have an interest in maintaining access and continuity of supply.
* Potential arrangements include:
    * Commercial supply contracts.
    * Infrastructure leases.
    * Regulatory agreements.
    * Energy cooperation treaties.
    * Cross-border pipeline arrangements.

7. Wales’s conventional fiscal position

7.1 The fiscal deficit

The figures discussed from 2024–25 included:

* Welsh public revenue: approximately £41.5 billion.
* Net fiscal deficit: approximately £22.8 billion.
