* Net deficit per person: approximately £7,149.
* Public expenditure per person: approximately £20,168.
* Revenue per person: approximately £13,019.

The conventional public-finance calculation therefore shows that revenue attributed to Wales is lower than public expenditure attributed to Wales.

7.2 What this does and does not establish

It establishes:

- Wales has a substantial allocated public-finance deficit under the conventional regional accounting methodology.

It does not establish:

* That Wales is economically unproductive.
* That Welsh resources are a net cost to the UK.
* That the UK could reproduce the same economic relationship without Wales.
* That Welsh natural resources and strategic infrastructure have no wider economic value.
* That the fiscal relationship would necessarily continue unchanged under independence.

7.3 GDP and economic production

Figures discussed included:

* Welsh GDP in 2024: approximately £100.3 billion.
* Welsh GVA: approximately £87.4 billion.
* Goods exports in the year to June 2026: approximately £20 billion.
* Wales generated around 3.3% of UK GVA in 2023.

These figures demonstrate substantial economic production and trade, but do not by themselves establish net fiscal contribution.

7.4 Important accounting distinction

We identified three different accounting systems:

Public-finance accounts

* Tax receipts.
* Public expenditure.
* Fiscal balance.

National economic accounts

* GDP.
* GVA.
* Employment.
* Exports.
* Investment.
* Production.

Natural-capital and economic appraisal

* Water provisioning.
* Timber.
* Agricultural biomass.
* Carbon storage.
* Biodiversity.
* Soil and landscape.
* Environmental services.

These systems answer different questions.

8. Natural capital and the difficulty of valuing Wales’s contribution

8.1 Existing natural-capital accounting

We discussed:

* The Treasury Green Book.
* ENCA guidance.
* ONS natural-capital accounts.
* Welsh Government natural-capital assessments.

These recognise that natural assets provide economic and social services that are not necessarily traded in conventional markets.

8.2 Welsh natural-capital estimates

A Welsh Government assessment discussed:

* Approximately £2.5 billion in annual ecosystem-service value in 2022.
* An estimated natural-asset value of approximately £95.7 billion.

Illustrative annual values included:

Natural-capital service	Estimated value
Agricultural biomass	£522m
Water provisioning	£272m
Greenhouse-gas regulation	£239m
Timber provisioning	£52m
Renewable electricity provisioning	£40m
Wood fuel provisioning	£12.5m

Important qualifications:

* These are estimated service values, not cash receipts.
* They do not represent all strategic benefits.
* They do not necessarily measure the value received outside Wales.
* Some environmental services remain incompletely measured.
* They must not be added indiscriminately to GDP or fiscal figures.

8.3 The difference between market value and strategic value

We developed a central distinction:

* The market value of a resource is not the same as its total economic value.
* The value of having access to a resource can exceed the revenue generated by its actual use.
* A resource can provide insurance and resilience benefits.
* Strategic capacity can be valuable even when utilisation is relatively low.

9. Strategic reserve, redundancy and option value

This became one of the central analytical themes of the conversation.

9.1 The value of availability

* Water supply is valuable not only because of daily consumption, but because it remains available during drought.
* LNG infrastructure is valuable not only because of annual gas throughput, but because it provides an additional route for international energy supply.
* Military training land is valuable not only because of annual usage, but because it preserves operational capacity.
* Agricultural land is valuable not only because of current output, but because it preserves future productive capacity.
* Forests are valuable not only because of timber production, but because of their carbon, water and biodiversity services.

9.2 Strategic redundancy

We distinguished productive value from insurance value.

Productive value

- The resource is being used to produce goods or services.

Insurance value

- The resource provides an alternative capability when other systems are disrupted.

Option value

- Retaining the ability to use a resource or develop it in future.

9.3 Non-linear value

* A resource’s value can change dramatically during disruption.
* Water may have ordinary value during a wet year but very high value during drought.
* Energy infrastructure may become particularly valuable during international supply disruption.
* Military facilities may have high strategic value even when utilisation is not continuous.
* Domestic food production can become particularly valuable during international supply-chain disruption.

9.4 Replacement cost

We identified the importance of asking:

* What would it cost to replace a Welsh resource elsewhere?
* Could an equivalent resource actually be created?
* How long would it take?
* What alternative would be available during the transition?
* What disruption would occur?
* Would the replacement provide equivalent resilience?

Important qualification:

* Replacement cost is not automatically equal to economic value.
* An alternative must provide a genuinely comparable service.
* The probability and consequences of disruption must be considered.

9.5 Existing economic frameworks

Potential analytical methods include:

* Natural-capital accounting.
* Real-options analysis.
* Resilience economics.
* Contingent valuation.
* Replacement-cost analysis.
* Cost-benefit analysis.
* Scenario analysis.

10. Cultural value and strategic stock

You introduced an important comparison with cultural value and cultural stock.

The argument developed was:

* Some assets have value beyond the income they generate.
* Cultural identity, language, historic landscapes and inherited knowledge cannot be fully represented by annual economic output.
* They can nevertheless support:
    * Social cohesion.
    * Institutional continuity.
    * Tourism.
    * Creative industries.
    * Intergenerational knowledge.
    * Community resilience.
    * A sense of belonging.

The same general problem applies to strategic resources.

* Some benefits can be quantified.
* Others can be documented but not reliably monetised.
* It would be misleading to force every benefit into a single price per head.
* A responsible assessment should present monetary values alongside non-monetary strategic assessments.

11. The distribution of benefits and costs

An important issue emerged towards the end of the discussion.

The value of a resource to the UK is not automatically the same as the financial return received by Wales or its population.

Examples:

* Welsh water supplies benefit English cities.
* Welsh energy infrastructure supports the wider UK.
* Military training facilities serve UK defence requirements.
* Welsh agricultural production feeds wider markets.
* Welsh forests provide environmental services beyond their immediate location.

Questions raised:

* Who owns the assets?
* Who receives the revenue?
* Who pays for maintenance?
* Who bears environmental costs?
* Who receives local employment and investment?
* Who controls expansion or closure?
* Who has decision-making authority?
* How are benefits shared?

This is a distributional question, not simply an aggregate economic question.

12. Wales’s strategic contribution: consolidated categories

We developed a six-part framework.

Category	Welsh contribution
Food security	Livestock, dairy, poultry, agricultural land and food processing
Water security	Reservoirs, rivers, catchments and water transfers
Materials security	Timber, wood fibre, forestry and industrial minerals
Energy security	LNG, refining, gas generation, renewable generation and fuel infrastructure
Defence and security	Training areas, airfields and strategically useful geography
Environmental security	Carbon storage, biodiversity, soil, forests and landscape

The categories overlap:

* Forestry supports water security.
* Agriculture depends on water and land management.
* Renewable energy uses land and infrastructure.
* Energy infrastructure supports industrial production.
* Environmental management supports agriculture and water quality.
* Defence facilities contribute to local economies.

The cumulative nature of these assets matters.

No single resource necessarily gives Wales decisive bargaining power, but together they create substantial mutual dependence.

13. The broader constitutional argument

13.1 The conventional fiscal question is incomplete

The conventional question is:

- Does Wales generate enough tax revenue to cover the public expenditure allocated to it?

The broader question is:

- What is the total economic, strategic and resilience value that the UK derives from Welsh territory, infrastructure, productive capacity and natural resources?

These are not interchangeable questions.

13.2 The value of Welsh territory to the UK

The UK benefits from:

* Water infrastructure.
* Energy entry and processing infrastructure.
* Petroleum refining.
* Electricity generation.
* Agricultural production.
* Timber production.
* Military facilities.
* Environmental services.
* Transport geography.
* Industrial and commercial supply chains.

13.3 Independence would change the negotiating relationship

An independent Wales would not simply be a smaller state seeking financial support.

It would also be a neighbouring state with:

* Productive capacity.
* Strategic infrastructure.
* Natural resources.
* Geographical assets.
* Export potential.
* Water and energy relationships.
* Existing industrial supply chains.

The UK would have reasons to maintain access and cooperation.

13.4 This does not imply a zero-sum relationship

* Wales could benefit from continued trade with England.
* England could benefit from continued access to Welsh resources.
* Defence, energy, water and environmental agreements could be mutually beneficial.
* Independence would not necessarily require economic separation.
* The relationship would need to be negotiated on a new constitutional basis.

14. The proposed research framework

We ended by developing the idea of a Welsh Strategic and Economic Contribution Account.

I would retain this as the principal proposed output of the next stage.

Dimension A — Economic flows

* Tax revenue.
* Public expenditure.
* Production and GVA.
* Exports.
* Employment.
* Investment.
* Industrial profits.
* Supply-chain value.
* Infrastructure revenue.

Dimension B — Strategic stocks and capabilities

* Natural resources.
* Land.
* Water.
* Forestry.
* Agricultural capacity.
* Energy infrastructure.
* Fuel-processing capacity.
* Military infrastructure.
* Transport geography.
* Environmental assets.
* Cultural assets.
* Future development options.

Dimension C — Resilience and insurance value

* Reserve capacity.
* Alternative supply routes.
* Import substitution.
* Strategic redundancy.
* Drought resilience.
* Energy-security resilience.
* Food-security resilience.
* Military operational flexibility.
* Replacement costs.
* Disruption avoided.

Dimension D — Distribution

* Who owns each asset?
* Who controls it?
* Who receives the direct revenue?
* Who benefits from its services?
* Who pays for maintenance?
* Who bears environmental costs?
* Who has the authority to make decisions?

Dimension E — Sovereignty scenarios

Assess each resource under different constitutional arrangements:

* Wales remains within the UK with existing powers.
* Wales receives further devolution.
* Wales becomes independent but maintains close UK cooperation.
* Wales becomes independent and joins the EU.
* Wales becomes independent with different degrees of UK and EU integration.

For each scenario, consider:

* Legal authority.
* Ownership.
* Access.
* Revenue.
* Regulation.
* Trade.
* Infrastructure.
* Replacement.
* Strategic resilience.

15. Claims and questions requiring fact-checking

This is the most important section before we turn the discussion into a formal argument.

I would not treat every figure or interpretation above as established fact.

The following need checking.

A. EU membership

* Article 49 accession process and precise requirements.
* Whether the estimated 2–4, 4–7 and 8–10-year scenarios are defensible as illustrations.
* The lack of a direct precedent for Welsh accession following UK withdrawal.

B. Defence

* Current ownership and precise area of Sennybridge.
* Current functions and capabilities of RAF Valley.
* Which other British training areas could substitute for Sennybridge.
* Actual replacement costs and timescales.
* Ownership and legal status of MOD property under a hypothetical independence settlement.

C. Water

* Actual average water transfers, not just maximum capacity.
* Current dependency of Birmingham and the Midlands on Elan Valley.
* Current dependency of North West England on Vyrnwy and Dee supplies.
* The precise relationship between the Severn–Thames Transfer and Welsh water sources.
* Existing cross-border legal and regulatory arrangements.
* What international water law would apply following independence.

D. Agriculture

* The latest sheep and cattle populations.
* Whether the comparative UK percentages use consistent data.
* Agricultural output values and methodology.
* The value of Welsh food and drink industry output.
* Welsh contributions to UK food security.
* Import substitution and the limits of replacement elsewhere.

E. Forestry

* Current woodland area.
* Public estate area and management responsibilities.
* Annual timber production and actual marketed volume.
* Timber import figures.
* Welsh processing capacity.
* Ecosystem-service valuations.
* The contribution of woodland to catchment and flood management.

F. Fuel and energy

* Current LNG terminal capacity and throughput.
* The proportion of UK LNG imports handled at Milford Haven.
* Pembroke refinery’s actual capacity and production.
* The composition of Welsh oil exports.
* Current Aberpergwm coal output.
* Current Welsh electricity generation and consumption.
* The extent to which Welsh electricity is exported or supports the wider grid.

G. Fiscal and economic accounts

* The £22.8 billion Welsh fiscal deficit.
* The £41.5 billion public revenue figure.
* GDP and GVA figures.
* Export figures.
* The precise ONS regional public-finance methodology.
* Whether the regional deficit is being interpreted correctly.

H. Natural capital and strategic value

* The £2.5 billion annual ecosystem-service estimate.
* The £95.7 billion natural-asset valuation.
* The underlying assumptions and valuation methods.
* What is already included in GDP or other accounts.
* Which benefits accrue outside Wales.
* Which benefits are not currently monetised.

I. Strategic reserve and resilience

* Whether the proposed framework properly distinguishes:
    * Productive value.
    * Insurance value.
    * Option value.
    * Replacement cost.
    * Non-market value.
* How to avoid double-counting.
* How to value assets whose significance increases during disruption.
* How to compare different sovereignty scenarios.

16. The central thesis emerging from our discussion

I would record the argument in this form, without yet treating it as a proven conclusion:

Assessing Wales’s economic contribution to the United Kingdom solely through conventional regional public-finance accounts risks overlooking the wider value of Welsh productive capacity, natural resources, critical infrastructure, strategic geography and environmental services.Some of these contributions generate direct revenue and measurable economic output. Others provide insurance, redundancy, resilience and future options. Their value may be particularly significant during periods of scarcity, disruption or geopolitical instability.The economic relationship between Wales and the rest of the UK therefore cannot be fully understood by comparing tax receipts with allocated public expenditure alone.A more comprehensive assessment would need to examine economic flows, strategic stocks, natural capital, replacement costs, resilience benefits and the distribution of costs and benefits between Wales and the wider UK.The resulting picture would not necessarily establish that Wales is a net fiscal contributor. It would, however, provide a much more complete basis for assessing Wales’s economic importance and the consequences of different constitutional arrangements.

17. Suggested fact-checking order

I recommend that we do not fact-check this as one enormous undifferentiated exercise.

The most useful sequence would be:

* Economic and fiscal baseline — establish the actual public-finance deficit, GDP, GVA and exports.
* Energy and fuel — verify LNG, refinery, coal and electricity figures.
* Water — establish actual transfer volumes, dependency and replacement options.
* Agriculture and food — verify production, trade and domestic supply significance.
* Forestry and materials — establish woodland, timber production and industrial potential.
* Defence — verify facilities, ownership, capabilities and replacement options.
* Natural capital and resilience — establish what is monetised, what is not, and how to avoid double-counting.
* Constitutional scenarios — assess how the economic relationships would change under different settlements.

My recommendation is to start with the economic and fiscal baseline, but not to let it dictate the conclusions of the remaining analysis. We need it as one accounting layer, not as the definition of economic value.

That gives us a structured research project rather than an argument looking for supporting numbers.”.  

“Fact-check 1: Article 49 TEU — EU accession process and requirements

Verdict: The outline we discussed was broadly correct, but it needs a few legal and procedural refinements.

Article 49 of the Treaty on European Union is the formal treaty route by which a European state can apply to become a member of the EU. It does not provide an automatic route back into membership for a newly independent state, even if that state previously participated in the EU as part of another member state.

The key point is that an independent Wales would need to apply as a state in its own right.### 1.1 What Article 49 actually says

The operative requirements are:

* The applicant must be a European state.
* It must respect the values in Article 2 TEU and be committed to promoting them.
* It submits its application to the Council of the EU.
* The European Parliament and national parliaments are notified.
* The Council acts unanimously, after:
    * Consulting the European Commission.
    * Receiving the European Parliament’s consent, given by a majority of its component members.
* The eligibility conditions agreed by the European Council must be taken into account.
* The terms of admission and the necessary adjustments to the EU treaties must be agreed between the applicant and existing member states.
* That agreement must then be ratified by every contracting state according to its own constitutional requirements. EUR-Lex +1

A useful legal distinction: Article 49 itself does not spell out every administrative stage we normally associate with accession. The candidate-status stage, screening process and detailed negotiations are part of the established enlargement procedure developed around the treaty provision.

1.2 The actual accession stages

The European Commission describes the process in the following sequence:

* Application — the European state submits its application to the Council.
* Commission opinion — the Commission assesses the application and gives its opinion.
* Candidate status — member states decide unanimously whether to grant candidate status, following the Commission’s opinion.
* Opening negotiations — member states must unanimously agree to open accession negotiations.
* Negotiating framework — the framework governing the negotiations must be agreed.
* Screening and negotiations — the Commission examines the applicant’s readiness across the relevant areas of EU law and policy.
* Assessment of readiness — once negotiations have been completed, the Commission assesses whether the country is ready to join.
* Admission decision — member states decide unanimously on admission, and the European Parliament must give its consent.
* Accession treaty — the applicant and all existing member states sign and ratify the treaty. EUR-Lex +2

A particularly important point: candidate status does not guarantee that negotiations will open, and opening negotiations does not guarantee membership. There are multiple unanimous political decisions along the way. EUR-Lex +1

1.3 The substantive membership criteria

The Copenhagen criteria are the central framework for assessing whether a candidate is ready to join.

Criterion	What it requires
Political	Stable institutions guaranteeing democracy, the rule of law, human rights, and respect for and protection of minorities
Economic	A functioning market economy capable of coping with competitive pressure and market forces within the EU
Legal and institutional	Ability to take on the obligations of membership, including the aims of political, economic and monetary union

There are also wider considerations, including the EU’s capacity to integrate new members while maintaining the momentum of European integration. European Commission +1

A candidate must also demonstrate that it can actually implement and enforce EU law. This involves more than passing legislation: administrative capacity, judicial institutions, regulatory bodies and effective enforcement all matter. Enlargement and Eastern Neighbourhood +1

1.4 What this means for Wales

We need to be precise about what we can and cannot conclude.

* Wales is geographically a European country.
* If it became an independent sovereign state, it could seek to apply under Article 49.
* Its previous experience of EU membership as part of the UK could be relevant to its administrative and regulatory preparation.
* Its institutions would nevertheless need to demonstrate that they could discharge the responsibilities of an EU member state.
* The EU institutions and existing member states would assess the application under the applicable accession requirements.
* Previous participation in the EU would not confer an automatic entitlement to membership or an exemption from the accession procedure.

One point we should leave open rather than assume: Article 49 does not provide a special procedure for a constituent nation becoming independent from a former member state. The precise treatment of a future Welsh application would therefore depend on the circumstances and political decisions at the time.

1.5 Corrections to our earlier wording

Earlier wording	Fact-check adjustment
“Assessment by the European Commission”	Correct, but distinguish the Commission’s opinion on the application from its ongoing assessment throughout negotiations.
“Candidate status and accession negotiations”	Correct, but candidate status and the opening of negotiations are separate decisions.
“Adoption and implementation of the EU acquis”	Correct, but implementation and enforcement capacity are just as important as formal adoption.
“European Parliament consent”	Correct, but there are distinct parliamentary roles at different stages.
“Unanimous approval by member states”	Correct. Unanimity is required at important decision points, not merely at the end.
“Ratification by all relevant parties”	More precisely, ratification by every EU member state and the applicant state, under their respective constitutional requirements.

1.6 Corrected wording for our master document

I would replace the original section with this:

EU accession route: An independent Wales could apply for EU membership under Article 49 of the Treaty on European Union, which permits any European state respecting and committed to promoting the values in Article 2 to apply. The application would be considered through the established EU enlargement procedure. This includes a Commission opinion, a unanimous decision on candidate status, a separate unanimous decision to open negotiations, assessment against the Copenhagen criteria, screening and negotiations on the EU acquis, and a final admission decision requiring unanimous agreement among member states and the consent of the European Parliament. An accession treaty would then need to be signed and ratified by the applicant and every existing member state according to their respective constitutional requirements. Previous participation in the EU as part of the UK could provide Wales with useful administrative and regulatory experience, but would not confer automatic membership or remove the need to satisfy the accession criteria.

Overall assessment: The original account was substantively sound. The main improvement is to make the separate unanimous decisions and the distinction between treaty requirements and established accession practice explicit.

Primary sources

* Article 49 — Treaty on European Union (EUR-Lex)⁠￼.
* EUR-Lex — Treaty on European Union: Joining the EU⁠￼.
* European Commission — Steps towards joining⁠￼.
* European Commission — Enlargement and the Copenhagen criteria⁠￼.
* European Parliament — Intergovernmental decision-making and Article 49⁠￼.

Fact-check status: Article 49 and the accession criteria — checked against primary EU institutional sources.

The timetable estimates remain unverified: they are scenarios, not legal deadlines or established predictions. We should keep them out of the factual findings until we examine the available precedents and political uncertainties.”.  

“Fact-check 2: Are the 2–4, 4–7 and 8–10-year accession scenarios defensible?

Verdict: They are defensible as broad illustrative scenarios, but not as evidence-based forecasts for Wales. The shorter end is possible only under unusually favourable conditions; the longer ranges are more consistent with the time taken by many recent applicants. There is no treaty timetable requiring the EU to complete accession within a particular number of years.

We should also specify what the clock measures. “Years after independence” is not the same as “years after an application”: Wales could do preparatory work before independence, but could not apply as an independent state until it existed as one.### 2.1 What historical examples tell us

The examples are useful, but they need to be compared carefully. They measure different things: time from application, time from independence, time in negotiations, or time to actual membership.

Country	Relevant timeline	What it tells us
Finland	Applied March 1992; joined 1 January 1995	A relatively rapid accession, approximately 2 years 9 months from application
Croatia	Applied February 2003; joined 1 July 2013	Approximately 10 years from application to membership
Montenegro	Independent in 2006; applied December 2008; candidate December 2010; negotiations opened June 2012	Independence did not lead to rapid accession; it remains a candidate in November 2026
Albania	Applied April 2009; candidate June 2014; negotiations opened July 2022	Political and institutional obstacles can create long delays

Sources: European Commission and Council records for Finland’s accession⁠￼, Croatia⁠￼, Montenegro⁠￼ and Albania⁠￼. Audiovisual Service +3

There are important differences between these cases:

* Finland was already a well-established sovereign state, with mature democratic institutions and substantial existing European economic integration. It is a useful example of how quickly accession might proceed when a country is highly prepared, but not a direct model for a newly independent Wales.
* Croatia faced the additional circumstances of post-Yugoslav state-building, war-related issues and reconstruction. Its ten-year application-to-membership period is a useful example of a longer accession.
* Montenegro is particularly relevant because it became independent and subsequently applied to join the EU. Its experience demonstrates that independence can be followed by a lengthy accession process.
* Albania illustrates how reforms and political decisions by existing member states can delay progress even after an application has been submitted.

The historical examples do not establish a reliable average for Wales. Finnish Government +3

2.2 Assessing our three scenarios

Scenario A: 2–4 years after independence

Highly optimistic

Possible in principle, but it would require an unusually favourable combination of circumstances:

* Welsh independence negotiations already completed or substantially advanced.
* A clear and internationally accepted constitutional settlement.
* Welsh institutions ready to perform all sovereign-state functions.
* Extensive continuity with EU legislation and regulatory standards.
* Strong administrative capacity to implement and enforce EU law.
* No substantial unresolved questions about borders, trade or institutional responsibilities.
* Unanimous political support among EU member states.
* A relatively uncomplicated accession negotiation.

Finland provides evidence that a rapid accession can happen, but Wales would not automatically inherit Finland’s starting conditions.

My assessment: Retain this only as an exceptional fast-track possibility, not a realistic central expectation.

Scenario B: 4–7 years after independence

Ambitious

This is more defensible as an optimistic planning scenario.

It would allow time for:

* Establishment of sovereign institutions.
* Completion of constitutional and administrative arrangements.
* EU application and assessment.
* Candidate status.
* Accession negotiations.
* Implementation of outstanding EU requirements.
* Political agreement and ratification.

However, it still assumes considerable preparation and political cooperation. It is not a timetable that we can substantiate from the historical cases.

My assessment: Retain as an ambitious scenario, explicitly conditional on extensive pre-independence preparation and favourable EU politics.

Scenario C: 8–10 years or longer after independence

More defensible as a planning scenario

This is more consistent with the experience of countries that have encountered substantial accession work or political delays.

Croatia took approximately ten years from application to membership. Montenegro demonstrates that the process can take considerably longer.

Even this scenario should not be treated as an upper limit.

My assessment: Retain, but explicitly allow for ten years or more. There is no reliable basis for assuming that ten years would be sufficient.

2.3 The important variable: what happens before independence?

This is where the Welsh case could differ significantly from some historical examples.

Wales already has:

* A devolved legislature and government.
* A substantial public administration.
* A legal and regulatory system.
* Experience administering EU-derived policy.
* Established relationships with European institutions.
* Existing businesses and institutions operating within European regulatory and commercial frameworks.

But it does not currently have all the institutions and responsibilities of an independent state.

Preparation before independence could address:

* A Welsh constitutional settlement.
* Sovereign public-finance arrangements.
* Independent regulatory and judicial capacity.
* Customs and border administration.
* Immigration and external affairs.
* EU acquis mapping.
* Public procurement and state-aid arrangements.
* Agricultural and environmental regulatory alignment.
* International treaty obligations.

This could reduce the amount of work required after independence.

However, there is a difference between preparing for accession and being legally able to apply. The application under Article 49 would be made by the independent state.

2.4 What I would change in our original document

I would replace the earlier timetable with this more defensible wording:

Indicative accession timescales: There is no fixed treaty timetable for EU accession. Historical examples demonstrate substantial variation. Finland joined approximately two years and nine months after applying, whereas Croatia took approximately ten years from application to membership. Montenegro, which became independent in 2006, applied in 2008 and opened accession negotiations in 2012, but remains outside the EU in November 2026. These examples cannot be treated as direct predictions for Wales.For planning purposes, a Welsh accession within 2–4 years of independence should be regarded as an exceptional, highly optimistic scenario. A 4–7-year period could be considered an ambitious scenario if substantial institutional and regulatory preparation had taken place before independence and political conditions were favourable. An 8–10-year or longer period is a more cautious planning scenario, although accession could take considerably longer or be delayed indefinitely. The timing would depend on Welsh preparedness, the complexity of negotiations and unanimous political agreement among existing member states.

2.5 Final assessment

Original scenario	Assessment	Recommendation
2–4 years	Possible but exceptional	Keep as fast-track illustration
4–7 years	Ambitious, conditional	Keep as optimistic planning scenario
8–10+ years	More cautious, but not a ceiling	Keep as longer planning scenario

One further correction: We should avoid describing 4–7 years as the most likely outcome. We have not established enough evidence to make that prediction. The historical record is too variable, and the Welsh constitutional starting point is unusual.

The proper conclusion is that Wales could potentially prepare for a relatively rapid application, but neither independence nor previous EU participation guarantees rapid accession.

Fact-check status: historical comparisons checked against EU institutional and national government sources. The Welsh timescales remain illustrative scenarios, not statistically derived forecasts.

My recommendation is to retain the three scenarios with the revised qualifications above, rather than invent a more precise forecast.”.  

“Fact-check 3: Is there a direct precedent for Welsh accession following UK withdrawal from the EU?

Core claim supported, with a qualification

Verdict: The statement is accurate if we define the scenario precisely. There is no established direct precedent for a devolved territory of a former EU member state becoming independent after that parent state has left the EU, and then seeking EU membership in its own right.

There are, however, several related historical examples. They are useful comparisons, but none reproduces the Welsh circumstances.

3.1 The closest comparisons

Example	What happened	Why it is not a direct precedent
Scotland (hypothetical)	Considered independence while the UK was still an EU member	Scotland did not become independent; the UK subsequently left the EU.
Algeria (1962)	Became independent from France while Algeria was within the territorial scope of the European Economic Community	Algeria’s colonial relationship with France was constitutionally and politically different. It did not become an independent EU member through accession.
Greenland (1985)	Left the European Communities while remaining part of the Kingdom of Denmark	Greenland did not become an independent sovereign state.
Czech Republic and Slovakia (1993)	Became independent following the dissolution of Czechoslovakia, subsequently applying separately for EU membership	Czechoslovakia was not an EU member state when it dissolved.
Slovenia and Croatia	Became independent following the dissolution of Yugoslavia and subsequently joined the EU	Yugoslavia was not an EU member state.
German reunification (1990)	The former East Germany became part of the Federal Republic of Germany, which was already an EEC member	This was reunification within an existing member state, not the accession of a newly independent state.

The comparisons are supported by parliamentary evidence and academic analysis of territorial separation and EU membership. Cambridge University Press +3

3.2 The particularly relevant EU Commission statement

There is an important primary-source document here.

In March 2004, the European Commission answered a parliamentary question about what would happen if part of an EU member state’s territory became independent.

Commission President Romano Prodi’s response was that:

* EU treaties apply to member states.
* If a territory ceases to be part of a member state because it becomes independent, the treaties cease to apply to that territory.
* The newly independent state would become a third country in relation to the EU.
* It could then apply for membership under Article 49.
* Admission would require agreement on the conditions of membership and treaty adjustments, followed by ratification by all member states and the applicant.

This is unusually relevant to the Welsh question. EUR-Lex

There is an important legal qualification: this is an official Commission position expressed in a parliamentary answer, not a judgment of the Court of Justice resolving every possible form of state succession.

3.3 The Scotland debate

Before Brexit, Scotland’s potential independence generated extensive legal and political discussion about whether it could:

* Remain within the EU without interruption.
* Negotiate an accession arrangement before independence.
* Apply through Article 49 after independence.
* Obtain some specially negotiated transitional arrangement.

There was no agreed, tested procedure establishing that an independent Scotland could automatically retain EU membership.

The House of Commons Library explicitly identified the absence of a precedent for a devolved part of an EU member state becoming independent and determining its own EU membership. House of Commons Library +1

The later Brexit decision changed the starting conditions. An independent Wales would not be separating from an EU member state: it would be separating from the United Kingdom after the UK’s withdrawal.

That makes the Welsh scenario different from the pre-Brexit Scottish debate.

3.4 What we can safely conclude

There are three distinct propositions:

* There is no direct precedent for the precise Welsh scenario. Supported.
* There are historical examples of territories becoming independent from other states and subsequently joining the EU. Supported, but those examples are not directly equivalent.
* The EU would necessarily refuse a special transitional or negotiated arrangement for Wales. Not established. The absence of a precedent does not prove that such an arrangement is legally or politically impossible.

The third point matters. We should not turn the absence of a precedent into an assertion that every aspect of a future Welsh accession process is already legally settled.

3.5 Corrected wording for our master document

I suggest:

Absence of a direct precedent: There is no established precedent for a devolved territory of a former EU member state becoming independent after that state’s withdrawal from the EU and subsequently seeking membership in its own right. Earlier debates about Scottish independence took place while the UK was still an EU member, and therefore involved different circumstances. Other relevant examples, including Algeria, Greenland, the dissolution of Czechoslovakia and Yugoslavia, and German reunification, differ materially in their constitutional and international-law circumstances. The European Commission stated in 2004 that a territory becoming independent from an EU member state would cease to be covered by the EU treaties and would need to apply under Article 49. That statement is relevant institutional guidance, but does not settle every possible negotiated arrangement for a future Welsh application.

Sources

* European Commission, parliamentary answer of 1 March 2004 — newly independent territories and EU membership⁠￼.
* House of Commons Library — Brexit: what happens next? (Scotland and EU membership)⁠￼.
* European Constitutional Law Review — Secession from a Member State and EU Membership: The View from the Union⁠￼.
* UK Government legal opinion — Scottish independence and international law⁠￼.
* Stephen Tierney — Legal Issues Surrounding the Referendum on Independence for Scotland⁠￼.

Fact-check status: the absence of a direct precedent is supported by parliamentary and academic sources. The exact future legal treatment of a Welsh application remains unsettled.

One distinction worth carrying into the next stages: the legal route to membership, the political willingness of EU member states to admit Wales, and the possibility of transitional arrangements are three separate questions. We should keep them separate throughout the research.”.  

“Fact-check 4: Current ownership and precise area of Sennybridge Training Area

Main figures confirmed; small discrepancies in published figures

Verdict: The earlier description was broadly correct, but we should update the figures and be more precise about the landholding arrangements.

4.1 How much land does Sennybridge cover?

The published figures are:

Landholding	Area	Status
Ministry of Defence freehold	Approximately 12,400–12,500 hectares	Owned by the MOD
Forestry land	Approximately 2,400–2,500 hectares	Leased by the MOD
Combined training area	Approximately 14,800–15,000 hectares	Freehold plus leased land

Sources include:

* An MOD publication giving 12,400 hectares freehold and 2,500 hectares leased. GOV.UK Assets
* An MOD training-area briefing giving approximately 12,500 hectares. app-cdn.sportity.com
* The Royal Commission on the Ancient and Historical Monuments of Wales (RCAHMW), which records approximately 12,000 hectares of MOD freehold and 2,500 hectares of forested land. Coflein

The differences appear to reflect rounding and differences between published descriptions. We should not claim a surveyed boundary total accurate to the nearest hectare.

4.2 Who owns the land?

There are two distinct holdings.

A. MOD freehold

* Approximately 12,400–12,500 hectares.
* Acquired by the Ministry of Defence in 1939.
* The MOD holds the freehold.
* This is the principal Mynydd Epynt training estate.

B. Leased forestry land

* Approximately 2,400–2,500 hectares.
* The MOD leases this land for training.
* The current GOV.UK description identifies the lessor as Natural Resources Wales (NRW). GOV.UK +1

This distinction matters: the MOD does not own the entire training area outright.

Natural Resources Wales is the public body responsible for managing the Welsh Government Woodland Estate. Its role as lessor should not be confused with ownership of the MOD freehold.

4.3 An important source discrepancy

There is a problem with the current GOV.UK page.

Its Sennybridge description, updated on 16 March 2026, states:

* 125,000 hectares of MOD freehold land.
* 2,400 hectares leased from NRW. GOV.UK

The 125,000-hectare figure is inconsistent with the MOD’s own other published descriptions, which put the freehold at approximately 12,400–12,500 hectares.

It is also inconsistent with the historic 12,000-hectare description from RCAHMW.

I would treat the 125,000-hectare figure as an apparent tenfold transcription error, not as a genuine change in the training area’s size.

This is worth recording in our research notes rather than silently reproducing it.

4.4 What is the land used for?

The area is not simply an open military estate.

It contains:

* A controlled impact area.
* Live-fire facilities.
* Dry training areas.
* Artillery training facilities.
* Infantry training facilities.
* Woodland.
* Moorland and upland habitats.
* Archaeological sites.
* Areas of communal grazing.

The MOD’s published description also identifies approximately 1,500 hectares of woodland within the training area. GOV.UK Assets

RCAHMW describes the wider area as a major live-fire training landscape on Mynydd Epynt. Coflein

4.5 What we can say about ownership in a Welsh independence scenario

This is where we need to be careful not to jump from current ownership to a hypothetical constitutional outcome.

Established facts:

* The MOD owns the freehold of the principal estate.
* The MOD leases additional forestry land.
* Both holdings are geographically within Wales.
* The MOD’s landholding does not make the territory sovereign British territory.

Questions that would need to be addressed in an independence settlement:

* Would the MOD retain its freehold?
* Would the freehold be transferred, purchased or otherwise dealt with under an agreed settlement?
* Would existing leases continue?
* Would NRW continue as landlord of the forestry estate?
* Would the UK retain training rights under a separate defence agreement?
* What planning, environmental and operational jurisdiction would apply?

We cannot assume that independence automatically transfers the MOD’s property to Wales. Equally, we cannot assume that British ownership automatically guarantees continuing military access.

4.6 Corrected wording for our master document

Sennybridge Training Area: The training area on Mynydd Epynt covers approximately 14,800–15,000 hectares, comprising around 12,400–12,500 hectares of Ministry of Defence freehold land and approximately 2,400–2,500 hectares of additional forestry land leased by the MOD, currently identified by GOV.UK as land leased from Natural Resources Wales. The principal estate was acquired for military use in 1939. Published figures vary slightly, and a GOV.UK page updated in March 2026 appears to contain a tenfold error in its freehold-area figure, stating 125,000 rather than approximately 12,500 hectares. The MOD’s freehold ownership and its leasehold rights must be distinguished from sovereignty over the territory. Any future Welsh independence settlement would need to address property, leases, access and defence-use arrangements separately.

Sources

* MOD — Access Opportunities on the Defence Estate: Sennybridge⁠￼.
* GOV.UK — Wales: public access to military areas (updated March 2026)⁠￼.
* RCAHMW — Sennybridge Military Training Area site record⁠￼.
* MOD — Sennybridge Training Area briefing notes⁠￼.

Fact-check status: area and broad ownership split established from published MOD and Welsh public-body material. Exact current title boundaries and individual lease terms have not been independently verified against Land Registry records.

Research note: The apparent 125,000-hectare error is itself worth retaining in the audit trail. It illustrates why we should compare official sources rather than assume that the latest webpage necessarily contains the most accurate figure.”.  

“Fact-check 5: Current functions and capabilities of RAF Valley

Scope: RAF Valley, Anglesey, Wales, as at November 2026.

The key distinction is that RAF Valley is not simply an airfield where fast jets are based. It is a specialist military flying-training station, with a training system, aircraft, instructors, simulators, engineering support and access to designated training airspace.

I’ll separate its established functions from the wider strategic significance we might infer from them.### 5.1 Principal function: No. 4 Flying Training School

RAF Valley is home to No. 4 Flying Training School (4 FTS). Its principal role is training RAF and Royal Navy pilots for the fast-jet stream. Royal Air Force +1

The training pipeline has several distinct elements:

Basic fast-jet training — Texan T1

* Operated by 72 Squadron.
* Develops basic flying skills for students selected for fast-jet training.
* Prepares pilots for advanced jet training on the Hawk.
    Royal Air Force +1

Advanced fast-jet training — Hawk T2

* Operated by IV and XXV(F) Squadrons.
* Provides advanced jet-flying instruction.
* Includes tactical and weapons training.
* Prepares pilots to progress to operational aircraft such as Typhoon and F-35.
    Royal Air Force +2

The RAF’s own description identifies the Hawk T2 as an advanced trainer with synthetic radar and electronic-warfare simulation capabilities. It is a training aircraft, not an operational fighter squadron in its own right. Royal Air Force

5.2 Helicopter and maritime/mountain aircrew training

RAF Valley also has an important rotary-wing training role.

* 202 Squadron is based at RAF Valley.
* It provides mountain, maritime and search-and-rescue training for helicopter aircrew.
* It operates:
    * Juno HT1 (Airbus H135).
    * Jupiter HT1 (Airbus H145).
* Training prepares crews for operations in demanding environments, including mountain and maritime conditions.
* The aircraft and training system support RAF, Royal Navy and Army aircrew training through the wider UK Military Flying Training System. Royal Air Force +2

This is an important additional capability. RAF Valley is not exclusively a fixed-wing fast-jet training establishment.

5.3 RAF Mountain Rescue Service

RAF Valley is also home to the headquarters of the RAF Mountain Rescue Service.

Its responsibilities include:

* High-readiness search and rescue.
* Aircraft post-crash and incident management.
* Deployment in difficult terrain and adverse weather.
* Support to military aviation operations.
* Assistance to civilian emergency services when required.

There are three RAF Mountain Rescue teams, based at:

* RAF Valley.
* RAF Leeming.
* RAF Lossiemouth.

The headquarters at Valley coordinates and allocates taskings across the teams. Royal Air Force +2

Important distinction: this is principally a ground-based military mountain-rescue and aircraft-incident response capability. It should not be confused with the former RAF helicopter search-and-rescue stations that operated dedicated rescue helicopters.

5.4 Training infrastructure and technological capability

RAF Valley’s importance is not limited to the aircraft themselves.

* It has specialist training infrastructure for the Texan and Hawk pipelines.
* A £175 million MOD investment in basic flying training included expanded facilities, additional training capacity and simulator infrastructure.
* A 2023 building extension provided additional classrooms, briefing facilities and simulator accommodation.
* In April 2026, the RAF announced upgrades to 11 Texan and Hawk training devices using mixed-reality technology.
* These upgrades allow students to practise circuits, formation flying and low-level navigation in simulated environments before flying live sorties.
* The RAF stated that the programme could save up to £4 million annually in training costs. Royal Air Force +2

This means that relocating the function would involve more than moving aircraft. It would require reproducing the training system, facilities, instructors, simulators, technical support and operating arrangements.

5.5 Airfield and geographical characteristics

RAF Valley’s location on Anglesey is relevant to its operational training role.

* It is situated close to the Irish Sea.
* It supports training in maritime as well as inland environments.
* The RAF operates night flying training when required.
* The station’s flying information identifies RAF Valley and RAF Mona as protected military aerodromes with permanent flight-restriction zones. Royal Air Force

RAF Mona, also on Anglesey, operates as a relief landing ground for RAF Valley. This provides an additional airfield facility associated with Valley’s training operations. Mona Flying Club +1

5.6 What RAF Valley is not

For our strategic assessment, these distinctions are important:

* It is not currently a front-line Typhoon or F-35 operational combat base.
* It does not operate an operational fast-jet fighter squadron in the same sense as RAF Coningsby or RAF Lossiemouth.
* Its Hawk T2 aircraft are advanced training aircraft, not substitutes for front-line combat aircraft.
* Its mountain rescue function is not equivalent to maintaining a fleet of operational search-and-rescue helicopters.
* Its helicopter training role is separate from its fast-jet training role.

This avoids overstating its military function while recognising its importance.

5.7 What would the UK need to replace if access to RAF Valley were lost?

The UK would need to consider several distinct capabilities.

Capability	What would need to be replaced
Basic fast-jet training	Texan aircraft, instructors, training facilities and operating capacity
Advanced fast-jet training	Hawk T2 aircraft, tactical training, weapons instruction and supporting infrastructure
Helicopter training	Juno/Jupiter aircraft operations and mountain/maritime training environment
Mountain rescue	Valley-based team and headquarters functions
Airfield capacity	Runways, maintenance, air-traffic control, engineering and support infrastructure
Training geography	Access to suitable airspace, coastal environment and training areas

Some of these functions could potentially be relocated to existing UK bases. But that would not establish that equivalent capacity was immediately available.

We should not yet put a monetary replacement cost on this. We would need to establish:

* Which functions could move to existing facilities.
* Whether those facilities have spare capacity.
* What additional aircraft or infrastructure would be required.
* Whether training throughput would be reduced during relocation.
* What operational or financial consequences would follow.

5.8 Corrected wording for our master document

RAF Valley: RAF Valley on Anglesey is a specialist military flying-training station and a significant component of the UK’s Military Flying Training System. It houses No. 4 Flying Training School, including 72 Squadron, which provides basic fast-jet training using the Texan T1, and IV and XXV(F) Squadrons, which provide advanced fast-jet and tactical training using the Hawk T2. The station also supports helicopter aircrew training through 202 Squadron, operating Juno HT1 and Jupiter HT1 helicopters for mountain, maritime and search-and-rescue training. RAF Valley additionally houses the headquarters of the RAF Mountain Rescue Service, which coordinates three high-readiness teams and provides military search-and-rescue and aircraft post-crash response capabilities. Its importance therefore extends beyond the airfield itself to specialist training systems, instructors, simulators, aircraft support, airspace and geographical training conditions. It is not, however, a front-line operational fighter base.

Sources

* RAF — RAF Valley station overview⁠￼.
* RAF — 72 Squadron⁠￼.
* RAF — IV Squadron⁠￼.
* RAF — XXV(F) Squadron⁠￼.
* RAF — 202 Squadron⁠￼.
* RAF — Mountain Rescue Service⁠￼.
* RAF — Mixed-reality training investment, April 2026⁠￼.
* Defence Equipment and Support — UK Military Flying Training System investment⁠￼.

Fact-check status: current station functions and listed aircraft verified against RAF material available in November 2026. Exact aircraft numbers, annual training throughput, spare capacity and replacement costs have not yet been established.

The important addition to our wider Wales analysis: RAF Valley is not just a piece of military real estate. It is a functioning training and specialist-response system. Its strategic value therefore includes the institutional and operational capability concentrated there, not simply the value of its land and runways.”.
