“Fact-check 6: Which British training areas could substitute for Sennybridge?

The answer is: several could absorb particular parts of Sennybridge’s training programme, but the evidence does not establish a single like-for-like replacement.

The most relevant alternatives are Otterburn, Dartmoor, Catterick, Warcop and Salisbury Plain. Their suitability depends on the type and scale of training being relocated.

There is an important distinction here: a training area that can host infantry exercises is not necessarily capable of replacing Sennybridge’s combination of upland terrain, live firing, artillery and infantry training, built-up training facilities and available training space.

6.1 What exactly would need replacing?

Sennybridge provides a combination of:

* Upland and rugged terrain for infantry field training.
* Live-fire training.
* Artillery and infantry weapons training.
* Mortar and anti-tank weapons training.
* Tactical exercises.
* Training in a built-up environment, including its FIBUA village.
* Space for larger collective exercises.

The Welsh heritage record describes the Mynydd Epynt area as a major live-fire landscape, with infantry now its predominant users. Coflein +1

The MOD’s 2007 training-estate assessment explicitly categorised Sennybridge as a site for Phase 3 infantry training, while identifying other sites for different training functions. That document is old, so it is useful for understanding the historical division of roles, not proof of current spare capacity. GOV.UK Assets

6.2 The principal alternatives

1. Otterburn Training Area

Strong functional alternative

* More than 22,000 hectares.
* Largest single live-firing range in the UK, according to the MOD.
* Upland terrain in the Cheviot foothills.
* Used for artillery and infantry training.
* Approximately 30,000 soldiers train there annually.

Assessment: One of the strongest candidates for absorbing some of Sennybridge’s infantry and artillery activity. Its scale is an advantage, although its terrain, existing commitments and available capacity would need examination. GOV.UK +1

2. Dartmoor Training Area

Strong terrain alternative

* Approximately 11,000 hectares used by the MOD.
* Upland moorland and exposed terrain.
* Okehampton, Merrivale and Willsworthy ranges support live firing and training on foot.
* Additional dry-training areas support tactical exercises.

Assessment: A relevant alternative for light infantry, dismounted training and challenging upland conditions. It is not an equivalent replacement for every Sennybridge capability. Its National Park location and environmental constraints also matter. GOV.UK +2

3. Catterick Training Area

Partial alternative

* Established Army training area.
* Located on the eastern edge of the Yorkshire Dales.
* Catterick also houses the Infantry Training Centre, which delivers infantry recruit training.
* The MOD’s older training-estate assessment identifies it for Phase 2 infantry training and mechanised-unit training.

Assessment: Could absorb some infantry training and associated activities. However, the Infantry Training Centre’s recruit-training function should not be confused with replacing Sennybridge’s larger collective field-training role. GOV.UK +2

4. Warcop Training Area

Partial alternative

* Approximately 9,700 hectares.
* Located in the North Pennines.
* Includes upland terrain and a live-firing range complex.
* Used by regular and reserve units.
* The older MOD assessment identifies it for Phase 2 infantry training.

Assessment: Could absorb some upland and infantry training, but its smaller area and designated environmental land make it inappropriate to assume that it could take over Sennybridge’s whole programme. GOV.UK +1

5. Salisbury Plain Training Area

Different but important alternative

* Just over 38,000 hectares.
* Major collective training area.
* Supports large field exercises, tactical engagement simulation and armoured/mechanised manoeuvre.
* Has extensive live-firing facilities.

Assessment: Could absorb some larger-scale field exercises and other collective training. But its open chalk downland is not a like-for-like substitute for Sennybridge’s upland terrain and training environment. GOV.UK +1

6.3 What about the built-up training village?

This is a useful distinction because it prevents us from treating every facility at Sennybridge as irreplaceable.

* Sennybridge has a recognised FIBUA training village.
* The MOD has identified numerous other facilities for training in fighting in built-up areas.
* A 2017 parliamentary answer listed Catterick, Warcop, Stanford, Caerwent, Longmoor, Pirbright, Swynnerton and other sites among the facilities available for this type of training.
* A 2019 parliamentary answer also listed Sennybridge alongside Salisbury Plain, Dartmoor, Catterick, Warcop and other sites routinely used for urban training. UK Parliament +1

Therefore:

* The urban-training function is not exclusive to Sennybridge.
* Some of it could be distributed elsewhere.
* The question would be whether other sites had enough capacity, appropriate facilities and available training time.

6.4 Geographical comparison

The locations help show why there is no simple replacement immediately beside Sennybridge.

The geographical consequences of moving training include:

* Longer journeys for some units.
* Additional transport and accommodation costs.
* More pressure on the receiving training areas.
* Potential competition for firing slots and exercise periods.
* Changes to the relationship between barracks, training schools and training grounds.

6.5 The biggest issue: spare capacity

This is where the evidence becomes less complete.

A Ministry of Defence assessment published in 2007 made several relevant observations:

* The main UK training areas had substantial competing demands.
* Their use was constrained by environmental designations, legislation and firing restrictions.
* The assessment described sites as operating at or near full capacity.
* It warned that lost training capacity from disposing of or reducing the utility of existing training areas could not easily be absorbed or replaced. GOV.UK Assets

However, that is a 2007 assessment, not a measurement of spare capacity in November 2026.

We cannot use it to claim that every alternative is currently full. Nor can we assume that spare capacity exists.

To establish current substitutability, we would need:

* Current annual utilisation of each training area.
* Existing training commitments and priority bookings.
* Available firing days and restrictions.
* Capacity for different unit sizes.
* Facilities for artillery, mortars and infantry weapons.
* Availability of built-up training facilities.
* Transport and accommodation capacity.
* Any additional investment required.

6.6 Overall findings

Sennybridge function	Potential alternative(s)	Substitution assessment
Upland infantry training	Otterburn, Dartmoor, Warcop	Some functional substitution
Artillery and infantry live firing	Otterburn	Strongest identified alternative for overlapping roles
Large collective field exercises	Salisbury Plain	Possible, but different terrain and training emphasis
Infantry training	Catterick, Warcop, Dartmoor	Partial; depends on training phase and exercise type
Built-up training	Catterick, Salisbury Plain, Stanford and other sites	Distributed substitution possible
Combined Sennybridge training package	Several sites together	No demonstrated single-site replacement

6.7 Corrected wording for our master document

Alternative UK training areas: The UK has several other military training areas that could absorb parts of Sennybridge’s training programme, including Otterburn, Dartmoor, Catterick, Warcop and Salisbury Plain. Their capabilities overlap with Sennybridge in different ways: Otterburn offers extensive upland live-firing and artillery/infantry training; Dartmoor provides demanding upland and light-infantry training; Catterick and Warcop support infantry training; and Salisbury Plain provides extensive facilities for large collective and mechanised exercises. Built-up training is also available at multiple other sites. However, none has been demonstrated to provide a complete like-for-like replacement for Sennybridge’s combination of terrain, live firing, infantry and artillery facilities, training infrastructure and scale. The MOD’s 2007 assessment warned that existing training capacity could not easily be absorbed or replaced, but current utilisation and spare capacity at alternative sites have not yet been established. Any replacement would therefore need to be assessed as a distributed programme, with associated costs, travel, scheduling, infrastructure and training-capacity implications.

Sources

* MOD — The Defence Training Estate⁠￼.
* MOD — North England military training areas⁠￼.
* MOD — South West England military training areas⁠￼.
* MOD — The Need for Military Training on Defence Training Estate South West (2007)⁠￼.
* House of Commons — Army facilities for urban training (2017)⁠￼.
* British Army — Infantry Training Centre Catterick⁠￼.
* RCAHMW — Sennybridge Training Area⁠￼.

Fact-check status: alternative locations and overlapping functions established. Current spare capacity, precise replacement costs and the feasibility of transferring Sennybridge’s complete training programme remain unverified.

The important correction to our earlier discussion: It is reasonable to say that the UK could redistribute some training currently conducted at Sennybridge. It is not yet justified to say that it could replace Sennybridge without significant cost or loss of capability. Those are different claims, and the second requires a much more detailed MOD capacity assessment.”.  

“Fact-check 7: Actual replacement costs and timescales for Sennybridge

Verdict: I cannot identify a published, current MOD estimate for replacing Sennybridge Training Area in its entirety. That means we should not attach a confident monetary figure or completion date to its replacement.

There is, however, a useful distinction between three different propositions:

* Redistributing some exercises to other training areas.
* Replacing particular facilities or capabilities, such as a firing range or built-up training village.
* Replacing the complete training function: the land, terrain, firing capacity, infrastructure, environmental permissions and available training time.

The first two may be achievable without recreating Sennybridge. The third is a much larger undertaking. I’ll keep those separate rather than present the cost of one facility as the cost of replacing the whole training area.

7.1 What the available evidence tells us

A. The MOD has previously identified serious constraints on replacing large training areas.

A particularly relevant MOD study, The Need for Military Training on Defence Training Estate South West, dated August 2007, contains unusually direct evidence.

It found that:

* The MOD did not envisage major acquisitions of new training land.
* A search for a suitable 13,700-hectare site elsewhere in mainland Britain, after excluding environmentally or otherwise constrained land, failed to identify a suitable location.
* The study assessed the major UK training areas as operating at or near full capacity, once restrictions were taken into account.
* It concluded that training lost through disposal or reduction of existing training areas could not easily be absorbed or replaced. GOV.UK Assets +1

It also described Sennybridge as the UK’s principal light-force training area at the time, capable of supporting brigade-level exercises and seven concurrent live-fire company exercises. It recorded utilisation above 70% in 2007. GOV.UK Assets

Important qualification: these are historical figures, not current utilisation statistics. The study is useful evidence of the structural difficulty of replacing large training landscapes, but it cannot establish today’s spare capacity or prove that a replacement is impossible.

B. The MOD’s more recent defence review acknowledges wider infrastructure problems.

The 2025 Strategic Defence Review says that defence infrastructure has been serially underfunded and that capability planning has often failed to account adequately for infrastructure requirements and costs.

It recommends a ten-year infrastructure recapitalisation plan identifying investment priorities, estimated costs and provisional delivery timelines. GOV.UK +1

That is relevant context: replacing Sennybridge would be competing with an existing programme of defence infrastructure renewal. It would not be starting with an unlimited budget and an empty estate.

7.2 What can we use as actual cost benchmarks?

There are some published examples of individual training infrastructure projects.

Project	Published cost	What it tells us
Copehill Down urban-training improvements, 2015	Over £500,000	Enhancing an existing training village can cost hundreds of thousands
Herontree North firing range, Catterick, 2014	Multi-million pounds	A new specialist firing range is a substantial infrastructure project
New trench infrastructure across Salisbury Plain and Stanford, 2026	Over £4.4 million	Modern battlefield training infrastructure can require multi-million-pound investment

Sources: GOV.UK +2

The Catterick project was delivered in just over 18 months. That is a useful construction benchmark, but it concerns two 600-metre electronic target ranges, not a replacement for a major training estate. GOV.UK +1

Do not extrapolate these figures

None of these projects gives us a defensible cost per hectare for replacing Sennybridge. Nor can we multiply the cost of a firing range by the number of ranges and call the result a replacement estimate.

The largest costs may instead arise from land, environmental constraints, infrastructure, additional capacity and the need to maintain training continuity.

7.3 What about the cost of the existing training estate?

There is another figure that could easily be misused.

A 2023 parliamentary answer gave the projected whole-life cost of the MOD’s national Training Estate Support contract as £728 million. This was the cost of the national support contract, not the cost of operating Sennybridge alone and certainly not the cost of replacing it. The contract was scheduled to end in November 2030, with extension provisions. UK Parliament

Similarly, the £5.1 billion Defence Estate Optimisation programme is a much wider investment portfolio, not a Sennybridge replacement budget. GOV.UK

These figures are useful for understanding the scale of defence-estate management, but should not be included in a Sennybridge replacement-cost calculation.

7.4 What timescale could we responsibly put on replacement?

I would separate the timescale into four stages.

Stage	What it involves	What we can say
Immediate redistribution	Rebook some exercises at existing sites	Could begin relatively quickly, subject to available capacity
Short-term adaptation	Extend firing periods, upgrade facilities, adjust scheduling	Individual projects can be delivered within roughly 18 months, as the Catterick example shows
Medium-term expansion	Build additional ranges, accommodation and support infrastructure	No Sennybridge-specific delivery estimate found
Full replacement	Secure suitable land, establish permissions, develop facilities and reproduce the training programme	No published cost or completion date found

The first two stages are not the same as replacing the complete capability.

A new site or substantial expansion could require:

* Land acquisition or long-term access agreements.
* Environmental assessment and potentially extensive mitigation.
* Planning and statutory approvals.
* Consultation with local communities and landowners.
* Construction of firing ranges, danger areas and support infrastructure.
* New accommodation, roads, utilities and maintenance provision.
* Certification, safety testing and integration into the training calendar.

The 2007 MOD assessment specifically identifies the difficulty of finding suitable large areas and overcoming environmental, social and economic constraints. GOV.UK Assets +1

My judgement: it would be reasonable to plan on a multi-year transition for substantial replacement, but we do not have evidence to say that it would take five, ten or fifteen years. A precise estimate would be invented.

7.5 What would an actual replacement assessment need to cost?

I would want the MOD to provide the following figures, rather than one headline estimate.

Cost category	What needs measuring
Land	Purchase, leasing, access rights and compensation
Infrastructure	Ranges, impact areas, roads, camps, utilities and accommodation
Environmental	Surveys, mitigation, restoration and ongoing monitoring
Capacity expansion	Additional facilities and firing periods at receiving sites
Transition	Transport, temporary accommodation and disrupted training
Operating costs	Additional staffing, maintenance, security and estate management
Capability loss	Training delays, reduced exercise availability and any training that cannot be replicated
Long-term resilience	Whether the alternative sites would have sufficient capacity during increased operational demand

There is also an important counterfactual: if the UK retained Sennybridge, it would still incur maintenance and operating costs. Replacement cost should therefore be compared with the additional cost of the alternative arrangement, not treated as the entire cost of running the new sites.

7.6 Corrected wording for the master document

Actual replacement costs and timescales: No current, publicly available MOD estimate has been identified for the cost or timescale of replacing Sennybridge Training Area as a complete training capability. Individual training facilities can be built or upgraded within existing sites: for example, a new firing range at Catterick was delivered in just over 18 months, while urban-training improvements at Copehill Down cost over £500,000 and new trench-training infrastructure across two training areas received more than £4.4 million in investment in 2026. These are component-level benchmarks, not estimates for replacing Sennybridge. An MOD assessment published in 2007 found significant constraints on acquiring large new training areas, assessed existing major sites as operating at or near full capacity, and warned that lost training capacity could not easily be absorbed or replaced. Those findings are historical and require updating, but demonstrate why a full replacement cannot be assumed to be straightforward. A proper assessment would need to include land acquisition or access, environmental and planning requirements, additional infrastructure, receiving-site capacity, transition costs, operating costs and any loss of training capability. Some exercises could be redistributed relatively quickly; full replacement would require a multi-year programme, but its actual duration and cost remain unquantified.

7.7 The next piece of evidence worth pursuing

The most useful question is now not simply “How much would a replacement cost?”

It is:

Has the MOD assessed what would happen to its training capacity if Sennybridge became unavailable, and, if so, what were the identified receiving sites, capacity shortfalls, mitigation measures and costs?

That is a much more answerable parliamentary question than demanding a single hypothetical replacement price. It would also expose whether the MOD has a contingency plan or is relying on the assumption that other training areas could absorb the work.

Fact-check status: no defensible total replacement cost or completion date found. There is historical official evidence of constrained capacity and difficulty acquiring replacement land, plus modern component-level cost and delivery examples. The central uncertainty is the current MOD assessment of spare capacity and contingency planning.”.  

“Fact-check 8: Ownership and legal status of MOD property under a hypothetical Welsh independence settlement

Core distinction verified

The MOD’s ownership of land in Wales would not, by itself, give the UK continuing sovereignty or exclusive jurisdiction over that land after Welsh independence. Equally, Welsh independence would not automatically settle every question about who owns, occupies or uses existing MOD property.

There are two separate legal questions:

* Territorial sovereignty: which state has jurisdiction over the land?
* Property rights: who holds the freehold, leasehold, buildings, equipment and other interests in it?

An independent Wales would become the sovereign state exercising territorial jurisdiction over its territory, subject to any agreements it entered into. The status of particular UK-owned assets would need to be addressed through the independence settlement and applicable rules of state succession.

Sennybridge is a useful case study because its landholding is not all of one kind.

8.1 What does the MOD actually own at Sennybridge?

The established figures are:

Land	Approximate area	Existing legal relationship
Mynydd Epynt training area	12,400 hectares	MOD freehold
Crychan Forest land	2,500 hectares	Leased land
Combined area	14,900 hectares	Mixed ownership and tenure

The MOD’s own estate material describes the 12,400 hectares as freehold and the additional 2,500 hectares as leased. The Welsh heritage record independently describes the same broad split. GOV.UK Assets +1

The leasehold land is particularly important: it is not simply another 2,500 hectares of MOD-owned land. The MOD has rights under a lease, and those rights would need to be examined separately from the freehold.

8.2 Who is the legal owner?

There is a useful clarification in the Cabinet Office’s current Government Functional Standard for Property, updated 1 April 2026.

It says:

* Government property is held on behalf of the Crown.
* The Crown is treated as legally indivisible within the UK government property system.
* Defence property, both domestic and overseas, is generally held in the name of the Secretary of State for Defence.
* HM Land Registry records the relevant Secretary of State as proprietor, in their capacity as a corporation sole. GOV.UK

The Defence (Transfer of Functions) Act 1964 also provides for the Secretary of State for Defence to hold property, rights and liabilities previously vested in the former defence departments. Legislation.gov.uk +1

This matters because the legal title is not necessarily recorded as belonging to an organisation called the Ministry of Defence. The MOD manages the estate, but the registered proprietor may be the Secretary of State for Defence.

It also means we should avoid casually describing every MOD asset as the personal property of the British monarch. Government property held on behalf of the Crown is a distinct legal arrangement.

8.3 What does international law say about state property when part of a country becomes independent?

The main reference is the 1983 Vienna Convention on Succession of States in Respect of State Property, Archives and Debts.

Its Article 17 addresses the separation of part of a state to form a new state. It provides that, unless the predecessor and successor states agree otherwise:

* Immovable state property situated in the separating territory passes to the successor state.
* Movable state property connected with the predecessor state’s activities in that territory also passes to the successor state.
* Other movable state property is divided equitably. The Faculty of Law +1

On the face of that provision, it is highly relevant to MOD-owned freehold land in Wales.

But there is an important legal qualification.

Not a directly binding answer

The Convention is not yet in force. The UN Treaty Collection records seven signatories and seven parties as at 24 November 2026. The UK is not listed among them. It therefore cannot simply be invoked as a binding treaty that automatically settles the hypothetical Welsh case. treaties.un.org

There is also a distinction between:

* The Convention’s proposed rules.
* Customary international law.
* The actual terms of a negotiated independence settlement.

A 2025 academic treatment of state succession identifies territoriality as a central principle in state-property allocation, with a default of succession unless the states agree otherwise. That supports taking the territorial location of assets seriously, but it does not remove the need to examine the particular legal circumstances and settlement. OUP Academic

My assessment: there is a substantial international-law argument for Welsh succession to UK state-owned immovable property situated in Wales. It would be wrong, however, to present Article 17 as a guaranteed, automatically enforceable outcome in this particular hypothetical.

8.4 What could happen to Sennybridge in an independence settlement?

There are several plausible settlement models.

Model	Ownership	Military use	Implication
Transfer to Wales	Freehold transferred to Welsh state	UK use ends or continues under agreement	Wales controls the land and can negotiate future use
Welsh ownership, UK lease	Freehold transfers to Wales	UK retains a defined lease or licence	Wales receives an asset and a potential rental income
UK retains ownership by agreement	UK retains freehold	UK operates under a bilateral agreement	Wales retains territorial sovereignty but permits continued UK use
Sale or compensated transfer	Title transferred through negotiated transaction	Depends on subsequent agreement	Ownership and compensation are settled together
Phased withdrawal	Agreed interim ownership and use arrangements	UK use winds down over an agreed period	Allows training continuity and an orderly transfer

These are possible negotiating outcomes, not established predictions.

One especially important point: a UK-owned military training area would not automatically become British sovereign territory.

A UK military presence in an independent Wales would ordinarily depend on an agreement with the Welsh state. That agreement could address access, training permissions, policing, criminal jurisdiction, environmental responsibilities, safety and liability.

The existence of a military base or defence facility does not itself create an enclave outside the host state’s sovereignty.

8.5 What happens to the additional leased forestry land?

This needs its own treatment.

The additional 2,500 hectares are leased, rather than part of the MOD freehold. The MOD’s published estate description identifies the land as leased from Forest Enterprise; current MOD public-access material identifies the relevant forestry land as leased from Natural Resources Wales. GOV.UK Assets +1

That raises several questions:

* Who is the legal landlord at the date of independence?
* Does the lease continue under the applicable law?
* Does the lease contain provisions relating to changes in sovereignty or government?
* Would the successor Welsh government or a Welsh public body become the relevant landlord?
* Could the lease be varied, renewed or terminated?
* Would the land remain available for military training?

The answers depend on the actual lease, the legal identity of the landlord and the independence settlement. We have not examined the lease itself.

8.6 What about other MOD assets in Wales?

The same principle needs to be applied asset by asset.

Asset type	Main legal question
MOD freehold land	State-property succession and negotiated settlement
MOD leasehold land	Existing lease, landlord identity and continuation
Buildings and fixed infrastructure	Whether they form part of the land or are separately owned
Aircraft, vehicles and weapons	Movable state property and allocation under settlement
Equipment used specifically in Wales	Whether it is connected to the Welsh defence establishment
Shared UK infrastructure	Whether it is transferred, retained or subject to shared-use arrangements
Private defence-contractor property	Private ownership and contractual rights, not automatically UK state property

The last distinction matters. Not everything on a military estate is owned by the UK government. Contractors may own equipment, buildings or other assets; those rights cannot simply be treated as state property.

8.7 What would Wales need to establish before negotiating?

I would put a formal MOD Property and Defence Estate Schedule into the proposed Welsh Strategic and Economic Contribution Account.

For every relevant site, it should record:

* Exact Land Registry title and registered proprietor.
* Freehold, leasehold, licence or other tenure.
* Area and boundary.
* Buildings, fixed installations and infrastructure.
* Existing easements, access rights and restrictive covenants.
* Environmental designations and statutory obligations.
* Current military use and operational significance.
* Existing contracts and third-party interests.
* Estimated market value and replacement cost.
* Potential transfer, lease, sale or shared-use arrangements.

For Sennybridge in particular, I would want the actual title register, the lease documents for the forestry land, the relevant byelaws and a schedule of fixed infrastructure. The MOD’s public descriptions establish the broad ownership split, but they are not a substitute for the underlying legal documents.

8.8 Corrected wording for the master document

Ownership and legal status of MOD property under a hypothetical independence settlement: MOD property located in Wales would need to be addressed through the legal and financial settlement accompanying independence. Territorial sovereignty and property ownership are distinct: an independent Wales would exercise sovereignty over its territory, but the ownership, possession and continued use of individual defence assets would require legal determination and, where appropriate, agreement between the successor Welsh state and the continuing UK state. The 1983 Vienna Convention on Succession of States in Respect of State Property, Archives and Debts provides a relevant framework. Article 17 envisages immovable state property situated in a territory passing to the successor state when part of a state separates, unless the states agree otherwise. However, the Convention is not yet in force and should not be presented as a directly binding or conclusive answer to the Welsh case. The territoriality principle is also recognised in contemporary legal scholarship on state succession, but the precise customary-law position and its application would require legal advice. Sennybridge illustrates the need for an asset-by-asset assessment: approximately 12,400 hectares are MOD freehold, while a further 2,500 hectares are leased forestry land. Possible outcomes include transfer to Wales, continued UK ownership under a negotiated agreement, a lease or shared-use arrangement, sale or compensated transfer, or phased withdrawal. Any continued UK military presence would require an agreed legal basis; MOD ownership would not itself confer British sovereignty over the site. The actual title registers, lease documents, statutory instruments, third-party interests and infrastructure schedule would need to be examined before any valuation or negotiating position could be established.

8.9 Fact-check status

Established

Verified

* Sennybridge has a mixed freehold/leasehold structure.
* UK defence property is generally held in the name of the Secretary of State for Defence.
* International state-succession law provides a relevant territorial-property framework.
    Not settled
    Requires legal analysis
* Whether and how the freehold would pass in the precise Welsh scenario.
* Whether the parties would agree a different property settlement.
* The continuation or termination of existing leases.
* Any continuing UK rights to military use.
    Evidence still needed
    Documentary
* Land Registry title registers.
* The full forestry lease.
* Site-specific statutory instruments and applicable byelaws.
* A complete MOD property and infrastructure schedule.

The larger constitutional point: this is not simply a question of whether Wales could claim an MOD training area. It is a question of how a new state and its predecessor divide state property, contractual rights, liabilities and military arrangements. That distinction is important across the whole Welsh defence estate, not just Sennybridge.

Useful primary references:

* Cabinet Office — Government Functional Standard: Property (updated April 2026)⁠￼.
* UN Treaty Collection — 1983 State Succession Convention status⁠￼.
* Vienna Convention — Article 17⁠￼.
* MOD — Sennybridge estate description⁠￼.
* Defence (Transfer of Functions) Act 1964⁠￼.”.  

“8.1 Elan Valley: Birmingham and the Severn Trent network

Maximum transfer capacity: approximately 320 million litres per day (Ml/d), through the Elan Valley Aqueduct. The National Infrastructure Commission for Wales (NICW) confirms this figure in its 2026 infrastructure assessment. The National Infrastructure Commission for Wales +1

The important finding is in Severn Trent’s 2024 Water Resources Management Plan:

* The Elan transfer has a stated maximum capacity of 320 Ml/d.
* Severn Trent reports 282 Ml/d as average utilisation in its baseline 1-in-500-year deployable-output simulation.
* That is a modelled planning scenario, not a measurement of the actual average water transferred in an ordinary year. severntrent.com

ELAN VALLEY — MODELLED UTILISATION

282 Ml/d

Severn Trent’s 1-in-500-year baseline simulation

Maximum transfer capacity 320 Ml/d

Modelled utilisation as share of capacity 88.1%

That percentage describes the modelled scenario, not the observed annual average.

I have not found a sufficiently clear published annual outturn series that establishes the actual average Elan-to-Birmingham transfer over recent years. We should not substitute the 282 figure for it.

8.2 Lake Vyrnwy: North West England

Here we have a much more useful operational figure.

A United Utilities document submitted to Ofwat in August 2024 describes the Vyrnwy Large Diameter Trunk Main:

* Three parallel pipelines run from Oswestry Water Treatment Works to Prescot Service Reservoir.
* The system is designed for up to 250 Ml/d.
* Its stated maximum flow is 210 Ml/d.
* Its average flow is 165 Ml/d.
* The system supplies approximately 900,000 people in Cheshire and Merseyside and represents around 20% of regional supply. corporate.unitedutilities.com

VYRNWY AQUEDUCT — REPORTED AVERAGE FLOW

165 Ml/d

United Utilities’ reported average pipeline flow

Maximum flow 210 Ml/d

Average as share of maximum 78.6%

Annualised average volume 60,225 Ml

Annualised calculation: 165 Ml/d × 365 days. It is not a separately reported audited annual volume.

There is a qualification here too: the 165 Ml/d figure describes flow through the trunk-main system from Oswestry to Prescot. The pipeline feeds intermediate service reservoirs and distribution systems along the route. It is therefore not a clean measurement of the volume of water physically crossing the Welsh border or the volume ultimately consumed in England.

The wider NICW report describes United Utilities as able to take up to 205 Ml/d from Lake Vyrnwy and the River Dee. That is a combined system-level maximum, not the actual average Vyrnwy export. The National Infrastructure Commission for Wales +1

8.3 What we can responsibly put in the comparison

Measure	Elan Valley	Vyrnwy
Published maximum capacity/flow	320 Ml/d	210 Ml/d
Published actual average	Not established	165 Ml/d, pipeline flow
Other useful figure	282 Ml/d modelled in a 1-in-500 simulation	Up to 205 Ml/d combined Vyrnwy/Dee system transfer
Principal receiving area	Birmingham / Severn Trent	Cheshire and Merseyside / wider North West

The two average figures are not directly comparable: one is a modelled planning result, the other a reported average flow through a particular pipeline system.

8.4 Why this matters to the strategic-value argument

The previous combined figure of more than 525 Ml/d should be retained only as a broad statement about maximum transfer capability, not as a statement about the actual amount of water supplied each day.

For the economic and strategic assessment, we need three separate quantities:

* Actual annual transfer: measured water volumes over a defined reporting period.
* Available capacity: the maximum technically and legally deliverable volume.
* Drought and resilience contribution: the water that can be delivered under constrained conditions, including the value of reservoir storage and the ability to sustain supply.

The third is particularly important. A water system can have considerable strategic value even if its maximum capacity is not used every day.

8.5 Corrected wording for the master document

Actual average water transfers, not just maximum capacity: The Elan Valley Aqueduct has a published maximum transfer capacity of approximately 320 million litres per day to Severn Trent Water. Severn Trent’s 2024 Water Resources Management Plan reports 282 Ml/d as average utilisation in its baseline 1-in-500-year deployable-output simulation; this is a modelled planning figure, not an observed annual average. A recent actual average transfer from Elan Valley to Birmingham has not yet been established from published outturn data. For Lake Vyrnwy, United Utilities reports an average flow of 165 Ml/d through the Vyrnwy Large Diameter Trunk Main, against a stated maximum flow of 210 Ml/d. This pipeline runs from Oswestry Water Treatment Works to Prescot and supplies intermediate locations as well as the wider North West, so its average flow should not be treated as a precise measurement of water exported from Wales or consumed in England. The separate figure of up to 205 Ml/d refers to the combined potential transfer from the Vyrnwy and Dee systems, not their actual average delivery. Maximum capacity, observed annual transfer and drought-resilience contribution should be recorded separately.

8.6 Evidence still needed

The outstanding item is a proper annual outturn series for Elan Valley. I would request from Severn Trent:

* Annual metered volume entering the Elan Aqueduct, preferably for 2015–16 to 2025–26.
* Average daily transfer for each financial year.
* Minimum and maximum daily flows.
* Any periods of reduced transfer or interruption.
* The distinction between water delivered to Birmingham and water delivered elsewhere through the network.

For Vyrnwy, I would seek the equivalent annual source abstraction and pipeline-flow records from United Utilities, distinguishing Lake Vyrnwy releases, River Dee contributions and intermediate Welsh and English supply destinations.

Fact-check status: Vyrnwy has a useful published average-flow figure; Elan’s actual average remains unverified. The earlier maximum-capacity figures should not be represented as actual daily transfers.

Sources:

* NICW — Wales Infrastructure Assessment 2026: Water⁠￼.
* Severn Trent — Water Resources Management Plan 2024, Appendix A⁠￼.
* United Utilities — Vyrnwy Aqueduct operational and investment evidence⁠￼.
* United Utilities — North West Transfer feasibility report⁠￼.”.  

“Fact-check 9: Current dependency of Birmingham and the Midlands on Elan Valley

Strong dependence in Birmingham; more limited at regional scale

The claim is substantially correct for Birmingham, but too broad if it implies that the whole Midlands depends on Elan Valley water.

The distinction is between:

* Birmingham’s historical dependence on the Elan Valley Aqueduct (EVA).
* The wider Severn Trent network’s use of water transferred from Wales.
* The additional backup supplies now available to reduce the consequences of an aqueduct interruption.

9.1 Birmingham: a major and historically concentrated dependency

Severn Trent has repeatedly described the Elan Valley Aqueduct as Birmingham’s sole source of water supply.

Its own project information states that:

* The aqueduct transports water from Wales to Frankley Water Treatment Works.
* It supplies more than one million customers in Birmingham and surrounding areas.
* The aqueduct has historically been the sole source of supply into Birmingham.
* Frankley Reservoir’s storage capacity meant that the aqueduct could only be taken out of service for a few days at a time without an alternative supply. Severn Trent Water +2

This is significant because it describes an infrastructure dependency, not simply a preference for Welsh water.

However, the position has changed through the Birmingham Resilience Project.

9.2 The Birmingham Resilience Project has reduced the vulnerability

Severn Trent invested approximately £300 million in the Birmingham Resilience Project.

The project includes:

* A new River Severn intake near Lickhill, close to Stourport.
* A 26 km pipeline to Frankley.
* Pumping stations and treatment upgrades.
* Network improvements allowing water to be transferred into Birmingham from elsewhere in the Severn Trent region.

The new arrangements were designed to permit the Elan Valley Aqueduct to be taken out of service for maintenance while maintaining Birmingham’s supply. Severn Trent Water +2

A particularly important development is Severn Trent’s reported testing of its ability to bring water into Birmingham from nine water treatment works outside the city. It describes the new arrangement as providing backup for maintenance and emergencies. Severn Trent Water

This changes the wording we should use:

* Historically, Birmingham was exceptionally dependent on the Elan Valley Aqueduct.
* The city now has additional network and River Severn backup arrangements.
* Elan remains a major source, but it is no longer accurate to describe Birmingham as having no alternative supply capability.

The backup system is not evidence that Elan has become unimportant. It is evidence that Severn Trent has spent heavily to reduce the consequences of losing access to it temporarily.

9.3 The wider Midlands: important, but not uniformly dependent

The Elan Valley Aqueduct also feeds into Severn Trent’s wider water network.

Severn Trent explicitly says that water from the aqueduct supplies its wider network, reaching thousands of customers outside Birmingham. Severn Trent Water

Its 2024 Water Resources Management Plan gives a useful indication of the scale:

Measure	Figure
Elan Valley import capacity	320 Ml/d
Modelled average import in 1-in-500 simulation	282 Ml/d
Severn Trent regional supply	Approximately 1,800 Ml/d
Modelled Elan import as share of regional supply	Approximately 15.7%

The 282 Ml/d figure is a modelled planning result, not a measured average transfer. The approximate 15.7% calculation is therefore an indicative comparison, not a verified annual contribution. severntrent.com +1

This gives us a useful distinction:

* Birmingham: concentrated dependence on the Elan supply route, with new contingency arrangements.
* Wider Severn Trent region: a substantial imported resource within a much larger, diversified supply system.
* The Midlands as a whole: not one water supply zone. Different areas have different sources, operators and dependencies.

Severn Trent’s wider system uses groundwater, rivers and reservoirs. Its published resource assessment describes these as contributing approximately 35%, 35% and 30% respectively of its overall water supply. severntrent.com

So it would be misleading to suggest that the Midlands generally depends on Elan Valley water in the same way that Birmingham historically did.

9.4 What happens if the Elan supply is interrupted?

There are now several layers of resilience:

Layer	Function
Elan Valley reservoirs	Main strategic raw-water resource
Elan Valley Aqueduct	Long-distance transfer to Frankley
Frankley treatment and storage	Treatment and short-term buffering
River Severn intake and new pipeline	Alternative raw-water source
Wider Severn Trent network	Transfers from other treatment works and supply areas
Local boreholes	Additional support and emergency resources

Severn Trent’s planning documents also identify boreholes in Edgbaston and other Birmingham locations as additional sources for supply security. severntrent.com

The new infrastructure is particularly valuable because it allows planned maintenance and improves resilience during interruptions. It does not necessarily demonstrate that the alternative sources could replace Elan indefinitely, or meet every level of demand during a prolonged regional drought.

That last point remains important: backup capacity is not the same as equivalent long-term resource availability.

9.5 What this means for Welsh strategic value

I would make three separate claims:

* Historical infrastructure dependency: Birmingham’s water system was built around a major transfer from the Elan Valley, and that relationship has persisted for over a century.
* Current resource dependency: Elan remains a significant source for Birmingham and contributes materially to Severn Trent’s wider regional supply.
* Reduced interruption vulnerability: Severn Trent has invested heavily in alternative supplies and network connections, making Birmingham less vulnerable to a temporary interruption of the aqueduct.

The third point strengthens rather than destroys the strategic argument. The £300 million resilience investment demonstrates the importance Severn Trent places on protecting the city’s water supply and maintaining the Elan route.

It also gives us a concrete example of the costs associated with reducing dependence on a single Welsh-located resource.

9.6 Corrected wording for the master document

Current dependency of Birmingham and the Midlands on Elan Valley: Birmingham has historically had a particularly concentrated dependence on the Elan Valley Aqueduct, which transports water from Welsh reservoirs to Frankley Water Treatment Works and supplies more than one million customers in Birmingham and surrounding areas. Severn Trent has described the aqueduct as the sole source of supply into Birmingham. This vulnerability has been reduced by the approximately £300 million Birmingham Resilience Project, which introduced an alternative River Severn supply, a new pipeline to Frankley and improvements allowing water to be transferred from nine treatment works elsewhere in the Severn Trent network. Elan Valley water nevertheless remains an important source for Birmingham and contributes to the wider regional network. Severn Trent’s 2024 Water Resources Management Plan gives an Elan import capacity of 320 Ml/d and a modelled average import of 282 Ml/d in its baseline 1-in-500-year simulation. The latter is not an observed annual average. The modelled figure is equivalent to approximately 16% of Severn Trent’s reported regional supply of around 1,800 Ml/d, but this should be treated as an indicative comparison. The wider Midlands is not uniformly dependent on Elan: Severn Trent uses a diversified portfolio of groundwater, river and reservoir sources, and different areas have different supply arrangements. The strategic significance of Elan therefore lies in its concentrated importance to Birmingham and its substantial contribution to a wider regional network, rather than a claim that the whole Midlands depends on Welsh water.

9.7 Fact-check status

Claim	Finding
Birmingham historically depended heavily on Elan	
Elan was the sole source into Birmingham	
Birmingham still has no alternative supply	
Elan remains important to Birmingham	
Elan contributes to the wider Midlands network	
The whole Midlands depends on Elan	
Elan contributes roughly 16% of Severn Trent supply	

One particularly useful addition to our research: the £300 million Birmingham Resilience Project should be recorded as an actual investment in reducing dependence on a Welsh water-transfer route. It is not a valuation of Elan Valley itself, but it is evidence of the infrastructure investment required to make the receiving city more resilient.

Sources:

* Severn Trent — Testing Birmingham’s new backup water supply⁠￼.
* Severn Trent — Birmingham backup pipeline construction⁠￼.
* Severn Trent — WRMP 2024, Appendix A: Supply resources⁠￼.
* Severn Trent — Water Resources Management Plan, supply system overview⁠￼.
* National Infrastructure Commission for Wales — Water Infrastructure Assessment 2026⁠￼.”.  

“Fact-check 10: Current dependency of North West England on Vyrnwy and Dee supplies

Substantial dependency verified

The North West has a significant and continuing dependence on Welsh water, particularly in Cheshire and Merseyside. However, the evidence is stronger for Lake Vyrnwy than for a precise combined Vyrnwy-and-Dee percentage.

The key correction is that Vyrnwy and the Dee are not two interchangeable pipelines serving the whole North West. They are distinct sources within a highly interconnected regional water system.

10.1 Lake Vyrnwy: a substantial, measurable contribution

United Utilities’ March 2025 announcement about its Vyrnwy Aqueduct Modernisation Programme provides a particularly useful current figure:

* The aqueduct consists of three parallel pipelines from Lake Vyrnwy to Merseyside.
* It supplies approximately one million customers across Cheshire and Merseyside.
* United Utilities says it accounts for 12% of the company’s raw water across the North West.
* Its stated maximum transport capacity is 210 million litres per day (Ml/d). unitedutilities.com

VYRNWY — SHARE OF UNITED UTILITIES’ RAW WATER

12%

Company-reported share, March 2025

Customers served: approximately 1 million

Maximum aqueduct capacity: 210 Ml/d

Route: Lake Vyrnwy → Oswestry → Cheshire → Prescot / Merseyside

This is much more useful than simply quoting the maximum transfer capacity. It demonstrates an ongoing contribution to the receiving region’s water supply.

The distinction between ownership and use is also important:

* Hafren Dyfrdwy owns and operates the Lake Vyrnwy dam.
* United Utilities holds the abstraction licence for water supplied to its North West customers.
* Releases from the reservoir also support river flows in the Vyrnwy and Severn systems. hdcymru.co.uk +1

So the reservoir has several functions, not just supplying the aqueduct.

10.2 The River Dee: a wider and more complicated dependency

The River Dee is particularly important because it is a regulated water resource, supported by storage reservoirs in Wales.

The system involves:

* Llyn Celyn.
* Llyn Brenig.
* Llyn Tegid (Bala Lake).
* Regulation releases that maintain river flows and support abstraction downstream.

Natural Resources Wales explains that these reservoirs are operated as part of the Dee regulation system. The system can support abstraction of up to approximately 800 Ml/d by three water companies and the Canal & River Trust. Natural Resources Wales +1

Strategic importance established

The draft Dee River Basin Management Plan for 2027–2033 describes the Dee as a strategic source of potable surface water for nearly three million people. This figure covers the wider Dee system and its users; it should not be interpreted as three million United Utilities customers. Natural Resources Wales

The Dee is also important to United Utilities’ own operations:

* Its 2024 Water Resources Management Plan identifies Lake Vyrnwy and the River Dee as sources supplying Cheshire and Merseyside.
* It describes the Dee and Vyrnwy aqueducts as connected to the Thirlmere and Haweswater systems by two-way pipelines.
* Those connections allow water resources in the northern and southern parts of the network to be balanced. corporate.unitedutilities.com

That last point is particularly important for the strategic analysis. The value of the Welsh water system is not confined to the water delivered through a single aqueduct. It is also part of a regional network that can move water between resource areas.

10.3 Can we quantify the Dee’s contribution to United Utilities?

Not with the same confidence as Vyrnwy’s 12%.

United Utilities’ public-facing water-supply information says:

* More than two-thirds of its regional water comes from Cumbrian reservoirs, the Pennines and Lake Vyrnwy.
* Approximately one-quarter comes from rivers, including the Dee.
* The remainder comes from boreholes. Water to your tap

But the one-quarter river figure covers all river sources, not the Dee alone.

United Utilities’ 2024 ESG reporting gives a more precise breakdown by source type:

Source type	Daily water input	Share
Reservoirs	1,235.38 Ml/d	62.35%
Rivers	613.32 Ml/d	30.96%
Groundwater	132.64 Ml/d	6.69%

These figures are for the company’s reported water inputs, not a breakdown of Welsh versus English sources. They do not allow us to isolate the Dee’s contribution. United Utilities - Corporate

There is, however, an important operational figure in the WRMP24 technical report.

United Utilities identifies 109.3 Ml/d of non-potable bulk supply demand from the Dee, including a 28 Ml/d raw-water export to Dŵr Cymru Welsh Water. This is a planning demand figure, not a measurement of the entire Dee abstraction or the amount delivered to North West households. corporate.unitedutilities.com

That 28 Ml/d export is an interesting reminder that the Dee system is not simply a one-way Welsh-to-English resource transfer.

10.4 How dependent is the wider North West?

We need to be careful about geographical generalisation.

Area	Evidence of Welsh-water dependency
Cheshire	Strong: Vyrnwy aqueduct supplies the area; Dee is also part of the regional resource system
Merseyside	Strong: Vyrnwy aqueduct supplies approximately one million customers across Cheshire and Merseyside
Greater Manchester	Connected to the wider United Utilities resource system, including the Dee/Vyrnwy links, but not directly equivalent to the Vyrnwy-dependent area
Lancashire	Primarily served through the wider regional network, with major Cumbrian and Pennine sources
Cumbria	Predominantly dependent on local Cumbrian sources, rather than Welsh transfers

United Utilities describes its regional supply system as serving approximately 7.3 million customers. Its Strategic Resource Zone covers more than 98% of the population served and integrates the different aqueduct and local-source systems. unitedutilities.com +1

So the right conclusion is not that all North West England relies equally on Welsh water. It is that Welsh water is a substantial part of the regional supply architecture, with especially concentrated importance in Cheshire and Merseyside.

10.5 A further strategic point: the Dee is governed through shared arrangements

The River Dee is not simply a reservoir and pipeline controlled by one water company.

Its operation involves:

* Natural Resources Wales.
* The Environment Agency.
* United Utilities.
* Dŵr Cymru Welsh Water.
* Hafren Dyfrdwy.
* Severn Trent.
* The Canal & River Trust.

The Dee General Directions regulate abstraction and reservoir releases. During dry conditions, abstraction reductions are progressively applied to protect reservoir storage and river flows. corporate.unitedutilities.com +1

This has constitutional relevance.

An independent Wales would have sovereignty over its territory and important water infrastructure, but the Dee is a cross-border river system. Its long-term management would require cooperation and legally robust arrangements with English users and regulators.

It would not be sensible to frame this as Wales simply turning off a tap. The river’s environmental needs, existing abstraction arrangements, reservoir operations and downstream users all matter.

10.6 What about resilience and replacement?

The Vyrnwy Aqueduct Modernisation Programme is itself evidence of the importance of this infrastructure.

United Utilities is undertaking an eight-year programme to refurbish 139 km of pipework, with the stated aim of maintaining supply to around one million customers and improving the reliability and quality of water delivered. unitedutilities.com +1

The WRMP24 also confirms that the Vyrnwy and Dee systems are integrated with other regional sources, allowing the company to balance supplies across its network. corporate.unitedutilities.com

However, we still lack a current public assessment of:

* How much Vyrnwy water could be replaced by other sources during a prolonged interruption.
* How much Dee-derived supply could be replaced.
* Whether replacement capacity would be available during a simultaneous regional drought.
* The costs and time required to replace either resource permanently.

Temporary network resilience should not be confused with an equivalent alternative source of water.

10.7 Corrected wording for the master document

Current dependency of North West England on Vyrnwy and Dee supplies: Welsh water remains a substantial component of the North West’s water-supply infrastructure, with particularly important dependencies in Cheshire and Merseyside. United Utilities reported in March 2025 that the Vyrnwy Aqueduct accounts for approximately 12% of its raw water across the North West and supplies around one million customers across Cheshire and Merseyside. Its three parallel pipelines have a stated maximum capacity of 210 million litres per day. The River Dee is a separate but interconnected strategic resource. Its regulated supply system, supported by Llyn Celyn, Llyn Brenig and Llyn Tegid, supports abstraction by several water companies and the Canal & River Trust. Natural Resources Wales describes the Dee as a strategic source of potable water for nearly three million people across its wider supply area; this is not a United Utilities customer figure. United Utilities’ regional network connects the Vyrnwy and Dee aqueducts to the Thirlmere and Haweswater systems, allowing resources to be balanced across the North West. Although the company reports that approximately one-quarter of its water comes from rivers, this includes sources other than the Dee, and a precise current percentage attributable to the Dee has not been established. The evidence therefore supports a substantial Welsh-water dependency, especially in Cheshire and Merseyside, but does not justify treating the whole North West as equally dependent on Vyrnwy and Dee supplies. The Dee’s cross-border regulation arrangements also mean that its strategic value must be assessed alongside environmental obligations and cooperation with English water users.

10.8 Fact-check status

Claim	Finding
Vyrnwy supplies a significant share of North West water	
Vyrnwy serves around one million customers	
Dee is a strategic drinking-water resource	
Dee supports several water companies and other users	
Vyrnwy and Dee are integrated into the wider regional network	
The whole North West depends equally on Welsh water	
A precise combined Vyrnwy/Dee percentage is established	
Long-term replacement capacity is known	

10.9 The next useful evidence request

For the next research pass, I would seek a source-by-source breakdown from United Utilities covering its 2024–25 and 2025–26 actual raw-water abstractions:

* Lake Vyrnwy.
* River Dee.
* Other Welsh sources.
* Cumbrian reservoirs.
* Pennine reservoirs.
* Other rivers.
* Groundwater.

That would allow us to calculate the actual Welsh contribution to the North West’s supply, rather than relying on capacity figures and broad source categories.

Fact-check conclusion: Vyrnwy’s 12% share and the Dee’s strategic importance are well supported. The combined Welsh contribution to United Utilities’ current supply remains unquantified. We should keep those as separate evidence entries until actual source-level abstraction figures are available.”.   

“Fact-check 11: The precise relationship between the Severn–Thames Transfer and Welsh water sources

A direct Welsh source is identified — but the scheme is more complicated than a simple Welsh-to-English pipeline

The central finding: Lake Vyrnwy is explicitly part of the proposed Severn–Thames Transfer system. The Elan Valley is not identified as a direct source in the published Gate 2 design.

The scheme combines:

* Natural flows in the River Severn.
* Existing water licensed to United Utilities from Lake Vyrnwy, redirected through new infrastructure.
* Other support sources in England, including treated wastewater and reductions in existing abstractions.

It is therefore inaccurate to describe the whole scheme as either an entirely English water transfer or a straightforward additional abstraction from Wales.

11.1 The proposed route

The basic transfer route is:

* Water is made available in the River Severn catchment.
* It is abstracted from the River Severn near Deerhurst in Gloucestershire.
* A proposed pipeline, or an alternative involving restored Cotswold canals, carries it towards Culham in Oxfordshire.
* It is released into the River Thames for abstraction further downstream.

The proposed interconnector has been assessed at capacities of up to 500 million litres per day, although that is a design capacity, not a guaranteed volume of water available to transfer. Thames Water +2

11.2 The Welsh component: Lake Vyrnwy

The November 2022 Gate 2 technical design identifies up to 180 Ml/d of water already licensed to United Utilities from Lake Vyrnwy as a potential source for the scheme.

It divides this into two components:

Component	Proposed arrangement	Volume
Direct reservoir release	Release into the head of the River Vyrnwy	25 Ml/d
Aqueduct diversion	Water sent through the existing Vyrnwy Aqueduct to Oswestry, then through a new bypass pipeline to the River Severn	155 Ml/d
Total	Existing licensed Vyrnwy water	180 Ml/d

The bypass pipeline is intended to carry water from Oswestry to the River Severn, rather than allowing the diverted volume to travel down the lower River Vyrnwy. The design was changed partly to protect environmentally designated habitats in the River Vyrnwy. severntrent.com +2

The proposed Vyrnwy route

Lake Vyrnwy, Wales

Existing United Utilities licensed resource

25 Ml/d

Direct release

River Vyrnwy

155 Ml/d

Via aqueduct to Oswestry

New bypass pipeline

River Severn

Additional flow available for downstream abstraction

Deerhurst → Culham → River Thames

Proposed transfer towards South East England

Illustrative simplification of the Gate 2 design, not a complete hydraulic schematic. severntrent.com +1

11.3 Does this mean additional water is being taken from Wales?

This is where the wording needs care.

Severn Trent explicitly states:

“No additional water will be taken from Wales.” Severn Trent Plc

The companies’ explanation is that the scheme would use water already taken from Vyrnwy for North West England, redirecting some of it towards the Severn and Thames during transfer operations.

The distinction is:

* Not proposed: a new abstraction from an additional Welsh reservoir or a new allocation of water from Wales.
* Proposed: redirecting an existing licensed Lake Vyrnwy resource, changing where that water goes and how it is used.
* Required alongside it: replacement water resources and infrastructure to maintain United Utilities’ North West customer supplies.

The third point is crucial. The North West Transfer (NWT) scheme is designed to provide alternative water resources and modifications to the Vyrnwy Aqueduct so that North West customers can continue receiving supplies when Vyrnwy water is being traded elsewhere.

United Utilities’ Gate 2 report says that its maximum proposed 205 Ml/d transfer would require 167 Ml/d of new treated-water output to compensate for the resulting supply deficit. unitedutilities.com +1

In other words, the arrangement is not simply to take water away from North West customers and send it south. It is a proposed inter-regional water-trading system involving replacement sources, new infrastructure and changes to existing water movements.

But it does mean that an existing Welsh water resource would be contributing to supply in South East England.

11.4 What other sources feed the proposed transfer?

The Gate 2 design identifies several additional sources. These are not all Welsh.

Source	Proposed contribution	Geographic relationship
Lake Vyrnwy	Up to 180 Ml/d	Wales
Shrewsbury redeployment	25 Ml/d reduction in existing abstraction	England
Mythe abstraction reduction	15 Ml/d	England
Minworth treated wastewater	Up to 115 Ml/d	England
Netheridge treated wastewater	Up to 35 Ml/d	England

These are potential source-support elements, not a promise that all these volumes would be delivered simultaneously. The scheme’s operation depends on river flows, environmental restrictions, available sources and the timing of drought conditions. severntrent.com +1

The main River Severn transfer also relies on natural river flow. This matters geographically: the Severn catchment includes substantial Welsh headwaters and tributaries as well as English catchments. Water abstracted at Deerhurst therefore comes from a cross-border river system, not an exclusively English-origin water resource. GOV.UK +1

That is a different relationship from deliberately releasing water from Lake Vyrnwy.

11.5 What about Elan Valley, the Dee and Clywedog?

The distinction is important for our resource inventory.

Welsh resource	Directly identified as a Gate 2 STT source?	Finding
Lake Vyrnwy	Yes	Explicitly identified, up to 180 Ml/d in the Gate 2 design
Elan Valley reservoirs	No	Not identified as a direct STT source
River Dee	No	Not identified as a direct STT source
Llyn Clywedog	Not as a direct source	Part of wider Severn river regulation, rather than a separately identified STT source

The Severn’s wider regulation system does involve reservoirs in Wales, including Clywedog and Vyrnwy. Their existing role in managing river flows should not be confused with the specific additional source elements identified in the STT design. severntrent.com +1

So we should not describe the STT as a proposal to transfer Elan Valley or Dee water to the Thames. That would conflate separate water systems and existing supply arrangements.

11.6 Is the scheme actually going ahead?

Not as an approved, committed construction project.

The UK Government’s April 2025 parliamentary answer confirmed:

* The scheme had progressed through Gate 2.
* It was continuing to be developed towards Gate 3.
* It was not then a preferred option in the water companies’ water resource management plans.
* It remained a contingency option, with a possible transfer of up to 500 Ml/d during drought conditions. UK Parliament

There is also a newer development: the three water companies submitted a letter on changes to the scheme since Gate 2 in April 2026, published by Ofwat on 11 May 2026. The project design should therefore be treated as evolving, rather than assuming every 2022 Gate 2 quantity remains the final configuration. Ofwat +1

11.7 What this means for Welsh strategic-resource accounting

I would record this as a separate case from the existing Elan and Vyrnwy supply arrangements.

It demonstrates a potential additional form of strategic value:

The value of an existing Welsh water resource being made available for a different regional purpose through a wider network of infrastructure and water-resource trading.

There are at least four distinct values to record:

* Resource value: the existing licensed Vyrnwy water allocation.
* Infrastructure value: the aqueduct, proposed bypass pipeline and River Severn–Thames interconnector.
* Replacement-resource value: the new North West sources needed to maintain United Utilities’ customer supply.
* System value: the additional drought resilience potentially provided to the South East through the integrated network.

We should also record the environmental conditions and the implications for the River Vyrnwy and River Severn. The proposed system is subject to environmental assessment, licensing and regulatory approval; it is not an unrestricted right to redirect water.

11.8 Corrected wording for the master document

The precise relationship between the Severn–Thames Transfer and Welsh water sources: The proposed Severn–Thames Transfer (STT) is a regional drought-resilience scheme involving natural flows in the River Severn, additional source-support measures and a proposed interconnector carrying water from the Severn to the Thames. Lake Vyrnwy is explicitly identified as a Welsh source in the published Gate 2 design. That design envisages using up to 180 million litres per day of water already licensed to United Utilities from Lake Vyrnwy: 25 Ml/d released directly into the River Vyrnwy and 155 Ml/d diverted through the existing Vyrnwy Aqueduct to Oswestry and then through a proposed bypass pipeline into the River Severn. The companies describe this as using existing Vyrnwy water rather than taking additional water from Wales. However, the arrangement would redirect an existing Welsh water resource towards the South East during transfer operations, and requires alternative water resources and infrastructure to maintain United Utilities’ North West customer supplies. The associated North West Transfer scheme is designed to provide those replacement resources. Other STT support elements include reduced abstractions at Shrewsbury and Mythe and treated wastewater from Minworth and Netheridge in England. The main River Severn transfer also uses natural flows from a cross-border catchment, including water originating in Welsh headwaters, although this is distinct from the explicit Lake Vyrnwy source contribution. The published Gate 2 design does not identify the Elan Valley reservoirs or River Dee as direct STT sources. The scheme remains a developing contingency option rather than an approved operational transfer, and its design has been subject to further review since Gate 2. The distinction should therefore be maintained between existing Welsh water exports, the proposed redirection of existing Vyrnwy water, natural flows in the cross-border Severn catchment and new replacement resources developed elsewhere in the North West.

11.9 Fact-check status

Claim	Finding
The STT directly involves Lake Vyrnwy	
The Gate 2 design uses up to 180 Ml/d of existing licensed Vyrnwy water	
The scheme proposes a new additional abstraction from Wales	
Existing Vyrnwy water would be redirected towards the Severn and Thames	
North West replacement resources are required	
Elan Valley is a direct STT source	
The Dee is a direct STT source	
The natural Severn catchment includes Welsh-origin flows	
The scheme is approved and operational	

The key distinction for the constitutional and economic analysis: the STT does not simply propose taking a new volume of water from Wales. It proposes changing the destination of an existing Welsh water resource, supported by replacement resources elsewhere. That is still strategically relevant to Wales, but it is a different proposition from a new abstraction or a new Welsh export.

Sources:
