“Fact-check 12: Existing cross-border legal and regulatory arrangements

Substantial arrangements verified

England and Wales already have a formal legal and administrative framework for managing shared water resources. It is more developed than informal cooperation between neighbouring authorities: it includes statutory duties on both governments, a formal intergovernmental protocol, joint river-basin management, cross-border regulatory cooperation and specific legislation for important river systems.

But it is not one unified water authority, nor does it give English water companies an unconditional entitlement to Welsh water.

That distinction matters to the independence question. The existing arrangements show that cross-border water management is workable, but they do not establish what the legal relationship would be between two independent states.

12.1 The central instrument: the Intergovernmental Water Protocol

The Intergovernmental Protocol on Water Resources, Water Supply and Water Quality was agreed in November 2017 under section 50 of the Wales Act 2017 and came into force on 1 April 2018. GOV.UK Assets +2

Its purpose is reciprocal:

* Welsh government decisions and actions should not cause serious adverse effects on water resources, supply or quality in England.
* UK government decisions and actions should not cause serious adverse effects on water resources, supply or quality in Wales.
* Both administrations must communicate, consult and cooperate where their decisions may affect the other side of the border.

The protocol requires the administrations to:

* Alert each other to relevant policy developments and planned actions.
* Consult and consider the other administration’s evidence and views.
* Make reasonable efforts not to undermine the other administration’s responsibilities.
* Maintain working-level relationships between relevant departments.
* Escalate disagreements through official and ministerial channels.

It also has a dispute procedure. If a dispute cannot be resolved through discussion, it can be escalated through the intergovernmental dispute-resolution machinery. Pending resolution, the disputed function must be suspended, or, where there is inaction, exercised in a way that mitigates the serious adverse impact. GOV.UK Assets

This is more than a statement of goodwill. Section 50(5) of the Wales Act 2017 requires the Welsh Ministers and Secretary of State to exercise their functions in accordance with the protocol, unless it is revoked by agreement.

Section 51 also imposes reciprocal duties: Welsh Ministers must have regard to the interests of consumers in England, and the Secretary of State must have regard to consumers in Wales. Legislation.gov.uk +1

What it does not do: it does not give English consumers ownership of Welsh water, guarantee a particular transfer volume, or establish an unrestricted right for an English water company to abstract water in Wales.

12.2 Who regulates what?

The present system divides responsibilities geographically and by function.

Institution	Main responsibility
Welsh Government	Water policy, planning consent and devolved water-resource functions in Wales
Defra / UK Government	Water policy and relevant government functions in England
Natural Resources Wales (NRW)	Environmental regulation, abstraction licensing and water-resource management in Wales
Environment Agency (EA)	Environmental regulation, abstraction licensing and water-resource management in England
Ofwat	Economic regulation of water and sewerage companies in England and Wales
Drinking Water Inspectorate (DWI)	Drinking-water quality regulation
Consumer Council for Water	Consumer representation and complaints

The important point is that the water-company boundaries do not necessarily follow the national border. Some undertakers operate across England and Wales, reflecting historical water networks and river catchments. The Senedd’s research briefing describes this as one of the most complicated aspects of the devolution settlement. Senedd Cymru +2

Planning powers are also relevant. The UK Government confirmed in May 2025 that planning consent for water-resource infrastructure projects in Wales, including reservoirs, is devolved to the Welsh Government. Cross-border projects should involve the relevant administrations and regulators. UK Parliament

12.3 Water-resource management plans must account for cross-border effects

Water companies have statutory duties to plan for secure supplies to their customers.

Their Water Resources Management Plans (WRMPs) set out how they intend to balance supply and demand over the long term. These plans are scrutinised by regulators, with cross-border matters requiring cooperation between the regulators and relevant administrations.

There is particularly useful confirmation in a parliamentary answer dated 15 July 2026. Responding to a question about English water companies obtaining water from Wales, the Government stated:

* Water companies must prepare statutory WRMPs.
* Where cross-border matters arise, regulators on both sides of the border scrutinise the plans.
* Decisions on those plans are made in consultation with the relevant administration. UK Parliament

This is direct evidence that the current arrangements recognise the interests of both sides when English companies plan their future use of Welsh resources.

It is also a useful distinction between consultation and consent: the parliamentary answer confirms consultation with the relevant administration, but does not establish a general Welsh veto over every English water-resource proposal.

12.4 Cross-border abstraction licensing

The Environment Agency and NRW have arrangements for dealing with competing abstraction proposals in shared catchments.

Under current Environment Agency guidance, published in November 2025:

* The Environment Agency identifies whether proposals compete for the same available water.
* In a cross-border catchment, it checks with NRW.
* If competing proposals exist, the EA and NRW work together to determine which proposals should receive licences, if any.
* They can consider splitting or sharing the available resource, provided this is environmentally acceptable, enforceable and does not adversely affect existing rights. GOV.UK

This is important because it shows that water allocation is already treated as a cross-border management issue.

It also shows that a water company’s commercial demand is not, by itself, sufficient to establish an entitlement to abstract water. Environmental conditions, existing users and available flows remain relevant.

12.5 The Dee has particularly developed joint arrangements

The Dee is a particularly useful example because it has a bespoke statutory management system.

The Dee and Clwyd River Authority Act 1973 provides the legal basis for the Dee Regulation System.

According to NRW’s draft Dee River Basin Management Plan for 2027–2033:

* NRW and the Environment Agency are jointly responsible for managing the Dee Regulation System.
* The Dee Consultative Committee oversees the regulation of river flows.
* Reservoir releases in the upper catchment support drinking-water abstraction, modified flood response, fisheries, recreation and pollution-related benefits.
* The Dee Steering Committee brings together environmental regulators and four water companies to monitor water quality.
* The catchment upstream of Chester Weir is a designated Water Protection Zone. Natural Resources Wales

The draft plan also identifies the Dee as a strategic drinking-water source for nearly three million people.

This is a significant example of established joint governance rather than simply two regulators exchanging information when something goes wrong.

12.6 The Severn also has formal cross-border regulation

The Severn system has its own statutory and operational arrangements.

* The Clywedog Reservoir Joint Authority Act 1963 establishes the joint authority framework for the reservoir.
* The Severn Regulation system uses releases from Llyn Clywedog, Lake Vyrnwy and the Shropshire Groundwater Scheme to support river flows.
* The Environment Agency manages the relevant flow requirements in consultation with NRW.
* The Severn River Basin Management Plan is led by the Environment Agency, working with NRW on the cross-border catchment. ymgynghori.cyfoethnaturiol.cymru +3

A particularly current example is the 2026 River Severn drought-order application.

The Environment Agency and NRW jointly applied for an ordinary drought order to modify the Clywedog statutory release requirements and the Shropshire Groundwater Scheme licence. The purpose was to conserve reservoir storage during exceptional drought conditions while maintaining more sustainable river flows. ymgynghori.cyfoethnaturiol.cymru

This demonstrates that joint arrangements are not merely theoretical: they can be used for actual operational decisions under difficult conditions.

12.7 River-basin management plans

There is also a broader environmental-planning framework.

The Environment Agency and NRW coordinate river-basin management across catchments that cross or follow the England–Wales border.

The two important examples for our research are:

* Dee River Basin District: jointly managed by NRW and the Environment Agency, with separate responsibilities for coordinating future water planning in Wales and England.
* Severn River Basin District: led by the Environment Agency, with NRW collaborating on the Welsh and cross-border parts.

These plans cover water quality, ecological condition, pollution pressures, abstraction, protected habitats and measures to improve the water environment. Natural Resources Wales +2

This means the strategic value of Welsh water cannot be considered independently of the environmental duties attached to the river systems.

12.8 What would happen under Welsh independence?

This is where we must distinguish the existing arrangements from a future settlement.

The present protocol is an agreement between the UK Secretary of State and Welsh Ministers, operating within the constitutional structure of the United Kingdom. It is not an international treaty between two sovereign states.

Consequently, we should not assume that it would automatically continue unchanged after Welsh independence.

An independence settlement would need to establish new arrangements, potentially covering:

Subject	Existing arrangement	Issue for an independent Wales
Water-resource policy	Devolved Welsh and UK government responsibilities	Separate sovereign policy and coordination
Abstraction licences	EA and NRW cooperate in cross-border catchments	Mutual recognition, coordination and enforcement
Dee regulation	Joint statutory management	Continuity or replacement of joint authority arrangements
Severn regulation	Existing statutory releases and regulatory cooperation	Reservoir operations and cross-border river-flow obligations
Water-company planning	Cross-border WRMP scrutiny	Continued consultation and agreed planning procedures
Water quality	Joint monitoring and river-basin planning	Shared standards, data and pollution-response arrangements
Reservoir construction	Welsh planning consent, with cross-border consultation	Agreements on impacts and downstream interests
Drought management	Existing joint regulatory mechanisms	Emergency powers, priority rules and dispute resolution

Some of the existing statutory arrangements could potentially be preserved through transitional legislation or a bilateral agreement. Others might need replacement or amendment.

The Dee and Clywedog arrangements are especially important because they are grounded in specific legislation, rather than depending solely on the general intergovernmental protocol.

I would not assume that independence automatically extinguishes every existing right, licence or statutory mechanism. Equally, I would not assume that UK institutions could continue exercising powers inside Wales without an agreed legal basis.

12.9 What this means for the Welsh strategic-resource account

I would add a distinct category:

Cross-border governance and institutional dependency.

For each water system, record:

* The physical resource and its location.
* The current owner and operator.
* The relevant abstraction licences and statutory rights.
* The environmental regulator or regulators.
* The water companies and receiving populations.
* Existing statutory and intergovernmental arrangements.
* Emergency and drought-management provisions.
* The legal changes required under alternative constitutional arrangements.

This is important because Wales’s strategic contribution is not just the water itself. It also includes the reservoirs, river catchments, flow-regulation infrastructure, environmental management and institutional arrangements that make reliable cross-border supply possible.

12.10 Corrected wording for the master document

Existing cross-border legal and regulatory arrangements: England and Wales currently have a formal framework for managing shared water resources, water supply and water quality. The Intergovernmental Protocol on Water Resources, Water Supply and Water Quality, made under section 50 of the Wales Act 2017 and in force since April 2018, requires the Welsh Ministers and UK Secretary of State to communicate, consult and cooperate where decisions may have cross-border effects. The protocol includes a dispute-resolution process and reciprocal duties to have regard to consumers on the other side of the border. Water companies prepare statutory Water Resources Management Plans, and the UK Government confirmed in July 2026 that cross-border matters in these plans are scrutinised by regulators on both sides and considered in consultation with the relevant administration. Natural Resources Wales and the Environment Agency also cooperate on abstraction licensing and river-basin management in shared catchments. The River Dee has particularly developed statutory arrangements under the Dee and Clwyd River Authority Act 1973, including joint management of river regulation, reservoir releases and water-quality monitoring. The Severn has its own reservoir and river-flow regulation arrangements, including the Clywedog Reservoir Joint Authority Act 1963 and joint operational work by the Environment Agency and NRW. These arrangements demonstrate established institutional cooperation over cross-border water resources, but do not give English water companies unconditional rights to Welsh water. Under a hypothetical Welsh independence settlement, the existing UK intergovernmental protocol could not simply be assumed to continue unchanged. New bilateral or multilateral arrangements would need to address abstraction rights, reservoir operations, environmental standards, drought management, water-company planning, dispute resolution and the continuity of existing statutory river-management systems.

12.11 Fact-check status

Claim	Finding
A formal England–Wales water protocol exists	
The protocol has statutory backing	
Cross-border WRMPs receive regulatory scrutiny on both sides	
EA and NRW cooperate on cross-border abstraction	
The Dee has bespoke joint statutory arrangements	
The Severn has formal reservoir and flow-regulation arrangements	
English water companies have unconditional rights to Welsh water	
The current protocol would automatically survive independence	
Future independent-state arrangements would require negotiation	

Sources

* House of Lords written answer, 15 July 2026: cross-border water-resource planning⁠￼.
* Environment Agency: Water abstraction licences and competing proposals (2025)⁠￼.
* NRW: Draft Dee River Basin Management Plan 2027–2033⁠￼.
* NRW: River Severn drought-order environmental assessment (2026)⁠￼.
* Severn River Basin Management Plan and cross-border catchments⁠￼.
* Ofwat: Legislation governing the water industry⁠￼.

The key constitutional finding: cross-border water cooperation is already a normal and legally structured part of managing Welsh resources. Independence would not make cooperation unnecessary; it would change the legal basis on which that cooperation takes place. The existing Dee and Severn arrangements offer concrete starting points for examining what a successor agreement might need to preserve.”.  

“Fact-check 13: What international water law would apply following Welsh independence

The central point is that an independent Wales would not be starting from a legal vacuum. International water law already contains rules for rivers and aquifers shared by more than one state. Those rules could provide a framework for relations between Wales and England (as part of the continuing UK).

However, we need to distinguish three things:

* Customary international law — rules that may bind states independently of whether they have joined a particular treaty.
* Multilateral water treaties — binding obligations only where the relevant state is party to them, subject to their terms and any succession questions.
* A Wales–UK agreement — a bilateral settlement that could specify how shared rivers, reservoirs, abstractions and infrastructure would actually be managed.

The distinction is important: it would be unsafe to say that every international water treaty currently applicable to the UK would automatically apply to an independent Wales. Treaty succession would need to be examined treaty by treaty.

13.1 The two principal international water conventions

There are two major international instruments worth examining.

A. The 1992 UNECE Water Convention

Its full title is the Convention on the Protection and Use of Transboundary Watercourses and International Lakes.

It establishes a framework for:

* Preventing, controlling and reducing transboundary impacts.
* Using shared waters reasonably and equitably.
* Cooperating between states sharing rivers, lakes and groundwater.
* Establishing joint bodies and river-basin arrangements.
* Monitoring water quality and exchanging information.

It is no longer restricted to European states. Since 2016, all UN member states can accede. UNECE +2

Important finding about the UK: the UK signed the Convention on 18 March 1992 but has not ratified it. It is therefore a signatory, not a party. treaties.un.org

B. The 1997 UN Watercourses Convention

Its full title is the Convention on the Law of the Non-Navigational Uses of International Watercourses.

This is a global treaty covering non-navigational uses of international watercourses, including water supply, agriculture, industry, hydropower and environmental protection.

Its central provisions include:

Article	Principle
5	Equitable and reasonable utilisation
6	Factors used to assess equitable utilisation
7	Obligation to take appropriate measures to prevent significant harm
8	General obligation to cooperate
9	Regular exchange of relevant data and information
10	No inherent priority between uses, with special regard to vital human needs
11–12	Information and notification about planned measures
20–21	Protection of ecosystems and prevention of pollution

mandates.un.org +1

Important finding about the UK: the UK has not acceded to this Convention either. The UK Government’s own treaty publication confirms that. GOV.UK +1

13.2 The principles that would apply even without treaty membership

This is probably the most important part of the answer.

The absence of UK membership of those two conventions does not mean that the UK, or a future independent Wales, would be free to use shared rivers however it wished.

The UNECE identifies three central principles as part of customary international law:

* Equitable and reasonable utilisation.
* Prevention of significant transboundary harm.
* Cooperation between states sharing water resources.

These principles are reflected in both international water conventions. UNECE +1

Customary international law is important because it can bind states independently of whether they have ratified a particular treaty.

What would that mean in practice?

* Wales could use water resources within its territory.
* England could use the parts of shared watercourses within its territory.
* Neither state would have an unrestricted right to use shared water resources to the serious detriment of the other.
* Both would have duties to cooperate and take relevant cross-border impacts into account.

This is not a system of equal quantities divided automatically down the middle. Nor does it give either state absolute control over the entire river.

13.3 What counts as equitable and reasonable use?

Article 6 of the 1997 Convention provides a useful analytical framework.

It lists factors including:

* Geography and hydrology.
* Climate and ecology.
* Social and economic needs.
* Population dependent on the watercourse.
* Existing and potential uses.
* Conservation and environmental protection.
* The availability of alternative resources.

The factors must be considered together; no single factor automatically determines the result. mandates.un.org +1

For Wales, this is particularly interesting.

Imagine a dispute over water from the River Severn. An assessment could consider:

Factor	Welsh interest	English interest
Geography	Welsh headwaters and upper catchment	Downstream catchment and communities
Population	Welsh communities and water users	English communities and water users
Existing use	Reservoirs, abstraction, agriculture, ecology	Downstream abstraction, agriculture, industry
Environmental needs	Welsh river habitats and ecological flows	Downstream habitats and water quality
Alternatives	Welsh storage, demand management and other supplies	English reservoirs, transfers and alternative sources
Future demand	Welsh population and economic development	English population and economic development

The existence of established English users would be relevant. So would Welsh future demand, environmental requirements and the availability of alternative supplies.

This is not a legal guarantee that existing English transfers would continue unchanged.

But neither could Wales simply disregard the downstream consequences of a major alteration to a shared river.

13.4 The no-significant-harm principle is not an absolute veto

A particularly important legal qualification.

The obligation to prevent significant harm is generally understood as an obligation of due diligence: a state must take appropriate measures to prevent foreseeable significant harm. It is not an absolute guarantee that no harm will ever occur.

The International Court of Justice has considered this principle in cases involving shared water resources and environmental impacts. Its reasoning recognises the importance of equitable utilisation alongside the prevention of harm. Justia Law +2

This matters because an English objection to a Welsh water project would not automatically make that project unlawful.

The relevant questions would include:

* What harm is foreseeable?
* How serious is it?
* What preventive measures are available?
* Has the state assessed the consequences properly?
* Is the proposed use equitable and reasonable in the circumstances?

Where a project carries a risk of significant transboundary environmental harm, international case law also supports a requirement for environmental impact assessment and, where appropriate, notification and consultation. icj-web.leman.un-icc.cloud +1

13.5 Would Wales inherit the UK’s existing treaty obligations?

This requires care. It is a separate question from customary international law.

The 1978 Vienna Convention on Succession of States in Respect of Treaties addresses what happens to treaty obligations when states separate or new states emerge.

Its Article 34 provides for treaty continuity when part of a state separates to form a new state. However, the Convention’s relationship with customary international law is not straightforward: the UN’s own legal materials caution that Article 34 cannot necessarily be treated as reflecting customary international law in every case. Wikisource, the free online library +1

There is another important distinction:

* The Convention’s term newly independent State is specifically defined around a previously dependent territory.
* An independent Wales emerging from a devolved part of the UK would not straightforwardly fit that definition.
* The UK is not a party to the 1978 Succession Convention.

Consequently, we should not present treaty succession as an automatic, settled answer.

For the two water conventions, the practical position is clearer:

Instrument	UK position today	Likely position for independent Wales
1992 UNECE Water Convention	Signed, not ratified	Could accede in its own right
1997 UN Watercourses Convention	Not a party	Could accede in its own right
Customary international water law	Applicable to the UK	Would apply to Wales as a state, subject to the relevant rules

The first two would be matters for a new Welsh government to pursue through accession. The customary principles would not depend on that decision.

13.6 An important distinction: shared rivers versus exported water

This is especially relevant to the earlier research on Lake Vyrnwy and the Elan Valley.

International watercourse law is principally concerned with watercourses that physically form a connected system across state territories.

Under Article 2 of the 1997 Convention, an international watercourse is a system of connected surface waters and groundwater, parts of which are situated in different states. mandates.un.org

That gives us two different situations:

Situation A: A river crosses the border

The Severn, Dee and Wye systems are relevant examples. If Wales became independent, the relevant cross-border sections would become international watercourses.

The international principles of equitable utilisation, prevention of significant harm and cooperation would be directly relevant.

Situation B: Water is abstracted in Wales and transported to England

Lake Vyrnwy is the important example.

Water is abstracted from a reservoir in Wales and conveyed through infrastructure to customers in north-west England.

That is not automatically the same legal situation as two states sharing a natural river. The pipeline transfer is an inter-state water supply arrangement, involving infrastructure, abstraction rights, contracts and potentially treaty rights.

International watercourse principles could still be relevant where the abstraction affects a shared watercourse or its ecology. But they do not, by themselves, establish an unconditional right to continue exporting a particular volume through a pipeline.

This distinction should be retained in the research.

13.7 Would historic water transfers give England a permanent entitlement?

Not automatically.

Existing uses are expressly relevant to equitable and reasonable utilisation. The population dependent on a watercourse is also relevant.

That means the long-standing supply of water from Vyrnwy or the Elan Valley could be an important factor in negotiations.

But the existence of a historic transfer does not, by itself, establish a permanent and unrestricted international entitlement to that water.

The International Court of Justice’s 1997 Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros judgment is useful here. It rejected the unilateral appropriation of a shared water resource and emphasised the need to respect equitable and reasonable sharing. Justia Law +1

For the Welsh case, the right questions are therefore:

* What existing legal rights would remain valid?
* Which are domestic licences, contracts or statutory arrangements?
* Which would need to be renegotiated?
* What role would historic use play in an equitable allocation?
* What would happen if Welsh domestic demand increased?
* What would happen during prolonged drought?

These are not all questions that international water law answers on its own.

13.8 What a Wales–UK water treaty could establish

An independent Wales and the continuing UK could negotiate a bilateral water agreement, potentially building on the existing Dee and Severn institutions.

I would expect it to address:

Subject	Possible treaty provision
Water allocation	Agreed abstraction and transfer arrangements
Existing infrastructure	Access, operation, maintenance and ownership
Drought	Shared shortage planning and emergency arrangements
Environmental flows	Minimum flows and ecological protections
Reservoir operations	Joint operating rules and release requirements
Data	Hydrological monitoring and information sharing
New infrastructure	Notification, assessment and consultation
Disputes	Joint commission, mediation and arbitration
Compensation	Agreed rules for damage, interruption or infrastructure use
Review	Periodic review as climate and population demands change

The UNECE Water Convention is particularly useful as a framework for this. It encourages parties sharing transboundary waters to establish specific agreements and joint bodies rather than relying on general principles alone. UNECE +1

13.9 Corrected wording for the master document

International water law following Welsh independence: An independent Wales would be subject to the customary principles of international water law governing shared rivers and other international watercourses. The central principles include equitable and reasonable utilisation, cooperation between states sharing water resources, and the obligation to take appropriate measures to prevent significant transboundary harm. These principles are recognised as customary international law and therefore do not depend solely on membership of a particular water treaty. Two major instruments provide more detailed frameworks: the 1992 UNECE Convention on the Protection and Use of Transboundary Watercourses and International Lakes, and the 1997 UN Convention on the Law of the Non-Navigational Uses of International Watercourses. The UK signed the 1992 Convention but has not ratified it, and is not a party to the 1997 Convention. An independent Wales could pursue accession to both in its own right. The detailed rules of treaty succession should not be assumed to produce automatic Welsh membership or inheritance of every UK treaty obligation. Under international water law, the Severn, Dee and Wye systems would be relevant as shared international watercourses, requiring equitable and reasonable utilisation, cooperation and appropriate protection against significant cross-border harm. Existing English water use and dependent populations would be relevant factors in assessing equitable utilisation, but historic use would not automatically establish an unrestricted or permanent entitlement to Welsh water. Water exported through infrastructure such as the Lake Vyrnwy aqueduct presents an additional question: pipeline transfers, abstraction rights, contracts and infrastructure access would need to be addressed through the applicable legal arrangements and, potentially, a new bilateral treaty. A Wales–UK water agreement could preserve and develop existing Dee and Severn cooperation while establishing new arrangements for allocation, drought management, environmental flows, infrastructure, data-sharing and dispute resolution.

13.10 Fact-check status

Claim	Finding
International water law would apply to shared rivers after independence	
Equitable and reasonable utilisation is a central principle	
Prevention of significant transboundary harm is a customary principle	
Cooperation is a customary principle	
The UK is a party to the 1992 Water Convention	
The UK is a party to the 1997 Watercourses Convention	
Wales could accede to the two conventions independently	
Existing English water use would be irrelevant	
Historic English water use guarantees permanent access	
International law automatically guarantees continued Vyrnwy pipeline exports	
A bilateral Wales–UK water agreement would be useful	

Sources

* UNECE — Introduction to the Water Convention⁠￼.
* UN Treaty Collection — 1992 Water Convention status and UK signature⁠￼.
* UK Government — 1997 UN Watercourses Convention treaty publication⁠￼.
* UN — 1997 Watercourses Convention, full text⁠￼.
* UNECE — Water Convention frequently asked questions and customary international law⁠￼.
* UN International Law Commission — treaty succession and customary-law limitations⁠￼.
* ICJ — Gabčíkovo–Nagymaros Project judgment (1997)⁠￼.
* ICJ — Pulp Mills on the River Uruguay (2010)⁠￼.

My assessment: this is a strong addition to the Welsh strategic-resource analysis. It gives us a legal basis for saying that Welsh water is not simply an internal UK resource whose future use is determined unilaterally by Westminster. Once independence occurred, shared rivers would be governed by international obligations between neighbouring states. But those obligations would require equitable cooperation, not simply transfer the existing domestic settlement into international law.”.  

“you did not break me: I’m still fighting for peace.”.  

“Fact-check 14: Latest sheep and cattle populations in Wales

June 2025 headline figures verified

The latest published Welsh Government agricultural survey gives the following figures:

Sheep and lambs

8.81m

Up 0.7% year on year

June 2025

Cattle and calves

1.0795m

Down 1.0% year on year

June 2025

GOV.WALES +1

14.1 Sheep

The June 2025 survey recorded 8.81 million sheep and lambs, compared with approximately 8.75 million in June 2024.

Longer-term context:

* The Welsh flock peaked at approximately 11.8 million in 1999.
* Numbers declined over the following decade.
* The June 2023 figure, just under 8.7 million, was the lowest since 2011.
* Numbers rose slightly in 2024 and 2025. GOV.WALES

Important newer-data qualification: Wales also operates a compulsory annual sheep and goat inventory on 1 December. The UK livestock dataset was updated on 2 October 2026 to incorporate new Welsh sheep data for December 2025. That is a newer reference date than the June 2025 survey, although it is a different measurement series. I could verify the update but not independently retrieve the exact Wales-specific total from the published spreadsheet. GOV.UK +2

So I would retain 8.81 million as the verified June survey figure, but not describe it as the latest available sheep inventory without that qualification.

14.2 Cattle

The June 2025 survey recorded 1,079,500 cattle and calves, down 1.0% from June 2024.

The breeding-herd breakdown is particularly useful:

Category	June 2025
Dairy females aged 2+ years that had calved	254,100
Beef females aged 2+ years that had calved	133,700
Total cattle and calves	1,079,500

The dairy breeding herd increased by 1.1%, while the beef breeding herd fell by 5.0%. GOV.WALES

There is an important data-quality distinction here: cattle figures are derived from the Cattle Tracing System, which covers all cattle holdings. Sheep figures come from the agricultural survey and, separately, the annual keeper inventory. GOV.WALES +1

14.3 What this means for the resource account

The headline numbers establish that Wales has a substantial livestock population, particularly sheep. But animal numbers alone are not a measure of the economic value retained in Wales.

For the wider analysis, I would keep separate records for:

* Livestock stock: sheep, lambs, cattle and calves.
* Breeding capacity: breeding ewes, dairy herd and beef breeding herd.
* Production flows: meat, milk, wool and associated products.
* Economic value: farm-gate output, processing, exports and employment.
* Land dependency: grazing land, common land and the role of upland farming.
* Resilience: domestic food production, breeding stock and agricultural supply-chain capacity.

This avoids treating 8.81 million sheep as though they represent 8.81 million units of immediately available food production. The distinction between breeding animals, lambs, finishing stock and other sheep matters.

14.4 Corrected wording for the master document

Welsh sheep and cattle populations: The Welsh Government’s June 2025 agricultural survey recorded 8.81 million sheep and lambs in Wales, an increase of 0.7% on the previous year, and 1,079,500 cattle and calves, a decrease of 1.0%. Within the cattle population, the dairy breeding herd, measured as females aged two or over that had calved, stood at 254,100, while the equivalent beef breeding herd stood at 133,700. The dairy herd increased by 1.1% over the year, while the beef herd decreased by 5.0%. These figures establish the scale of Welsh livestock, but should be distinguished from breeding capacity, annual meat and milk production, farm-gate value and the wider economic contribution of agricultural supply chains. A separate Welsh sheep and goat inventory is taken each December; the UK livestock dataset was updated in October 2026 to include new Welsh sheep data for December 2025, so the June 2025 sheep figure should be labelled by its reference date rather than presented as the latest inventory count.

Sources

* Welsh Government — Survey of agriculture and horticulture: June 2025⁠￼.
* Defra — Livestock populations in the United Kingdom (updated October 2026)⁠￼.
* Welsh Government — Sheep and goat annual inventory guidance⁠￼.

My recommendation: use the June 2025 figures as the main comparable agricultural survey baseline, and keep the December 2025 sheep inventory as a separate, newer data point pending extraction of its exact Welsh total.”.  

“
